ABA ADVANCED Exam Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (The thyroid cartilage.): The cricothyroid membrane spans between the inferior border of the thyroid cartilage superiorly and the superior border of the cricoid cartilage inferiorly. The thyroid isthmus lies inferior to the cricoid cartilage, over the second through fourth tracheal rings, and is not a boundary of this membrane. The hyoid bone sits well above the thyroid cartilage and is unrelated to cricothyrotomy landmarks. Correctly identifying the thyroid cartilage as the superior boundary prevents inadvertent puncture too high or too low during an emergency surgical airway.
  2. The correct answer is A (Vagally mediated reflex bradycardia from peritoneal stretch.): Rapid peritoneal distension during insufflation activates stretch receptors that trigger a vagally mediated reflex, producing sudden bradycardia or even asystole. This reflex is distinct from direct nerve trauma and typically resolves with desufflation, atropine, or glycopyrrolate. Hypercarbia from CO2 insufflation more typically causes tachycardia and hypertension via sympathetic stimulation, not bradycardia. Neuromuscular blockers do not potentiate beta blockade and are unrelated to this reflex.
  3. The correct answer is A (MAC is decreased by approximately 6 percent per decade after age 40.): MAC declines progressively with advancing age, roughly 6 percent per decade beyond age 40, reflecting age-related changes in central nervous system sensitivity to anesthetics. This effect applies broadly to volatile agents, not selectively to nitrous oxide. Reduced cardiac output in elderly patients actually speeds inhalational induction by increasing the rate of rise of alveolar concentration, but this does not raise MAC. Anesthetic dosing should therefore be reduced in elderly patients to avoid excessive depth and hemodynamic instability.
  4. The correct answer is C (The respiratory compensation is appropriate for the degree of metabolic acidosis.): Winter's formula predicts an expected PaCO2 of 1.5 times the bicarbonate plus 8, plus or minus 2, which for a bicarbonate of 13 gives a predicted range of about 25.5 to 29.5 mmHg. The measured PaCO2 of 28 mmHg falls within this predicted range, confirming appropriate respiratory compensation rather than a second primary disorder. Respiratory compensation for metabolic acidosis is never complete enough to fully normalize pH, so the persistently low pH of 7.28 is expected. A PaCO2 outside the predicted range would instead indicate a superimposed respiratory acid-base disturbance.
  5. The correct answer is A (Ipsilateral phrenic nerve blockade causing hemidiaphragm paralysis.): The phrenic nerve runs on the anterior surface of the anterior scalene muscle immediately adjacent to the brachial plexus at the interscalene level, so local anesthetic spread here produces ipsilateral hemidiaphragm paralysis in nearly all patients. Recurrent laryngeal nerve involvement can occur but produces unilateral, not bilateral, vocal cord dysfunction and is less consistent. Pneumothorax is a much greater risk with supraclavicular or infraclavicular approaches, since the interscalene injection occurs above the level of the pleural dome. Femoral nerve involvement is unrelated to an interscalene injection site in the neck.
  6. The correct answer is B (Delivery of a dangerously high, potentially overdosed anesthetic concentration.): Variable-bypass vaporizers are calibrated for a specific agent's vapor pressure, and desflurane's vapor pressure is nearly triple that of isoflurane at room temperature, so far more vapor would enter the bypass chamber than the dial setting intends. This mismatch would deliver a dangerously high, overdosed concentration rather than a diluted one. Agent-specific keyed filling systems exist precisely to prevent this scenario, but a variable-bypass vaporizer itself has no mechanism to detect or correct for the wrong agent. This is why desflurane requires an electrically heated, pressurized vaporizer rather than a standard variable-bypass design.
  7. The correct answer is D (Sugammadex directly encapsulates rocuronium, allowing reversal even from deep blockade.): Sugammadex is a modified gamma-cyclodextrin that forms a tight, inactive complex with rocuronium molecules, reversing even profound neuromuscular blockade that neostigmine cannot reliably reverse. Sugammadex and its rocuronium complex are actually renally excreted, so effect can be prolonged in significant renal impairment, though it remains usable in moderate impairment as described here. Sugammadex does not inhibit acetylcholinesterase at all, which is neostigmine's mechanism, and it does not require an antimuscarinic co-administration since it does not produce cholinergic side effects. Its unique encapsulation mechanism is what allows reliable reversal at train-of-four counts too low for neostigmine to work well.
  8. The correct answer is C (90 percent.): Sensitivity is the proportion of patients with true disease who test positive, calculated as true positives divided by all patients with the condition, or 90 divided by 100, giving 90 percent. Specificity would instead be calculated from the disease-free group, 810 true negatives divided by 900 total without injury, which also happens to equal 90 percent here, but that is a separate calculation from sensitivity. Confusing sensitivity with specificity or with positive predictive value is a common error when only total counts are given rather than a full outcome table. A highly sensitive test like this one is useful for ruling out disease when the result is negative.
  9. The correct answer is B (T4.): Although the surgical incision itself is near the T10 to T12 dermatomes, peritoneal traction and exteriorization of the uterus stimulate visceral afferents traveling with the vagus and phrenic nerves, requiring a sensory block extending to approximately T4, the nipple line, for patient comfort. A block only to T10 would anesthetize the skin incision but often leaves patients with significant visceral pain or nausea during uterine manipulation. L1 and T6 levels are both too low to reliably prevent this visceral discomfort during cesarean delivery. This is why obstetric anesthesiologists routinely target a T4 sensory level rather than the lower level that would suffice for a purely cutaneous procedure.
  10. The correct answer is D (The circle system allows rebreathing of exhaled gas after CO2 absorption.): The circle system's unidirectional valves and CO2 absorbent canister permit exhaled gas to be recirculated safely after carbon dioxide removal, allowing much lower fresh gas flows than a Mapleson circuit. Mapleson D circuits have no CO2 absorbent and instead rely on adequately high fresh gas flow to flush exhaled carbon dioxide out of the system, actually requiring higher flows than a circle system during spontaneous ventilation. The circle system is fully compatible with both spontaneous and controlled mechanical ventilation, making that option incorrect. Soda lime or another absorbent is a defining feature of the circle system, not the Mapleson D.
  11. The correct answer is A (The agent with a pKa of 7.6, since more of the drug exists in the nonionized form at physiologic pH.): Local anesthetics must cross the lipid nerve membrane in their uncharged, nonionized form, and the closer an agent's pKa is to physiologic pH of 7.4, the greater the nonionized fraction able to diffuse into the axon. An agent with a pKa of 7.6 will have a higher nonionized fraction at tissue pH than one with a pKa of 8.1, translating into a faster onset of blockade. The ionized fraction is needed intracellularly to bind the sodium channel, but the nonionized fraction is rate limiting for membrane penetration and therefore onset speed. This relationship is a key reason agents like lidocaine, with a pKa near 7.9, have a faster onset than bupivacaine, with a pKa near 8.1.
  12. The correct answer is C (Selection bias.): Selection bias occurs when the groups being compared differ systematically in ways related to the outcome, here because healthier and younger patients were preferentially assigned to regional anesthesia, confounding any true treatment effect on delirium. Recall bias applies to differential accuracy of remembered exposures, typically in case-control studies asking about past exposures, which is not the issue described here. Observer bias involves differential assessment of outcomes by an investigator aware of group assignment, not differential assignment to groups. Lead-time bias is specific to screening studies where earlier detection falsely appears to prolong survival, which does not apply to this delirium comparison.
  13. The correct answer is A (The vein is compressible with gentle probe pressure while the artery remains patent.): The internal jugular vein has a thin, compliant wall and low intraluminal pressure, so gentle ultrasound probe pressure collapses it while the thicker-walled, higher-pressure carotid artery remains patent and open. The artery, not the vein, has the thick muscular wall, so that description is reversed in one of the distractor options. The internal jugular vein typically lies anterolateral to the carotid artery, not medial and posterior, though positional variation exists. Respirophasic size variation and Valsalva-induced distension are characteristic of the vein, not the artery, since venous return is affected by intrathoracic pressure changes.
  14. The correct answer is B (Malignant hyperthermia with a hypermetabolic state.): A progressive, sustained rise in end-tidal CO2 despite unchanged minute ventilation, combined with masseter rigidity and tachycardia after succinylcholine, is a classic early presentation of malignant hyperthermia driven by uncontrolled skeletal muscle hypermetabolism. Esophageal intubation typically shows an absent or rapidly diminishing capnography waveform rather than a rising trend, since no true gas exchange is occurring. A circuit leak would dilute and generally lower measured EtCO2 rather than cause a steady rise. Inadequate neuromuscular blockade could cause movement artifact but would not explain this sustained, escalating hypermetabolic capnography pattern.
  15. The correct answer is D (Intravenous 20% lipid emulsion bolus followed by infusion.): Lipid emulsion therapy is the specific treatment for local anesthetic systemic toxicity, thought to work by creating a lipid sink that sequesters the lipophilic local anesthetic away from cardiac tissue and by providing energy substrate to myocardium. Naloxone reverses opioid effects and has no role in local anesthetic toxicity. High-dose vasopressin is specifically discouraged in LAST resuscitation guidance, which recommends reduced epinephrine doses rather than vasopressin. Calcium chloride is not a standard specific therapy for LAST, unlike its role in hyperkalemia or calcium channel blocker overdose.
  16. The correct answer is A (The monitor correctly distinguishes aware from unaware patients 95 percent of the time when comparing random pairs.): The area under an ROC curve represents the probability that a randomly selected positive case will have a higher test score than a randomly selected negative case, so an AUC of 0.95 means the monitor correctly ranks an aware patient above an unaware patient 95 percent of the time. This value does not equal sensitivity or specificity individually, since those are single-point measures dependent on a chosen threshold, while AUC summarizes performance across all thresholds. Positive predictive value depends heavily on disease prevalence in the population and is not directly given by the AUC. The AUC also says nothing about the actual incidence of awareness in the population being monitored.
  17. The correct answer is A (Ligamentum flavum.): The needle passes sequentially through skin, subcutaneous tissue, the supraspinous ligament, the interspinous ligament, and finally the ligamentum flavum, which offers a characteristic increase in resistance immediately before the sudden loss of resistance signaling entry into the epidural space. The supraspinous and interspinous ligaments are traversed earlier in the needle's path and do not border the epidural space directly. The posterior longitudinal ligament lies within the vertebral canal along the posterior surface of the vertebral bodies, anterior to the epidural space, and is not encountered during a posterior midline approach. Recognizing the ligamentum flavum's density is central to correctly identifying the loss-of-resistance endpoint.
  18. The correct answer is D (Methemoglobinemia causing pulse oximetry to read falsely near 85 percent.): Benzocaine is a well-recognized cause of methemoglobinemia, and methemoglobin absorbs light at both measured wavelengths in a standard two-wavelength pulse oximeter, driving the displayed saturation toward approximately 85 percent regardless of the true oxygen saturation. The calculated saturation from a standard blood gas machine does not detect methemoglobin, which is why co-oximetry is needed to directly measure methemoglobin fraction. Carbon monoxide poisoning causes a falsely high, not low, pulse oximeter reading because carboxyhemoglobin is misread as oxyhemoglobin. Peripheral vasoconstriction can cause signal dropout or inaccurate readings but would not produce this classic persistent reading near 85 percent unresponsive to supplemental oxygen.
  19. The correct answer is C (Remifentanil's context-sensitive half-time remains short regardless of infusion duration.): Remifentanil is metabolized by nonspecific plasma and tissue esterases, giving it a context-sensitive half-time of only a few minutes even after prolonged infusions, unlike fentanyl, whose context-sensitive half-time increases substantially with infusion duration due to accumulation in peripheral fat and muscle compartments. This means remifentanil actually provides essentially no residual postoperative analgesia once stopped, which is a disadvantage rather than an advantage in the immediate postoperative period. Remifentanil's elimination half-life is short, not long, which is precisely why it does not accumulate. Its ester-based metabolism is independent of hepatic or renal function, in contrast to fentanyl's dependence on hepatic cytochrome P450 metabolism.
  20. The correct answer is B (Treating the severe hyperkalemia with calcium, insulin and glucose, and consideration of dialysis.): A potassium of 7.2 mEq/L in a dialysis-dependent patient poses an immediate risk of life-threatening dysrhythmia and cardiac arrest, so it must be treated urgently with intravenous calcium for membrane stabilization plus insulin and glucose to shift potassium intracellularly, alongside consideration of emergent dialysis if time permits. The metabolic acidosis is a real finding but is secondary in urgency to the hyperkalemia and often improves with correction of the underlying potassium and renal issues. Succinylcholine is relatively contraindicated in significant hyperkalemia because it can transiently raise serum potassium further and precipitate arrhythmia. Proceeding to surgery without addressing a potassium this high risks intraoperative cardiac arrest.
  21. The correct answer is D (Unilateral recurrent laryngeal nerve injury.): Unilateral recurrent laryngeal nerve injury denervates most intrinsic laryngeal muscles on that side, causing the vocal cord to rest in a paramedian position, producing hoarseness from glottic incompetence without airway obstruction since the opposite cord can still compensate. Bilateral recurrent laryngeal nerve injury is far more dangerous, since both cords typically move toward the midline, causing stridor and potential airway obstruction requiring emergent intervention. The external branch of the superior laryngeal nerve supplies the cricothyroid muscle and affects voice pitch rather than causing a fixed paramedian cord position. The internal branch of the superior laryngeal nerve is purely sensory to the supraglottic larynx and would not produce this motor deficit.
  22. The correct answer is A (The index can be unreliable in the presence of significant electromyographic muscle activity.): Frontal EMG activity from facial or scalp muscle tone can artifactually raise the processed index number, making it appear the patient is lighter than they actually are, a well-recognized limitation of the algorithm. This index is derived from EEG signal characteristics reflecting hypnotic depth and does not directly assess analgesic adequacy, which is influenced separately by opioid dosing and surgical stimulus. There is no fixed threshold value that guarantees or excludes awareness, since individual variability and monitor artifact both affect the number. Large randomized trials have not demonstrated a clear mortality benefit from this depth-of-anesthesia monitoring compared with standard end-tidal agent monitoring.
  23. The correct answer is B (Extrajunctional acetylcholine receptor upregulation can cause life-threatening hyperkalemia.): After denervation injuries such as spinal cord injury, extrajunctional acetylcholine receptors proliferate across the muscle membrane, and succinylcholine's depolarizing action at these numerous additional receptors can cause massive potassium efflux and life-threatening hyperkalemia, a risk beginning around 24 to 72 hours after injury and persisting for many months. This risk is unrelated to pseudocholinesterase levels, which affect duration of action but not this hyperkalemic mechanism. Succinylcholine does not directly trigger autonomic dysreflexia, though the hyperkalemic response and its cardiac effects are the primary safety concern here. Onset time for succinylcholine is not meaningfully delayed by denervation, so a nondepolarizing agent should be used instead specifically to avoid the hyperkalemia risk, not an onset problem.
  24. The correct answer is C (Apply continuous positive airway pressure to the nonventilated lung.): Applying low-level CPAP to the nonventilated, collapsed lung provides supplemental oxygen to alveoli in that lung without significantly reinflating it or disrupting the surgical field, directly addressing the shunt causing progressive hypoxemia during one-lung ventilation. PEEP applied to the nonventilated lung, rather than CPAP, would not deliver oxygen to that lung's alveoli in the same way and is not the standard first intervention. Abandoning one-lung ventilation entirely is an overly drastic step before simpler measures such as CPAP or lung isolation confirmation have been tried. Decreasing tidal volume on the ventilated, dependent lung would not address the shunt from the nonventilated lung and could worsen atelectasis and hypoxemia in the ventilated lung instead.
  25. The correct answer is C (L3-L4.): The conus medullaris typically terminates around the L1 to L2 vertebral level in most adults, so needle insertion at L3-L4 or lower provides a wide safety margin below the spinal cord's termination, minimizing risk of direct cord trauma. Inserting at T12-L1 or even L1-L2 carries meaningfully higher risk of needle contact with the conus in patients whose termination is lower than average. While L5-S1 is also below the conus and technically safe from a cord-injury standpoint, it is not typically the landmark chosen since the L3-L4 or L2-L3 interspace, corresponding to the level of the iliac crests, is the standard clinically taught and used interspace. Choosing an interspace clearly below the conus is the key safety principle regardless of exact interspace selected.
  26. The correct answer is A (Approximately 330 liters.): For a compressed gas like oxygen that remains above its critical temperature at room conditions, Boyle's law applies and pressure is directly proportional to the remaining volume of gas, so a pressure that has fallen to half of the full 2000 psi, or 1000 psi, indicates approximately half of the original 660 liters remain, roughly 330 liters. This linear pressure-to-volume relationship is unique to gases like oxygen that behave as true gases at ambient temperature, unlike nitrous oxide, which exists partly as a liquid in its cylinder and does not show a linear pressure drop until nearly all liquid has vaporized. Estimating 660 liters would incorrectly assume the cylinder is still full despite the pressure having dropped by half. Understanding this relationship is essential for anticipating when a cylinder will run out during patient transport.
  27. The correct answer is B (Compound A, formed when sevoflurane degrades in contact with strong base CO2 absorbents.): Sevoflurane reacts with strong base CO2 absorbents, particularly under low fresh gas flow conditions that allow the compound to accumulate, forming compound A, which has shown dose-dependent nephrotoxicity in rat models, prompting flow rate recommendations in some jurisdictions. Carbon monoxide production from desiccated absorbent is a real phenomenon but is classically associated with desflurane and isoflurane, which contain a difluoromethoxy group, rather than being the primary concern with sevoflurane at low flows. Fluoride ion is indeed released during sevoflurane hepatic metabolism, but clinically significant fluoride-induced nephrotoxicity has not been demonstrated in humans despite measurable fluoride levels. Trifluoroacetic acid is a metabolite specifically associated with older agents such as halothane, not sevoflurane.
  28. The correct answer is D (5.): Number needed to treat is calculated as the reciprocal of the absolute risk reduction, and here the absolute risk reduction is 40 percent minus 20 percent, or 0.20, so the number needed to treat equals 1 divided by 0.20, which is 5. This means, on average, 5 patients would need to receive prophylactic ondansetron to prevent one additional case of postoperative nausea and vomiting compared with placebo. Confusing relative risk reduction, which here would be 50 percent, with absolute risk reduction is a common error that leads to an incorrect NNT calculation. A lower NNT generally indicates a more clinically impactful intervention, and an NNT of 5 for a common, bothersome outcome like PONV is considered quite favorable.
  29. The correct answer is D (The hyoepiglottic ligament.): The Macintosh blade tip sits in the vallecula, the space between the base of the tongue and the epiglottis, and forward traction along the ligament connecting the hyoid bone to the epiglottis, the hyoepiglottic ligament, indirectly lifts the epiglottis to expose the glottic opening without directly touching the epiglottis. This is distinct from a Miller straight blade technique, which directly lifts the epiglottis itself. The thyrohyoid membrane connects the thyroid cartilage to the hyoid bone and is not the structure tensioned by vallecular pressure. The cricothyroid membrane and vocal ligaments are located below the level being manipulated during this maneuver and play no role in this indirect lifting mechanism.
  30. The correct answer is A (The distal esophagus, positioned behind the heart to reflect true core blood temperature.): A temperature probe placed in the distal third of the esophagus, positioned behind the heart, closely reflects core, or blood, temperature and is considered one of the most accurate and practical intraoperative core temperature monitoring sites for a patient under general anesthesia with a secured airway. Axillary temperature is a peripheral measurement influenced by local blood flow and positioning, making it less reliable as a true core measure. Liquid crystal skin strips estimate skin surface temperature, which can lag substantially behind true core temperature, especially during active warming or cooling. Oral sublingual temperature is not practical or accurate during general anesthesia with an endotracheal tube in place and is also affected by recent oral intake or mouth breathing in awake patients.
  31. The correct answer is B (It increases moderately due to drug accumulation in peripheral compartments.): Propofol's context-sensitive half-time does increase with longer infusion durations as drug accumulates in peripheral tissue compartments, though this increase is much more modest than seen with agents like fentanyl, which is why propofol remains a favored agent for prolonged infusions despite this accumulation. It does not remain completely unchanged, since some accumulation genuinely occurs even with propofol's rapid redistribution and clearance. Propofol metabolism is not meaningfully auto-induced over the course of a single anesthetic, unlike enzyme induction that can occur with chronic dosing of some other drugs. Context-sensitive half-time is a well-established, predictable pharmacokinetic concept modeled from population data, not an unpredictable value.
  32. The correct answer is C (Acute primary respiratory acidosis from opioid-induced hypoventilation.): The markedly elevated PaCO2 of 65 mmHg with a correspondingly low pH of 7.25 indicates a primary respiratory acidosis, and the bicarbonate of 27 is only minimally elevated, consistent with the expected acute renal compensation of roughly 1 mEq/L rise per 10 mmHg rise in PaCO2 rather than the larger bicarbonate rise seen in chronic compensation. This pattern, together with somnolence following opioid administration and accompanying hypoxemia, points to opioid-induced hypoventilation as the acute cause requiring prompt intervention such as naloxone or ventilatory support. Calling this a chronic, fully compensated process requiring no intervention would be dangerous, since the pH remains significantly acidemic and the compensation is clearly incomplete. A primary metabolic alkalosis would present with an elevated pH and elevated bicarbonate as the primary abnormality, which is the opposite of what is shown here.
  33. The correct answer is A (Nerve, artery, vein, from lateral to medial.): Within the femoral triangle at the level of the inguinal ligament, the standard anatomic arrangement from lateral to medial follows the mnemonic NAVEL, meaning nerve, artery, vein, and then lymphatics, which is why ultrasound scanning laterally to medially reveals the femoral nerve first, followed by the pulsatile femoral artery, then the compressible femoral vein. Reversing this order, such as placing the vein lateral to the artery, would misidentify the anatomy and risk needle placement into the wrong structure. The femoral nerve lies lateral to the artery, not between the artery and vein, distinguishing it from options that misplace the nerve medially. Correctly recalling this arrangement helps operators identify the nerve, which often appears as a hyperechoic structure lateral to the artery, before needle advancement.
  34. The correct answer is D (Higher frequency improves image resolution but decreases depth of penetration.): Higher frequency ultrasound waves have shorter wavelengths, which improves spatial resolution and allows finer discrimination of superficial structures, but these higher frequency waves also attenuate more rapidly in tissue, reducing the depth to which they can effectively image. This is why linear high-frequency probes are chosen for superficial structures like the brachial plexus at the interscalene or supraclavicular level, while curvilinear low-frequency probes are preferred for deeper structures such as neuraxial or deep plexus blocks. The relationship between frequency, resolution, and penetration is an inherent physical tradeoff, not something that can be avoided by probe selection. Choosing the wrong probe frequency for the target depth results in either poor resolution of a deep structure or an inability to adequately visualize a target that is beyond the effective penetration depth.
  35. The correct answer is B (Glycopyrrolate counteracts the muscarinic side effects produced by neostigmine's cholinesterase inhibition.): Neostigmine inhibits acetylcholinesterase nonselectively, increasing acetylcholine at both the desired nicotinic neuromuscular junction and at unwanted muscarinic sites throughout the body, causing bradycardia, salivation, and bronchospasm, so an antimuscarinic agent like glycopyrrolate is co-administered to block these muscarinic effects while leaving the beneficial nicotinic reversal intact. Glycopyrrolate has no direct effect on neuromuscular blocking agents or their receptors and does not itself reverse blockade. It also does not enhance neostigmine's nicotinic activity, since its mechanism is purely antimuscarinic antagonism rather than potentiation. Neostigmine is not a recognized trigger of malignant hyperthermia, so this rationale is incorrect.
  36. The correct answer is C (Withdraw and reposition the catheter under fluoroscopic or repeat imaging guidance.): A catheter tip that has looped back into the internal jugular vein rather than terminating in the superior vena cava is malpositioned but the line itself and the venous access are functional, so the appropriate management is to reposition the catheter tip into correct location rather than remove the entire line and start over. Infusing vasoactive medications through a malpositioned catheter is inappropriate because a tip sitting in the internal jugular vein poses risks including vessel wall irritation and inaccurate central pressure readings. Since no pneumothorax or mediastinal widening is described, there is no indication for emergent CT chest to evaluate for aortic injury. Discarding a functioning catheter and reattempting an entirely new venipuncture is unnecessary when simple repositioning can correct the malposition.
  37. The correct answer is A (C3 through C5.): The phrenic nerve arises from the anterior rami of cervical spinal nerves C3, C4, and C5, commonly remembered by the mnemonic that C3, 4, and 5 keep the diaphragm alive, and provides the primary motor innervation to the diaphragm. A thoracic epidural with cephalad local anesthetic spread reaching these cervical roots, though the phrenic nerve itself originates well above the thoracic epidural catheter tip, can rarely still impair diaphragmatic function through high spread or direct effects on accessory respiratory muscles. C6 through C8 and C1 through C2 do not correspond to the phrenic nerve's actual root levels. T1 through T3 are too caudal and are more associated with intercostal and upper limb innervation rather than diaphragmatic function.
  38. The correct answer is D (The oxygen failure protection, or fail-safe, device, which senses low oxygen pressure and cuts off nitrous oxide flow.): The oxygen failure safety device senses a drop in oxygen supply pressure and proportionally or completely shuts off the flow of nitrous oxide and other non-oxygen gases, preventing delivery of a hypoxic gas mixture when oxygen pressure is lost. The pin index safety system prevents cylinders from being connected to the wrong yoke but plays no role in this pipeline pressure failure scenario. The vaporizer interlock system prevents more than one vaporizer from being active simultaneously, which is unrelated to oxygen supply failure. Unidirectional valves in the circle system direct gas flow appropriately through the breathing circuit but do not detect or respond to a loss of oxygen supply pressure.
  39. The correct answer is B (Faster onset of adequate intubating conditions but a substantially prolonged duration of blockade.): Doubling the rocuronium dose to 1.2 mg/kg shortens onset time to approximately 60 seconds, approaching that of succinylcholine, making it a useful alternative when succinylcholine is contraindicated, but this comes at the cost of a substantially prolonged duration of blockade, often 60 to 90 minutes or longer. Higher doses of nondepolarizing agents generally shorten onset by increasing the concentration gradient driving receptor occupancy more quickly, but total duration is prolonged because more drug must be metabolized or redistributed before recovery occurs. This dose-duration relationship means clinicians must be prepared for a longer period of paralysis, or plan for reversal with sugammadex, when using this higher dose strategy. Assuming no change in duration would risk inadequate recovery time before extubation.
  40. The correct answer is C (Case-control studies sample based on outcome status rather than following exposed groups forward, so true incidence and therefore relative risk cannot be directly calculated.): In a case-control study, subjects are selected based on whether they already have the outcome, here acute kidney injury, rather than being followed forward from exposure, so the true incidence rate of the outcome in exposed and unexposed groups cannot be calculated, making relative risk inappropriate and the odds ratio the standard reported measure instead. Odds ratios and relative risk converge and become numerically similar only when the outcome of interest is rare, but they are not mathematically identical in general. Sample size is not the determining factor for why odds ratio is used in this design. The choice of odds ratio versus relative risk is driven by study design, not by an inherent claim that one measure is always more accurate than the other.
  41. The correct answer is D (The lateral cord and the medial cord.): The median nerve is formed by two roots, one arising from the lateral cord and one from the medial cord of the brachial plexus, which join together anterior to the axillary artery to form the median nerve proper. The posterior cord instead gives rise to the axillary and radial nerves, not the median nerve, making any option involving the posterior cord for median nerve formation incorrect. The lateral cord alone gives rise to the musculocutaneous nerve and only the lateral contribution to the median nerve, not the complete nerve by itself. Understanding this dual-cord origin helps explain patterns of median nerve sparing or involvement with selective cord-level regional anesthesia blocks or injuries.
  42. The correct answer is A (Uneven alveolar emptying from heterogeneous airway resistance and time constants.): In obstructive airway disease such as bronchospasm, different lung units have markedly different time constants for emptying due to variable airway resistance, so alveoli with faster time constants empty first while others continue emptying later, producing a sloped rather than flat phase III alveolar plateau on the capnogram. Absorbent exhaustion causing rebreathing would raise the baseline of the waveform above zero during inspiration rather than changing the slope of the expiratory plateau. A sampling line leak would typically dilute the peak and lower the overall measured EtCO2 value rather than specifically produce an upsloping plateau shape. Esophageal intubation classically produces an absent, low, or rapidly disappearing waveform altogether rather than a sustained but sloped capnogram.
  43. The correct answer is C (Ester agents are metabolized to para-aminobenzoic acid, a known allergenic compound.): Ester local anesthetics are hydrolyzed by plasma cholinesterase into metabolites including para-aminobenzoic acid, or PABA, which is a recognized allergen responsible for most true immunologic reactions attributed to local anesthetics, explaining the much higher rate of genuine allergy with esters compared with amides. Amide agents are actually metabolized in the liver, not in plasma, which is a distinguishing pharmacokinetic feature separate from their lower allergenic potential. Both ester and amide local anesthetics contain an aromatic benzene ring as part of their basic structure, so the absence of a benzene ring is not the reason amides are less allergenic. Lipid solubility relates to potency and onset characteristics of local anesthetics, not to their allergic potential.
  44. The correct answer is B (If the study were repeated many times, 95 percent of calculated intervals would contain the true relative risk.): The frequentist interpretation of a 95 percent confidence interval is that if the same study were repeated many times, 95 percent of the confidence intervals calculated from those repeated samples would contain the true population parameter, in this case the true relative risk. Since this interval does not cross 1.0, meaning both bounds are below 1.0, the result is in fact statistically significant, making the statement about non-significance the opposite of correct reasoning. A confidence interval describes uncertainty around an estimated population parameter, not a probability distribution of outcomes for individual patients. The point estimate of 0.65 is the best single estimate from this particular study, but the confidence interval reflects the range of plausible values for the true effect, not a claim that the true value is exactly 0.65.
  45. The correct answer is D (The fresh gas inlet and valve arrangement of the Mapleson D circuit makes it more efficient during controlled ventilation, unlike the Mapleson A.): The Mapleson D circuit's fresh gas inlet near the patient and its adjustable pressure limiting valve positioned near the reservoir bag arrangement make it far more efficient, requiring lower fresh gas flows, during controlled positive pressure ventilation, while the Mapleson A configuration is comparatively inefficient in this mode and requires much higher flows to prevent rebreathing. The Mapleson A is actually the most efficient configuration during spontaneous ventilation, so their relative efficiencies genuinely reverse between spontaneous and controlled ventilation modes, but this reversal does not mean the Mapleson A retains any efficiency advantage in every mode as one option incorrectly states. Neither the Mapleson A nor the Mapleson D contains a carbon dioxide absorbent, since both circuits rely entirely on fresh gas flow rather than chemical absorption to eliminate exhaled carbon dioxide. This efficiency difference is a classic teaching point explaining why circuit selection may depend on the planned ventilation mode.
  46. The correct answer is C (The C8 and T1 nerve roots, which form the lower trunk supplying the ulnar hand and forearm.): interscalene blocks deposit local anesthetic at the C5-C6 level, reliably anesthetizing the shoulder and upper arm but frequently sparing the lower trunk contributions from C8 and T1 that supply the ulnar distribution. This is why interscalene blocks are excellent for shoulder surgery but unreliable for hand or forearm procedures. A supraclavicular or axillary approach targets the trunks or cords more distally and covers the C8-T1 territory more consistently. Anticipating this sparing pattern is essential when selecting a block for distal upper extremity surgery.
  47. The phrenic nerve runs on the anterior surface of the anterior scalene muscle and is more at risk during interscalene blocks than central line placement. The thoracic duct injury would present with lymphatic leak, not respiratory compromise. This proximity is why ultrasound guidance has reduced pneumothorax rates in subclavian access.

The dura mater lies anterior to the epidural space, not posterior to it. The posterior longitudinal ligament lies within the vertebral canal anterior to the dura, well beyond the epidural space. Recognizing the ligamentum flavum's characteristic resistance is the key tactile cue for correct needle placement.

The glossopharyngeal nerve supplies the posterior tongue, oropharynx, and vallecula but not the region below the epiglottis. Combining both blocks provides comprehensive topical anesthesia for awake intubation.
  50. The correct answer is B (The L4 vertebral body or the L4-L5 interspace, a level well below the termination of the adult conus medullaris.): Tuffier's line is a clinically useful surface landmark that typically crosses the spine at the L4 vertebral body or the L4-L5 interspace, well below the adult conus medullaris which usually terminates around L1-L2. Using this landmark helps the anesthesiologist select an interspace at or below L4-L5 for spinal needle insertion, minimizing the risk of spinal cord injury. Studies have shown that clinical estimation using this line can still be one or two levels higher than intended, which is why choosing an interspace clearly below L3-L4 is prudent. This anatomic safety margin is central to safe neuraxial technique.
  51. The correct answer is D (A temperature-compensating bypass valve that adjusts the splitting ratio.): variable-bypass vaporizers divide fresh gas flow between a bypass chamber and a vaporizing chamber, and a temperature-sensitive bimetallic strip or bellows automatically adjusts the splitting ratio as flow and temperature change, keeping output concentration constant despite evaporative cooling. There is no heating element in a standard variable-bypass vaporizer; that design is used in desflurane vaporizers instead, which heat the agent to a supraheated vapor state. Complete saturation regardless of flow is not achievable at very high flows without compensation. This automatic temperature and flow compensation is what distinguishes modern vaporizers from older, uncompensated designs.
  52. The correct answer is A (Its vapor pressure near ambient atmospheric pressure at room temperature.): desflurane has a boiling point of approximately 22.8 degrees Celsius, meaning its vapor pressure at typical room temperature is close to atmospheric pressure, so it would boil rather than vaporize predictably in a standard variable-bypass vaporizer. The Tec 6 style vaporizer heats the liquid desflurane to about 39 degrees Celsius and pressurizes it, delivering it as a metered gas that is then blended with fresh gas flow through an electronic valve. Its pungent odor is relevant to airway irritability during mask induction but does not explain the vaporizer design. The low blood-gas partition coefficient explains its rapid onset and offset but is unrelated to the vaporizer mechanism itself.
  53. The correct answer is B (Using fresh gas flows two to three times the patient's minute ventilation.): the Mapleson D circuit has poor efficiency for spontaneous ventilation because exhaled gas can mix with fresh gas near the patient, so high fresh gas flows, typically two to three times minute ventilation, are required to flush carbon dioxide out through the expiratory limb before it can be rebreathed. Adding an absorbent canister would convert the circuit into a circle system rather than a Mapleson circuit. Reducing tubing length does not meaningfully change the rebreathing dynamics of a D configuration. The fresh gas inlet in a Mapleson D is already positioned near the patient by design, which is precisely why high flows are needed during spontaneous breathing.
  54. The correct answer is D (Chemical absorption of carbon dioxide combined with unidirectional valves preventing rebreathing.): the circle system's soda lime chemically neutralizes exhaled carbon dioxide while the inspiratory and expiratory unidirectional valves ensure gas flows in one direction only, so exhaled gas passes through the absorbent before being rebreathed, allowing fresh gas flows as low as metabolic oxygen consumption. The reservoir bag simply accommodates variable inspiratory flow and does not itself remove carbon dioxide. Tubing diameter affects resistance but not the fundamental rebreathing prevention mechanism. This combination of chemical absorption and directional valves is the defining efficiency advantage of circle systems over Mapleson circuits.
  55. The correct answer is B (Poor peripheral perfusion from vasoconstriction reducing the pulsatile signal and producing an unreliable or falsely reassuring saturation reading.): pulse oximetry depends on detecting pulsatile arterial blood flow, and severe vasoconstriction from hypothermia or high-dose vasopressors reduces the pulsatile signal amplitude, which can cause the device to display an erroneously high or simply unreliable saturation rather than reflecting true arterial oxygenation. Carboxyhemoglobin does falsely elevate readings toward 100 percent but there is no history suggesting carbon monoxide exposure here. Methemoglobinemia typically drives readings toward the mid-80s regardless of true saturation, not toward a falsely normal 100 percent. When perfusion is poor, clinicians should trust arterial blood gas measurements over a questionable oximetry waveform.

The correct answer is A (Absorption of insufflated carbon dioxide across the peritoneal surface into the bloodstream.): the peritoneum is highly vascular and readily absorbs the insufflated carbon dioxide used to create pneumoperitoneum, increasing systemic carbon dioxide delivery to the lungs and raising end-tidal carbon dioxide even with stable minute ventilation. Increased dead space from reduced pulmonary blood flow would tend to widen the arterial to end-tidal gradient rather than simply raise end-tidal values from absorption. Exhausted absorbent would cause a progressively rising inspired baseline carbon dioxide on the capnogram, a distinct waveform finding. Diaphragmatic splinting from pneumoperitoneum affects ventilation mechanics but does not itself generate carbon dioxide.

The correct answer is A (Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, in which expiratory airflow obstruction produces heterogeneous alveolar emptying.): in obstructive airway disease, heterogeneous emptying of lung units with varying time constants produces a slurred, upsloping transition between phase II and the alveolar plateau, sometimes called a shark-fin pattern, reflecting delayed and uneven alveolar gas emptying. Esophageal intubation produces a capnogram with little or no carbon dioxide waveform at all rather than an abnormal shape. Cardiac arrest causes a sudden drop in end-tidal carbon dioxide due to loss of pulmonary blood flow but preserves waveform shape initially. Malignant hyperthermia classically causes a rapid and sustained rise in end-tidal carbon dioxide, not a change in waveform morphology.
  58. The correct answer is D (High-dose ketamine anesthesia, which produces a distinct EEG pattern that can cause the index to read falsely high.): processed EEG indices like the bispectral index are derived from algorithms trained predominantly on GABAergic agents such as propofol and volatile anesthetics, and ketamine's NMDA receptor antagonism produces a distinct EEG pattern with paradoxically preserved or increased high-frequency activity, causing the index to read higher than the true depth of anesthesia would suggest. Volatile agents like sevoflurane and isoflurane generally track processed EEG indices in the expected direction, though not perfectly. Propofol alone is actually one of the agents these monitors are best validated against. This limitation means processed EEG values should be interpreted cautiously whenever ketamine constitutes a significant component of the anesthetic.

Nasopharyngeal probes are influenced by inspired gas temperature and mouth breathing around the endotracheal tube cuff, which can introduce artifact. The esophageal probe does not contact cerebrospinal fluid, and the carotid sheath relationship pertains to more proximal esophageal or pharyngeal placement, not the accurate distal position. This distal esophageal location is why it is considered one of the more reliable core temperature monitoring sites during general anesthesia.
  60. The correct answer is D (Reduced depth of tissue penetration.): higher frequency ultrasound waves are attenuated more rapidly as they pass through tissue, which improves resolution of superficial structures but limits the depth to which the beam can effectively penetrate and return a usable signal. This is why high-frequency linear probes are chosen for superficial blocks like supraclavicular or interscalene approaches, while lower frequency curvilinear probes are used for deeper structures. Tissue heating and posterior acoustic enhancement are separate ultrasound phenomena not directly tied to the resolution-penetration tradeoff. Doppler function remains available across a range of frequencies and is not lost at higher settings.
  61. The correct answer is C (Acoustic shadowing.): bone strongly reflects and absorbs ultrasound waves, preventing significant energy from passing through to deeper tissues, which produces a dark, signal-free shadow directly beneath the bony surface on the ultrasound image. Posterior acoustic enhancement is the opposite phenomenon, occurring deep to fluid-filled structures that transmit sound well, producing a brighter than expected region. Reverberation artifact produces multiple equally spaced parallel lines from sound bouncing between two strong reflectors, commonly seen with needle tips or pleura. Mirror image artifact duplicates a structure on the far side of a strong reflector, often seen near the diaphragm, and is unrelated to bony shadowing.
  62. The correct answer is A (Rapid hydrolysis by nonspecific plasma and tissue esterases.): remifentanil contains an ester linkage that is rapidly broken down by nonspecific esterases present in blood and tissue, independent of hepatic or renal function, giving it a context-sensitive half-time that remains short and essentially constant regardless of infusion duration. This is fundamentally different from fentanyl or sufentanil, which rely on hepatic metabolism and accumulate with prolonged infusions due to redistribution from fat and muscle. Renal clearance plays a negligible role in remifentanil's termination of effect. This unique esterase-based metabolism is what allows remifentanil to be titrated precisely even during very long infusions.
  63. The correct answer is D (Sugammadex reverses neuromuscular block through direct encapsulation of rocuronium rather than relying on organ-based clearance for its onset of action.): sugammadex works by forming a tight, inactive complex with rocuronium molecules in the plasma, physically removing free rocuronium available to bind the neuromuscular junction, and this mechanism does not depend on hepatic enzymatic function to produce its reversal effect. Neostigmine works indirectly by inhibiting acetylcholinesterase to increase acetylcholine at the junction, and its reversal can be less predictable when baseline neuromuscular recovery is already impaired by comorbid illness. Sugammadex itself is primarily renally excreted essentially unchanged rather than hepatically metabolized. This direct chelation mechanism is why sugammadex reversal onset is not delayed by hepatic dysfunction in the way that other physiologic factors could theoretically slow recovery.
  64. The correct answer is B (Preferential inhibition of inhibitory cortical pathways at lower toxic concentrations, leading to unopposed excitatory neuronal activity.): at lower toxic plasma concentrations, local anesthetics preferentially block inhibitory neurons in the cortex, removing tonic inhibitory control and producing the excitatory phase of toxicity, including perioral numbness, tinnitus, and seizures, before higher concentrations eventually cause global central nervous system and cardiac depression. This is not a peripheral neuromuscular junction phenomenon, since local anesthetic systemic toxicity primarily reflects sodium channel blockade in the central nervous system and myocardium. Cerebral hypoperfusion from vasodilation is not the primary mechanism of the early excitatory seizure phase. Understanding this biphasic excitatory-then-depressant pattern is central to recognizing and managing local anesthetic systemic toxicity.
  65. The correct answer is D (It acts as a lipid sink, an expanded intravascular lipid phase that sequesters lipophilic bupivacaine away from cardiac tissue.): the leading proposed mechanism for lipid emulsion therapy is the lipid sink theory, in which the infused lipid creates an expanded intravascular lipid phase that draws highly lipophilic bupivacaine out of cardiac tissue and into the plasma compartment, reducing the free drug available to block cardiac sodium channels. Additional proposed mechanisms include providing an energy substrate to the myocardium, but direct receptor antagonism and enzyme induction are not established mechanisms. Chelation in the manner of sugammadex is a distinct and unrelated concept specific to that reversal agent. This lipid sink concept underlies the current recommendation to administer intravenous lipid emulsion promptly during severe local anesthetic systemic toxicity.
  66. The correct answer is B (Acute respiratory alkalosis with appropriate metabolic compensation.): the elevated pH with a low PaCO2 indicates a primary respiratory alkalosis from hyperventilation, and using the acute compensation rule of a 2 mEq/L decrease in bicarbonate for every 10 mmHg drop in PaCO2, a 12 mmHg decrease in PaCO2 would predict roughly a 2 to 3 mEq/L drop in bicarbonate, which closely matches the measured value of 22. This is consistent with an acute process rather than chronic, since chronic respiratory alkalosis would show a larger compensatory drop in bicarbonate over days as renal compensation matures. A primary metabolic alkalosis would present with an elevated rather than low bicarbonate. The clinical context of acute anxiety-driven tachypnea further supports an acute respiratory process with expected acute compensation.
  67. The correct answer is C (Inadequate respiratory compensation, suggesting a coexisting respiratory acidosis.): using Winter's formula, expected PaCO2 equals 1.5 times the bicarbonate plus 8, plus or minus 2, which for a bicarbonate of 15 predicts an expected PaCO2 of roughly 28.5 mmHg, so a measured PaCO2 of 45 mmHg is much higher than expected and indicates the patient is not compensating adequately, pointing to a coexisting respiratory acidosis, likely from fatigue, sedation, or inadequate ventilation in the trauma setting. If compensation were appropriate, the PaCO2 would be closer to the calculated expected value rather than elevated. A superimposed metabolic alkalosis would raise bicarbonate and pH, not explain an elevated PaCO2. Recognizing failed compensation using Winter's formula is essential to identifying a second, independent acid-base process.
  68. The correct answer is A (Sensitivity, the proportion of patients who truly have the disease who correctly test positive on the assay.): sensitivity is defined as the proportion of patients who truly have the disease who correctly test positive, so a test that identifies 95 percent of patients with a true myocardial infarction as positive has a sensitivity of 95 percent. Specificity instead describes the proportion of patients without disease who correctly test negative. Positive predictive value and negative predictive value both depend on disease prevalence in the tested population and describe the probability of disease given a positive or negative result, not the proportion of true positives or negatives correctly classified. A highly sensitive test is particularly useful for ruling out disease when the result is negative.
  69. The correct answer is B (The biomarker has poor discriminative ability, only slightly better than chance.): an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve of 0.5 represents no better discrimination than random chance, while 1.0 represents perfect discrimination, so a value of 0.62 indicates only modest improvement over chance and generally poor clinical usefulness for distinguishing patients who will develop acute kidney injury from those who will not. The area under the curve is a global summary measure across all possible cutoffs and does not by itself describe prevalence in the population or a specific sensitivity and specificity pairing. Most clinically useful diagnostic tests are considered to need an area under the curve well above 0.7 to 0.8 to be reliably informative. This modest value would generally prompt further biomarker refinement before clinical adoption.
  70. The correct answer is A (Confounding by indication.): confounding by indication occurs when the reason a treatment or exposure was chosen for a patient, such as underlying illness severity, is itself related to the outcome being studied, making it appear that the treatment itself caused a difference in outcome when the underlying patient characteristics actually drove the association. Recall bias refers to differential accuracy of remembering past exposures, typically relevant in case-control studies relying on patient recollection. Lead-time bias is specific to screening studies where earlier detection appears to prolong survival without truly changing the disease course. Observer performance bias relates to differences in how outcomes are assessed or measured rather than how patients were selected for treatment, making confounding by indication the correct concept here.
  71. The correct answer is C (External validity due to a nonrepresentative sample.): external validity refers to the degree to which study findings can be generalized beyond the specific population studied, and restricting enrollment to a single academic center's patient population, which may differ systematically in acuity, resources, and case mix from community or rural settings, limits the ability to extrapolate those findings broadly. Internal validity concerns whether the study accurately measures the effect within its own sample, which is a separate issue from generalizability. Sample size relates to statistical power, not the representativeness of the population. Construct validity concerns whether the study is truly measuring the concept it intends to measure, which is unrelated to the generalizability problem described here.
  72. The correct answer is B (The cricoid cartilage is the only complete cartilaginous ring in the airway, allowing it to compress the esophagus against the vertebral body.): unlike the thyroid cartilage or tracheal rings, which are incomplete or C-shaped posteriorly, the cricoid cartilage forms a complete ring, so posterior pressure applied to it can effectively compress the underlying esophagus against the cervical vertebral bodies, theoretically occluding it and reducing passive regurgitation. The thyroid cartilage sits superior to the cricoid and houses the vocal cords but is not used for this compression maneuver. The cricoid cartilage does lie anterior to the esophagus, and this anatomic relationship, not an ineffective or posterior position, is exactly why the maneuver was historically taught, even though its clinical efficacy has since been questioned in more recent literature. The thyroid cartilage is readily palpable as the laryngeal prominence, so that is not the distinguishing feature.
  73. The correct answer is A (A compressible structure that collapses with gentle transducer pressure and distends with a Valsalva maneuver.): veins are thin-walled, low-pressure structures that readily collapse with light probe pressure and distend with maneuvers that increase venous return or intrathoracic pressure, such as Valsalva or Trendelenburg positioning, distinguishing them from the noncompressible, pulsatile, thick-walled carotid artery. A pulsatile, noncompressible vessel with a thick wall describes the carotid artery, the structure to be avoided. Continuous high-velocity flow and triphasic Doppler waveforms are arterial characteristics, not venous ones. Confirming compressibility and venous flow characteristics before needle advancement is a key safety step in ultrasound-guided central venous access.
  74. The correct answer is B (Decreased hydrolysis of succinylcholine by plasma pseudocholinesterase.): succinylcholine is normally rapidly metabolized by plasma pseudocholinesterase, and patients with genetic variants producing atypical forms of this enzyme have markedly reduced hydrolysis capacity, causing the drug to persist at the neuromuscular junction far longer than usual and producing prolonged paralysis. Succinylcholine's termination of action does not depend on hepatic metabolism or renal excretion under normal circumstances. There is no evidence that atypical pseudocholinesterase changes receptor binding affinity itself; the defect is purely in the enzymatic breakdown of the drug. Recognizing this enzyme deficiency is important for anticipating prolonged recovery and providing supportive ventilation until neuromuscular function returns.
  75. The correct answer is D (Propofol infusion syndrome, related to impaired mitochondrial fatty acid oxidation and electron transport chain dysfunction.): propofol infusion syndrome is a rare but potentially fatal complication associated with prolonged, high-dose propofol infusions, thought to result from impaired mitochondrial fatty acid oxidation and electron transport chain dysfunction, producing the characteristic picture of rhabdomyolysis, severe metabolic acidosis, cardiac arrhythmias, and cardiovascular collapse. Propofol is not considered a triggering agent for malignant hyperthermia, which is instead associated with volatile anesthetics and succinylcholine in susceptible individuals. While allergic reactions to the lipid emulsion vehicle can occur, they typically present with urticaria or anaphylaxis rather than this metabolic and muscular syndrome. Serotonin syndrome presents with a distinct clinical picture including clonus, hyperreflexia, and autonomic instability, not primarily rhabdomyolysis and acidosis.
  76. The correct answer is B (A 3.5 mEq/L rise in bicarbonate for every 10 mmHg rise in PaCO2.): in chronic respiratory acidosis, renal compensation over several days increases bicarbonate reabsorption, producing an expected rise of approximately 3.5 mEq/L in bicarbonate for every 10 mmHg increase in PaCO2 above normal, compared to only about 1 mEq/L per 10 mmHg in the acute setting before renal compensation has occurred. A decrease in bicarbonate would be inappropriate and would suggest a coexisting metabolic acidosis rather than expected compensation. The chronic compensation formula reflects the kidney's greater capacity to compensate over time compared to the immediate, more limited buffering response seen acutely. Distinguishing acute from chronic compensation rules is essential to correctly identify mixed acid-base disorders.
  77. The correct answer is D (Statistical significance does not necessarily equate to a clinically meaningful effect size.): a statistically significant p value simply indicates the observed difference is unlikely to be due to chance alone, but with a large sample size even a small, clinically trivial absolute risk reduction of 2 percent and a high number needed to treat of 50 can reach statistical significance, so clinicians must evaluate the magnitude and practical importance of the effect separately from its statistical significance. A p value of 0.03 does not mean there is a 3 percent chance the result is due to chance in the way many clinicians mistakenly interpret it, and it certainly does not guarantee clinical importance. A number needed to treat of 50 actually reflects a fairly modest treatment effect, requiring 50 patients to be treated to prevent one case of the outcome, rather than indicating high effectiveness.

  81. The correct answer is B (It artificially inflates both sensitivity and specificity due to spectrum bias.): spectrum bias occurs when a study population includes only patients at the extremes of disease severity, making it easier for a diagnostic test to correctly classify obviously healthy or obviously very sick patients, which inflates apparent sensitivity and specificity compared to how the test would perform in a real-world population with a broader spectrum of intermediate disease severity. Sensitivity and specificity are often mistakenly assumed to be entirely independent of the tested population, but spectrum composition can meaningfully distort these measures despite being prevalence-independent in theory. This bias would tend to inflate rather than lower the area under the curve, since discrimination appears artificially better between extreme groups. The effect is not selective to only one metric, since excluding intermediate cases tends to improve both sensitivity and specificity together.

The correct answer is C (Chaotic, disorganized atrial electrical activity without a single dominant pacemaker.): atrial fibrillation results from multiple simultaneous reentrant wavelets and disorganized electrical activity throughout the atria, preventing coordinated atrial contraction and producing fibrillatory waves instead of discrete P waves, with an irregularly irregular ventricular response due to variable conduction through the atrioventricular node. A single ectopic atrial focus firing regularly would more closely resemble atrial tachycardia or atrial flutter with organized flutter waves, not fibrillatory activity. Retrograde junctional conduction produces inverted P waves in a specific relationship to the QRS complex, which is distinct from the complete absence of organized atrial activity seen in fibrillation.

The correct answer is D (Increased vagal tone from surgical traction, such as peritoneal manipulation, or from high-dose opioid administration.): the rhythm strip finding of progressively lengthening PR intervals culminating in a dropped QRS beat describes a Mobitz type I, or Wenckebach, second-degree atrioventricular block, which is commonly precipitated intraoperatively by increased vagal tone from stimuli such as peritoneal or ocular traction, or from high-dose opioids like fentanyl that increase vagal activity at the atrioventricular node. Recognizing vagally mediated Wenckebach block is useful because it is often benign and transient, resolving with removal of the vagal stimulus or anticholinergic administration if hemodynamically significant.

The correct answer is A (A false negative rate of 40 percent.): the false negative rate is the proportion of patients who truly have the condition, in this case intraoperative awareness, but are missed by the test showing a normal or reassuring value, so if 40 percent of patients with true awareness had a normal monitor reading, this directly describes a 40 percent false negative rate, equivalent to a sensitivity of 60 percent. The correct answer is C (Central and peripheral alpha-2 adrenergic agonism that reduces sympathetic outflow and inhibits norepinephrine release at nerve terminals.): dexmedetomidine is a highly selective alpha-2 adrenergic agonist that acts centrally in the locus coeruleus to reduce sympathetic outflow and peripherally at presynaptic terminals to inhibit norepinephrine release, producing sedation along with a predictable dose-dependent bradycardia and sometimes hypotension from reduced sympathetic tone. It does not act as a direct beta-1 receptor antagonist, calcium channel blocker, or muscarinic agonist, distinguishing its mechanism from other bradycardia-inducing drug classes. The bradycardia seen with dexmedetomidine is often well tolerated in otherwise healthy patients but should be anticipated and monitored, particularly in patients with baseline conduction abnormalities. This alpha-2 mediated mechanism also explains dexmedetomidine's analgesic-sparing and anxiolytic properties.

The correct answer is B (Left-sided atelectasis or complete lung collapse.): volume loss from atelectasis or complete collapse of a lung causes the mediastinum and trachea to shift toward the affected side as the ipsilateral hemithorax loses volume, distinguishing it from processes that increase volume on one side. The correct answer is D (Potassium depletion causes increased renal hydrogen ion secretion along with bicarbonate reabsorption, a mechanism that helps preserve total body potassium.): in states of potassium depletion, renal tubular cells preferentially secrete hydrogen ions in exchange for sodium reabsorption to help conserve potassium, and this increased hydrogen ion excretion along with enhanced bicarbonate reabsorption perpetuates and worsens the metabolic alkalosis, creating a self-sustaining cycle until potassium is repleted. Hypokalemia does not directly stimulate gastric acid secretion, though vomiting is the primary driver of volume and chloride loss in this scenario. Hepatic bicarbonate metabolism is not the mechanism by which potassium depletion sustains alkalosis, since the effect occurs at the renal tubular level. Chloride does shift with potassium disturbances, but the alkalosis-perpetuating mechanism specifically relates to renal hydrogen ion handling rather than an independent intracellular chloride shift.
  90. The correct answer is C (Sudden loss of pulmonary blood flow delivering carbon dioxide to the alveoli.): end-tidal carbon dioxide depends on adequate pulmonary blood flow delivering carbon dioxide-laden venous blood to the alveoli for exchange and exhalation, so a sudden cardiac arrest with pulseless electrical activity abruptly halts this delivery, causing an immediate and dramatic drop in end-tidal carbon dioxide while the capnogram waveform shape itself remains largely preserved since ventilation continues. Bronchospasm would more typically produce an upsloping, shark-fin shaped waveform reflecting delayed emptying rather than simply a sharp drop in peak value. Absorbent depletion would raise the baseline inspired carbon dioxide rather than lower the end-tidal value. While an air embolism can also cause a sudden drop in end-tidal carbon dioxide by increasing dead space, it typically also compromises cardiac output secondarily, and in this vignette the abrupt pulseless electrical activity itself is the direct cause of lost pulmonary blood flow.

This combination decreases preload and afterload, causing the characteristic hypotension seen after spinal placement. The reflex tachycardia reflects baroreceptor-mediated compensation for reduced venous return rather than direct cardiac toxicity. This response occurs too rapidly to represent epidural local anesthetic uptake, which takes several minutes. The correct answer is D (Ipsilateral hemidiaphragmatic paralysis from phrenic nerve blockade.): The phrenic nerve runs directly on the anterior surface of the anterior scalene muscle immediately adjacent to the brachial plexus at the interscalene level, so local anesthetic spread blocks it in nearly all patients. This produces a 25 to 30 percent reduction in pulmonary function from unilateral diaphragmatic paralysis, which is poorly tolerated in patients with underlying pulmonary disease. Pneumothorax is less likely at this level given the distance from the pleural dome and the described timing coinciding with expected block onset. Ultrasound guidance and lower volumes reduce but do not eliminate this predictable phrenic effect.
  94. The correct answer is A (The pleural dome and apex of the lung, which lie just deep to the plexus above the first rib.): At the supraclavicular level the brachial plexus divisions lie superficial and lateral to the subclavian artery directly above the first rib and pleural dome, historically earning this block the nickname spinal of the arm for its reliability but also its pneumothorax risk. Real-time ultrasound visualization of the needle tip and the pleura allows the operator to avoid advancing beyond the plexus into the lung. The subclavian vein and phrenic nerve are relevant nearby structures but are not the reason this specific block carries the highest pneumothorax risk of the brachial plexus approaches. The vertebral artery is a concern at the interscalene level, not supraclavicular.
  95. The correct answer is A (Lateral, posterior, and medial cords surrounding the axillary artery.): At the infraclavicular level the brachial plexus has organized into its three cords, named for their position relative to the axillary artery, and effective block requires local anesthetic spread around the artery to reach all three. This U-shaped or circumferential spread pattern is why infraclavicular blocks are well suited to catheter placement for continuous elbow and forearm analgesia. Roots and trunks describe more proximal levels such as interscalene and supraclavicular, not infraclavicular. Terminal nerve separation occurs more distally at the axillary level.
  96. The correct answer is A (A separate injection targeting the musculocutaneous nerve within the coracobrachialis muscle.): The musculocutaneous nerve exits the brachial plexus proximal to the axillary level and travels within or between the coracobrachialis and biceps muscles, so it is not reliably anesthetized by a perivascular axillary injection alone. Failure to block it separately is a common reason for incomplete lateral forearm and biceps anesthesia after an otherwise successful axillary block. Simply increasing volume around the artery does not reliably reach this nerve given its separate anatomic course. Waiting longer will not produce anesthesia in a nerve that was never exposed to local anesthetic.
  97. The correct answer is B (Quadriceps motor weakness from femoral nerve blockade impairing knee extension.): The femoral nerve supplies the quadriceps muscle group responsible for knee extension, and femoral nerve blocks reliably produce quadriceps weakness that increases fall risk during early postoperative ambulation. This is a well recognized tradeoff that has driven the shift toward adductor canal blocks, which spare most quadriceps strength while still providing analgesia. Orthostatic hypotension and peroneal injury are possible but do not specifically explain buckling of the operative knee during weight bearing. Fall precautions and physical therapy supervision are recommended whenever a femoral block is used for knee surgery.
  98. The correct answer is C (The saphenous nerve, a purely sensory terminal branch of the femoral nerve.): Within the adductor canal the saphenous nerve travels alongside the femoral artery as a purely sensory nerve supplying the medial leg and knee, having already given off the motor branches to the quadriceps more proximally. Targeting it there provides effective analgesia after knee surgery while largely sparing quadriceps strength compared with a proximal femoral nerve block. The obturator nerve is not consistently blocked by an adductor canal injection and is not the primary target. Blocking the femoral nerve proper or the sciatic nerve would produce more extensive motor weakness than desired for this indication.
  99. The correct answer is C (Within the popliteal fossa, dividing into tibial and common peroneal nerves.): The sciatic nerve typically bifurcates into the tibial and common peroneal nerves within the popliteal fossa, and injecting local anesthetic proximal to this division allows a single injection to anesthetize both terminal branches for foot and ankle surgery. Combining this with a saphenous nerve block covers the medial ankle territory supplied by the femoral nerve, which the sciatic distribution does not reach. The nerve remains undivided more proximally at the buttock and thigh levels. The peroneal nerve itself, not the sciatic nerve, divides further at the ankle into deep and superficial branches.
  100. The correct answer is B (A transversus abdominis plane block, targeting the thoracolumbar sensory nerves supplying the T10 through L1 dermatomes.): The TAP block deposits local anesthetic in the fascial plane between the internal oblique and transversus abdominis muscles, anesthetizing the thoracolumbar sensory nerves that supply the anterior abdominal wall without covering visceral pain. It provides effective somatic analgesia for lower abdominal incisions such as appendectomy with a favorable safety profile compared with neuraxial techniques. A thoracic epidural at T4 would be too high and carries more hemodynamic risk than needed for this indication. A stellate ganglion or pudendal block targets entirely different anatomic regions unrelated to the abdominal wall incision.
  101. The correct answer is A (Cephalocaudal spread of local anesthetic in the plane beneath the muscle, reaching the dorsal rami and paravertebral space.): The erector spinae plane block places local anesthetic in the interfascial plane deep to the erector spinae muscle and superficial to the transverse process, from which it spreads cephalocaudally and can track medially toward the paravertebral space to affect the dorsal and ventral rami over multiple levels. This produces multidermatomal truncal analgesia without the technical demands or risks of deeper paravertebral or epidural injection. It is not an epidural injection since the needle tip stays well lateral to the neuraxis. Systemic absorption contributes to overall analgesia but is not the primary proposed mechanism of the multidermatomal spread.
  102. The correct answer is D (It may spread to the thoracic paravertebral space, extending coverage and duration.): Local anesthetic deposited around the quadratus lumborum muscle can track along fascial planes toward the thoracic paravertebral space, potentially providing broader dermatomal coverage and longer duration of analgesia than a more anterior transversus abdominis plane injection. This makes it an attractive option for flank and posterior abdominal incisions such as nephrectomy. It does not reliably produce lower extremity motor blockade, which would be an undesired effect for this indication. Multimodal analgesia still typically includes some systemic component, since no single regional block eliminates all opioid requirement.
  103. The correct answer is B (Early central nervous system signs of local anesthetic systemic toxicity.): Perioral numbness, metallic taste, tinnitus, and agitation are classic early central nervous system warning signs of local anesthetic systemic toxicity, reflecting inhibition of inhibitory pathways before excitatory pathways predominate and can progress to seizure. These symptoms after a substantial total local anesthetic dose should prompt immediate cessation of injection, airway management, and preparation for lipid emulsion therapy. They are not an expected benign side effect and should never be dismissed as anxiety. A vasovagal episode typically presents with bradycardia and hypotension rather than this specific symptom cluster.
  104. The correct answer is C (Intravenous 20% lipid emulsion bolus followed by continuous infusion.): Local anesthetic systemic toxicity causing cardiac instability such as ventricular tachycardia is treated with 20% lipid emulsion, typically a 1.5 mL/kg bolus followed by a continuous infusion, which is thought to act as a lipid sink and improve mitochondrial fatty acid metabolism. Standard resuscitation should continue but with reduced epinephrine doses, since high doses may worsen outcomes in this specific setting per ASRA guidance. Amiodarone can be considered but lipid emulsion administration should not be delayed. Patients require extended monitoring after such an event given the risk of recurrence, not immediate discharge.
  105. The correct answer is D (5 to 7 days before the procedure.): ASRA guidelines recommend discontinuing clopidogrel for 5 to 7 days before neuraxial anesthesia to allow adequate platelet function recovery, since its antiplatelet effect is irreversible for the life of the platelet. Shorter intervals such as 24 hours or 3 days leave clinically significant residual platelet inhibition and unacceptable spinal hematoma risk. No discontinuation at all would be inappropriate given clopidogrel's well documented association with neuraxial bleeding complications.
