The cardiology team should be consulted regarding the safety of interrupting antiplatelet therapy in a patient with a coronary stent.
  106. The correct answer is A (At least 12 hours after the last prophylactic dose.): ASRA guidelines recommend a minimum interval of 12 hours between a prophylactic dose of low molecular weight heparin and neuraxial needle or catheter placement, reflecting the drug's pharmacokinetic clearance and anti-Xa activity profile. Therapeutic-dose enoxaparin requires a longer interval, typically 24 hours, because of its greater residual anticoagulant effect. Waiting only 2 hours or proceeding without any delay would leave clinically relevant anticoagulation present and increase spinal hematoma risk. A 48-hour interval is longer than necessary for prophylactic dosing and is more consistent with therapeutic-dose or renally impaired patient recommendations.
  107. The correct answer is A (Yes, an INR of 1.2 is within the acceptable range for neuraxial placement.): ASRA guidelines consider an INR of 1.4 or less to reflect adequate normalization of coagulation for safe neuraxial needle or catheter placement after warfarin has been held. An INR of 1.2 falls comfortably within this accepted threshold. Requiring a fixed 30-day interval is not supported by guidelines, since the appropriate hold time depends on the patient's individual INR trend rather than a rigid calendar rule. INR is in fact the standard and clinically validated way to assess residual warfarin anticoagulant effect before neuraxial procedures.
  108. The correct answer is C (72 hours to allow adequate clearance of the anticoagulant effect.): ASRA guidelines recommend withholding rivaroxaban for approximately 72 hours, or three elimination half-lives, before neuraxial block placement in patients with normal renal function to allow sufficient clearance of its anticoagulant effect. A 24-hour interval is inadequate and would leave clinically significant factor Xa inhibition present. Direct oral anticoagulants still require planned interruption before neuraxial procedures despite their more predictable pharmacokinetics compared with warfarin. The clopidogrel interval is longer because clopidogrel irreversibly inhibits platelets for their entire lifespan, an entirely different mechanism from a reversible factor Xa inhibitor.
  109. The correct answer is D (Keep the patient in the lateral decubitus position with the operative side down.): Hyperbaric local anesthetic solutions are denser than cerebrospinal fluid and settle toward the dependent side under gravity, so maintaining the lateral decubitus position with the operative side down for several minutes after injection preferentially concentrates the block on that side. This unilateral or asymmetric block technique reduces hemodynamic effects and motor blockade on the nonoperative side. Immediately turning supine would allow the solution to spread more symmetrically and defeat this goal. Trendelenburg positioning affects cephalad spread along the long axis of the spine rather than side-to-side distribution.
  110. The correct answer is B (An epidural blood patch using the patient's own autologous blood.): Postdural puncture headache from a large-gauge needle such as an 18-gauge Tuohy needle has a high incidence and, when conservative measures like hydration, caffeine, and analgesics fail, an epidural blood patch using 15 to 20 mL of the patient's own blood is the most effective definitive treatment. The blood is thought to form a clot that tamponades the dural leak and restores cerebrospinal fluid pressure. Strict prolonged bed rest alone does not reliably prevent or treat established postdural puncture headache. Repeating dural puncture would worsen rather than relieve the underlying problem.
  111. The correct answer is C (Total spinal anesthesia requiring immediate airway control and hemodynamic support.): Excessive cephalad spread of intrathecal local anesthetic can block the cervical spinal cord and brainstem, producing apnea from phrenic and intercostal paralysis, profound bradycardia from blockade of cardioaccelerator fibers, and severe hypotension, the classic triad of total spinal anesthesia. Immediate priorities are securing the airway with positive pressure ventilation, treating bradycardia and hypotension with atropine and vasopressors, and maintaining cardiovascular support until the block resolves. Lipid emulsion is the treatment for local anesthetic systemic toxicity, not for excessive neuraxial spread of an appropriately placed dose. This presentation is not consistent with amniotic fluid embolism or uterine rupture, which would have different clinical features.
  112. The correct answer is D (In-plane technique aligns the needle within the ultrasound beam allowing continuous shaft and tip visualization.): The in-plane approach advances the needle parallel to the long axis of the transducer so that the entire needle shaft and tip remain within the thin ultrasound beam, providing continuous real-time visualization of the needle's trajectory. The out-of-plane approach crosses the beam perpendicularly, typically showing only a cross-sectional dot representing the needle at a single point, which can make the exact tip location less certain. These are fundamentally different visualization patterns, not identical as suggested by one distractor. Understanding this distinction helps operators choose the safer approach for structures where precise tip tracking is critical, such as near vascular or pleural structures.
  113. The correct answer is A (Interscalene block, targeting the plexus at the C5-C6 nerve root level.): The interscalene approach targets the brachial plexus at the level of the C5 and C6 roots as they pass between the anterior and middle scalene muscles, providing excellent coverage of the shoulder while frequently sparing the C8-T1 distribution supplying the ulnar side of the hand. This makes it the standard regional technique for shoulder arthroscopy and rotator cuff surgery. Axillary and infraclavicular blocks target more distal levels of the plexus and provide better hand and forearm coverage but less reliable shoulder anesthesia. Wrist-level blocks would not provide any shoulder anesthesia at all.
  114. The correct answer is B (The level of the trunks and divisions just above the first rib.): The supraclavicular block is performed at the level of the trunks and their divisions, where the brachial plexus is most tightly packed together just superior to the first rib and lateral to the subclavian artery. This compact arrangement allows relatively small local anesthetic volumes to achieve dense, rapid-onset anesthesia of the entire arm, earning it the nickname spinal of the arm. The root level corresponds to the interscalene approach, the cord level to the infraclavicular approach, and the terminal nerve level to the axillary approach. Each level offers a different balance of onset speed, block density, and complication profile.
  115. The correct answer is D (Infraclavicular.): The infraclavicular region overlying the pectoralis muscles moves less with patient positioning and arm motion than the neck or axilla, making catheters placed there less prone to dislodgement and kinking during multi-day continuous infusion. Interscalene catheters are more mobile with neck movement and carry a higher migration and dislodgement rate. Axillary catheters still require careful securement since the axilla is a highly mobile region. The cervical plexus is not a standard site for continuous brachial plexus catheter techniques.
  116. The correct answer is A (The intercostobrachial nerve, a branch of the second intercostal nerve.): The intercostobrachial nerve supplies sensation to the skin of the medial upper arm and axilla where a pneumatic tourniquet is typically applied, and it arises from the second intercostal nerve rather than the brachial plexus, so it is not blocked by standard axillary or other brachial plexus techniques. A subcutaneous field block at the medial upper arm is often added specifically to improve tourniquet tolerance. The suprascapular, long thoracic, and dorsal scapular nerves supply shoulder girdle structures unrelated to tourniquet discomfort on the arm.
  117. The correct answer is B (The 3-in-1 technique unreliably blocks the obturator nerve despite the theoretical concept.): The original 3-in-1 block concept proposed that a single injection at the femoral crease would spread proximally to block the femoral, obturator, and lateral femoral cutaneous nerves, but subsequent studies have consistently shown that obturator nerve blockade is unreliable with this technique because the nerve often follows a separate fascial course. Persistent medial thigh pain despite good femoral coverage is a well documented limitation rather than a technical failure requiring repeat injection. It does not represent local anesthetic toxicity, which would produce systemic rather than isolated nerve-territory symptoms. Clinicians should not rely on a single femoral-level injection to guarantee obturator coverage.
  118. The correct answer is C (Adductor canal blocks preserve quadriceps strength, facilitating earlier safe ambulation.): The primary rationale for favoring adductor canal blocks in enhanced recovery protocols is that they target the predominantly sensory saphenous nerve distal to the motor branches to the quadriceps, preserving enough quadriceps strength to allow earlier and safer ambulation compared with femoral nerve blocks. Analgesic efficacy between the two techniques is generally comparable rather than clearly superior with the adductor canal approach. Multimodal analgesia with other agents is still needed regardless of which peripheral block is chosen. Technical difficulty was not the driving factor behind this widespread practice change.
  119. The correct answer is A (Context-sensitive half-time increases with longer infusion durations for propofol.): Context-sensitive half-time describes the time required for the plasma concentration of a drug to decrease by 50 percent after stopping an infusion of a given duration, and for propofol this value increases as infusion duration lengthens because peripheral tissue compartments become progressively more saturated. A six-hour infusion will therefore have a longer context-sensitive half-time than a two-hour infusion at the same rate, translating into a slower expected emergence. Propofol clearance is not a fixed value independent of context, which is precisely why this concept exists. Context-sensitive half-time is a pharmacokinetic principle that applies broadly to intravenous infusions, not exclusively to inhaled agents.
  120. The correct answer is B (Use processed electroencephalographic monitoring to help titrate propofol dosing.): Because total intravenous anesthesia lacks the built-in safety net of end-tidal volatile agent concentration monitoring, processed electroencephalographic monitors such as the bispectral index can help guide propofol titration and reduce the risk of inadequate anesthetic depth and awareness, particularly when neuromuscular blockade prevents movement as a clinical sign. Hemodynamic parameters alone are unreliable indicators of anesthetic depth and can be blunted by other medications. A fixed dose regardless of stimulation risks either underdosing during intense stimulation or overdosing during quieter periods. Omitting neuromuscular blockade is not a practical solution when the surgery specifically requires paralysis.
  121. The correct answer is D (It compensates for the plasma-to-brain equilibration delay, briefly overshooting plasma concentration to speed onset.): Effect-site targeting incorporates a rate constant describing drug equilibration between plasma and the biophase, or effect site, and the software may transiently drive plasma concentration above the target to accelerate achievement of the desired effect-site concentration. This can produce faster clinically apparent onset compared with simple plasma targeting, which does not account for this delay. It does not eliminate the equilibration delay itself, since that delay is a real pharmacokinetic property, but rather compensates for it through infusion algorithm design. A validated pharmacokinetic-pharmacodynamic model is still required to perform these calculations, not the absence of one.
  122. The correct answer is C (Deep sedation, with purposeful response only to repeated or painful stimulation.): The ASA continuum defines deep sedation as a drug-induced depression of consciousness during which patients cannot be easily aroused but respond purposefully following repeated or painful stimulation, with spontaneous ventilation that may be inadequate and airway intervention occasionally required. This matches the description given, which is deeper than moderate sedation, where patients respond purposefully to verbal or light tactile stimulation without airway intervention typically needed. Minimal sedation preserves normal response to verbal commands with no ventilatory or airway compromise. General anesthesia is characterized by unarousability even to painful stimulation, often requiring airway and ventilatory support.
  123. The correct answer is B (Continuous monitoring of exhaled carbon dioxide unless precluded by the procedure or equipment.): ASA standards for moderate and deep sedation require monitoring of ventilation by capnography in addition to pulse oximetry, because capnography can detect apnea and airway obstruction earlier than pulse oximetry alone, particularly in patients receiving supplemental oxygen. Invasive arterial monitoring and transesophageal echocardiography are not routine requirements for standard sedation cases and are reserved for specific higher-acuity situations. Processed electroencephalographic monitoring such as bispectral index is not a mandatory standard for sedation cases, unlike capnography.
  124. The correct answer is D (The asleep-awake-asleep technique, using sedation during exposure and closure with a fully awake period for intraoperative testing.): The asleep-awake-asleep technique uses deeper sedation or general anesthesia with an airway device during the painful exposure and closure phases, combined with a scalp block for analgesia, then allows the patient to awaken fully for cortical mapping and neurological testing during the critical resection near eloquent brain regions before returning to deeper sedation for closure. This allows real-time functional assessment that continuous general anesthesia would preclude. Uniform deep sedation without an awake testing period would not permit the language or motor testing that makes awake craniotomy valuable. A scalp block alone with no sedation would leave the patient uncomfortable during the lengthy exposure and closure phases.
  125. The correct answer is A (Benzodiazepines, which can suppress epileptiform activity needed for mapping.): Benzodiazepines can raise seizure threshold and suppress epileptiform discharges, which may interfere with intraoperative electrocorticography and functional mapping during the awake phase of craniotomy, so many protocols avoid or minimize their use. Dexmedetomidine is often favored in awake craniotomy protocols specifically because it provides sedation with relatively preserved cortical electrical activity and minimal respiratory depression. Remifentanil is commonly used for its short context-sensitive half-time and rapid titratability without significantly disrupting mapping. Scalp block local anesthetics act peripherally at the scalp and skull and do not meaningfully alter cortical electrical activity.
  126. The correct answer is C (Hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction in the alveoli of the collapsed lung.): Hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction is a local reflex in which alveolar hypoxia in the collapsed, nonventilated lung causes constriction of adjacent pulmonary vasculature, redirecting blood flow toward the well-oxygenated ventilated lung and helping to minimize shunt and preserve arterial oxygenation during one-lung ventilation. This reflex can be blunted by volatile anesthetics at high concentrations and by vasodilating drugs, which is one reason total intravenous anesthesia is sometimes favored during one-lung ventilation. Surgical vessel compression may contribute incidentally during manipulation but is not the primary physiologic mechanism. Bronchoconstriction in the ventilated lung would worsen rather than improve oxygenation.
  127. The correct answer is D (Apply continuous positive airway pressure to the nonventilated lung.): After confirming correct tube position, applying low-level continuous positive airway pressure to the nonventilated lung is a standard next step to improve oxygenation during one-lung ventilation by providing some oxygen exchange in that lung without significantly reinflating it or obstructing the surgical field. Increasing tidal volume beyond protective limits on the ventilated lung risks ventilator-induced lung injury and would not be the first-line maneuver. Discontinuing one-lung ventilation entirely is not usually necessary or desirable unless oxygenation remains critically impaired despite standard maneuvers. Decreasing inspired oxygen would worsen rather than improve an already hypoxemic patient.
  128. The correct answer is C (The carina is clearly visualized, with the bronchial lumen and its cuff entering the left mainstem bronchus just below it.): Correct positioning of a left-sided double-lumen tube is confirmed by visualizing the carina through the tracheal lumen and observing the bronchial lumen and its cuff appropriately seated within the left mainstem bronchus just distal to the carina, with the upper border of the blue bronchial cuff barely visible below the carina. A bronchial cuff herniating above the carina into the trachea indicates the tube is not advanced far enough and risks loss of lung isolation. The tracheal lumen opening within the right mainstem bronchus would indicate a malpositioned tube advanced too far or an inappropriate side chosen. Both lumens entering the same bronchus would represent a significant malposition preventing effective lung isolation.
  129. The correct answer is B (Malposition or migration of the double-lumen tube from its confirmed position.): Double-lumen tubes are prone to migration with patient repositioning, such as turning from supine to lateral decubitus, and acute high peak pressures, poor compliance, and desaturation shortly after initiating one-lung ventilation should prompt immediate reassessment of tube position, ideally with repeat fiberoptic bronchoscopy. This is a far more common and immediately correctable explanation than anaphylaxis, malignant hyperthermia, or pulmonary embolism in this specific clinical sequence. Malignant hyperthermia typically presents with hypercarbia, tachycardia, and rising temperature rather than isolated airway pressure and oxygenation changes tied temporally to a position change. Prompt bronchoscopic reassessment should precede consideration of these less likely alternative diagnoses.
  130. The correct answer is A (Sodium nitroprusside, titrated continuously to the desired mean arterial pressure.): Sodium nitroprusside is a direct arterial and venous vasodilator with an extremely rapid onset and short duration of action, allowing precise, continuously titratable control of mean arterial pressure during deliberate hypotension techniques used to reduce surgical bleeding and improve field visualization. Its effects dissipate quickly once the infusion is adjusted or stopped, which is valuable for fine hemodynamic control. Long-acting beta blockade alone lacks the rapid titratability needed and does not directly produce vasodilation. Hydralazine has a much longer and less predictable duration of action than nitroprusside, making it poorly suited to a technique requiring second-to-second titration.
  131. The correct answer is C (Reduced cerebral perfusion pressure risking cerebral ischemia given impaired autoregulation and stenosis.): Patients with significant carotid stenosis and chronic hypertension often have a rightward-shifted cerebral autoregulation curve and reduced collateral flow reserve, making them particularly vulnerable to cerebral ischemia if mean arterial pressure is deliberately lowered during controlled hypotension. This risk must be weighed carefully against the surgical benefit of reduced blood loss in such patients. Deliberate hypotension does carry renal and other organ perfusion risks but does not universally cause irreversible renal failure, and age alone is not an absolute contraindication. Careful patient selection, individualized blood pressure targets, and vigilant neurologic monitoring are required rather than blanket avoidance in all older patients.
  132. The correct answer is A (Yes, an ACT of 480 seconds meets the typical threshold required before initiating bypass.): Most institutions require an activated clotting time of at least 400 to 480 seconds after heparin administration before initiating cardiopulmonary bypass to ensure adequate anticoagulation and prevent clot formation within the extracorporeal circuit. An ACT of 200 seconds would actually be far too low, not excessive, for bypass initiation, since much higher anticoagulation is needed than for routine surgical prophylaxis. While comparing to a patient's own baseline ACT can provide additional context, meeting the absolute institutional threshold is the key requirement rather than baseline comparison alone. ACT is in fact the standard bedside test used to monitor heparin effect throughout cardiopulmonary bypass.
  133. The correct answer is D (To arrest the heart in diastole and reduce myocardial oxygen demand during ischemia.): Cardioplegia solutions, typically cold and potassium-rich, are delivered into the coronary circulation to rapidly arrest the heart in diastole, dramatically reducing myocardial oxygen demand and protecting the myocardium from ischemic injury during the period of aortic cross-clamping when normal coronary perfusion is interrupted. The heart does not remain beating during this period, since electromechanical arrest is the intended effect. Cardioplegia is not a substitute for systemic heparin anticoagulation, which is required separately to prevent clotting throughout the bypass circuit. Increasing contractility would be counterproductive during a period intended to minimize oxygen consumption and protect ischemic myocardium.
  134. The correct answer is B (Venous drainage from the right atrium or vena cavae with arterial return through the ascending aorta.): Standard adult cardiac surgery typically uses venous cannulation of the right atrium or separately the superior and inferior vena cavae to drain deoxygenated blood into the bypass circuit, with oxygenated blood returned to the patient through a cannula placed in the ascending aorta, completing the extracorporeal circuit while bypassing the heart and lungs. Peripheral arm veins do not provide adequate flow for venous drainage in standard bypass. The pulmonary artery and coronary sinus are not typical cannulation sites for standard full cardiopulmonary bypass, though femoral vessels may be used as alternative sites in specific circumstances such as minimally invasive or emergency cannulation.
  135. The correct answer is D (Proceed immediately to an emergency surgical or cannula-based front-of-neck airway.): In a true cannot-intubate, cannot-oxygenate emergency, difficult airway algorithms mandate immediate progression to an emergency invasive airway, such as a surgical cricothyrotomy or cannula-based front-of-neck access, rather than persisting with repeated attempts at conventional laryngoscopy or supraglottic devices that have already failed. Continuing the same failed technique wastes critical time while oxygen saturation continues to fall. Additional neuromuscular blockade will not resolve an anatomic or mechanical inability to ventilate and only delays definitive action. Waking the patient is not feasible once profound hypoxemia is already developing and the patient cannot be ventilated by any noninvasive means.
  136. The correct answer is A (Nebulized and direct application of topical local anesthetic to the oropharynx, hypopharynx, and larynx.): Successful awake fiberoptic intubation depends on thorough topical anesthesia of the airway, typically achieved through a combination of nebulized local anesthetic, direct spray or atomized application during scope advancement, and sometimes targeted techniques such as superior laryngeal nerve blocks, covering the oropharynx, hypopharynx, and larynx to suppress gag and cough reflexes. General anesthesia with muscle relaxation defeats the purpose of an awake technique, which preserves spontaneous ventilation and airway reflexes in a high-risk airway. Intravenous local anesthetic alone does not provide adequate mucosal anesthesia for airway instrumentation. A single cricothyroid injection alone, without oropharyngeal and laryngeal topicalization, would leave much of the airway inadequately anesthetized for scope passage.
  137. The correct answer is C (Consider extubation over an airway exchange catheter with equipment ready for reintubation.): For a patient with a known difficult airway, extubation planning should include strategies such as extubation over an airway exchange catheter, which can serve as a conduit for reintubation if the patient develops respiratory distress, along with having difficult airway equipment and personnel immediately available. Extubating deep under anesthesia removes protective airway reflexes and increases risk in a patient who may be difficult to reintubate if problems arise. Extubation without any contingency plan is inappropriate given the documented difficulty encountered at induction. Tracheostomy is not routinely required for every difficult airway patient and is reserved for specific indications, not a default extubation strategy.
  138. The correct answer is B (Malignant hyperthermia, treated with immediate discontinuation of triggering agents and dantrolene.): The combination of unexplained tachycardia, rising end-tidal carbon dioxide, masseter rigidity, and a blood gas showing mixed acidosis, hypercarbia, hyperkalemia, and hyperthermia after exposure to a volatile agent and succinylcholine is the classic presentation of malignant hyperthermia. Immediate management requires stopping all triggering agents, hyperventilating with 100% oxygen, and administering intravenous dantrolene without delay, alongside active cooling and treatment of hyperkalemia and acidosis. Anaphylaxis typically presents with hypotension, bronchospasm, and urticaria rather than this specific metabolic and muscular picture. Thyroid storm and sepsis do not explain the temporal relationship to triggering anesthetic agents and masseter rigidity seen here.
  139. The correct answer is A (2.5 mg/kg intravenously, repeated as needed until symptoms resolve.): The recommended initial dose of intravenous dantrolene for treating an acute malignant hyperthermia crisis is 2.5 mg/kg, repeated every few minutes as needed based on clinical response such as decreasing end-tidal carbon dioxide, heart rate, and temperature, with total doses sometimes exceeding 10 mg/kg in severe cases. Waiting for genetic confirmation before treating would be dangerous and inappropriate, since malignant hyperthermia is a clinical diagnosis requiring immediate empiric treatment. A dose of 0.1 mg/kg would be far too low to produce any meaningful therapeutic effect on ryanodine receptor-mediated calcium release. Dantrolene must be titrated to clinical response rather than given as an excessively large single bolus of 25 mg/kg.
  140. The correct answer is D (Possible early sign of malignant hyperthermia susceptibility warranting close monitoring for other features.): Masseter muscle rigidity after succinylcholine administration can be an early and sometimes isolated sign of malignant hyperthermia susceptibility, and while mild jaw tightening is common and often benign, more severe or prolonged masseter spasm should prompt heightened vigilance for other developing signs such as tachycardia, hypercarbia, and rising temperature. It should not simply be treated as inadequate dosing requiring repeat succinylcholine, since further triggering agent exposure could worsen an evolving malignant hyperthermia episode. This finding is not something to dismiss as entirely normal and insignificant without further observation. Abandoning all further monitoring would be inappropriate given the ongoing need to watch for a life-threatening reaction.
  141. The correct answer is A (Secure the airway, ventilate the patient, and give atropine and vasopressors.): total spinal anesthesia causes apnea from phrenic nerve blockade and profound hypotension and bradycardia from extensive sympathectomy, so airway support, ventilation, and hemodynamic resuscitation take priority. Lipid emulsion treats local anesthetic systemic toxicity, not a total spinal, and is not indicated here. Watchful waiting delays essential resuscitation in an unresponsive, apneic patient. The diagnosis is clinical and treatment must not be delayed for imaging.
  142. The correct answer is B (Hyperbaric bupivacaine with the operative side dependent, kept lateral 10-15 minutes.): hyperbaric solution settles toward the dependent operative side, and maintaining lateral positioning allows fixation of a predominantly unilateral block, limiting sympathectomy and hemodynamic swings in this fixed-cardiac-output patient. Rapid repositioning would allow the solution to spread bilaterally before fixation occurs. Hypobaric solution with the nonoperative side dependent would preferentially block the wrong side. Immediate supine positioning defeats the purpose of unilateral technique.
  143. The correct answer is C (Blockade of cardioaccelerator sympathetic fibers originating from T1-T4.): a high spinal block extending to T2 interrupts the cardioaccelerator fibers, removing sympathetic tone to the heart and unopposing vagal activity, which produces bradycardia often accompanied by profound hypotension from venodilation and reduced preload. A vasovagal reflex is possible but is not the primary mechanism in a block already extending this high. Spinal doses are far too small to cause systemic cardiotoxicity. There is no indication of preexisting conduction disease in this vignette.
  144. The correct answer is D (Low-dose hyperbaric bupivacaine injected while seated, kept upright several minutes.): this saddle block technique confines the hyperbaric solution to the sacral roots while the patient remains upright, providing dense perineal anesthesia while limiting cephalad spread, motor blockade, and sympathectomy. Isobaric solution would not settle preferentially to the sacral dermatomes. High-dose hyperbaric solution with Trendelenburg positioning would produce an unnecessarily high, hemodynamically destabilizing block. A T4 epidural level is far more extensive than required for anorectal surgery.
  145. The correct answer is B (An epidural blood patch using the patient's own autologous blood.): once conservative therapy fails for a symptomatic post-dural puncture headache, an epidural blood patch is the most effective treatment, sealing the dural defect and restoring cerebrospinal fluid pressure with success rates well above conservative management alone. Bed rest and caffeine provide only temporary symptomatic relief and do not treat the underlying leak. Analgesics and reassurance alone are inadequate once symptoms are severe and persistent. A repeat lumbar puncture would risk worsening the dural leak rather than treating it.
  146. The correct answer is A (Intravascular placement of the epidural catheter.): a heart rate increase of at least 10 beats per minute within about 30-60 seconds after an epinephrine-containing test dose is a classic sign of inadvertent intravascular injection, prompting catheter repositioning before further dosing. Labor pain alone would not typically produce such an abrupt, temporally linked tachycardic response to injection. Subarachnoid placement would more typically produce rapid, dense motor and sensory blockade rather than isolated tachycardia. The test dose given is far too small to produce systemic toxicity.
  147. The correct answer is C (Delayed intrathecal migration of the previously functioning epidural catheter.): an epidural catheter can migrate through the dura over time, and administering a standard epidural volume through a now-intrathecal catheter produces an unexpectedly extensive, rapidly ascending block that can progress toward a high or total spinal. A normal T4 epidural block does not typically ascend to the chin with respiratory compromise. Anxiety alone would not produce objective ascending sensory loss to this level. True amide allergy is exceedingly rare and would not present as an ascending motor and sensory block.
  148. The correct answer is D (Thread the catheter intrathecally and manage it as a continuous spinal.): converting to an intentional continuous spinal catheter after a recognized wet tap provides prompt, titratable analgesia and has been shown to reduce the incidence and severity of post-dural puncture headache compared with reattempting the epidural at another level. Abandoning neuraxial analgesia denies effective pain relief unnecessarily. A larger-gauge needle would increase, not decrease, headache risk if another dural puncture occurred. A single-shot spinal does not provide the titratable, continuous analgesia labor requires.
  149. The correct answer is A (Reduce the local anesthetic volume, since epidural venous engorgement narrows the epidural space.): pregnancy-related epidural venous engorgement decreases epidural space volume and cerebrospinal fluid volume, so a given dose spreads further, and this effect is often more pronounced in obese parturients, warranting reduced local anesthetic volumes to avoid an unexpectedly high block. Increasing the volume assumes an incorrect anatomic relationship and risks excessive spread. Omitting any adjustment ignores well-established physiologic changes of pregnancy. Simply changing concentration without addressing total dose does not correctly address the underlying volume issue.
  150. The correct answer is C (Epidural abscess requiring emergent surgical drainage and antibiotics.): the delayed onset of fever, progressive back pain, elevated inflammatory markers, and new neurologic deficits days after catheter removal is the classic presentation of epidural abscess, an infectious complication requiring emergent imaging and surgical decompression. Epidural hematoma typically presents more acutely, often within hours of catheter removal or placement, and is not associated with fever. Local anesthetic effects would have resolved days earlier and do not cause fever. Direct needle trauma would present immediately after placement, not days after catheter removal with signs of infection.
  151. The correct answer is B (A low-volume, low-concentration superior trunk block using less than 5 mL.): reducing the volume and concentration of local anesthetic and targeting the superior trunk rather than the classic interscalene groove markedly decreases the incidence of phrenic nerve blockade while still providing effective shoulder analgesia, which is important in a patient with limited pulmonary reserve. A standard-volume interscalene block reliably blocks the phrenic nerve given its close proximity to the brachial plexus at this level. Adding intercostal blocks does not reduce phrenic involvement from the interscalene component. Bilateral interscalene blocks would risk bilateral diaphragmatic paresis, which is contraindicated in this patient.
  152. The correct answer is D (The pleura and first rib lying deep to the brachial plexus.): the supraclavicular approach places the needle tip near the apex of the lung, making pneumothorax the most feared complication, so identifying the pleura and first rib and keeping the needle superficial to them is essential. The phrenic nerve is more relevant to the interscalene approach performed at a higher level. The recurrent laryngeal nerve is not in the immediate field of a supraclavicular block. The vertebral artery is a concern for cervical spine and stellate ganglion procedures rather than the supraclavicular fossa.
  153. The correct answer is C (The musculocutaneous nerve often exits the sheath early, requiring separate injection in the coracobrachialis.): the musculocutaneous nerve frequently departs the axillary neurovascular sheath more proximally than the other terminal branches, so it commonly requires a separate targeted injection within or near the coracobrachialis muscle to ensure complete blockade. Nerve transection would be an unusual and serious complication, not the routine explanation for incomplete coverage. If dosing were insufficient overall, the other nerves would likely be underblocked as well. Anomalous brachial plexus anatomy is uncommon and is not the typical reason for this well-described pattern.
  154. The correct answer is A (An adductor canal block, which is predominantly a sensory nerve block.): the adductor canal contains mainly the saphenous nerve and articular branches with minimal motor fibers to the quadriceps, so it provides comparable analgesia to a femoral block while substantially preserving quadriceps strength and reducing fall risk. A traditional femoral nerve block reliably weakens the quadriceps, increasing fall risk during early mobilization. A psoas compartment block anesthetizes the entire lumbar plexus, producing even greater motor weakness. A sciatic block alone does not adequately address the predominant anterior knee pain from total knee arthroplasty.
  155. The correct answer is D (Between the internal oblique and transversus abdominis muscles.): the transversus abdominis plane lies between the internal oblique and transversus abdominis muscles, and this is where the anterior rami of the thoracolumbar nerves travel before piercing the abdominal wall, so injection there provides effective anterior abdominal wall analgesia. Injection between the external and internal oblique would miss the target plane entirely. Injection deep to the transversalis fascia risks intraperitoneal placement and visceral injury. Superficial subcutaneous injection would not reach the nerves supplying the abdominal wall musculature and skin.
  156. The correct answer is B (Spread of local anesthetic into the paravertebral space, affecting dorsal and ventral rami.): local anesthetic deposited in the fascial plane deep to erector spinae is thought to spread medially into the paravertebral space, bathing the dorsal and ventral rami of the spinal nerves and producing multidermatomal thoracic analgesia. This block does not target the phrenic nerve, which arises higher in the cervical plexus. Pleural diffusion is not the primary accepted mechanism of this fascial plane block. While some systemic absorption occurs with any block, it is not the principal analgesic mechanism described for this technique.
  157. The correct answer is A (A saphenous nerve block, since this territory is not covered by the sciatic nerve.): the saphenous nerve, a terminal branch of the femoral nerve, supplies sensation to the medial aspect of the lower leg and ankle, an area not covered by the sciatic nerve, so it must be blocked separately for surgery extending to the medial malleolus. The superficial peroneal nerve is part of the common peroneal division of the sciatic nerve and would already be anesthetized. The sciatic nerve does not supply the medial ankle region. A full femoral nerve block is unnecessary when only saphenous coverage of the medial ankle is required.
  158. The correct answer is C (A pectoral nerve, or PECS, block performed in the interfascial planes of the chest wall.): PECS blocks deposit local anesthetic between the pectoralis major and minor and between pectoralis minor and serratus anterior, anesthetizing the pectoral nerves and lateral cutaneous branches of the intercostal nerves without entering the pleural or paravertebral space, avoiding pneumothorax risk. An interscalene block anesthetizes the upper extremity, not the chest wall. A deep cervical plexus block targets the neck and is unrelated to breast surgery. A stellate ganglion block treats sympathetically mediated pain conditions, not surgical chest wall analgesia.
  159. The correct answer is B (Stop injecting immediately, call for help, and prepare lipid emulsion and airway equipment.): metallic taste, perioral numbness, and tinnitus are classic early central nervous system warning signs of local anesthetic systemic toxicity, and injection must be stopped immediately with preparation for escalating treatment before progression to seizures or cardiovascular collapse. Continuing injection risks worsening toxicity. Sedating the patient without addressing the underlying toxicity is inappropriate and could mask progression. Simply repositioning the needle does not address the fact that a toxic dose may already be circulating.
  160. The correct answer is D (Use reduced epinephrine doses, less than 1 mcg/kg, if a vasopressor is required.): ASRA and resuscitation guidance for local anesthetic systemic toxicity recommend smaller epinephrine doses than standard ACLS boluses, since high-dose epinephrine may impair the efficacy of lipid emulsion resuscitation and worsen arrhythmias. Propofol is a poor substitute for lipid emulsion, as its lipid content is far too low and it carries cardiodepressant effects. Vasopressors should not be entirely withheld if hemodynamic support is needed. Standard 1 mg epinephrine doses are specifically discouraged in this setting.
  161. The correct answer is C (Slow dissociation from cardiac sodium channels, prolonging channel blockade.): bupivacaine binds cardiac fast sodium channels with slow dissociation kinetics, so blockade persists between heartbeats even at normal heart rates, producing conduction slowing and reentrant ventricular arrhythmias that are notoriously resistant to standard resuscitation. Rapid dissociation is characteristic of less cardiotoxic agents like lidocaine, not bupivacaine. Bupivacaine's principal cardiac target is the sodium channel, not potassium channels. Bupivacaine is an amide metabolized hepatically, not by plasma cholinesterase.
  162. The correct answer is B (Use incremental injection with frequent aspiration and ultrasound guidance throughout.): incremental injection with repeated aspiration allows detection of inadvertent intravascular placement before a large toxic dose is delivered, and real-time ultrasound visualization of needle tip and local anesthetic spread further reduces vascular puncture risk. Rapid full-dose injection removes the opportunity to detect early warning signs. Avoiding ultrasound would increase, not decrease, the risk of unrecognized intravascular injection. Using maximum allowable doses without weight-based adjustment increases toxicity risk in smaller or elderly patients with reduced clearance.
  163. The correct answer is A (Delay the neuraxial block until at least 12 hours have elapsed since the prophylactic dose.): ASRA guidelines recommend waiting at least 12 hours after a prophylactic dose of low molecular weight heparin before performing neuraxial anesthesia to allow adequate clearance and reduce spinal hematoma risk. Prophylactic dosing still carries a measurable bleeding risk and is not risk-free. Protamine only partially reverses low molecular weight heparin and is not a routine strategy to expedite neuraxial timing. The 24-hour interval applies specifically to therapeutic, not prophylactic, dosing.
  164. The correct answer is D (Aspirin monotherapy does not require discontinuation before neuraxial anesthesia.): ASRA guidelines specifically state that aspirin alone, without other antiplatelet or anticoagulant agents, does not need to be held before neuraxial procedures, as its associated spinal hematoma risk is not significantly increased. Discontinuing aspirin unnecessarily exposes the patient to cardiovascular risk without a clear anesthetic benefit. Bridging with another antiplatelet agent would increase, not decrease, bleeding risk. Routine platelet function testing is not required or recommended before neuraxial blockade in this setting.
  165. The correct answer is B (Approximately 72 hours, given that reduced renal clearance prolongs drug elimination.): direct oral anticoagulants like apixaban have a partially renal elimination pathway, and ASRA guidance recommends roughly 72 hours before neuraxial instrumentation even with normal renal function, extended further when renal clearance is impaired. A 24-hour hold is too short for apixaban regardless of renal status; its half-life alone does not justify such a brief interval. Direct oral anticoagulants carry a real and clinically significant spinal hematoma risk requiring appropriate holding periods. The 5-day interval is specific to clopidogrel, an irreversible antiplatelet agent, not apixaban.
  166. The correct answer is C (Catheter removal should be timed the same as needle placement, with dosing delayed afterward.): ASRA guidelines treat epidural catheter removal as an event with bleeding risk equivalent to needle placement, so the same timing intervals relative to anticoagulant dosing apply, and the next anticoagulant dose should be delayed after removal just as it would be after initial placement. Removing the catheter without regard to anticoagulant timing ignores this established risk. Giving anticoagulation immediately before removal increases spinal hematoma risk at the time of catheter withdrawal. Catheter removal is not a risk-free event and does require specific precautions.
  167. The correct answer is D (An INR of 1.6, with the most recent warfarin dose given two days earlier.): ASRA guidelines generally require a normalized INR, typically 1.4 or less, before neuraxial anesthesia in a patient recently on warfarin, so a persistently elevated INR of 1.6 indicates residual anticoagulant effect and unacceptable spinal hematoma risk. A normal platelet count and INR pose no contraindication. Therapeutic enoxaparin held for 26 hours meets the recommended interval of at least 24 hours for therapeutic dosing. Mild anemia with stable vital signs does not by itself contraindicate a neuraxial technique.
  168. The correct answer is A (Discuss delaying therapeutic anticoagulation and increase neurologic monitoring given the traumatic placement.): a traumatic or bloody neuraxial placement significantly increases the subsequent risk of spinal epidural hematoma if therapeutic anticoagulation follows too soon, so ASRA guidance supports discussing a delay in anticoagulation timing along with heightened neurologic monitoring. Proceeding as scheduled ignores the increased bleeding risk documented with the traumatic tap. Removing the catheter without coordinating anticoagulant timing does not by itself mitigate the hematoma risk already created. Increasing the anticoagulant dose would only increase bleeding risk further.
  169. The correct answer is C (Remifentanil is metabolized by nonspecific plasma and tissue esterases, independent of organ function.): remifentanil undergoes rapid hydrolysis by nonspecific blood and tissue esterases rather than hepatic or renal clearance, giving it a consistently short context-sensitive half-time regardless of infusion duration or organ dysfunction, making it ideal for this patient. Its context-sensitive half-time actually stays remarkably flat with prolonged infusion, unlike most opioids. Because clearance is esterase-dependent rather than organ-dependent, it does not accumulate in hepatic or renal failure. Morphine, not remifentanil, relies on hepatic glucuronide conjugation for elimination.
  170. The correct answer is A (Processed electroencephalographic monitoring used to titrate hypnotic depth.): in patients receiving neuromuscular blockade during total intravenous anesthesia, clinical signs of light anesthesia may be masked, so processed electroencephalographic monitoring provides an objective measure of hypnotic depth and helps reduce the risk of awareness. Train-of-four monitoring assesses neuromuscular blockade, not depth of hypnosis, and cannot detect awareness risk. Hemodynamic parameters are unreliable surrogates for anesthetic depth, particularly in cardiac surgery with hemodynamic instability from other causes. End-tidal gas monitoring is irrelevant when no volatile anesthetic is administered.
  171. The correct answer is B (Propofol infusion syndrome resulting from prolonged high-dose administration.): the combination of metabolic acidosis, hyperkalemia, rhabdomyolysis, and cardiac failure after a prolonged high-dose propofol infusion is the characteristic presentation of propofol infusion syndrome, a rare but potentially fatal complication associated with doses above approximately 4 mg/kg/hr for more than 48 hours. Propofol is not a recognized malignant hyperthermia trigger, unlike volatile anesthetics and succinylcholine. Serotonin syndrome presents with neuromuscular hyperactivity and autonomic instability, not this specific metabolic picture. Adrenal insufficiency does not typically cause rhabdomyolysis and hyperkalemia in this pattern.
  172. The correct answer is D (A dexmedetomidine infusion, which preserves respiratory drive at sedative doses.): dexmedetomidine produces cooperative, arousable sedation with anxiolysis while largely preserving ventilatory drive, making it particularly useful for awake airway procedures in patients with obstructive sleep apnea. Propofol titrated to deep sedation carries substantial risk of airway obstruction and apnea in this population. Repeated midazolam dosing risks cumulative respiratory depression and unpredictable sedation depth. Remifentanil alone can cause significant respiratory depression and does not reliably provide the anxiolysis needed for cooperation.
  173. The correct answer is C (Capnography, which detects hypoventilation before oxygen desaturation occurs.): capnography detects a fall in respiratory rate, apnea, or airway obstruction in real time, often before pulse oximetry shows any decline, especially in a patient receiving supplemental oxygen that delays desaturation despite hypoventilation. Electrocardiography detects arrhythmias but not ventilatory status. Intermittent blood pressure measurement is too infrequent and nonspecific to detect early respiratory compromise. Bispectral index reflects hypnotic depth, not ventilatory adequacy, and does not substitute for ventilation monitoring.
  174. The correct answer is A (General anesthesia, regardless of the absence of an airway device.): the continuum of sedation is defined by physiologic response, not by whether an airway device is placed, and loss of responsiveness to painful stimuli combined with loss of protective airway reflexes meets the definition of general anesthesia, requiring the same monitoring and vigilance regardless of intended sedation level. Deep sedation still requires purposeful response to painful stimulation, which is absent here. Moderate sedation preserves purposeful response to verbal or tactile stimulation. Minimal sedation preserves normal response to verbal stimulation and does not affect airway reflexes.
  175. The correct answer is B (A scalp block targeting the supraorbital, supratrochlear, auriculotemporal, and occipital nerves.): a scalp block anesthetizes the sensory nerves supplying the scalp and pin sites, providing excellent analgesia for pinning and craniotomy while allowing systemic sedatives to be minimized, preserving the patient's neurologic function and cooperation during awake mapping. Maintaining general anesthesia throughout would defeat the purpose of the asleep-awake-asleep technique, which requires an awake phase. A cervical epidural does not anesthetize the scalp or cranial structures. High-dose systemic opioids alone risk excessive sedation and respiratory depression that would impair cooperation during mapping.
  176. The correct answer is D (Irrigate the cortex with iced saline and be prepared to give a short-acting anticonvulsant.): cold saline irrigation of the cortical surface is the first-line intervention for stimulation-induced seizures during awake craniotomy, often terminating the seizure quickly, with a short-acting benzodiazepine or other rapidly acting anticonvulsant available if it does not resolve. Immediately abandoning mapping is unnecessary if the seizure resolves promptly with simple measures, and would compromise surgical goals. Increasing stimulation current would worsen, not treat, the seizure. A long-acting anticonvulsant would impair subsequent neurologic testing needed for continued mapping.
  177. The correct answer is A (Fiberoptic bronchoscopy performed through both lumens to directly visualize tube position.): fiberoptic bronchoscopy is the gold standard for confirming double-lumen tube position, directly visualizing the carina and bronchial cuff position, and is more reliable than clinical signs alone, which can be misleading. Auscultation has poor sensitivity and specificity for detecting malposition of double-lumen tubes. Chest radiography is not routinely used intraoperatively for this purpose and lacks the real-time precision of bronchoscopy. Equal chest rise during two-lung ventilation does not confirm correct bronchial lumen placement needed for lung isolation.
  178. The correct answer is B (Apply continuous positive airway pressure to the nonventilated, nondependent lung.): once tube malposition has been excluded, applying low-level CPAP to the nonventilated lung is a standard next step to improve oxygenation during one-lung ventilation by providing some gas exchange in that lung without significantly interfering with surgical exposure. Clamping the pulmonary artery is a surgical maneuver, not a routine anesthetic intervention for hypoxemia. Nitrous oxide is avoided during thoracic surgery and would not improve oxygenation. Decreasing PEEP on the ventilated lung would promote atelectasis and worsen, not improve, oxygenation.
  179. The correct answer is D (Volatile agents can attenuate hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction, and total intravenous anesthesia preserves this protective reflex.): volatile anesthetics in a dose-dependent fashion can blunt hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction, the reflex that diverts blood flow away from the underventilated lung, and switching to a total intravenous technique with propofol preserves this reflex and can improve oxygenation during one-lung ventilation. This means volatile agents impair rather than improve the reflex. Total intravenous anesthesia does affect pulmonary vascular tone favorably by preserving the reflex rather than having no effect. Volatile agents do have a measurable, dose-dependent inhibitory effect on this reflex, contrary to having none.
  180. The correct answer is A (Placement of a bronchial blocker through the existing single-lumen endotracheal tube.): a bronchial blocker can be advanced through or alongside an already-placed single-lumen tube under fiberoptic guidance, achieving effective lung isolation without the need to exchange the airway, which is especially valuable in a patient with a known difficult airway. Extubating and reintubating with a double-lumen tube unnecessarily repeats the difficult airway management, risking loss of the secured airway. Abandoning lung isolation may compromise surgical conditions unnecessarily when a safer alternative exists. Awake tracheostomy is an overly invasive option not indicated once the airway is already secured.
  181. The correct answer is C (Significant carotid artery stenosis with reduced cerebral perfusion reserve.): deliberate hypotension reduces cerebral perfusion pressure, and in a patient with significant carotid stenosis and limited collateral flow, even modest reductions in blood pressure can precipitate cerebral ischemia, making this technique relatively contraindicated. Young, healthy patients with good cardiovascular reserve generally tolerate deliberate hypotension well. A low physical status classification indicates good baseline health and is not a contraindication. Low anticipated blood loss does not itself contraindicate the technique, though it may reduce the indication for using it.
  182. The correct answer is A (Cyanide toxicity from prolonged, high-dose sodium nitroprusside infusion.): nitroprusside is metabolized to cyanide, and when hepatic detoxification capacity is exceeded during prolonged, high-dose infusion, cyanide accumulates and impairs cellular oxygen utilization, producing paradoxically elevated mixed venous oxygen saturation, metabolic acidosis, and altered mental status despite adequate systemic blood pressure. Renal failure is not the classic mechanism of nitroprusside-associated toxicity described here. Methemoglobinemia is more classically associated with nitroglycerin or other nitrite-generating compounds, not this presentation. Rebound hypertension occurs with abrupt discontinuation, not during an ongoing infusion, and does not explain this metabolic picture.
  183. The correct answer is D (Antithrombin deficiency requiring fresh frozen plasma or antithrombin concentrate.): heparin requires adequate antithrombin III to exert its anticoagulant effect, and acquired antithrombin deficiency, often from prior heparin exposure or critical illness, causes heparin resistance manifesting as a persistently low activated clotting time despite large heparin doses, treated with fresh frozen plasma or antithrombin concentrate. Protamine reversal would worsen the problem by further inhibiting anticoagulation. Warfarin effect would not cause heparin resistance and is treated differently. Factor VIII deficiency is unrelated to heparin's mechanism of action and would not explain this laboratory picture.
  184. The correct answer is B (Afterdrop, from redistribution of previously cooled peripheral blood back to the core.): after deep hypothermic circulatory arrest, peripheral tissues remain colder than the rewarmed core, and as peripheral vasodilation occurs and mixing resumes, cooled peripheral blood returns centrally, producing a secondary decline in core temperature known as afterdrop. Malignant hyperthermia produces rising, not falling, temperature and is not the described presentation. Sepsis typically produces fever or, in severe cases, hypothermia with hemodynamic instability, not this predictable postbypass pattern. Adrenal insufficiency does not characteristically cause this specific delayed temperature drop after deep hypothermic bypass.
  185. The correct answer is C (Vasopressin infusion, which is effective in catecholamine-resistant vasoplegia.): post-cardiopulmonary bypass vasoplegic syndrome often responds poorly to escalating catecholamine doses alone, and vasopressin, acting through a distinct V1 receptor mechanism, is particularly effective in restoring vascular tone in this catecholamine-resistant state. Fluid boluses alone do not address the underlying loss of vascular tone and risk volume overload. A pure beta-agonist like dobutamine would worsen vasodilation and is not indicated for low systemic vascular resistance. Discontinuing vasopressors in a profoundly hypotensive patient would risk further hemodynamic collapse.
  186. The correct answer is A (Proceed immediately to an emergency invasive airway, such as a cricothyrotomy.): once both intubation and supraglottic airway ventilation have failed and oxygen saturation is declining, this represents a cannot-intubate, cannot-oxygenate emergency, and the algorithm mandates immediate progression to an emergency invasive airway rather than further attempts at the failed techniques. Repeated laryngoscopy attempts waste critical time and worsen hypoxemia in a true cannot-oxygenate scenario. Awakening the patient is not feasible once oxygenation cannot be maintained and hypoxemia is progressing. Additional neuromuscular blockade will not resolve an anatomic or mechanical inability to ventilate the patient.
  187. The correct answer is D (Awake fiberoptic intubation performed with topicalization and light sedation.): when multiple significant difficult airway predictors are present together, securing the airway while the patient maintains spontaneous ventilation and airway reflexes through an awake technique is the safest approach, avoiding a cannot-intubate, cannot-oxygenate scenario after induction. Standard rapid sequence induction risks losing the airway entirely in a patient predicted to be difficult to intubate. A supraglottic airway as the primary plan does not secure a definitive airway and may fail given these predictors. Deep sedation followed by direct laryngoscopy still risks loss of airway control before intubation is achieved.
  188. The correct answer is B (Extubation over an airway exchange catheter, left in place until the airway is confirmed adequate.): an airway exchange catheter left in the trachea after extubation allows rapid reintubation over the catheter if the patient develops respiratory distress, providing a safety net specifically valuable after a difficult intubation. Standard extubation without precautions ignores the documented difficulty in securing this patient's airway initially. Elective tracheostomy is an overly invasive step not routinely indicated simply because an awake intubation was required. Deep extubation removes airway reflex protection and does not provide any mechanism for rapid airway reestablishment if needed.
  189. The correct answer is C (Rising end-tidal carbon dioxide that is out of proportion to ventilation.): an unexplained, rapid rise in end-tidal carbon dioxide despite constant minute ventilation reflects the massive hypermetabolic state of malignant hyperthermia and is typically the earliest and most sensitive clinical sign, often preceding significant temperature elevation. Temperature rise, while characteristic, is frequently a later finding rather than the earliest sign. Malignant hyperthermia classically causes tachycardia, not bradycardia, as an early sign. Malignant hyperthermia typically causes hyperkalemia from rhabdomyolysis, not hypokalemia.
  190. The correct answer is A (Dantrolene sodium 2.5 mg/kg intravenously, repeated as clinically needed.): dantrolene interferes with calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum through the ryanodine receptor and is the definitive, life-saving treatment for malignant hyperthermia, given as an initial bolus and repeated until signs resolve. Calcium gluconate may be used to treat associated hyperkalemia but is not the definitive treatment for the underlying hypermetabolic crisis. Nondepolarizing neuromuscular blockers do not address the abnormal intracellular calcium release causing malignant hyperthermia. Corticosteroids have no established role in the acute treatment of malignant hyperthermia.
  191. The correct answer is B (Total intravenous anesthesia with propofol and remifentanil.): Propofol reduces cerebral metabolic rate and cerebral blood flow in a coupled fashion while preserving autoregulation across a wide range of mean arterial pressures. Volatile agents such as isoflurane, sevoflurane, and desflurane cause dose-dependent cerebral vasodilation and progressively impair autoregulation above roughly 1 MAC. Nitrous oxide further increases cerebral blood flow and can worsen intracranial pressure when combined with a volatile agent. TIVA is therefore preferred in patients with poor intracranial compliance or impaired autoregulation.
  192. The correct answer is A (Cerebrospinal fluid bicarbonate re-equilibrates, so the vasoconstrictive effect on cerebral vessels diminishes over 24 to 48 hours.): Acute hypocapnia alkalinizes cerebrospinal fluid and causes cerebral vasoconstriction, but choroid plexus and glial cells actively transport bicarbonate to normalize CSF pH over roughly one to two days, blunting the vasoconstrictive benefit. Prolonged hyperventilation therefore loses efficacy and can leave the brain vulnerable to ischemia once PaCO2 is normalized because cerebral vessels remain adapted to the lower CO2 level. This is why sustained prophylactic hyperventilation is discouraged in current neurocritical care guidelines.
  193. The correct answer is C (85 mmHg.): Cerebral perfusion pressure equals mean arterial pressure minus intracranial pressure, so achieving a CPP of 60 mmHg with an ICP of 25 mmHg requires a MAP of at least 85 mmHg. Maintaining adequate CPP is essential to prevent secondary ischemic injury after traumatic brain injury while avoiding excessive pressure that could worsen cerebral edema. Vasopressors are frequently required to reach this MAP target when intracranial pressure remains elevated despite osmotherapy and drainage.
  194. The correct answer is D (Open the external ventricular drain to allow cerebrospinal fluid drainage.): Cerebrospinal fluid drainage produces the fastest and most reliable reduction in intracranial pressure when an external ventricular drain is already positioned, since it directly reduces intracranial volume. Further hyperventilation to a PaCO2 of 20 mmHg risks cerebral ischemia from excessive vasoconstriction, and repeating mannitol too soon increases the risk of hyperosmolar renal injury and rebound intracranial hypertension. Volatile agents actually increase cerebral blood volume and would worsen intracranial pressure rather than lower it.
  195. The correct answer is B (Precordial Doppler ultrasound placed over the right sternal border area.): Precordial Doppler placed over the right heart border detects the characteristic mill-wheel murmur produced by even small volumes of intravascular air, making it the most sensitive clinically practical monitor for venous air embolism. Transesophageal echocardiography is more sensitive still but is not routinely used for this purpose. A sudden decrease in end-tidal carbon dioxide is a useful but less sensitive sign that occurs only after a larger volume of air has entered the circulation and increased dead space. Arterial pressure and pulse oximetry changes are late and nonspecific findings.
  196. The correct answer is D (A patient with congestive heart failure and oliguric renal failure.): Mannitol acts as an osmotic diuretic that initially expands intravascular volume before promoting diuresis, which can precipitate acute pulmonary edema in patients with poor cardiac reserve, and it can accumulate and worsen renal injury when clearance is impaired by renal failure. Diabetes, migraine history, and mild allergic rhinitis do not represent significant contraindications to mannitol therapy. Careful volume status and renal function assessment are required before repeated dosing in high-risk cardiac or renal patients.
  197. The correct answer is A (Autonomic hyperreflexia triggered by bladder distension.): In patients with spinal cord injury at or above T6, a noxious stimulus below the level of injury such as bladder or bowel distension can trigger massive unopposed sympathetic outflow, producing severe hypertension with reflex vagally mediated bradycardia and flushing above the lesion. Malignant hyperthermia presents with hyperthermia, tachycardia, and rigidity rather than bradycardia. Myocardial infarction and anaphylaxis do not typically produce this specific pattern of hypertension paired with bradycardia and flushing confined above the injury level.
  198. The correct answer is C (Ensure adequate depth of general or spinal anesthesia before instrumentation begins.): Spinal or adequately deep general anesthesia blocks the afferent sympathetic reflex arc responsible for autonomic hyperreflexia and is the most reliable preventive strategy in patients with lesions at or above T6. Beta-blockade alone does not block the underlying reflex and treats only the cardiovascular consequence. Minimal sedation without adequate anesthetic depth fails to prevent the noxious afferent stimulus from triggering the reflex, and waiting to treat reactively risks a hypertensive crisis, stroke, or seizure.
  199. The correct answer is B (Endotracheal intubation to protect the airway.): A Glasgow Coma Scale score of 8 or less indicates an inability to reliably protect the airway and is a well-established indication for endotracheal intubation prior to transport or imaging in a patient with traumatic brain injury. Discharge or ward observation would be unsafe given the depressed level of consciousness and need for emergent surgical evacuation. Analgesics and a cervical collar alone do not address the airway risk posed by a severely depressed mental status.
  200. The correct answer is A (Permit relative hypertension to maintain perfusion to the ischemic penumbra.): In acute ischemic stroke prior to reperfusion, cerebral autoregulation in the ischemic territory is impaired, so blood flow to the penumbra becomes pressure dependent, and permissive hypertension helps preserve collateral perfusion until the vessel is recanalized. Aggressively lowering blood pressure or inducing hypotension before reperfusion can extend the area of infarction by reducing collateral flow. Blood pressure targets are lowered only after successful reperfusion to reduce hemorrhagic transformation risk.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Maintain blood pressure at or slightly above the patient's baseline.): During carotid cross-clamping, maintaining blood pressure at or modestly above the patient's baseline maximizes collateral flow through the circle of Willis to the ipsilateral hemisphere, reducing the risk of clamp-related cerebral ischemia. Lowering blood pressure below baseline during this critical period reduces collateral perfusion pressure and increases stroke risk. Strict normotension without allowance for increased collateral demand may be insufficient in patients with limited collateral circulation.
  2. The correct answer is C (Methohexital.): Methohexital is the classic induction agent for electroconvulsive therapy because it produces a brief anesthetic with minimal anticonvulsant activity, preserving adequate seizure duration and quality. Propofol and benzodiazepines both raise the seizure threshold and shorten seizure duration, which can reduce the therapeutic efficacy of the treatment. Dexmedetomidine is a sedative-hypnotic with anticonvulsant, not proconvulsant, properties and is not used for this indication.
  3. The correct answer is A (It markedly reduces cerebral metabolic rate of oxygen consumption.): Burst suppression, typically induced with high-dose propofol or barbiturates, dramatically reduces the electrical component of cerebral metabolic demand, allowing neurons to better tolerate a period of reduced oxygen delivery during temporary vascular occlusion. It does not increase cerebral blood flow or directly affect the clipped vessel, and it does not raise intracranial pressure. The protective benefit is metabolic suppression that better matches oxygen supply and demand during ischemia.
  4. The correct answer is B (Unopposed sympathetic discharge below the injury with reflex vagal bradycardia above it.): In autonomic hyperreflexia, a noxious stimulus below the level of a high spinal cord injury triggers massive sympathetic outflow that cannot be modulated by descending inhibitory pathways, causing severe vasoconstriction and hypertension below the lesion. The intact baroreceptor reflex above the lesion responds to this hypertension with vagally mediated bradycardia, which is why the heart rate falls even as blood pressure rises sharply. This combination is distinct from hypovolemic or opioid-related bradycardia, which would not present with this hemodynamic pattern.
  5. The correct answer is D (Either technique can be appropriate, provided hypotension is avoided throughout the procedure.): Randomized trials comparing general anesthesia and conscious sedation for mechanical thrombectomy have shown that either approach can achieve good functional outcomes when strict blood pressure control is maintained, with hypotension being the key modifiable factor associated with worse outcomes. Neither technique is absolutely contraindicated, and evidence does not support one approach as uniformly superior. The anesthetic plan should be individualized while prioritizing avoidance of hypotension and rapid procedural time.
  6. The correct answer is C (Document the baseline neurologic deficit in detail before performing the block.): Careful documentation of a preexisting neurologic deficit before any regional anesthetic or positioning intervention is essential to distinguish preoperative findings from any new postoperative nerve injury and to guide medicolegal and clinical decision-making. A preexisting neuropathy is not an absolute contraindication to regional anesthesia, though the double-crush phenomenon suggests some added vulnerability that should be discussed with the patient. Increasing local anesthetic concentration does not address this issue and could increase toxicity risk.
  7. The correct answer is B (The curve shifts rightward, so a higher mean arterial pressure is needed to maintain adequate cerebral perfusion.): Chronic hypertension causes structural and functional adaptation of cerebral vasculature that shifts the autoregulatory curve to the right, meaning both the lower and upper limits of autoregulation occur at higher pressures than in normotensive individuals. Consequently, a mean arterial pressure that would be entirely adequate in a normotensive patient may fall below this patient's lower autoregulatory limit and risk cerebral hypoperfusion. This is why blood pressure targets are often kept closer to the patient's own baseline rather than a fixed population value.
  8. The correct answer is C (Placement of an intracranial pressure monitor.): Guidelines recommend intracranial pressure monitoring in patients with severe traumatic brain injury, defined as a Glasgow Coma Scale score of 8 or less, who also have an abnormal head CT showing hematoma, contusion, swelling, or compressed cisterns, since these patients are at high risk for intracranial hypertension. Discharge and delayed monitoring would be unsafe given the severity of injury. Immediate extubation is contraindicated because the depressed level of consciousness places the patient at risk for airway compromise.
  9. The correct answer is A (Maximal reduction of cerebral oxygen demand to improve tolerance of temporary ischemia.): Titrating propofol to achieve burst suppression on EEG, as documented in the report, maximally suppresses the electrical component of cerebral metabolism, providing the greatest available margin of protection for neurons during the period of temporary vessel occlusion. This technique does not meaningfully improve surgical exposure, does not prevent postoperative nausea, and has no direct effect on aneurysm bleeding. The goal is purely metabolic protection during a period of reduced oxygen delivery.
  10. The correct answer is D (Excessive cerebral vasoconstriction causing regional cerebral ischemia.): Aggressive hyperventilation to a PaCO2 this low produces profound cerebral vasoconstriction that can reduce cerebral blood flow below the ischemic threshold in already vulnerable brain tissue, worsening secondary injury despite lowering intracranial pressure. Cerebral blood volume actually decreases, not increases, with hypocapnia because vessels constrict. Metabolic alkalosis and altered airway reflexes are not the primary concerns driving current recommendations against prophylactic aggressive hyperventilation in traumatic brain injury.
  11. The correct answer is C (Sevoflurane.): Sevoflurane at high concentrations, particularly when combined with hyperventilation, is well documented to produce epileptiform EEG activity and has occasionally been associated with clinical seizure-like movements, especially in pediatric patients. Isoflurane and desflurane are not associated with this proconvulsant EEG pattern and are generally considered safer choices in patients with a seizure history. Halothane is not associated with epileptiform activity and is rarely used in modern practice.
  12. The correct answer is B (6 to 7 percent.): Cerebral metabolic rate of oxygen consumption decreases by approximately 6 to 7 percent for every 1 degree Celsius reduction in brain temperature, which is the physiologic basis for using mild to moderate hypothermia as a neuroprotective strategy in select settings. A reduction of only 1 to 2 percent would be too small to explain the clinically observed metabolic suppression, while 20 percent or greater per degree substantially overstates the actual effect. This relationship also underlies the concern that fever worsens secondary brain injury by increasing metabolic demand.
  13. The correct answer is A (Neurogenic shock from loss of sympathetic tone, distinct from the later hyperreflexia phase that follows spinal shock resolution.): Neurogenic shock occurs in the acute phase after high spinal cord injury due to interruption of descending sympathetic pathways, producing hypotension, bradycardia, and vasodilation with warm extremities, which is the opposite hemodynamic pattern from the hypertensive autonomic hyperreflexia that can develop weeks later once spinal reflexes below the injury recover. Hypovolemic, septic, and cardiogenic shock would not typically present with this combination of bradycardia and warm flushed skin in the immediate postinjury period. Recognizing neurogenic shock is important because volume resuscitation alone is often insufficient and vasopressor support is frequently required.
  14. The correct answer is D (Ulnar nerve injury from compression at the elbow.): Ulnar neuropathy, presenting with numbness and weakness in the fourth and fifth fingers, is the most commonly reported perioperative peripheral nerve injury and typically results from compression of the nerve as it passes superficially through the cubital tunnel at the elbow, particularly in supinated or unpadded arm positions. Radial and median nerve injuries produce different sensory and motor patterns and are less common overall. Brachial plexus injury from arm abduction typically produces a broader pattern of upper extremity weakness rather than isolated fourth and fifth finger involvement.
  15. The correct answer is B (Blood pressure must not be lowered below the lower limit of cerebral autoregulation.): Controlled hypotension can be used cautiously to reduce transmural pressure across an aneurysm wall, but mean arterial pressure must be kept above the lower limit of the patient's cerebral autoregulatory range to avoid global or regional cerebral ischemia, particularly in areas with impaired collateral flow. Autoregulation actively buffers cerebral blood flow across a normal pressure range, so pressure reduction does affect perfusion once that limit is exceeded. The technique is used selectively rather than being universally contraindicated, and it does not require concurrent aggressive hyperventilation.
  16. The correct answer is D (The ventilation-perfusion ratio is highest at the apex (West zone 1) and lowest at the base.): Both ventilation and perfusion increase from apex to base due to gravity, but perfusion increases proportionally more than ventilation, resulting in a high ventilation-perfusion ratio at the apex and a low ratio at the base, described by West's zone model.
