Ventilation is not highest at the apex in absolute terms, and perfusion is not highest at the apex either. Ventilation and perfusion are never perfectly matched throughout the lung even in healthy individuals, which is why physiologic V/Q mismatch always exists to some degree.
  17. The correct answer is C (Acute pulmonary embolism reducing perfusion to ventilated alveoli.): Alveolar dead space increases when alveoli continue to be ventilated but perfusion is reduced or absent, exactly as occurs when a pulmonary embolus obstructs blood flow to a ventilated lung segment, widening the arterial to end-tidal CO2 gradient. Mucus plugging and pneumonia both reduce ventilation to perfused alveoli, producing shunt physiology rather than dead space. One-lung ventilation with the operative lung collapsed removes ventilation from that lung entirely, which primarily creates a shunt through the nonventilated, still-perfused lung rather than increasing dead space.
  18. The correct answer is A (Intrapulmonary shunt, where blood bypasses ventilated alveoli entirely.): The hallmark of significant intrapulmonary shunt is failure of hypoxemia to correct with 100 percent supplemental oxygen, because shunted blood never contacts ventilated alveoli and therefore cannot be oxygenated regardless of the inspired oxygen concentration. Increased dead space and diffusion limitation typically respond, at least partially, to supplemental oxygen. Simple hypoventilation also generally responds well to increased FiO2 since the alveoli remain ventilated and perfused, just with lower overall minute ventilation.
  19. The correct answer is B (Obstructive disease shows a reduced FEV1/FVC ratio, while restrictive disease shows a normal or increased ratio with reduced FVC.): Obstructive lung disease is characterized by airflow limitation that disproportionately reduces FEV1 relative to FVC, lowering the FEV1/FVC ratio, whereas restrictive disease reduces both FEV1 and FVC proportionally, keeping the ratio normal or even slightly increased. Air trapping and increased residual volume are features of obstructive, not restrictive, disease. Reduced total lung capacity is a defining feature of restrictive disease, not obstructive disease, which typically shows increased or preserved total lung capacity.
  20. The correct answer is D (Ketamine.): Ketamine produces bronchodilation through sympathomimetic stimulation and is therefore a preferred induction agent in patients with active bronchospasm or poorly controlled asthma. Morphine and atracurium both promote histamine release, which can precipitate or worsen bronchospasm in susceptible patients. Thiopental can also provoke bronchospasm through histamine release and vagally mediated airway reflexes and is not preferred in this setting.
  21. The correct answer is A (88 to 92 percent, to avoid worsening hypercapnia from oxygen-induced changes in ventilatory drive and V/Q matching.): Chronic CO2 retainers with compensated respiratory acidosis are conventionally targeted to an oxygen saturation of 88 to 92 percent because excessive supplemental oxygen can worsen hypercapnia through changes in hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction and V/Q relationships as well as modest effects on chemoreceptor drive. Targeting saturations of 95 to 100 percent, as used in patients without chronic lung disease, risks clinically significant hypercapnia and respiratory acidosis in this population. Allowing saturation below 85 percent is not an appropriate target and risks tissue hypoxia.
  22. The correct answer is C (Smoking cessation and optimization of bronchodilator therapy before surgery.): Smoking cessation, ideally initiated well before surgery, along with optimization of inhaled bronchodilator and, when indicated, corticosteroid therapy to achieve the patient's best baseline pulmonary function has the strongest evidence for reducing postoperative pulmonary complications in patients with COPD. Empiric systemic corticosteroids are not routinely recommended for all patients regardless of exacerbation status. Prophylactic postoperative ventilation is not standard practice, and routine pulmonary function testing has not been shown to reliably predict individual surgical risk in this population.
  23. The correct answer is D (Neck circumference greater than 40 centimeters.): The STOP-BANG questionnaire includes Snoring, Tiredness, Observed apnea, blood Pressure, BMI, Age, Neck circumference, and Gender, with a large neck circumference being a recognized independent risk factor for obstructive sleep apnea due to increased soft tissue around the upper airway. Asthma or chronic bronchitis history, gastroesophageal reflux disease, and family history of sudden cardiac death are not components of this specific screening instrument. Each STOP-BANG component is scored to stratify patients into low, intermediate, or high risk for obstructive sleep apnea.
  24. The correct answer is B (Extended monitoring for opioid-induced respiratory depression before discharge.): Patients with obstructive sleep apnea have increased upper airway collapsibility and heightened sensitivity to the respiratory depressant effects of opioids, so extended postoperative monitoring for hypoxemia and hypoventilation before discharge is a key safety measure in this population. Routine chest radiography is not indicated in this scenario, and mandatory ICU admission is not required for every patient with obstructive sleep apnea undergoing routine outpatient surgery. Regional anesthesia is actually often preferred over systemic opioids in these patients when feasible, rather than being avoided.
  25. The correct answer is C (Administration of a volatile anesthetic agent at concentrations above 1 MAC.): Volatile anesthetics inhibit hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction in a dose-dependent manner, and concentrations above approximately 1 MAC can significantly blunt this protective reflex, worsening shunt and hypoxemia during one-lung ventilation. Lateral positioning actually helps by favoring perfusion to the dependent, ventilated lung due to gravity. Low tidal volume ventilation of the dependent lung and the choice between a double-lumen tube and bronchial blocker do not directly inhibit the hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction reflex itself.
  26. The correct answer is A (Apply continuous positive airway pressure to the nondependent, nonventilated lung.): Once tube position is confirmed and FiO2 is maximized, applying low-level continuous positive airway pressure to the collapsed nondependent lung provides some oxygenation to that lung while minimally interfering with surgical exposure, directly reducing shunt fraction. Converting back to two-lung ventilation is reserved for severe, refractory desaturation because it interrupts surgical access. Decreasing PEEP on the dependent lung would worsen atelectasis and shunt rather than improve oxygenation, and a fluid bolus does not address the underlying ventilation-perfusion mismatch causing the desaturation.
  27. The correct answer is B (The upper abdominal surgical site combined with the smoking history and reduced functional capacity.): Upper abdominal and thoracic surgical sites are among the strongest independent predictors of postoperative pulmonary complications because they impair diaphragmatic function and reduce functional residual capacity, and this risk is compounded by patient-level factors such as heavy smoking history and poor baseline functional capacity. Age alone is a weaker independent predictor compared with these combined factors. The airway device used and choice of a short-acting neuromuscular blocker are modifiable technical factors but are not the dominant drivers of risk in this scenario, though residual neuromuscular blockade from an inadequately reversed longer-acting agent would be more concerning.
  28. The correct answer is C (6 mL/kg of predicted body weight.): The ARDSNet trial demonstrated a mortality benefit with a lung-protective strategy using tidal volumes of approximately 6 mL/kg of predicted body weight, which reduces overdistension and volutrauma in the heterogeneously affected ARDS lung. Tidal volumes of 12 to 15 mL/kg reflect older, higher-volume strategies now known to worsen outcomes in ARDS. Predicted body weight, not actual body weight, is used for dosing because it correlates with lung size, and permissive hypercapnia is generally accepted rather than pursuing normal PaCO2 at the expense of higher tidal volumes.
  29. The correct answer is D (30 cmH2O.): Current lung-protective ventilation guidelines target a plateau pressure below 30 cmH2O because pressures above this threshold are associated with increased alveolar overdistension, barotrauma, and worsened outcomes in patients with ARDS. Thresholds of 15, 20, or 25 cmH2O are lower than the accepted clinical limit and would be overly restrictive for many patients while still achieving adequate ventilation. Plateau pressure, measured during an inspiratory hold, better reflects alveolar distending pressure than peak airway pressure because it eliminates the resistive pressure component from gas flow through the airways.
  30. The correct answer is A (Recruitment of collapsed alveoli to improve ventilation-perfusion matching and reduce shunt.): Increasing PEEP helps recruit and stabilize collapsed alveoli at end-expiration, reducing cyclic atelectasis and intrapulmonary shunt, which improves oxygenation in patients with ARDS. PEEP typically increases, rather than decreases, intrathoracic pressure, which can reduce venous return and cardiac output as an unwanted side effect rather than a therapeutic goal. It does not directly reduce pulmonary vascular resistance and is not applied simply to permit a lower FiO2 without regard to actual oxygenation status.
  31. The correct answer is C (Severe ARDS, based on this patient's ratio.): The Berlin definition classifies ARDS severity by the PaO2/FiO2 ratio measured with a minimum PEEP of 5 cmH2O, with severe ARDS defined as a ratio of 100 or less; a PaO2 of 90 mmHg on an FiO2 of 1.0 gives a ratio of 90, meeting the threshold for severe disease. Mild ARDS corresponds to a ratio between 201 and 300, and moderate ARDS corresponds to a ratio between 101 and 200. This patient's markedly reduced ratio, combined with bilateral infiltrates and the appropriate clinical context, is inconsistent with ruling out ARDS.
  32. The correct answer is A (Reduced FVC and reduced total lung capacity with a normal or increased FEV1/FVC ratio.): Restrictive lung diseases such as pulmonary fibrosis reduce lung compliance, leading to proportional reductions in both FVC and total lung capacity while the FEV1/FVC ratio remains normal or is slightly increased because airflow itself is not obstructed. A reduced FEV1/FVC ratio with increased residual volume is characteristic of obstructive disease with air trapping, not restrictive disease. While diffusion capacity is often reduced in fibrosis, total lung capacity is also reduced rather than normal, and FVC is decreased rather than increased.
  33. The correct answer is B (Pulmonary embolism increases dead space, while pneumonia produces shunt physiology.): Pulmonary embolism obstructs blood flow to ventilated alveoli, creating high V/Q units that behave as dead space, whereas pneumonia fills alveoli with exudate and fluid, preventing ventilation to still-perfused alveoli and creating low V/Q, shunt-like physiology. These two processes therefore have essentially opposite effects on the ventilation-perfusion relationship rather than producing the same abnormality. Both conditions clearly alter V/Q matching significantly, so neither can be described as having no meaningful effect.
  34. The correct answer is D (Deepen anesthesia with a volatile agent and administer inhaled beta-2 agonist.): Once tube position and circuit patency are confirmed, intraoperative bronchospasm is best treated by deepening anesthesia with a volatile agent, which has bronchodilating properties, combined with an inhaled beta-2 agonist delivered through the circuit, with epinephrine reserved for severe or refractory cases. Extubation is unsafe and unnecessary once the airway is secured and the tube position is confirmed correct. A fluid bolus does not address bronchospasm, and changing tube size intraoperatively is impractical and does not treat the underlying bronchoconstriction.
  35. The correct answer is C (Reduced functional residual capacity that falls below closing capacity, promoting earlier alveolar collapse.): Obesity and obstructive sleep apnea reduce functional residual capacity, which can fall below closing capacity even during normal tidal breathing, promoting small airway closure and atelectasis that reduces the oxygen reservoir available during apnea. This smaller oxygen reserve, combined with often increased oxygen consumption, leads to faster desaturation during apnea compared with patients who have normal functional residual capacity. Minute ventilation and dead space are not relevant during a true apneic period since there is no gas exchange occurring, and hemoglobin oxygen affinity is not characteristically altered by obesity.
  36. The correct answer is B (Permissive hypercapnia is accepted to avoid the greater harm of ventilator-induced lung injury from higher tidal volumes.): Permissive hypercapnia is a deliberate strategy in lung-protective ventilation for ARDS, accepting a moderate degree of respiratory acidosis as a trade-off for maintaining low tidal volumes and plateau pressures that reduce the risk of volutrauma and barotrauma. Hypercapnia does have physiologic effects, including increased cerebral blood flow and myocardial depression at extreme levels, so it is not simply ignored but rather tolerated within accepted limits and monitored closely. The blood gas findings reflect the expected consequence of the protective strategy rather than a ventilator malfunction, and tidal volume is not automatically increased for any degree of hypercapnia above 45 mmHg.
  37. The correct answer is A (An absolute indication, given the risk of air leak and lung cross-contamination.): Bronchopleural fistula, along with conditions like massive hemoptysis, unilateral lung infection risking spillage, and a giant lung cyst or bulla at risk of rupture, is classified as an absolute indication for lung isolation because continued two-lung ventilation could worsen the air leak or allow soiling of the healthy contralateral lung. Relative indications, such as improved surgical exposure for thoracic procedures, are important but not as critical to patient safety. This scenario is neither a non-indication nor a contraindication to lung isolation, since isolation is precisely the intervention needed to protect the patient.
  38. The correct answer is D (Incentive spirometry, early ambulation, and adequate multimodal pain control.): A bundle combining incentive spirometry to promote lung expansion, early ambulation to improve functional residual capacity and mobilize secretions, and adequate pain control that allows effective deep breathing and coughing has the strongest evidence for reducing postoperative pulmonary complications after upper abdominal surgery. Prolonged bed rest actually worsens atelectasis risk by further reducing functional residual capacity. Prophylactic antibiotics alone do not address the mechanical causes of atelectasis, and unnecessarily delaying oral intake does not reduce pulmonary complication risk and may harm overall recovery.
  39. The correct answer is C (Prolong the expiratory time by decreasing respiratory rate or increasing inspiratory flow rate.): The described waveform indicates dynamic hyperinflation from auto-PEEP, or breath stacking, common in severe COPD, and the primary treatment is to increase available expiratory time by reducing respiratory rate or increasing inspiratory flow to shorten inspiratory time, allowing more complete exhalation. Increasing respiratory rate or tidal volume would further shorten expiratory time and worsen air trapping and hemodynamic compromise. While matching extrinsic PEEP can sometimes help trigger effort in spontaneously breathing patients, it does not address the underlying air trapping and is not the first corrective step in this hemodynamically unstable, fully ventilated patient.
  40. The correct answer is B (Continuous pulse oximetry monitoring with supplemental oxygen and resumption of home positive airway pressure therapy.): Patients with obstructive sleep apnea are at increased risk for recurrent postoperative desaturation events, particularly when receiving opioids, so continuous oximetry monitoring, supplemental oxygen as needed, and prompt resumption of the patient's home continuous positive airway pressure device are recommended to reduce the risk of respiratory events. Discontinuing monitoring would remove the ability to detect further desaturation episodes. Increasing the opioid dose would worsen respiratory depression and upper airway collapsibility, and withholding positive airway pressure therapy removes an effective treatment for this patient's underlying airway obstruction.
  41. The correct answer is A (Restrictive lung disease, with reduced lung volumes but a preserved or increased FEV1/FVC ratio.): pulmonary fibrosis reduces lung compliance, producing proportionally reduced FVC and FEV1 with a preserved or supranormal FEV1/FVC ratio. Obstructive disease instead lowers the FEV1/FVC ratio because expiratory flow is preferentially limited. A ratio of 92% argues strongly against obstruction. These findings are the classic restrictive pattern and require no bronchodilator testing to confirm.
  42. The correct answer is B (Postpone surgery and optimize her asthma with systemic corticosteroids and bronchodilators until wheezing resolves.): active wheezing on exam indicates poorly controlled asthma with heightened airway reactivity, which increases the risk of severe intraoperative and postoperative bronchospasm. Elective surgery should be delayed to allow optimization with bronchodilators and, if needed, a short course of systemic steroids. Proceeding despite active wheezing accepts avoidable risk. Regional anesthesia does not eliminate the risk of bronchospasm from anxiety, positioning, or airway manipulation in an unoptimized asthmatic.
  43. The correct answer is A (Smoking cessation started now, ideally at least four to eight weeks before surgery.): smoking cessation of four to eight weeks or longer before surgery meaningfully reduces postoperative pulmonary complications by improving mucociliary clearance and reducing airway inflammation. Empiric antibiotics are not indicated absent evidence of infection. Repeat PFTs rarely change management in a patient with known severe COPD. An echocardiogram assesses cardiac, not pulmonary, risk and is not the intervention that reduces pulmonary complications.
  44. The correct answer is C (Score of 6, high risk for obstructive sleep apnea.): he scores one point each for snoring, tiredness, treated hypertension (pressure), BMI over 35, age over 50, and male gender, for a total of 6, which places him in the high-risk category (score of 5 or greater). Neck circumference is one of the eight STOP-BANG items but is not required to calculate a valid score from the other seven components. His score is far higher than options A or B suggest.
  45. The correct answer is B (Multimodal analgesia that minimizes opioid use, combined with extended postoperative pulse oximetry monitoring.): patients with OSA have heightened sensitivity to opioid-induced airway obstruction and respiratory depression, so nonopioid multimodal analgesics reduce opioid requirements while extended monitoring detects desaturation before it becomes clinically severe. Opioid-only regimens and scheduled long-acting opioids increase respiratory depression risk. Discharging a patient with residual sedation ignores the increased vulnerability of OSA patients to unrecognized hypoventilation.
  46. The correct answer is D (It is hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction, which can be blunted by high concentrations of volatile anesthetic.): hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction redirects blood flow away from the hypoxic, nonventilated lung toward the ventilated lung, limiting shunt during one-lung ventilation. Volatile anesthetics can attenuate this reflex in a dose-dependent manner at higher concentrations, which is why total intravenous anesthesia is sometimes preferred when oxygenation is marginal. It is a local vascular response, not a bronchial shunting mechanism, and it acts on the nondependent lung's perfusion, not on ventilation-perfusion matching in the dependent lung directly.
  47. The correct answer is A (Perform fiberoptic bronchoscopy to confirm correct double-lumen tube position.): tube malposition is a common and readily reversible cause of hypoxemia during one-lung ventilation, so bronchoscopic confirmation should occur before escalating to other therapies. Abandoning one-lung ventilation entirely is premature and may not be necessary once malposition is corrected. Nitric oxide and rate changes are reasonable later steps but should follow, not precede, confirmation that the tube itself is correctly positioned.
  48. The correct answer is C (An upper abdominal or thoracic surgical incision site.): surgical site is one of the strongest predictors of postoperative pulmonary complications, because upper abdominal and thoracic incisions impair diaphragmatic function and reduce functional residual capacity more than lower abdominal or peripheral procedures. Age 40 is not itself a major risk factor compared with advanced age. Regional anesthesia, when appropriate, is generally associated with lower pulmonary risk than general anesthesia. Shorter procedures are associated with lower, not higher, risk.
  49. The correct answer is B (Tidal volume of 6 mL/kg predicted body weight with plateau pressure kept below 30 cmH2O.): the ARDSNet protocol uses low tidal volumes based on predicted body weight, not actual body weight, together with a plateau pressure limit to minimize ventilator-induced lung injury. Higher tidal volumes such as 10 to 15 mL/kg worsen alveolar overdistension and mortality in ARDS. Permissive hypercapnia is accepted in this strategy rather than normalizing PaCO2 at the expense of lung-protective settings.
  50. The correct answer is D (Increase PEEP to promote alveolar recruitment and reduce shunt.): higher PEEP in moderate to severe ARDS recruits collapsed alveoli, improving ventilation-perfusion matching and reducing intrapulmonary shunt without increasing tidal volume. Decreasing PEEP would worsen atelectasis and shunt. Increasing tidal volume conflicts with lung-protective ventilation and raises the risk of volutrauma. Increasing respiratory rate alone does not address the underlying recruitment deficit.
  51. The correct answer is A (Deepen anesthesia and administer an inhaled beta-2 agonist such as albuterol through the breathing circuit.): intraoperative bronchospasm is treated by deepening anesthesia to reduce airway reactivity and administering a beta-2 agonist to relax bronchial smooth muscle. Beta blockers worsen bronchospasm and are contraindicated in this setting. Extubation is not indicated for bronchospasm alone with a secured airway and does not address the underlying cause. Neuromuscular blockade alone does not treat bronchoconstriction.
  52. The correct answer is B (Disconnect the patient from the ventilator to allow full exhalation, then reduce respiratory rate or increase expiratory time.): a brief ventilator disconnection allows trapped gas to escape, and resolution of hypotension confirms auto-PEEP as the cause; subsequently reducing respiratory rate or prolonging expiratory time prevents recurrence. Increasing respiratory rate or tidal volume worsens dynamic hyperinflation by shortening the time available for exhalation. Fluid alone does not address the underlying mechanical cause of obstructive shock from air trapping.
  53. The correct answer is D (CPAP should be continued postoperatively, as it reduces the risk of upper airway obstruction and hypoxemia.): continuing a patient's usual CPAP after surgery maintains upper airway patency and reduces postoperative hypoxemic events, particularly when sedatives and opioids are on board. CPAP is not routinely contraindicated for most abdominal procedures, and blanket discontinuation is not standard practice absent a specific surgical concern discussed with the surgical team. Supplemental oxygen alone does not address upper airway obstruction the way positive pressure does.
  54. The correct answer is C (Lower tidal volumes with higher respiratory rates may be needed given reduced lung compliance and volumes.): restrictive disease reduces lung compliance and total lung capacity, so smaller tidal volumes delivered at a higher rate help maintain minute ventilation while limiting excessive airway pressures. Expiratory flow limitation and auto-PEEP are hallmarks of obstructive, not restrictive, disease. Bronchodilators target airway obstruction and have limited benefit in a purely restrictive process driven by parenchymal fibrosis.
  55. The correct answer is A (Apply continuous positive airway pressure to the nonventilated, nondependent lung.): low-level CPAP to the collapsed lung provides some oxygen transfer and reduces shunt without meaningfully interfering with surgical exposure. High-frequency jet ventilation is typically reserved for the nondependent lung in specific circumstances, not the already-ventilated dependent lung. Discontinuing one-lung ventilation is a last resort, not an early maneuver for mild hypoxemia. Decreasing PEEP on the ventilated lung would worsen, not improve, shunt and atelectasis.
  56. The correct answer is B (High-risk surgery, history of ischemic heart disease, congestive heart failure, cerebrovascular disease, insulin-dependent diabetes, and renal insufficiency.): the six original Revised Cardiac Risk Index criteria are exactly these factors, each contributing one point toward the composite risk score. Age, smoking, and obesity are not RCRI components, though they may influence risk through other pathways. Anemia, albumin, and frailty are used in other risk models but are not part of the RCRI. ASA class and emergency status are separate risk considerations outside the RCRI itself.
  57. The correct answer is C (4 METs.): the ability to climb two flights of stairs or perform equivalent activity corresponds to approximately 4 metabolic equivalents, which is the threshold used in the ACC/AHA algorithm to identify patients with adequate functional capacity who can often proceed to surgery without further cardiac stress testing. One to two METs represents very limited activity such as self-care, well below this threshold. Ten METs represents vigorous activity such as strenuous sports, a much higher level than required at this decision point.
  58. The correct answer is A (The test results would change perioperative management, such as delaying surgery for revascularization.): the guiding principle of preoperative stress testing is that it should only be pursued if the results will actually alter management, since testing purely for information without a management consequence exposes the patient to delay and risk without benefit. Emergent surgery precludes preoperative testing altogether. Excellent functional capacity or low-risk surgery generally obviates the need for further testing rather than indicating it.
  59. The correct answer is D (Delay elective surgery until at least 30 days after bare-metal stent placement, continuing dual antiplatelet therapy if possible.): current guidelines recommend delaying elective noncardiac surgery for at least 30 days after bare-metal stent placement to allow adequate endothelialization and to minimize stent thrombosis risk while dual antiplatelet therapy continues if feasible. Stopping antiplatelet therapy at only four weeks disregards ongoing thrombosis risk. Bare-metal stents do not endothelialize instantly, and the stent type described here is bare-metal, not drug-eluting.
  60. The correct answer is B (Ideally delay elective surgery until at least six months after drug-eluting stent placement, continuing aspirin perioperatively if possible.): current recommendations favor delaying elective surgery for at least six months after drug-eluting stent placement, with aspirin continued through the perioperative period whenever bleeding risk allows, to minimize the risk of stent thrombosis. Drug-eluting stents actually require a longer minimum delay than bare-metal stents because they delay endothelialization. Discontinuing all antiplatelet therapy with no waiting period disregards well-established thrombosis risk.
  61. The correct answer is C (Continue aspirin perioperatively, since the cardiac risk of stopping generally outweighs the bleeding risk in low-bleeding-risk procedures.): for patients on aspirin for established coronary disease undergoing procedures with low bleeding risk, continuing aspirin is generally favored because the thrombotic risk of discontinuation typically exceeds the modest increase in surgical bleeding. Routine discontinuation seven to ten days beforehand is not appropriate in this setting. Substituting an NSAID does not provide equivalent antiplatelet protection and introduces its own risks.
  62. The correct answer is D (Elective surgery is generally delayed at least 60 days after myocardial infarction when possible, allowing myocardial healing and risk stratification.): perioperative risk of reinfarction and cardiac complications is highest in the weeks immediately following a myocardial infarction, so elective surgery is generally postponed at least 60 days to allow the myocardium to heal and to complete appropriate risk stratification. Six weeks falls short of this window. A rigid one-year delay is not required for all patients, and ejection fraction alone does not determine appropriate timing independent of time since the event.
  63. The correct answer is A (Maintain adequate preload and afterload while avoiding myocardial depressant doses of volatile anesthetics or excessive negative inotropy.): patients with reduced ejection fraction have limited contractile reserve, so anesthetic management focuses on preserving preload and afterload while minimizing additional myocardial depression from volatile agents or other negative inotropes. Aggressive diuresis immediately before induction risks hypovolemia and hemodynamic instability. High-dose ketamine can have direct myocardial depressant effects when sympathetic reserve is exhausted, and invasive monitoring is often valuable, not contraindicated, in significant heart failure.
  64. The correct answer is B (Maintain normal sinus rhythm and adequate diastolic filling time, avoiding tachycardia and excessive fluid administration.): in HFpEF, the stiff, noncompliant ventricle depends on adequate diastolic filling time and atrial contribution to filling, so tachycardia shortens diastole and worsens filling while excessive fluid raises filling pressures and risks pulmonary edema. Aggressive fluid loading is poorly tolerated rather than beneficial. Deliberately inducing tachycardia worsens, rather than compensates for, the physiology. Vasopressors are not universally contraindicated and may be needed to maintain adequate perfusion pressure.
  65. The correct answer is D (Maintain normal sinus rhythm, adequate preload, avoid tachycardia, and avoid significant decreases in systemic vascular resistance.): severe aortic stenosis produces a fixed obstruction to left ventricular outflow, so cardiac output depends heavily on adequate preload and diastolic filling time, while both tachycardia and vasodilation reduce coronary perfusion pressure and can precipitate ischemia or hemodynamic collapse. Tachycardia and marked vasodilation are both poorly tolerated, not beneficial. Cardiac output is not fixed and unresponsive to hemodynamic management; it is precisely the fragility of this balance that makes anesthetic management critical.
  66. The correct answer is C (Phenylephrine, a pure alpha-agonist that raises systemic vascular resistance with minimal chronotropic effect.): phenylephrine restores systemic vascular resistance and coronary perfusion pressure without significantly increasing heart rate, which is important in aortic stenosis where tachycardia is poorly tolerated. Isoproterenol markedly increases heart rate and would worsen the physiology. Nitroglycerin reduces preload, which is dangerous in a preload-dependent fixed-obstruction lesion. Ephedrine has more chronotropic effect than phenylephrine and is a less ideal first choice when avoiding tachycardia is a priority.
  67. The correct answer is A (Maintain a relatively fast heart rate and reduced afterload to favor forward flow over regurgitant flow.): in mitral regurgitation, reducing afterload decreases the pressure gradient favoring regurgitant flow into the left atrium and promotes forward stroke volume, while a modestly faster heart rate shortens the time available for regurgitation each cycle. Bradycardia and increased afterload both worsen regurgitant fraction. Avoiding all vasodilators is counterproductive since afterload reduction is specifically beneficial in this lesion.
  68. The correct answer is B (Maintain a slower heart rate and adequate preload to allow sufficient diastolic filling across the stenotic valve.): mitral stenosis restricts flow across the valve during diastole, so a slower heart rate allows more time for left ventricular filling and reduces left atrial pressure, while adequate preload supports flow across the fixed obstruction. Tachycardia sharply reduces diastolic filling time and can precipitate pulmonary edema. Aggressively reducing preload without regard for cardiac output risks inadequate ventricular filling. Heart rate strongly affects transmitral flow given the fixed valve area.
  69. The correct answer is C (Maintain adequate preload and afterload while avoiding tachycardia and increased contractility.): in hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy, a larger ventricular chamber size and reduced contractility both reduce dynamic outflow tract obstruction, so maintaining preload and afterload while avoiding tachycardia and inotropic stimulation is protective. Reducing preload and afterload while increasing contractility, as in options A and D, all worsen outflow obstruction. Tachycardia and hypovolemia together dramatically worsen the obstruction by reducing chamber size and filling time.
  70. The correct answer is D (Intravenous rate control with a beta blocker or calcium channel blocker, since the patient is hemodynamically stable.): the irregularly irregular rhythm with absent P waves describes atrial fibrillation with rapid ventricular response, and in a hemodynamically stable patient the initial approach is rate control rather than immediate cardioversion. Cardioversion is reserved for hemodynamic instability or specific rhythm-control indications. Epinephrine would worsen the tachyarrhythmia. This rhythm is not sinus arrhythmia, which produces a regular P wave for every QRS with only mild variation.
  71. The correct answer is A (Begin advanced cardiac life support with immediate defibrillation, since this is pulseless ventricular tachycardia.): a wide-complex tachycardia with no palpable pulse is a cardiac arrest rhythm and requires immediate defibrillation and ACLS protocols, not medication trials or delay. Oral amiodarone has no role in an emergent pulseless arrhythmia. Vagal maneuvers and adenosine are appropriate for stable, narrow-complex supraventricular tachycardia, not for a pulseless wide-complex rhythm.
  72. The correct answer is B (This is Mobitz type I (Wenckebach) second-degree AV block, which is usually benign and often does not require immediate pacing if asymptomatic.): progressive PR prolongation culminating in a dropped beat is the classic description of Mobitz type I block, which typically arises from a reversible conduction delay at the AV node and rarely progresses to complete heart block, so asymptomatic patients can often be observed. Complete heart block shows no relationship between P waves and QRS complexes, unlike the pattern described here. This is a bradyarrhythmia, not ventricular tachycardia, and atropine is reserved for symptomatic bradycardia, not applied automatically.
  73. The correct answer is C (Placing a magnet over most pacemakers converts them to a fixed-rate asynchronous pacing mode, reducing the risk of inappropriate inhibition.): magnet application on a pacemaker typically triggers asynchronous pacing, which protects against inappropriate output inhibition from electrocautery-induced electromagnetic interference. Magnet effects on ICDs are different and generally only suspend antitachyarrhythmia therapy without changing pacing mode, unlike option A implies. Bipolar electrocautery actually poses less interference risk than monopolar because current does not need to travel through the body to a distant grounding pad. Precautions are still warranted based on surgical site relative to the device.
  74. The correct answer is D (Deactivate the ICD's antitachyarrhythmia detection and therapy before surgery, with external defibrillation immediately available, then reactivate postoperatively.): electromagnetic interference from electrocautery near the device could be misinterpreted as a tachyarrhythmia and trigger inappropriate shocks, so antitachyarrhythmia therapy is disabled preoperatively with external defibrillation equipment ready, then reactivated after surgery. Leaving therapies fully active risks an inappropriate shock during cautery use. Device removal is not warranted for routine surgery. A magnet on an ICD generally suspends shock therapy but does not reliably convert pacing mode the way it does for a pacemaker, so device-specific interrogation and programming are preferred.
  75. The correct answer is B (Use short bursts of electrocautery and place the grounding pad so the current path avoids the pacemaker and leads.): using short cautery bursts limits the duration of any single episode of interference, and positioning the grounding pad so that current does not pass near the pulse generator or leads reduces the amount of electromagnetic interference reaching the device. Placing the grounding pad near or over the device, as in options A and D, increases the current path through the pacing system. Continuous prolonged activation increases, rather than decreases, cumulative interference exposure.
  76. The correct answer is A (Proceed with surgery, since isolated stage 2 hypertension without acute end-organ dysfunction rarely warrants cancellation of elective surgery.): isolated elevated blood pressure without evidence of acute target-organ injury is not, by itself, a reliable predictor of perioperative cardiac events, so most elective surgery can proceed with appropriate intraoperative monitoring and management. Mandating a strict threshold such as 140/90 before proceeding is not supported by current evidence for elective, non-urgent cases. Indefinite cancellation is an overreaction absent acute symptoms, and invasive monitoring is not automatically required for a low-risk procedure based on blood pressure alone.
  77. The correct answer is C (Many clinicians hold ACE inhibitors or ARBs on the morning of surgery to reduce the risk of intraoperative hypotension, particularly with general anesthesia.): ACE inhibitors and ARBs blunt the renin-angiotensin system's compensatory response to anesthetic-induced vasodilation, and holding the morning dose is a common practice to reduce refractory intraoperative hypotension. Absolute rules to always continue or always stop for two weeks are not consistently supported and can be harmful in patients whose hypertension is primarily managed with these agents. These medications clearly do affect intraoperative hemodynamics.
  78. The correct answer is D (Continue the chronic beta blocker perioperatively, since abrupt withdrawal can precipitate rebound tachycardia and myocardial ischemia.): patients on chronic beta blocker therapy should continue it through the perioperative period, because sudden discontinuation can cause rebound sympathetic activation, tachycardia, hypertension, and myocardial ischemia. Discontinuing the medication before surgery is therefore inappropriate. Initiating beta blockade for the first time on the day of surgery is associated with increased risk of stroke and hypotension in trial data and is not recommended. Beta blockers are not universally avoided; the concern is specifically about de novo initiation, not continuation of chronic therapy.
  79. The correct answer is C (Equalization of diastolic pressures across all four cardiac chambers with pulsus paradoxus.): cardiac tamponade compresses all cardiac chambers roughly equally during diastole, producing equalization of diastolic pressures, and interdependence between the ventricles produces an exaggerated inspiratory drop in systolic blood pressure known as pulsus paradoxus. Pulse pressure is typically narrowed, not widened, and tachycardia rather than bradycardia is the usual compensatory response. Right and left heart pressures rise together rather than being isolated to one side, and cardiac output falls rather than increases as tamponade progresses.
  80. The correct answer is A (Maintain spontaneous ventilation and adequate preload, heart rate, and contractility, avoiding agents that cause vasodilation or myocardial depression.): patients with tamponade depend on preserved preload, heart rate, and contractility to maintain cardiac output against the compressive effect of pericardial fluid, so spontaneous ventilation is favored and agents causing vasodilation or myocardial depression, such as high-dose propofol, should be avoided or minimized. Positive pressure ventilation reduces venous return and can precipitate hemodynamic collapse in tamponade. Beta blockade would remove a critical compensatory tachycardia and is contraindicated in this setting.
  81. The correct answer is C (Stroke volume is relatively fixed due to limited diastolic filling, so cardiac output depends heavily on maintaining adequate heart rate.): a rigid, noncompliant pericardium in constrictive pericarditis limits diastolic filling and fixes stroke volume, so cardiac output becomes heavily dependent on heart rate, and bradycardia is poorly tolerated rather than well tolerated. Positive pressure ventilation further reduces venous return in a filling-limited physiology and is generally less favorable than spontaneous ventilation. Afterload is not the primary determinant of cardiac output in this restrictive, filling-limited condition.
  82. The correct answer is D (Preoperative serum creatinine greater than 2.0 mg/dL.): the original Revised Cardiac Risk Index defines the renal insufficiency criterion specifically as a preoperative serum creatinine above 2.0 mg/dL, contributing one point toward the total score. Estimated GFR below 60 is a general marker of chronic kidney disease but is not the specific RCRI threshold. A vague history of chronic kidney disease without a defined creatinine cutoff does not match the RCRI's explicit criterion, and blood urea nitrogen is not the laboratory value used in this index.
  83. The correct answer is B (MINS can occur without ischemic symptoms and is independently associated with increased 30-day mortality, warranting further evaluation.): myocardial injury after noncardiac surgery is frequently silent, without classic ischemic symptoms, yet it independently predicts increased short-term mortality, which is why postoperative troponin surveillance in at-risk patients is used to identify it and prompt further cardiac evaluation and secondary prevention. Asymptomatic elevation is not prognostically benign. While renal clearance can affect troponin kinetics, it does not account for genuine myocardial injury patterns seen in MINS, and surveillance troponin testing is recommended in select high-risk postoperative populations.
  84. The correct answer is B (An elevated BNP supports a diagnosis of heart failure and can help risk-stratify patients before noncardiac surgery.): BNP is released in response to ventricular wall stress and is elevated in heart failure, supporting the diagnosis and adding prognostic information for perioperative risk assessment when combined with clinical findings. BNP is not specific to acute coronary syndrome and is instead more classically associated with volume overload states like heart failure. Renal function and volume status do influence BNP levels, and while a low BNP makes heart failure less likely, it does not provide absolute certainty in excluding all cardiac causes of dyspnea.
  85. The correct answer is C (Increased heart rate reducing diastolic filling time while hypotension reduces coronary perfusion pressure.): coronary perfusion occurs predominantly during diastole, so tachycardia shortens the time available for coronary flow while concurrent hypotension lowers the driving pressure gradient across stenotic coronary vessels, together sharply increasing the risk of subendocardial ischemia. Decreased heart rate and increased diastolic pressure, described in the other options, would each improve rather than worsen coronary perfusion. Hemodilution reduces, rather than increases, oxygen-carrying capacity.
  86. The correct answer is D (A relatively faster heart rate with reduced afterload to shorten diastole and favor forward flow over regurgitant flow.): in aortic regurgitation, a faster heart rate shortens the diastolic period during which blood regurgitates backward across the incompetent valve, while afterload reduction lowers the pressure gradient driving regurgitant flow and favors forward stroke volume. Bradycardia lengthens diastole and worsens regurgitant volume, and increasing afterload similarly worsens regurgitation rather than improving valve function. Categorically avoiding vasodilators disregards the specific benefit of afterload reduction in this lesion.
  87. The correct answer is A (Torsades de pointes, with risk increased by hypokalemia, hypomagnesemia, and certain QT-prolonging medications.): a prolonged QT interval predisposes to torsades de pointes, a polymorphic ventricular tachycardia, and this risk is amplified by electrolyte disturbances such as hypokalemia and hypomagnesemia as well as by additional QT-prolonging drugs commonly used perioperatively. Atrial fibrillation and heart block are not the characteristic arrhythmias linked to QT prolongation. Hypercalcemia typically shortens rather than prolongs the QT interval, and hyperthermia is not the primary electrolyte-related risk factor for torsades.
  88. The correct answer is B (It paces and senses both the atrium and ventricle, and responds to sensed activity with both triggering and inhibition.): in the standard pacemaker code, DDD indicates pacing of both chambers, sensing in both chambers, and a dual response mode in which sensed activity can either trigger or inhibit pacing output, allowing the device to track intrinsic atrial activity and maintain AV synchrony. Atrium-only or ventricle-only pacing would be described by different single-chamber codes. DDD is the physiologic dual-chamber mode used in normal operation, not the fixed-rate asynchronous mode seen with magnet application, which is typically denoted differently.
  89. The correct answer is C (Continue the statin perioperatively, since abrupt discontinuation may be associated with increased cardiovascular events.): statins have pleiotropic vascular-stabilizing effects beyond lipid lowering, and observational data associate perioperative statin discontinuation with increased cardiovascular events, so chronic statin therapy is generally continued through the perioperative period. Discontinuing for myopathy concerns is not standard practice absent a specific contraindication. Statins do have documented perioperative cardiovascular benefit, and fibrates are not an equivalent substitute for perioperative cardiovascular protection.
  90. The correct answer is D (3 points, since high-risk surgery, insulin-treated diabetes, and cerebrovascular disease each count as one point.): this patient has three of the six RCRI criteria present, high-risk (intraperitoneal, intrathoracic, or suprainguinal vascular) surgery, insulin-treated diabetes mellitus, and a history of cerebrovascular disease, each worth one point, for a total of three, without heart failure, ischemic heart disease, or renal insufficiency contributing additional points. The other point totals do not correctly reflect the three criteria actually met in this vignette.
  91. The correct answer is A (A documented history of ischemic heart disease such as prior myocardial infarction.): the Revised Cardiac Risk Index includes six factors: high-risk surgery, ischemic heart disease, congestive heart failure, cerebrovascular disease, insulin-dependent diabetes mellitus, and renal insufficiency with creatinine above 2.0 mg/dL. This patient's insulin-dependent diabetes and renal insufficiency already contribute two RCRI points, so a documented history of ischemic heart disease would add a third qualifying factor and further raise his risk category. Age over 65, obstructive sleep apnea, and chronic obstructive pulmonary disease all carry independent perioperative risk but are not among the six validated RCRI criteria.
  92. The correct answer is B (Greater than 4 METs, generally proceed without additional stress testing.): climbing a flight of stairs or walking up a hill corresponds to roughly 4 to 10 METs, and patients who achieve this without symptoms generally have adequate functional reserve to proceed to surgery without further noninvasive cardiac testing. Angiography is not mandated by functional capacity alone. Absolute statements about never needing clearance or indefinite deferral ignore that functional capacity is only one input into a broader perioperative risk assessment algorithm.
  93. The correct answer is B (Postpone elective surgery until at least six months of dual antiplatelet therapy is completed.): current guidelines recommend delaying elective noncardiac surgery for a minimum of six months after drug eluting stent placement, and ideally continuing dual antiplatelet therapy uninterrupted, because premature discontinuation dramatically increases stent thrombosis risk. Stopping both agents at eight weeks would leave the stent essentially unprotected during its highest risk endothelialization period. Stent age of eight weeks does not eliminate thrombosis risk, so the elective procedure should wait.
  94. The correct answer is A (Continue aspirin, stop the P2Y12 inhibitor perioperatively, and proceed with the necessary surgery.): when urgent surgery cannot be delayed, the accepted compromise is to continue aspirin throughout the perioperative period for its cardioprotective effect while temporarily withholding the P2Y12 inhibitor, then resuming it as soon as hemostasis allows. Stopping both agents maximizes thrombosis risk, and an incarcerated hernia cannot simply be deferred. Continuing the P2Y12 inhibitor alone while stopping aspirin is not the standard compromise strategy.
  95. The correct answer is A (Postpone elective surgery until his volume status and heart failure regimen are optimized.): a recent hospitalization for decompensated heart failure signals an unstable clinical state, and elective surgery should be delayed until euvolemia and guideline directed medical therapy are re-established, since operating during decompensation markedly raises perioperative morbidity and mortality. Ejection fraction alone does not capture acute clinical stability. Avoiding volatiles or relying only on intraoperative echocardiography does not address the underlying decompensated state that needs treatment before elective surgery.
  96. The correct answer is D (Administer a bolus of phenylephrine to raise diastolic blood pressure.): in severe aortic stenosis, maintaining diastolic pressure and coronary perfusion is critical because the hypertrophied left ventricle is highly dependent on adequate diastolic coronary filling, and a pure alpha agonist like phenylephrine restores pressure without significantly increasing heart rate or myocardial oxygen demand. Nitroglycerin drops preload and afterload, worsening hypotension against a fixed obstruction. Dobutamine and epinephrine increase heart rate and contractility, which can precipitate dynamic obstruction and arrhythmia in this population.
  97. The correct answer is C (Maintain a modestly elevated heart rate with reduced afterload to favor forward flow.): in chronic mitral regurgitation, a faster heart rate shortens the time available for regurgitant flow and reduced systemic vascular resistance decreases the pressure gradient driving blood backward into the left atrium, both favoring effective forward stroke volume. Bradycardia allows more time for regurgitation and increases ventricular volume. High systemic vascular resistance, appropriate for aortic stenosis, worsens the regurgitant fraction in mitral regurgitation rather than helping it.
  98. The correct answer is A (Perform immediate synchronized electrical cardioversion of the rhythm.): atrial fibrillation with rapid ventricular response causing hemodynamic instability, evidenced by hypotension and altered mental status, is an indication for immediate synchronized cardioversion rather than pharmacologic rate control, which acts too slowly and can worsen hypotension with agents like diltiazem. Amiodarone loading and digoxin also have delayed onset unsuitable for an unstable patient. Electrical cardioversion restores an organized rhythm and adequate cardiac output most rapidly in this emergent setting.
  99. The correct answer is C (Adenosine given as an AV nodal blocking agent.): AV nodal blocking drugs such as adenosine, verapamil, diltiazem, and digoxin should be avoided in pre-excited atrial fibrillation because blocking the AV node preferentially can promote conduction entirely down the accessory pathway, accelerating ventricular rate and risking degeneration into ventricular fibrillation. Procainamide and amiodarone are acceptable pharmacologic options that slow conduction through the accessory pathway. Synchronized cardioversion remains appropriate and safe if the patient becomes hemodynamically unstable.
  100. The correct answer is B (Ondansetron given intravenously for standard postoperative nausea prophylaxis today.): serotonin antagonists such as ondansetron are well recognized to prolong the QT interval and should be avoided or used cautiously in patients with congenital long QT syndrome, since further QT prolongation increases the risk of torsades de pointes. Rocuronium, fentanyl, and propofol do not carry the same significant QT prolonging risk profile at standard clinical doses. Magnesium supplementation and avoidance of hypokalemia are also important adjuncts in managing these patients.
  101. The correct answer is A (The hypertrophied, noncompliant left ventricle relies on adequate diastolic filling time, which tachycardia shortens, reducing stroke volume across the fixed outflow obstruction.): in severe aortic stenosis the left ventricle is hypertrophied and stiff, so adequate ventricular filling depends heavily on a sufficient diastolic filling time; tachyarrhythmias shorten diastole, reduce preload and stroke volume, and can precipitate hypotension because the fixed outflow obstruction prevents compensatory augmentation of stroke volume. Option B is wrong because the stenotic valve is precisely why stroke volume cannot increase to compensate. Option C overstates the effect: the atrial contribution to filling is important in a stiff ventricle, but aortic stenosis does not abolish it entirely in normal sinus rhythm, and losing it in an arrhythmia like atrial fibrillation worsens filling further rather than making no baseline difference. Option D is wrong because coronary perfusion occurs during diastole and is directly reduced when diastolic time shortens, compounding the risk of ischemia in a hypertrophied, oxygen-hungry ventricle.
  102. The correct answer is B (Deactivate antitachycardia and shock therapies immediately before surgery with external defibrillation available.): electrocautery interference can be misread by an ICD as ventricular tachycardia or fibrillation, triggering inappropriate shocks, so tachyarrhythmia detection and therapy should be temporarily deactivated with external defibrillation pads placed and immediately available throughout the case. Leaving therapies active during monopolar cautery risks an inappropriate shock. Permanent explantation is unnecessary and excessive, and reprogramming pacing mode alone does not address the shock therapy interference risk.
  103. The correct answer is A (Maintain a relatively faster heart rate and reduced systemic vascular resistance.): in aortic regurgitation, a faster heart rate shortens diastole and thereby limits the time available for regurgitant backflow across the incompetent valve, while afterload reduction favors forward flow into the systemic circulation. Bradycardia lengthens diastole and worsens the regurgitant volume. Increasing systemic vascular resistance increases the pressure gradient driving regurgitant flow backward, which is detrimental rather than helpful in this lesion.
  104. The correct answer is A (Loss of atrial kick combined with a shortened diastolic filling time across the stenotic valve.): in mitral stenosis, the fixed valve orifice already limits flow during diastole, and new atrial fibrillation both eliminates the atrial contraction that normally contributes a significant portion of left ventricular filling and shortens the diastolic interval available for flow across the stenotic valve as heart rate rises, precipitating a rise in left atrial pressure and pulmonary edema. Tachycardia worsens rather than improves the transmitral gradient in this setting. Right ventricular function is a secondary, not primary, mechanism of this acute event.
  105. The correct answer is D (Proceed with elective surgery while continuing dual antiplatelet therapy if the procedure allows.): although six months is the generally preferred minimum interval after drug eluting stent placement, guidelines allow individualized decisions after shared discussion with cardiology when the patient is stable, asymptomatic, and on optimal medical therapy, particularly if antiplatelet therapy can be continued through the procedure. Automatically canceling all elective surgery for a fixed period ignores this individualized approach. Repeat angiography is not routinely required simply based on time elapsed, and abrupt discontinuation of antiplatelet therapy is not advised.
  106. The correct answer is D (Maintain adequate preload, avoid tachycardia, and maintain or increase afterload.): in hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy, adequate ventricular filling, slower heart rates that allow more filling time, and higher afterload all increase left ventricular cavity size and reduce the dynamic outflow tract obstruction caused by systolic anterior motion of the mitral valve. Tachycardia and afterload reduction both shrink ventricular volume and worsen obstruction. Inotropic agents increase contractility and can markedly worsen the outflow gradient, and aggressive diuresis reduces preload, which also worsens obstruction.
  107. The correct answer is A (Maintain spontaneous ventilation and avoid agents that decrease preload or heart rate.): in tamponade, cardiac output is critically dependent on preserved preload and compensatory tachycardia, so spontaneous ventilation should be maintained as long as possible and drugs that reduce venous return or heart rate should be avoided until the pericardium is decompressed. A large propofol bolus causes venodilation and myocardial depression that can precipitate cardiovascular collapse. Positive pressure ventilation increases intrathoracic pressure and further reduces venous return, and beta blockade removes the compensatory tachycardia these patients depend upon.
  108. The correct answer is B (Preload dependence is preserved and volume depletion should be avoided.): in constrictive pericarditis, the rigid pericardium limits diastolic filling similarly to tamponade, so adequate intravascular volume must be maintained to sustain stroke volume, and hypovolemia or excessive positive pressure ventilation can precipitate a sharp fall in cardiac output. Afterload reduction does not address the mechanical restriction to filling. Beta blockade removing compensatory tachycardia can worsen cardiac output in a condition where stroke volume is fixed and rate dependent for output.
  109. The correct answer is C (The subcutaneous device still requires deactivation of shock therapy since it senses surface electromagnetic interference similarly.): although a subcutaneous ICD lacks transvenous leads and does not provide pacing, it still senses cardiac signals and can be susceptible to electromagnetic interference from monopolar electrocautery, potentially triggering inappropriate shocks, so tachyarrhythmia therapies should still be deactivated perioperatively with external defibrillation available. Claiming immunity to interference or that no planning is needed is incorrect and could result in an inappropriate shock during surgery. The device does deliver shocks, which is its entire therapeutic purpose.
  110. The correct answer is A (Reverse anticoagulation with prothrombin complex concentrate or plasma and bridge with heparin.): mechanical mitral valves carry high thromboembolic risk, so urgent reversal of excessive anticoagulation using prothrombin complex concentrate or fresh frozen plasma allows safe surgery while heparin bridging is used perioperatively to minimize the time without anticoagulation. Proceeding with an INR of 3.2 unaddressed risks catastrophic surgical bleeding. Vitamin K alone takes too long for urgent surgery, and providing no bridging at all unnecessarily raises the risk of valve thrombosis or embolic stroke.
  111. The correct answer is D (Apply a magnet over the pacemaker generator to restore asynchronous pacing.): placing a magnet over most permanent pacemakers converts the device to a fixed asynchronous pacing mode that is unaffected by electromagnetic interference, immediately restoring reliable pacing in a bradycardic pacemaker dependent patient. Lidocaine has no role in treating pacing failure and cardioversion is not indicated for bradycardia. Simply increasing anesthetic depth and observing does not address the hemodynamically significant bradycardia caused by device malfunction.
  112. The correct answer is B (The hypertrophied, noncompliant ventricle depends heavily on adequate diastolic filling time that tachycardia shortens.): concentric left ventricular hypertrophy in aortic stenosis reduces chamber compliance, making stroke volume highly dependent on adequate diastolic filling time, so tachycardia shortens this filling period and can precipitously reduce cardiac output while simultaneously increasing myocardial oxygen demand against already elevated wall stress. Tachycardia does not improve coronary perfusion pressure, since coronary flow occurs primarily in diastole, which is shortened. The stenotic orifice area is fixed by valve pathology and does not change with heart rate.
  113. The correct answer is C (A slow, titrated induction using etomidate or reduced dose propofol with opioid co-induction.): patients with severely reduced ejection fraction tolerate myocardial depression poorly, so a slow titrated induction using hemodynamically favorable agents such as etomidate, or reduced doses of propofol combined with opioids to blunt the sympathetic response without large bolus dosing, minimizes abrupt hypotension. A large rapid propofol bolus can cause profound hypotension in this population. Standard healthy-patient dosing does not account for reduced cardiac reserve and can precipitate cardiovascular collapse.
  114. The correct answer is D (Hold rivaroxaban for the guideline recommended interval based on renal function and bleeding risk before surgery.): direct oral anticoagulants like rivaroxaban are typically held for a defined period, often one to two days depending on renal function and procedural bleeding risk, without routine heparin bridging, since their rapid offset and onset make bridging generally unnecessary and potentially harmful. Continuing through a moderate to high bleeding risk procedure is unsafe. Idarucizumab is a reversal agent specific to dabigatran, not rivaroxaban, and is not given prophylactically.
  115. The correct answer is D (Proceed with surgery since it is low risk and the pressure is near his chronic baseline.): for low risk procedures such as cataract surgery, chronic hypertension without evidence of acute end organ dysfunction and a pressure near the patient's usual baseline generally does not warrant cancellation, since the perioperative risk associated with the surgery itself is minimal. Admission for intravenous titration is excessive for a low risk case. Attempting to acutely normalize a chronically elevated pressure can actually impair cerebral and coronary autoregulation and precipitate hypoperfusion.
  116. The correct answer is C (Slow the ventricular rate to allow more diastolic filling time across the stenotic valve.): because flow across a stenotic mitral valve occurs during diastole, slowing an excessively fast ventricular rate lengthens diastolic filling time and improves left ventricular filling and cardiac output, while also reducing left atrial pressure and pulmonary congestion risk. Increasing heart rate further shortens diastole and worsens the transvalvular gradient. Avoiding rate control entirely ignores that tachycardia is specifically detrimental in this lesion, unlike in some other valvular diseases.
  117. The correct answer is C (Mechanical vibration and patient movement may be misinterpreted as physical activity, causing inappropriate rate increases.): accelerometer based rate responsive sensors detect motion and vibration to increase pacing rate during exertion, so surgical manipulation, drilling, or patient repositioning can be falsely interpreted as increased activity, leading to an inappropriately elevated paced heart rate. This sensor type is clearly affected by mechanical vibration, not immune to it. Rate responsive functionality is often disabled preoperatively for this exact reason, and this sensor detects motion rather than minute ventilation, which is a different sensor modality entirely.
  118. The correct answer is B (Avoid marked hypertension and wide swings in blood pressure to reduce aortic wall stress.): a dilated ascending aorta associated with bicuspid aortic valve disease carries a risk of dissection or rupture that is influenced by aortic wall stress, so blood pressure should be carefully controlled to avoid marked hypertension or abrupt surges, similar to management principles used in known aortic aneurysm disease. Permitting unrestricted hypertension ignores this dissection risk. An arbitrary heart rate target below 50 is not the primary goal, and aortic diameter is clinically relevant to anesthetic management regardless of the valve lesion itself.
  119. The correct answer is B (Routine heparin bridging is generally not recommended when a direct oral anticoagulant is interrupted.): because direct oral anticoagulants have a rapid onset and offset compared with warfarin, randomized evidence has shown that routine bridging does not reduce thromboembolic events and instead increases bleeding risk, so bridging is generally reserved for select very high risk situations rather than applied routinely. Bridging is not required in all atrial fibrillation patients on a direct oral anticoagulant. Bridging has not been shown to definitively reduce stroke risk in this population and is not restricted to only low risk score patients.
  120. The correct answer is D (Baseline function should be reconfirmed, but management otherwise resembles a patient without cardiomyopathy.): Takotsubo cardiomyopathy, often triggered by severe emotional or physical stress and catecholamine surge, is typically reversible, and with documented normalization of ejection fraction, anesthetic management can generally proceed as for a patient without significant structural heart disease, though confirming current function is prudent. Describing it as permanent and irreversible is incorrect. It is strongly associated with catecholamine surge, and there is no absolute contraindication to anesthesia after full recovery is documented.
  121. The correct answer is D (Hypoxemia, hypercarbia, and acidosis, which increase pulmonary vascular resistance.): in a patient with pulmonary hypertension and a failing right ventricle, hypoxemia, hypercarbia, and acidosis all raise pulmonary vascular resistance, increasing right ventricular afterload and precipitating acute decompensation and reduced cardiac output. Hyperventilation with high inspired oxygen actually lowers pulmonary vascular resistance and is often used therapeutically. Mild hypervolemia with normal gas exchange does not acutely worsen right ventricular afterload, and inhaled nitric oxide is a selective pulmonary vasodilator used to treat, not worsen, this condition.
  122. The correct answer is C (Preoperative cardiology evaluation for consideration of permanent pacemaker placement before elective surgery.): Mobitz type II second degree block carries a significant risk of unpredictable progression to complete heart block, and pacemaker implantation is generally indicated regardless of symptoms, so elective surgery should ideally follow appropriate cardiology evaluation and pacing if indicated. Relying on atropine alone is unreliable, as infranodal block often responds poorly to atropine. Mobitz type II behaves quite differently from the generally more benign Mobitz type I, or Wenckebach, block, which rarely progresses to complete heart block.
  123. The correct answer is C (Administer intravenous fluid bolus and a pure alpha agonist such as phenylephrine.): treatment of acute dynamic outflow obstruction in hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy centers on increasing ventricular volume with fluid and restoring afterload with a pure vasoconstrictor like phenylephrine, both of which increase left ventricular cavity size and reduce systolic anterior motion of the mitral valve. Inotropic agents such as epinephrine, dobutamine, or isoproterenol increase contractility and can worsen the outflow obstruction and hypotension in this specific condition, unlike in most other causes of hypotension.
  124. The correct answer is D (Administer a rapid intravenous bolus of adenosine through a proximal vein.): for a hemodynamically stable narrow complex supraventricular tachycardia, rapid intravenous adenosine is the first line pharmacologic agent because it transiently blocks conduction through the AV node, terminating most reentrant tachycardias within seconds. Cardioversion is reserved for unstable patients or when pharmacologic therapy fails. Amiodarone and digoxin have slower onset and are not first line for acute termination of a stable reentrant supraventricular tachycardia in this setting.
  125. The correct answer is B (He is likely still within a defined perioperative antiplatelet therapy period.): patients undergoing transcatheter aortic valve replacement are typically maintained on a defined course of single or dual antiplatelet therapy following the procedure, and decisions about interrupting this therapy for elective surgery at only four weeks should be individualized in consultation with cardiology given ongoing valve thrombosis risk. Claiming no antiplatelet therapy is needed or that the valve is already fully endothelialized at four weeks is inaccurate. Transcatheter bioprosthetic valves do not require lifelong warfarin like a mechanical valve.
  126. The correct answer is D (Abrupt beta blocker withdrawal can precipitate rebound tachycardia, hypertension, and myocardial ischemia.): chronic beta blockade should generally be continued through the perioperative period because sudden discontinuation can cause a rebound sympathetic surge with tachycardia, hypertension, and increased risk of myocardial ischemia or arrhythmia. This is not merely a matter of convenience, since a real withdrawal phenomenon exists. Stopping beta blockers acutely does not improve hemodynamic stability and there is no universal rule requiring cessation 48 hours before every surgery.
  127. The correct answer is D (The noncompliant, stiff ventricle causes markedly elevated filling pressures with only modest increases in volume.): in heart failure with preserved ejection fraction, impaired ventricular relaxation and increased chamber stiffness mean that even modest volume administration produces a disproportionate rise in left ventricular filling pressure, which transmits back to the pulmonary circulation and can precipitate pulmonary edema. Systolic contractile function is preserved by definition in this condition, not impaired. These patients do not have inherently low blood volume, and diastolic dysfunction increases rather than decreases wedge pressure with volume loading.
  128. The correct answer is A (The device is a passive pressure sensor without pacing function and needs no deactivation.): the CardioMEMS sensor is a passive, wireless pulmonary artery pressure monitoring device with no pacing or shock delivery capability, so unlike a pacemaker or defibrillator it does not require special electrocautery deactivation protocols. It cannot deliver a shock, so no magnet application is needed for that purpose. It also has no pacing function, so asynchronous reprogramming precautions used for pacemakers are not applicable to this device.
  129. The correct answer is C (Proceed with careful hemostasis since the INR is only mildly elevated and reversal risks valve thrombosis.): an INR of 1.4 represents only mild anticoagulation, and full reversal in a patient with a mechanical valve carries meaningful thrombotic risk, so for a moderate bleeding risk urgent procedure it is often reasonable to proceed with careful surgical hemostasis rather than aggressively reversing anticoagulation. High dose vitamin K or prothrombin complex concentrate would be excessive for such a modest INR elevation and unnecessarily raise thrombosis risk. Delaying surgery indefinitely for an urgent indication until a specific INR target is inappropriate.
  130. The correct answer is C (Loss of biventricular pacing during the case can acutely worsen ventricular dyssynchrony and reduce cardiac output.): patients dependent on cardiac resynchronization therapy rely on synchronized biventricular pacing to optimize cardiac output in the setting of severe systolic dysfunction, so any interruption of pacing, whether from electromagnetic interference or inappropriate device management, can acutely worsen ventricular dyssynchrony and precipitate hemodynamic deterioration. This pacing modality does meaningfully affect cardiac output, contrary to the second option. Permanently disabling the left ventricular lead is not appropriate, and biventricular devices remain susceptible to the same electrocautery interference as other cardiac implantable electronic devices.
  131. The correct answer is A (Cardiology evaluation for consideration of valve intervention, with revascularization addressed as indicated, before elective noncardiac surgery.): symptomatic severe aortic stenosis with coexisting angina represents combined high perioperative risk, and current guidelines favor addressing the valvular lesion, often with transcatheter or surgical aortic valve replacement, and evaluating for revascularization before elective noncardiac surgery when feasible. These two conditions interact significantly, since the fixed obstruction and ischemia both limit the ability to increase cardiac output under stress. Ignoring the valve lesion or refusing any specialist referral both disregard well established management pathways that can meaningfully reduce perioperative risk.
  132. The correct answer is C (Flecainide is a sodium channel blocker whose proarrhythmic risk increases with acidosis or electrolyte abnormalities.): flecainide is a class IC antiarrhythmic that blocks fast sodium channels, and its proarrhythmic potential, including widened QRS complexes and ventricular arrhythmia, is exacerbated by acidosis, hyperkalemia, or other electrolyte disturbances that further impair cardiac conduction. It clearly does affect cardiac conduction, contrary to the second option, and it is not a potassium channel blocker nor protective against hyperkalemia. Its toxicity is directly related to acid base and electrolyte status, making careful correction of these disturbances important perioperatively.
  133. The correct answer is A (Holding the ACE inhibitor on the morning of surgery is often reasonable to reduce hypotension risk.): ACE inhibitors and angiotensin receptor blockers have been associated with an increased incidence of refractory intraoperative hypotension under general anesthesia, so many practitioners hold these agents on the morning of surgery while continuing beta blockade, which has a different withdrawal risk profile. Always giving the ACE inhibitor as scheduled ignores this recognized hypotension risk. Beta blockers are generally continued rather than held due to withdrawal concerns, making the fourth option inaccurate.
  134. The correct answer is A (Begin cardiopulmonary resuscitation and deliver asynchronous external defibrillation promptly.): with the internal device intentionally deactivated for the surgical case, pulseless ventricular tachycardia is a cardiac arrest requiring immediate cardiopulmonary resuscitation and prompt external defibrillation using the pads already placed as a precaution. The device will not reactivate automatically once turned off, so waiting for it wastes critical time. Amiodarone alone without defibrillation is inadequate for pulseless ventricular tachycardia, and reprogramming the device intraoperatively during active resuscitation is impractical and would delay lifesaving defibrillation.
  135. The correct answer is B (Minimize excessive positive end expiratory pressure and avoid factors that raise pulmonary vascular resistance.): in severe tricuspid regurgitation with right heart failure, excessive positive end expiratory pressure can reduce venous return and increase right ventricular afterload by raising pulmonary vascular resistance, both of which worsen an already compromised right ventricle, so ventilator settings should be chosen to avoid unnecessary elevation of intrathoracic pressure. High PEEP does not improve filling in this context and instead impairs it. Deliberately inducing hypercarbia raises pulmonary vascular resistance and worsens, rather than improves, right ventricular function.
  136. The correct answer is B (Obtain cardiology or electrophysiology evaluation before proceeding with elective surgery.): new nonsustained ventricular tachycardia in a patient with known arrhythmic disease and an ICD warrants further evaluation to assess for worsening substrate, medication issues, or device related concerns before proceeding with elective surgery, since this finding could signal increased perioperative arrhythmic risk.
