Dismissing nonsustained runs as never significant is inaccurate, since they can herald clinical deterioration. Explanting the device is an extreme and unnecessary step, and device interrogation remains an important, not dispensable, part of perioperative planning in this scenario.
  137. The correct answer is B (Loss of atrial kick and shortened filling time can still lower forward stroke volume some.): even in chronic mitral regurgitation, new atrial fibrillation causes loss of coordinated atrial contraction and can shorten diastolic filling, reducing effective forward stroke volume, although a compliant, already volume loaded left atrium in chronic regurgitation may tolerate this somewhat better than the fixed obstruction of mitral stenosis. Claiming no hemodynamic consequence at all is inaccurate. Atrial fibrillation does not improve forward flow, and the regurgitant fraction is not eliminated simply because the rhythm changes.
  138. The correct answer is A (Impaired cerebral and coronary autoregulation from chronic hypertension raises hypoperfusion risk at otherwise acceptable pressures.): chronic hypertension shifts the autoregulatory curve for cerebral and coronary blood flow to the right, meaning these patients require a relatively higher blood pressure to maintain adequate organ perfusion, so an abrupt drop to a pressure that would be well tolerated in a normotensive patient can cause cerebral or myocardial hypoperfusion. The shift is to higher, not lower, pressures, making the second option incorrect. Coronary perfusion pressure is very much affected by rapid pressure changes in this population, given the hypertrophied, less compliant myocardium.
  139. The correct answer is A (The same general electrocautery precautions apply, including asynchronous programming if the patient is pacing dependent.): although a leadless pacemaker lacks a transvenous lead and pocket, it remains susceptible to electromagnetic interference from electrocautery, so the same general principles, including preoperative device interrogation and consideration of asynchronous pacing for a pacing dependent patient, still apply. These devices are not immune to interference simply due to their intracardiac location. Some leadless devices do have a defined magnet response, and preoperative and postoperative interrogation remains an important part of perioperative planning regardless of device type.
  140. The correct answer is D (Low to intermediate risk noncardiac surgery can generally proceed with appropriate intraoperative hemodynamic monitoring and management.): truly asymptomatic severe aortic stenosis with preserved ejection fraction and a normal exercise tolerance test carries lower perioperative risk than symptomatic disease, and current guidelines support proceeding with low to intermediate risk noncardiac surgery using careful hemodynamic management rather than mandating valve replacement first. Requiring valve replacement in every case of severe stenosis regardless of symptoms is overly rigid and not guideline concordant. Given the known risks of this lesion, dispensing with special intraoperative monitoring would be inappropriate.
  141. The correct answer is A (Rapid sequence induction with preoxygenation, cricoid pressure, and immediate intubation without mask ventilation.): a full stomach after recent trauma and eating mandates RSI to minimize the time the airway is unprotected. Avoiding positive pressure mask ventilation before intubation limits gastric insufflation and reduces regurgitation risk. Slow induction, awake techniques without securing the airway rapidly, and inhalational induction with preserved spontaneous ventilation all prolong the period of aspiration vulnerability compared to a properly executed RSI.
  142. The correct answer is B (Child-Pugh class C, associated with very high perioperative surgical mortality.): scoring albumin below 2.8, bilirubin above 3, INR above 2, moderate ascites, and grade 2 encephalopathy each yields near-maximum points, placing this patient in class C with a score of roughly 12. Class C cirrhosis carries markedly elevated perioperative mortality, historically cited near 75 to 80 percent for major elective surgery. Class A and B classifications require substantially lower composite scores than this patient demonstrates.
  143. The correct answer is C (Model for End-Stage Liver Disease score.): the MELD score incorporates serum creatinine, bilirubin, and INR and has been validated for predicting perioperative mortality risk in cirrhotic patients undergoing non-transplant surgery. ASA classification is a general anesthetic risk descriptor without these specific liver-related variables. The Revised Cardiac Risk Index predicts cardiac complications, and the Charlson Comorbidity Index estimates long-term mortality but neither uses the same laboratory inputs as MELD.
  144. The correct answer is A (INR alone poorly reflects bleeding risk because both pro- and anticoagulant factors are reduced.): cirrhosis reduces synthesis of both procoagulant and natural anticoagulant proteins, producing a rebalanced hemostatic system that INR alone does not capture. Prophylactic FFP does not reliably reduce bleeding and adds volume risk without proven benefit. Vitamin K is largely ineffective when coagulopathy stems from impaired hepatic synthetic function rather than deficiency, and cirrhotic patients can experience either bleeding or thrombotic complications depending on clinical context.
  145. The correct answer is C (Histamine-releasing drugs such as morphine and atracurium.): histamine release can trigger vasoactive mediator release from carcinoid tumor cells, precipitating flushing, bronchospasm, and profound hypotension. Volatile agents and balanced crystalloids are generally considered safe and do not directly provoke mediator release. Beta blockers are not typically the trigger of concern, though catecholamines and sympathomimetics are avoided since they can also stimulate mediator secretion.
  146. The correct answer is D (Cisatracurium, since it undergoes organ-independent Hofmann elimination unaffected by hepatic or renal dysfunction.): cisatracurium is degraded by temperature- and pH-dependent Hofmann elimination and nonspecific ester hydrolysis, making its clearance independent of hepatic or renal function and its duration predictable in cirrhosis. Rocuronium and vecuronium both rely substantially on hepatic uptake and biliary excretion, prolonging their effect in liver disease. Pancuronium depends primarily on renal elimination and does not compensate for hepatic dysfunction.
  147. The correct answer is C (It raises gastric fluid pH without adding particulate matter that worsens pneumonitis.): non-particulate antacids such as sodium citrate increase gastric pH, and if aspiration occurs, higher pH aspirate causes less severe chemical pneumonitis than low pH or particulate-containing fluid. These agents do not significantly accelerate gastric emptying or paralyze the lower esophageal sphincter. They act locally in the stomach rather than by binding circulating enzymes.
  148. The correct answer is C (Hepatorenal syndrome, supported by urine sodium less than 10 mEq/L.): hepatorenal syndrome is a functional renal failure in advanced cirrhosis characterized by intense renal vasoconstriction, persisting despite adequate volume expansion and marked by avid sodium retention with very low urine sodium. Acute tubular necrosis typically shows muddy brown casts and does not respond as this diagnosis requires exclusion of ATN. Prerenal azotemia should improve with volume repletion, and there is no described contrast exposure here.
  149. The correct answer is C (Administer octreotide bolus; crisis was likely triggered by succinylcholine fasciculations.): octreotide, a somatostatin analog, inhibits release of vasoactive mediators and is first-line treatment for carcinoid crisis. Succinylcholine-induced muscle fasciculations can mechanically compress the tumor and provoke mediator release, making it a recognized trigger. Epinephrine and other direct catecholamines can worsen crisis by further stimulating mediator secretion rather than treating it, and propofol and volatile agents are not classic precipitants.
  150. The correct answer is D (Reverse Trendelenburg or head-elevated position during preoxygenation and induction.): head-elevated positioning improves functional residual capacity and preoxygenation efficacy while reducing the gradient favoring passive regurgitation in a patient with elevated intraabdominal pressure. Steep Trendelenburg worsens reflux risk and further compromises lung volumes. Flat supine positioning does not optimize oxygen reserve in this setting, and lateral decubitus is not standard for RSI airway management despite some aspiration-related theoretical benefits.
  151. The correct answer is B (Full stomach precautions apply given possible recent hematemesis and delayed gastric emptying.): patients with a history of variceal bleeding may have retained blood in the stomach and cirrhosis-related delayed gastric emptying, warranting aspiration precautions even for endoscopic procedures. Deep general anesthesia with paralysis is not routinely required for band ligation. Assuming no airway risk ignores real aspiration concerns, and regional anesthesia is not applicable to an upper endoscopic procedure.
  152. The correct answer is C (Benzodiazepines, due to prolonged sedation from reduced hepatic clearance.): benzodiazepines rely heavily on hepatic metabolism, and reduced clearance in cirrhosis leads to prolonged sedative effect that can mimic or worsen hepatic encephalopathy. Agents metabolized by plasma esterases bypass hepatic dependence, and volatile anesthetics with minimal biotransformation are less likely to accumulate. Nondepolarizing agents with organ-independent elimination, such as cisatracurium, are specifically chosen to avoid this accumulation risk.
  153. The correct answer is A (Use judicious crystalloid with albumin supplementation to help maintain oncotic pressure.): supplementing albumin helps restore plasma oncotic pressure and limit interstitial fluid accumulation while judicious crystalloid maintains intravascular volume during ongoing losses. Large-volume saline alone worsens edema given already low oncotic pressure. Rigid fluid restriction can compromise perfusion during a lengthy case, and vasopressors alone without fluid support do not address the underlying volume and oncotic deficits.
  154. The correct answer is B (Increased venous return and preload from diversion of portal blood flow.): creation of the shunt diverts portal blood directly into the systemic venous circulation, acutely increasing venous return and preload, which can precipitate volume overload or decompensated heart failure in susceptible patients. Coagulopathy does not resolve immediately since hepatic synthetic function is unchanged acutely. Cardiac output typically increases rather than decreases, and serum ammonia often rises after TIPS due to portosystemic shunting rather than normalizing.
  155. The correct answer is D (Dextrose without thiamine can precipitate or worsen Wernicke encephalopathy.): administering glucose to a thiamine-deficient patient accelerates thiamine-dependent glucose metabolism, depleting remaining stores and precipitating or worsening Wernicke encephalopathy, so thiamine should be given first or concurrently. Thiamine does not specifically prevent withdrawal seizures or lower ammonia concentration. It is not required for cytochrome enzyme activation related to drug metabolism.
  156. The correct answer is D (Titrate opioids to effect with extended dosing intervals and close monitoring.): titrating to effect with longer intervals accounts for reduced hepatic clearance and biliary excretion, minimizing the risk of drug accumulation and prolonged sedation. Fixed standard intervals ignore altered pharmacokinetics in cholestasis. No opioid is exclusively renally cleared, and avoiding all opioids in favor of NSAIDs alone is inappropriate given bleeding risk and inadequate analgesia for significant pain.
  157. The correct answer is B (Autonomic neuropathy delays gastric emptying despite standard fasting guidelines.): diabetic autonomic neuropathy commonly causes gastroparesis, delaying gastric emptying so that standard fasting periods may not guarantee an empty stomach, increasing aspiration risk. It does not accelerate emptying, and chronic hyperglycemia does have well-documented effects on gastrointestinal motility. The primary mechanism involves autonomic dysfunction of gastric motility rather than direct esophageal sphincter impairment from peripheral neuropathy.
  158. The correct answer is A (Postpone elective surgery and optimize liver disease or consider nonoperative management.): Child-Pugh class C carries extremely high perioperative mortality for elective procedures, so deferring surgery to optimize hepatic status, pursue transplant evaluation, or consider nonoperative alternatives is generally recommended. Proceeding as scheduled ignores this substantial risk. Regional anesthesia does not eliminate the systemic risks of advanced liver failure, and no anesthetic technique, including monitored care, removes the underlying perioperative mortality risk.
  159. The correct answer is A (Hepatic metastases allow vasoactive mediators to bypass first-pass hepatic clearance.): systemic carcinoid syndrome typically requires hepatic metastases, allowing serotonin and other vasoactive substances to enter the systemic circulation without undergoing hepatic first-pass metabolism. A tumor confined to the gut without liver involvement usually has mediators cleared by the liver before reaching systemic circulation. Bronchial carcinoids can also secrete serotonin and cause syndrome without hepatic metastases, making option D inaccurate as stated.
  160. The correct answer is A (Octreotide and vasopressin, which avoid catecholamine-mediated mediator release.): octreotide inhibits mediator secretion directly, and vasopressin raises blood pressure through a non-catecholamine mechanism, avoiding the risk of provoking further mediator release. Epinephrine, dopamine, and ephedrine all act through catecholamine pathways that can paradoxically worsen carcinoid crisis by stimulating additional hormone secretion from tumor cells.
  161. The correct answer is D (Ensuring adequate periprocedural intravenous isotonic fluid hydration.): periprocedural isotonic fluid administration remains the best-supported intervention for reducing contrast-induced nephropathy risk by maintaining renal perfusion and diluting contrast concentration in the tubules. Loop diuretics have not shown consistent benefit and may worsen volume status. Withholding fluids increases risk rather than reducing it, and NSAIDs are nephrotoxic and would compound rather than reduce renal injury risk.
  162. The correct answer is C (Stage 2 acute kidney injury based on a doubling of baseline creatinine.): KDIGO stage 2 is defined by creatinine rising to 2.0 to 2.9 times baseline, and a rise from 0.9 to 1.9 mg/dL represents just over a twofold increase, meeting stage 2 criteria. The oliguria present for 14 hours would independently satisfy stage 2 urine output criteria as well, since KDIGO stage 2 by urine output requires urine output below 0.5 mL/kg/hr for 12 hours or more. Both criteria therefore point to the same overall stage.
  163. The correct answer is B (Dialysis within 24 hours before surgery, avoiding same-day aggressive ultrafiltration.): dialysis within roughly one day before surgery corrects hyperkalemia and volume overload while allowing time for hemodynamic equilibration, reducing the risk of acute intraoperative hypotension from same-day aggressive fluid removal. Dialysis immediately before surgery increases hypotension risk from rapid shifts. Delaying dialysis for a week risks dangerous electrolyte and fluid accumulation, and timing is clearly clinically relevant to perioperative stability.
  164. The correct answer is D (Avoid blood pressure cuff placement and venipuncture on the fistula arm.): compression from a blood pressure cuff or tourniquet, and needle trauma from venipuncture, can compromise fistula patency and blood flow, so this arm should be protected from these interventions whenever possible. Placing the cuff on the fistula arm or using it preferentially for intravenous access risks thrombosis of this vital dialysis access. A tourniquet applied proximal to the fistula similarly risks compressing and damaging the vessel.
  165. The correct answer is B (Accumulation of active morphine-6-glucuronide metabolite due to reduced renal excretion.): morphine-6-glucuronide is an active metabolite normally cleared renally, and its accumulation in renal failure can cause profound and prolonged sedation and respiratory depression exceeding what the parent drug dose would predict. Morphine is not directly nephrotoxic in this manner. It does have clinically significant active metabolites, and renal failure does not accelerate hepatic conjugation or overall drug clearance.
  166. The correct answer is D (Peaked T waves, then P wave flattening, then QRS widening, then sine wave pattern.): the classic hyperkalemic ECG progression begins with peaked, narrow T waves, followed by PR prolongation and P wave flattening, then QRS widening, and ultimately a sine wave pattern preceding ventricular fibrillation or asystole. The reverse order described in option B does not reflect actual progression. QRS widening follows rather than precedes T wave changes, and ST elevation is not the characteristic earliest hyperkalemic finding.
  167. The correct answer is A (Calcium stabilizes the cardiac membrane, reducing arrhythmia risk without lowering potassium.): calcium gluconate raises the threshold potential relative to the elevated resting membrane potential caused by hyperkalemia, stabilizing cardiac conduction and reducing arrhythmia risk, but it does not change serum potassium concentration. It does not promote renal excretion or shift potassium intracellularly, and it does not chemically bind potassium ions in plasma.
  168. The correct answer is A (Upregulated extrajunctional receptors cause massive potassium efflux and life-threatening hyperkalemia.): chronic denervation from spinal cord injury leads to proliferation of extrajunctional acetylcholine receptors across the muscle membrane, and succinylcholine depolarization of these numerous receptors can cause massive potassium efflux and cardiac arrest. Prolonged blockade is not the primary hazard in this scenario. Malignant hyperthermia and altered renal potassium handling are not the mechanisms responsible for this specific risk.
  169. The correct answer is C (Absorption of hypotonic irrigant through open prostatic venous sinuses causing dilutional hyponatremia.): TURP syndrome results from systemic absorption of large volumes of hypotonic glycine irrigation fluid through open venous sinuses during resection, producing dilutional hyponatremia, hypoosmolality, and associated neurologic and cardiovascular symptoms. This is not primarily a hemorrhagic process, and no local anesthetic is typically used as bladder irrigant. There is no described allergic exposure consistent with anaphylaxis in this presentation.
  170. The correct answer is D (Cautious administration of hypertonic saline with slow, monitored sodium correction.): severely symptomatic acute hyponatremia with seizures warrants hypertonic saline to raise sodium promptly enough to control symptoms, while correction rate is still monitored to avoid overly rapid change. A free water bolus would worsen the hyponatremia. Isotonic saline alone corrects sodium too slowly for this degree of symptomatic hyponatremia, and fluid restriction alone is insufficient for an acute symptomatic crisis.
  171. The correct answer is A (Syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion.): SIADH classically presents with euvolemic hyponatremia, inappropriately concentrated urine relative to plasma osmolality, and elevated urine sodium, after excluding adrenal insufficiency and hypothyroidism. Diabetes insipidus produces dilute urine and hypernatremia rather than this pattern. Hypovolemic hyponatremia would present with volume depletion rather than euvolemia, and primary polydipsia typically produces dilute rather than concentrated urine.
  172. The correct answer is A (Using a balanced crystalloid solution with a chloride content closer to plasma.): balanced crystalloids contain a chloride concentration closer to physiologic plasma levels than normal saline, reducing the risk of hyperchloremic non-anion gap metabolic acidosis with large-volume administration. Giving more normal saline would worsen rather than address the chloride load. Colloid-only strategies do not by themselves prevent this specific electrolyte derangement, and rigid fluid restriction ignores legitimate intraoperative volume needs.
  173. The correct answer is C (Renal blood flow falls proportionally, increasing risk of ischemic acute kidney injury.): below the lower limit of autoregulation, renal blood flow becomes pressure-dependent and falls proportionally with mean arterial pressure, increasing the risk of ischemic injury, particularly in patients whose autoregulatory range may already be altered by chronic kidney disease. Renal blood flow does not remain constant below this threshold, glomerular filtration rate does not compensatorily increase under hypoperfusion, and autoregulation is not preserved at arbitrarily low pressures.
  174. The correct answer is B (NSAIDs inhibit prostaglandin-mediated afferent arteriolar vasodilation needed to maintain glomerular perfusion.): during hypotension, prostaglandins help maintain afferent arteriolar dilation to preserve glomerular perfusion, and NSAIDs block this compensatory mechanism, predisposing to ischemic acute kidney injury. NSAIDs do not cause rapid irreversible scarring within hours, nor do they increase renal blood flow; they reduce it by removing this protective vasodilatory response, so their hemodynamic effect during hypotension is clinically significant.
  175. The correct answer is A (Reduced renal erythropoietin production leading to decreased red blood cell synthesis.): the kidneys are the primary source of erythropoietin, and progressive nephron loss in advanced chronic kidney disease reduces erythropoietin production, impairing red blood cell synthesis and causing normocytic anemia. While uremic toxins and mild reduced red cell survival can contribute, erythropoietin deficiency is the dominant and most clinically targeted mechanism, and gastrointestinal blood loss is not the predominant cause in most patients.
  176. The correct answer is B (Reduced renal buffering capacity limits compensation, worsening acid-base derangement intraoperatively.): impaired renal bicarbonate regeneration and hydrogen ion excretion in chronic kidney disease limit the ability to buffer additional acid loads, so intraoperative lactate accumulation can produce more severe and prolonged acidosis than in patients with normal renal function. This scenario clearly warrants monitoring rather than being ignored. Anesthetic-induced hypoventilation does impair respiratory compensation, and buffering capacity is not preserved as kidney function declines.
  177. The correct answer is C (A substantial fraction of rocuronium clearance depends on renal excretion.): although rocuronium is primarily cleared hepatically, a meaningful portion of its elimination depends on renal excretion, and this fraction becomes clinically significant in renal failure, prolonging its duration of action. It is not entirely liver-dependent with zero renal contribution, it does not undergo Hofmann elimination like cisatracurium, and renal failure does not accelerate hepatic clearance of the drug.
  178. The correct answer is D (Cisatracurium, which is eliminated by organ-independent Hofmann elimination.): cisatracurium's degradation through Hofmann elimination and ester hydrolysis occurs independently of renal or hepatic function, giving it a predictable duration of action even in end-stage renal disease. Vecuronium and pancuronium both have significant renal excretion components that prolong their effect in renal failure. Rocuronium is primarily hepatically cleared with a renal contribution but is not cleared almost exclusively through the kidneys.
  179. The correct answer is C (Potassium leaks from red cells into storage plasma over time, raising extracellular potassium.): during storage, red blood cell membrane sodium-potassium pump function declines, allowing intracellular potassium to leak passively into the extracellular storage plasma, which is then transfused along with the cells. This is a passive leak rather than active pumping outward, citrate primarily affects calcium and does not directly raise potassium, and irradiated units actually have higher extracellular potassium due to further membrane injury from irradiation.
  180. The correct answer is C (Osmotic demyelination syndrome from overly rapid sodium correction.): correcting chronic hyponatremia too quickly, generally exceeding recommended limits over 24 hours, can cause osmotic demyelination presenting with delayed quadriparesis, dysarthria, and altered mental status days after correction. This picture reflects excessive rather than inadequate correction, so cerebral edema from undercorrection does not fit. There is no described thiamine deficiency or insulin administration to explain the alternative diagnoses.
  181. The correct answer is D (Using the lowest effective volume of iso-osmolar or low-osmolar contrast agent.): minimizing total contrast dose and selecting iso-osmolar or low-osmolar agents reduces direct nephrotoxic exposure and is a well-supported strategy alongside adequate hydration. High-dose mannitol has not shown consistent benefit and can cause diuresis-related volume depletion. Withholding fluids increases rather than decreases risk, and high-dose furosemide is not recommended and may worsen outcomes in this setting.
  182. The correct answer is D (Cerebral edema from an osmotic gradient favoring water movement into brain cells.): dialysis disequilibrium syndrome occurs when rapid removal of urea from the blood outpaces its removal from brain tissue, creating a transient osmotic gradient that draws water into brain cells and causes cerebral edema, producing headache, confusion, and nausea. Hyperkalemia would not typically result from effective dialysis, and there is no described mechanism supporting hypoglycemia or an allergic membrane reaction in this classic presentation.
  183. The correct answer is B (Continuing CRRT helps maintain fluid and electrolyte control during a hemodynamically unstable case.): continuing CRRT intraoperatively in a hemodynamically unstable, critically ill patient helps maintain ongoing control of fluid balance, electrolytes, and acid-base status during a physiologically demanding procedure. CRRT does not need to be stopped for all surgeries, it meaningfully affects drug clearance and volume status, and modern CRRT circuits can be safely operated in the operating room with appropriate coordination.
  184. The correct answer is B (Central diabetes insipidus, characterized by dilute urine and rising serum sodium.): central diabetes insipidus following pituitary surgery results from reduced antidiuretic hormone secretion, producing large volumes of inappropriately dilute urine, rising serum sodium, and increasing serum osmolality. SIADH produces the opposite pattern with concentrated urine and hyponatremia. Cerebral salt wasting causes hyponatremia with volume depletion, and primary polydipsia would not typically show rising serum sodium or osmolality.
  185. The correct answer is A (Cautious potassium correction is reasonable, weighing arrhythmia risk against surgical urgency.): moderate chronic hypokalemia without acute symptoms or ECG changes does not necessarily mandate delaying surgery, but cautious correction is reasonable given the theoretical arrhythmia risk under anesthesia, balanced against the urgency of the procedure. Proceeding with no consideration ignores real risk, hypokalemia can potentiate neuromuscular blockade, and succinylcholine is not absolutely contraindicated in mild to moderate hypokalemia.
  186. The correct answer is C (Inability to excrete excess fluid volume leads to pulmonary edema from volume overload.): an anuric patient cannot excrete excess administered fluid, so large-volume crystalloid administration accumulates intravascularly and can precipitate volume overload and pulmonary edema. Excess fluid does not simply redistribute harmlessly, anuric patients have reduced rather than enhanced compensatory capacity for volume handling, and fluid administration clearly has direct relevance to pulmonary status in this population.
  187. The correct answer is B (Maintaining adequate mean arterial pressure and euvolemia throughout the procedure.): maintaining adequate perfusion pressure and euvolemia supports renal blood flow and reduces the risk of ischemic acute kidney injury during major surgery. Permitting prolonged hypotension directly increases renal ischemic risk despite any bleeding benefit. Unnecessary high-dose nephrotoxic antibiotics add renal insult, and restricting fluids without regard to actual losses can produce hypovolemia and further compromise renal perfusion.
  188. The correct answer is A (Myoglobin-induced tubular obstruction and direct nephrotoxicity from rhabdomyolysis.): prolonged immobility with muscle compression causes rhabdomyolysis, releasing myoglobin that obstructs renal tubules and exerts direct nephrotoxic effects, producing the classic dark urine, markedly elevated creatine kinase, and rising creatinine. This is not an infectious or immune-mediated glomerular process, and there is no described evidence of bladder outlet obstruction explaining this laboratory picture.
  189. The correct answer is D (Sugammadex-rocuronium complex clearance is delayed, but the drug is generally used cautiously.): sugammadex is cleared renally, and clearance of the sugammadex-rocuronium complex is delayed in renal impairment, though it can still be used cautiously with awareness of prolonged elimination and potential recurarization concerns. It is not absolutely contraindicated across all degrees of renal impairment. It is not hepatically metabolized, and its immediate reversal efficacy at the neuromuscular junction is not reduced by renal failure.
  190. The correct answer is D (Intravenous insulin with concurrent glucose administration.): insulin drives potassium intracellularly via stimulation of the sodium-potassium ATPase pump, providing a rapid though temporary reduction in serum potassium, and glucose is co-administered to prevent hypoglycemia. Oral potassium-binding resins act too slowly for emergent management. Postponing surgery without addressing life-threatening hyperkalemia is inappropriate, and furosemide alone acts too slowly and depends on adequate renal function to be the priority intervention here.
  191. The correct answer is C (Withhold transfusion and reassess, since restrictive thresholds near 7-8 g/dL are safe even with stable cardiac disease.): large trials including patients with cardiovascular disease show restrictive transfusion strategies are noninferior to liberal ones for mortality and morbidity. This patient is asymptomatic and hemodynamically stable, so transfusion is not indicated at this hemoglobin level. Liberal transfusion increases exposure risk without proven benefit in stable patients. Bone marrow biopsy is unrelated to acute perioperative anemia management.
  192. The correct answer is B (Administer recombinant factor VIII concentrate to raise activity to 80-100% before incision and maintain levels postoperatively.): severe hemophilia A requires factor VIII concentrate dosed to achieve near-normal activity before major surgery, with continued replacement to maintain hemostatic levels during healing. Fresh frozen plasma cannot achieve adequate factor VIII concentrations without massive volume. Desmopressin releases only modest endogenous factor VIII and is inadequate for severe deficiency or major surgery.
  193. The correct answer is B (Simple transfusion to raise hemoglobin to approximately 10 g/dL without necessarily lowering hemoglobin S percentage.): the TAPS trial showed simple transfusion targeting hemoglobin around 10 g/dL is as effective as aggressive exchange transfusion for preventing perioperative complications in low to medium risk sickle cell surgery. Exchange transfusion with rigid hemoglobin S targets is reserved for high-risk or complex procedures. Waiting until intraoperative desaturation ignores established preventive strategy.
  194. The correct answer is B (Bridge with therapeutic dose low molecular weight heparin, held 24 hours before surgery and resumed postoperatively.): patients with high thromboembolic risk, such as a CHA2DS2-VASc score of 5, warrant therapeutic bridging anticoagulation during the interruption of warfarin to minimize stroke risk while allowing safe surgery. Prophylactic dosing does not provide equivalent protection against arterial thromboembolism. Proceeding without any bridge would leave a high-risk patient unprotected during the anticoagulation gap.
  195. The correct answer is D (Citrate toxicity from rapid blood product administration, treated with intravenous calcium chloride.): citrate anticoagulant in stored blood products chelates ionized calcium, and rapid massive transfusion can outpace hepatic citrate metabolism, causing hypocalcemia with QT prolongation and myocardial depression. Calcium chloride rapidly corrects ionized calcium and should be given empirically during massive transfusion. Hyperkalemia and hemolytic reaction would present with different electrolyte and clinical patterns, not primarily hypocalcemia.
  196. The correct answer is A (Initiate within 24 to 48 hours once hemostasis is confirmed and no ongoing bleeding or planned reoperation exists.): trauma patients have high VTE risk, and guidelines recommend early initiation of pharmacologic prophylaxis once bleeding risk has been assessed as acceptable, typically within 24 to 48 hours. Mechanical prophylaxis alone is inadequate in high-risk trauma without a contraindication to pharmacologic agents. Prolonged delay significantly increases thromboembolic risk without added safety benefit.
  197. The correct answer is D (Proceed with oral iron supplementation started immediately to allow time for erythropoietic response before surgery.): the laboratory pattern of microcytosis, low ferritin, and elevated iron binding capacity confirms iron deficiency anemia, and preoperative optimization with oral or intravenous iron over several weeks can meaningfully raise hemoglobin and reduce transfusion need. Unnecessary transfusion exposes the patient to risk without addressing the underlying deficiency. Vitamin B12 treats macrocytic, not microcytic, anemia.
  198. The correct answer is A (Desmopressin, since type 1 disease typically responds well by releasing stored von Willebrand factor.): type 1 von Willebrand disease, the most common and mildest form, usually responds to desmopressin, which stimulates release of endothelial stores of von Willebrand factor and factor VIII. Fresh frozen plasma provides insufficient concentration for reliable hemostasis. Vitamin K and platelet transfusion do not address the underlying von Willebrand factor deficiency and are not indicated.
  199. The correct answer is B (Acute chest syndrome, requiring supplemental oxygen, incentive spirometry, and consideration of exchange transfusion.): this clinical picture of fever, chest pain, hypoxia, and new infiltrate in a sickle cell patient is classic for acute chest syndrome, a leading cause of death in this population that can be precipitated by postoperative atelectasis and hypoventilation. Management includes oxygen, aggressive pulmonary toilet, and exchange transfusion for worsening hypoxia. This is a medical emergency, not a benign or purely psychiatric process.
  200. The correct answer is C (Discuss with the surgical and cardiology team continuing aspirin and clopidogrel through surgery given the very high thrombosis risk this early after stenting.): stent thrombosis risk is highest within the first four to six weeks after bare metal stent placement, and interrupting dual antiplatelet therapy during this period carries catastrophic cardiac risk that generally outweighs surgical bleeding concerns. A multidisciplinary discussion balancing both risks is essential rather than a rigid stop date. Substituting a single heparin dose does not provide equivalent antiplatelet protection against stent thrombosis.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Platelet transfusion, since reduced maximum amplitude reflects inadequate clot strength from platelet dysfunction or thrombocytopenia.): maximum amplitude on thromboelastography primarily reflects platelet number and function contribution to clot strength, along with fibrinogen. A normal reaction time and alpha angle suggest adequate clotting factor activation and fibrin polymerization rate, pointing toward platelets as the deficient component. Fibrinogen deficiency and hyperfibrinolysis produce distinct tracing patterns that differ from isolated low maximum amplitude with normal upstream parameters.
  2. The correct answer is C (A balanced ratio of approximately one unit each of packed red cells, plasma, and platelets.): the PROPPR trial demonstrated that a 1:1:1 ratio of red cells, plasma, and platelets improved hemostasis and reduced death from exsanguination in the first 24 hours compared with a 2:1:1 ratio, without significant differences in overall 24-hour or 30-day mortality. Delaying plasma and platelets until late in resuscitation worsens dilutional coagulopathy. Withholding blood products until laboratory confirmation delays treatment in actively hemorrhaging patients.
  3. The correct answer is A (Placement of a retrievable inferior vena cava filter until bleeding resolves and pharmacologic prophylaxis can be started.): patients with high VTE risk and an absolute contraindication to both mechanical and pharmacologic prophylaxis, such as active hemorrhage with injuries precluding compression devices, may benefit from a temporary retrievable filter until safer prophylaxis can be resumed. Withholding all prophylaxis leaves a high-risk patient unprotected. Giving therapeutic anticoagulation during active bleeding would worsen hemorrhage.
  4. The correct answer is A (Cardiopulmonary bypass causes hemodilution and increased oxygen demand, reducing tolerance for anemia during the perioperative period.): patients with fixed cardiac lesions like severe aortic stenosis have limited ability to increase cardiac output to compensate for anemia, and bypass-associated hemodilution can further lower hemoglobin intraoperatively, justifying a somewhat higher transfusion threshold than in healthy patients. This differs from a blanket rule applied identically to all surgeries. Valve replacement does not instantaneously reverse the myocardial demand mismatch present at induction.
  5. The correct answer is C (Factor VII deficiency, which selectively prolongs the extrinsic pathway assessed by prothrombin time.): factor VII is the sole component of the extrinsic coagulation pathway measured by prothrombin time, so its isolated deficiency prolongs prothrombin time while leaving the intrinsic pathway, and thus partial thromboplastin time, unaffected. Factor VIII and IX deficiencies prolong partial thromboplastin time, not prothrombin time. Factor XIII deficiency classically presents with normal prothrombin and partial thromboplastin times despite bleeding, since it acts after the standard clotting cascade.
  6. The correct answer is B (Maintaining normothermia, adequate oxygenation, avoiding acidosis, and ensuring adequate hydration.): hypoxia, acidosis, hypothermia, dehydration, and venous stasis all promote red cell sickling, so perioperative management focuses on avoiding these triggers through warming, supplemental oxygen, adequate hydration, and avoiding hypoventilation. Fluid restriction and diuresis increase dehydration risk and worsen sickling. Deliberate hyperventilation to extreme alkalosis is not a standard protective strategy and can cause other complications.
  7. The correct answer is D (Idarucizumab, a monoclonal antibody fragment that specifically and rapidly binds and neutralizes dabigatran.): idarucizumab is a humanized antibody fragment with high affinity for dabigatran that achieves rapid, complete reversal of its anticoagulant effect, making it first line for emergent reversal before urgent neurosurgery. Vitamin K reverses warfarin, not direct thrombin inhibitors. Fresh frozen plasma does not reliably reverse dabigatran's effect and protamine reverses heparin, not dabigatran.
  8. The correct answer is C (Cryoprecipitate, which is concentrated in fibrinogen and effectively raises low levels.): cryoprecipitate is rich in fibrinogen, factor VIII, von Willebrand factor, and factor XIII, and is the preferred product to rapidly correct hypofibrinogenemia in the setting of obstetric hemorrhage, where fibrinogen often falls disproportionately early. Fibrinogen below 100 to 150 mg/dL in an actively bleeding patient warrants targeted repletion. Vitamin K does not acutely raise fibrinogen, and red cells or platelets alone do not correct this specific deficiency.
  9. The correct answer is B (Hold the last therapeutic dose at least 24 hours before neuraxial needle placement.): consensus guidelines recommend a minimum 24 hour interval after a therapeutic dose of low molecular weight heparin before neuraxial needle or catheter placement to reduce the risk of spinal or epidural hematoma. Performing the block sooner substantially increases bleeding risk in the epidural space. Continuing therapeutic anticoagulation through needle placement is a well recognized contraindication to neuraxial technique.
  10. The correct answer is B (Disseminated intravascular coagulation from sepsis-triggered widespread activation of coagulation and fibrinolysis.): sepsis is a common trigger for disseminated intravascular coagulation, which classically presents with prolonged prothrombin and partial thromboplastin times, low fibrinogen from consumption, elevated D-dimer from fibrinolysis, and thrombocytopenia, along with diffuse bleeding from multiple sites. Dilutional coagulopathy alone would not typically produce elevated D-dimer. Heparin induced thrombocytopenia causes thrombosis rather than this diffuse bleeding and consumptive coagulopathy pattern.
  11. The correct answer is B (Exchange transfusion effectively lowers hemoglobin S percentage while avoiding excessive elevation of hematocrit and blood viscosity.): for high-risk procedures such as neurosurgery, exchange transfusion reduces the proportion of sickle hemoglobin substantially without raising the hematocrit to levels that increase blood viscosity and stroke risk, unlike simple transfusion which can overshoot hematocrit if hemoglobin S reduction is the goal. It requires more resources and coordination, not less. Standard sickle cell precautions regarding oxygenation and temperature remain necessary regardless of transfusion method.
  12. The correct answer is C (Discontinue all heparin products immediately and start a non-heparin anticoagulant such as argatroban.): the combination of thrombocytopenia after five or more days of heparin exposure and new thrombosis strongly suggests heparin induced thrombocytopenia, which requires immediate discontinuation of all heparin sources and initiation of an alternative anticoagulant like a direct thrombin inhibitor. Continuing or increasing heparin would worsen the prothrombotic state. Platelet transfusion is generally avoided since it can precipitate further thrombosis in this condition.
  13. The correct answer is B (Transfusion-related acute lung injury caused by donor antibodies triggering pulmonary leukocyte activation.): transfusion-related acute lung injury presents within six hours of transfusion with acute hypoxemia, bilateral infiltrates, and often hypotension, typically caused by donor antibodies activating recipient leukocytes and causing pulmonary capillary leak, and importantly occurs without evidence of volume overload. Circulatory overload would show signs of fluid excess like elevated filling pressures. Anaphylactic reactions typically present with urticaria and bronchospasm rather than isolated bilateral infiltrates.
  14. The correct answer is C (Remove the catheter, wait a minimum recommended interval such as 10 to 12 hours after the last prophylactic dose before removal, and delay the next dose after removal.): consensus guidelines recommend specific time intervals both before catheter removal after a prophylactic low molecular weight heparin dose and before administering the next dose after removal, to reduce spinal hematoma risk during a period when the epidural space may still be vulnerable. Removing the catheter immediately after dosing disregards peak anticoagulant effect. Prophylactic dosing still carries meaningful bleeding risk around neuraxial catheter manipulation.
  15. The correct answer is A (Iron overload causing cardiomyopathy and endocrine dysfunction, requiring focused preoperative cardiac and endocrine evaluation and workup.): chronically transfused thalassemia patients accumulate iron because the body has no active excretion mechanism, leading to cardiomyopathy, arrhythmias, diabetes, and hypothyroidism, all of which warrant preoperative cardiac and endocrine assessment before major surgery. Chronic transfusion does not typically cause hypercalcemia or acquired hemophilia. Thalassemia patients remain anemic despite transfusion, not polycythemic.
  16. The correct answer is C (Recognize that standard coagulation tests poorly reflect the rebalanced hemostatic state in cirrhosis, and avoid routine prophylactic correction absent active bleeding or a high-risk procedure.): cirrhotic patients have concurrent reductions in both procoagulant and anticoagulant factors, so INR and platelet count alone overestimate bleeding risk and do not reliably predict perioperative hemorrhage, making routine prophylactic correction unnecessary for low-risk procedures without active bleeding. Automatically normalizing INR with plasma exposes the patient to volume and transfusion risk without proven benefit. Elective low-risk surgery can often proceed safely with appropriate monitoring.
  17. The correct answer is B (Regional anesthesia is generally safe and preferred, as it reduces catecholamine release and pain-induced hypoventilation that can trigger vaso-occlusive crisis.): effective regional analgesia limits pain-related sympathetic activation, hypoventilation, and stress response, all of which can promote sickling, making neuraxial techniques a favorable option in sickle cell patients without a specific contraindication. Sickle cell disease itself does not cause a bleeding diathesis that would preclude neuraxial anesthesia. There is no established increased incidence of epidural hematoma specific to sickle cell disease.
  18. The correct answer is C (Continue low dose aspirin through the perioperative period, as it does not meaningfully increase major bleeding and may reduce graft thrombosis and ischemic events.): evidence in cardiac surgery supports continuing low dose aspirin perioperatively because it does not significantly increase major bleeding complications and may reduce early graft occlusion and postoperative ischemic events. Routinely stopping aspirin for ten days removes cardioprotection without proven safety benefit in this population. Nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs are not an appropriate substitute and carry their own bleeding and renal risks.
  19. The correct answer is C (Transfuse platelets to maintain a count above approximately 50,000 per microliter in an actively bleeding patient.): guidelines for massive transfusion recommend maintaining platelet counts above 50,000 per microliter, or higher in patients with traumatic brain injury or multiple trauma, when active microvascular bleeding is present. Waiting until the count is critically low below 10,000 is appropriate only for prophylaxis in nonbleeding patients, not active hemorrhage. Fresh frozen plasma does not contain functional platelets and cannot substitute for platelet transfusion.
  20. The correct answer is D (Acute pulmonary embolism causing right ventricular strain and hemodynamic collapse.): the combination of sudden hypotension, tachycardia, hypoxia, an abrupt drop in end-tidal carbon dioxide reflecting increased dead space, and new right ventricular strain on echocardiography is a classic presentation of acute pulmonary embolism obstructing pulmonary blood flow. Malignant hyperthermia typically presents with hypercarbia, muscle rigidity, and hyperthermia rather than isolated right heart strain. Local anesthetic toxicity and anaphylaxis do not characteristically produce this specific echocardiographic finding of acute right ventricular strain.
  21. The correct answer is A (Target a moderate range of approximately 140 to 180 mg/dL, avoiding both significant hyperglycemia and hypoglycemia.): current perioperative guidelines recommend a moderate glucose target generally between 140 and 180 mg/dL for most surgical patients, since intensive tight control below 110 mg/dL increases hypoglycemia risk without proven benefit and may increase mortality. Allowing glucose above 250 mg/dL increases infection risk, impairs wound healing, and worsens outcomes. Continued monitoring is essential throughout an insulin infusion to detect and prevent hypoglycemia.
  22. The correct answer is C (Give propylthiouracil or methimazole, beta blockade with esmolol or propranolol, corticosteroids, and iodine solution after thionamide administration.): this presentation is consistent with thyroid storm, treated with a thionamide to block new hormone synthesis, followed by iodine to block hormone release, beta blockade to control adrenergic symptoms, and corticosteroids to reduce peripheral conversion of T4 to T3 and address relative adrenal insufficiency. Dantrolene treats malignant hyperthermia, a different syndrome with distinct triggers and mechanism. Levothyroxine would worsen this hyperthyroid crisis rather than treat it.
  23. The correct answer is D (Administer stress dose hydrocortisone, such as 50 to 100 mg intravenously before incision with continued dosing for major surgery, in addition to her maintenance dose.): chronic corticosteroid use suppresses the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis, and major surgical stress in this setting requires supplemental stress dose steroids to prevent hemodynamically significant adrenal crisis. Continuing only her home maintenance dose is inadequate to meet the physiologic cortisol demands of major surgery. Abruptly discontinuing steroids perioperatively risks precipitating acute adrenal insufficiency.
  24. The correct answer is B (Alpha blockade first with an agent such as phenoxybenzamine, followed by beta blockade once adequate alpha blockade is established.): alpha blockade must precede beta blockade because unopposed alpha-adrenergic vasoconstriction with beta blockade first can precipitate a severe hypertensive crisis and pulmonary edema from unopposed alpha stimulation. Once alpha blockade is adequate, beta blockade is added to control reflex or catecholamine-induced tachycardia. Beta blockade alone is dangerous and simultaneous same-day initiation risks the same unopposed alpha stimulation problem.
  25. The correct answer is A (Use of ramped positioning with head elevation, preoxygenation with positive pressure, and readiness for a difficult airway given increased risk of rapid desaturation and difficult mask ventilation.): obese patients with obstructive sleep apnea have reduced functional residual capacity, increased oxygen consumption, and often difficult mask ventilation or intubation, so ramped positioning and thorough preoxygenation with positive pressure significantly improve safety margins during induction. Standard flat positioning is less effective in this population due to reduced chest wall compliance and cephalad diaphragm displacement. Preoxygenation is essential, not optional, given rapid desaturation risk in this population.
  26. The correct answer is C (Euglycemic or near-euglycemic diabetic ketoacidosis related to SGLT2 inhibitor use, which can occur with only modestly elevated glucose.): SGLT2 inhibitors can cause ketoacidosis with only mildly elevated or even normal glucose levels, particularly in the perioperative period due to fasting and surgical stress, making this diagnosis easy to miss if clinicians rely on classic markedly elevated glucose criteria. This is why SGLT2 inhibitors are typically held for several days before elective surgery. Lactic acidosis from metformin would not primarily produce elevated ketones, and this presentation is not explained by simple dehydration alone.
  27. The correct answer is D (Treatment includes intravenous levothyroxine or liothyronine, stress dose corticosteroids given possible concurrent adrenal insufficiency, and cautious warming and hemodynamic support.): myxedema coma is a life-threatening manifestation of severe hypothyroidism presenting with hypothermia, bradycardia, hypotension, and altered mental status, requiring intravenous thyroid hormone replacement plus empiric stress dose steroids because concurrent adrenal insufficiency may be masked or coexistent. Hyperthermia and tachycardia are the opposite of the expected presentation. Rapid external rewarming alone without hormone and steroid replacement can precipitate cardiovascular collapse.
  28. The correct answer is A (Acute adrenal crisis, requiring immediate intravenous hydrocortisone bolus, aggressive fluid resuscitation, and correction of any hypoglycemia.): a patient with known adrenal insufficiency who develops acute hypotension, abdominal pain, fever, and altered mental status during physiologic stress from infection is at high risk for adrenal crisis, requiring emergent intravenous hydrocortisone along with fluids and glucose correction. Treating only the underlying infection without addressing adrenal crisis can be fatal. This presentation is not consistent with an isolated anaphylactic reaction or simple dehydration given her underlying disease and stressor.
  29. The correct answer is B (Intravenous sodium nitroprusside or phentolamine titrated to rapidly lower blood pressure.): during pheochromocytoma resection, tumor manipulation can cause massive catecholamine release producing severe hypertension, best managed with rapid-acting, easily titratable intravenous vasodilators or alpha blockers such as nitroprusside or phentolamine. Phenylephrine would dangerously worsen the hypertensive crisis. Oral clonidine has too slow an onset and atropine addresses bradycardia, not the hypertensive surge from catecholamine release.
  30. The correct answer is A (Increased cardiovascular risk from underlying insulin resistance, dyslipidemia, and hypertension warranting careful perioperative cardiac risk assessment.): metabolic syndrome specifically combines insulin resistance, hypertension, dyslipidemia, and abdominal obesity, all of which increase the risk of coronary artery disease and warrant thorough perioperative cardiovascular risk stratification independent of body mass index alone. Difficult intravenous access and difficult mask ventilation relate more directly to obesity itself rather than the metabolic syndrome components specifically. Prolonged emergence from volatile agent accumulation in fat relates to adiposity, not the metabolic derangements defining the syndrome.
  31. The correct answer is D (Treat the hypoglycemia with oral or intravenous dextrose, confirm normalization, and proceed only once glucose is stable.): hypoglycemia unawareness is particularly dangerous because the patient cannot rely on typical adrenergic warning symptoms, and sedation further masks recognition, so any confirmed low glucose should be corrected with dextrose and rechecked before proceeding. Administering additional insulin would worsen an already low glucose level and risk severe neuroglycopenia. Waiting for symptoms defeats the purpose of identifying hypoglycemia unawareness as a specific risk factor requiring proactive management.
  32. The correct answer is A (Unexplained tachycardia, hyperthermia, and hemodynamic instability disproportionate to surgical stimulation, suggesting impending thyroid storm.): despite apparent adequate preoperative preparation, thyroid gland manipulation during thyroidectomy can release stored hormone and precipitate thyroid storm, so unexplained tachycardia and hyperthermia beyond what surgical stimulation would cause should raise immediate suspicion. Mild hypotension from induction agents, vagally mediated bradycardia, and ventilator-related hypercapnia are common, expected anesthetic phenomena unrelated to thyroid status. Recognizing storm early allows prompt treatment before cardiovascular collapse occurs.
  33. The correct answer is D (Etomidate, which inhibits 11-beta-hydroxylase and can cause clinically significant adrenal suppression, especially with repeated dosing or infusion.): etomidate reversibly inhibits 11-beta-hydroxylase, an enzyme required for cortisol synthesis, and this effect is particularly concerning in patients who already have impaired adrenal reserve from chronic opioid-induced hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal suppression. Even a single induction dose can measurably reduce cortisol levels for many hours. Propofol, ketamine, and midazolam do not share this specific adrenal suppressive mechanism.
  34. The correct answer is B (Persistent significant orthostatic hypotension with mild nasal congestion and blood pressure controlled in the normal range while supine.): adequate alpha blockade with phenoxybenzamine is classically indicated by controlled supine blood pressure, mild orthostatic hypotension reflecting sufficient alpha blockade, and nasal stuffiness from alpha-mediated vasodilation of nasal mucosa. Persistent significant supine hypertension suggests inadequate blockade and increased surgical risk. Beta blockade, not alpha blockade, is the mechanism most directly responsible for controlling tachycardia.
  35. The correct answer is C (Dose rocuronium based on lean body weight for rapid sequence induction, since ideal body weight alone may underdose and delay optimal intubating conditions.): lean body weight dosing for rocuronium during rapid sequence induction provides a good balance, achieving adequate intubating conditions quickly while avoiding the prolonged duration of action seen with total body weight dosing in obese patients. Ideal body weight alone can underestimate the dose needed for rapid onset in this population. Avoiding neuromuscular blockade entirely is not appropriate for a rapid sequence induction requiring optimal intubating conditions.
  36. The correct answer is B (Hold metformin at the time of or before iodinated contrast administration and for a period afterward, particularly if renal function is impaired, then restart once renal function is confirmed stable.): metformin is renally cleared, and acute kidney injury from iodinated contrast can lead to metformin accumulation and lactic acidosis, so guidelines recommend holding it around the time of contrast exposure, especially with reduced renal function, and confirming stable renal function before resuming. Continuing without interruption in at-risk patients raises lactic acidosis risk. Substituting an SGLT2 inhibitor introduces a different perioperative risk, including euglycemic ketoacidosis, and is not an appropriate substitution strategy here.
  37. The correct answer is B (Hypothyroid patients often have increased sensitivity to anesthetic and sedative agents, decreased cardiac output, and slower drug metabolism, warranting reduced dosing and careful titration.): hypothyroidism reduces metabolic rate, cardiac output, and hepatic drug clearance, increasing sensitivity to anesthetic, sedative, and analgesic agents and prolonging their effects, so doses should generally be reduced and titrated carefully. This is the opposite of increased anesthetic requirements seen with hyperthyroidism. Neuromuscular blocking agent clearance is typically slowed, not accelerated, in significant hypothyroidism.
  38. The correct answer is C (Contralateral adrenal gland suppression from chronic cortisol excess leading to postoperative adrenal insufficiency until recovery.): chronic cortisol excess from a functioning adenoma suppresses the contralateral adrenal gland and pituitary ACTH production through negative feedback, so after removal of the source tumor, the remaining suppressed gland cannot immediately produce adequate cortisol, resulting in postoperative adrenal insufficiency with hypotension and hyponatremia until the axis recovers. This is why perioperative stress dose steroids are given after adrenalectomy for Cushing syndrome. Residual tumor tissue causing continued excess would not produce insufficiency symptoms, and pancreatitis is not the expected mechanism here.
  39. The correct answer is B (Elevated plasma free metanephrines or elevated 24-hour urine fractionated metanephrines and catecholamines.): biochemical confirmation of pheochromocytoma relies on elevated plasma free metanephrines or 24-hour urine fractionated metanephrines and catecholamines, which have high sensitivity for catecholamine-secreting tumors and should prompt alpha blockade before any surgical procedure on that patient. Normal metanephrine levels would argue against pheochromocytoma as the cause of an incidentaloma. An elevated aldosterone to renin ratio with hypokalemia instead suggests primary hyperaldosteronism, a different adrenal pathology with different management.
  40. The correct answer is A (Initiate noninvasive positive pressure ventilation, minimize sedating medications, and closely monitor arterial blood gases and mental status.): patients with obesity hypoventilation syndrome are at high risk for postoperative respiratory failure due to blunted central respiratory drive and residual anesthetic or opioid effects, and noninvasive positive pressure ventilation along with minimizing further sedation is the appropriate initial management for progressive hypercapnia and somnolence. High flow oxygen alone can worsen hypercapnia by removing the hypoxic respiratory drive without providing ventilatory support. Naloxone is appropriate only if opioid-induced respiratory depression is specifically suspected, not as an automatic reflexive treatment.
  41. The correct answer is C (Increased sensitivity to nondepolarizing agents with prolonged effect.): myasthenia gravis reduces the number of functional postjunctional acetylcholine receptors, so fewer receptors remain available to bind acetylcholine once a nondepolarizing blocker occupies a proportion of them. This markedly increases sensitivity to nondepolarizing agents, and doses should be reduced with careful twitch monitoring. These patients are often relatively resistant to succinylcholine and may need a higher dose, opposite to option D.
  42. The correct answer is B (Reduced postjunctional receptor number requires more agonist to depolarize enough receptors.): because myasthenia gravis reduces functional acetylcholine receptors, a depolarizing agent like succinylcholine must occupy a larger fraction of the remaining receptors to achieve the same degree of depolarization block, producing relative resistance. Chronic pyridostigmine does inhibit acetylcholinesterase, which can modestly prolong succinylcholine's effect by reducing its own hydrolysis, but this does not explain the primary resistance mechanism. Sodium channel density and metabolism rate are not the underlying cause.
  43. The correct answer is B (Succinylcholine, given its known depolarizing action in this clinical setting.): patients with dystrophinopathies such as Duchenne muscular dystrophy have unstable, dystrophin-deficient sarcolemma with upregulated extrajunctional acetylcholine receptors, and succinylcholine can trigger massive potassium efflux, rhabdomyolysis, and cardiac arrest even without classic malignant hyperthermia. Succinylcholine is therefore avoided in all patients with known or suspected myopathy. While volatile agents can also trigger rhabdomyolysis in these patients and should be minimized, succinylcholine carries the most acute and severe hyperkalemic risk. Rocuronium and remifentanil do not carry this specific hyperkalemic arrest risk.
  44. The correct answer is A (Volatile agents can trigger rhabdomyolysis and MH-like reactions in dystrophinopathies.): patients with Duchenne and Becker muscular dystrophy have unstable muscle membranes and are at risk for anesthesia-induced rhabdomyolysis and hyperkalemic cardiac arrest triggered by volatile anesthetics, a phenomenon distinct from but resembling malignant hyperthermia. Total intravenous anesthesia avoids this trigger while still providing adequate depth of anesthesia. Restrictive lung disease affects perioperative respiratory management but is not the reason volatile agents are avoided intraoperatively for triggering purposes.
  45. The correct answer is A (RYR1, the ryanodine receptor calcium release channel gene.): mutations in the ryanodine receptor type 1 gene, which encodes the calcium release channel of the skeletal muscle sarcoplasmic reticulum, account for the large majority of confirmed malignant hyperthermia susceptibility cases. A smaller subset of cases involves CACNA1S, which encodes the dihydropyridine receptor, but it is not the sole locus as stated in option C. SCN4A mutations cause sodium channelopathies like periodic paralysis, and DMD mutations cause dystrophinopathies, neither of which is the primary MH gene.
  46. The correct answer is D (Administer dantrolene sodium and discontinue any potential triggering agents immediately.): rising end-tidal carbon dioxide with masseter rigidity is a hallmark early sign of a malignant hyperthermia crisis, and dantrolene, which reduces calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum by acting on the ryanodine receptor, must be given promptly along with stopping all triggering agents and hyperventilating with 100 percent oxygen. Delaying treatment to observe risks rapid clinical deterioration and death. Switching volatile agents does not address an ongoing crisis since all volatile agents and succinylcholine are triggers, and calcium is not first-line therapy.
  47. The correct answer is C (Recurrent rise in end-tidal carbon dioxide, temperature, and creatine kinase.): malignant hyperthermia recrudescence occurs in a meaningful proportion of treated patients within the first 24 to 36 hours, and recurring hypermetabolic signs such as rising end-tidal carbon dioxide, fever, tachycardia, and rising creatine kinase indicate the process is reactivating and warrants additional dantrolene dosing. Gradual normalization and stable labs are reassuring rather than concerning findings. Mild soreness alone is a nonspecific finding common after any MH episode and does not by itself indicate recrudescence.
  48. The correct answer is A (Labile blood pressure and heart rate with exaggerated responses to stimulation.): Guillain-Barre syndrome frequently involves autonomic dysfunction causing labile blood pressure, tachycardia or bradycardia, and exaggerated or unpredictable hemodynamic responses to stimuli such as laryngoscopy, suctioning, or position changes. This instability requires careful hemodynamic monitoring and cautious titration of induction agents. Autonomic involvement is variable rather than completely absent, and it is not limited to bradycardia alone.
  49. The correct answer is C (Succinylcholine can cause life-threatening hyperkalemia from receptor upregulation in denervated muscle.): Guillain-Barre syndrome causes a functional denervation state in affected muscles with upregulation of extrajunctional acetylcholine receptors, and succinylcholine administration can trigger exaggerated potassium efflux and severe hyperkalemia, similar to other denervating conditions. This risk generally develops after the acute onset period and persists for a variable time, making succinylcholine avoidance prudent once the diagnosis is established. Nondepolarizing agents are preferred, though sensitivity to these can also be altered.
  50. The correct answer is B (Perioperative fever and physiologic stress, not general anesthesia itself, are the main concerns for relapse.): general anesthesia has not been shown to reliably cause exacerbation of multiple sclerosis, but perioperative hyperthermia and significant physiologic stress can theoretically worsen conduction in demyelinated fibers and are associated with symptom flares. General anesthesia does not guarantee prevention of relapse. Regional techniques are not absolutely contraindicated, though epidural anesthesia is generally favored over spinal in some discussions due to a theoretical concern with concentrated local anesthetic exposure to demyelinated spinal cord tracts, an association that is not definitively proven.
  51. The correct answer is C (Spinal anesthesia exposes demyelinated central nervous system tracts to higher local anesthetic concentrations.): the theoretical concern is that spinal anesthesia delivers local anesthetic directly into the cerebrospinal fluid bathing the spinal cord, potentially exposing already demyelinated axons to higher drug concentrations than the more distant epidural space allows, which could theoretically worsen neurologic dysfunction. This remains a theoretical rather than definitively proven risk. The other options describe differences unrelated to the specific rationale behind this preference in multiple sclerosis.
  52. The correct answer is B (Progressive respiratory muscle weakness with declining vital capacity.): myasthenic crisis is defined by respiratory or bulbar muscle weakness severe enough to compromise airway protection or ventilation, and a declining vital capacity or negative inspiratory force are objective markers used to identify patients who may need noninvasive support or intubation. Isolated ocular symptoms or fatigue that resolves with rest are consistent with baseline myasthenic fluctuation rather than crisis. Stable respiratory parameters would argue against, not for, an evolving crisis.
  53. The correct answer is B (Use reduced doses of nondepolarizing agents with quantitative neuromuscular monitoring before extubation.): given the heightened sensitivity to nondepolarizing agents in myasthenia gravis, using reduced doses guided by quantitative train-of-four monitoring helps avoid residual paralysis, which is a major contributor to postoperative respiratory failure in this population. Clinical criteria alone are less reliable and can miss significant residual block. Larger neostigmine doses do not guarantee reversal and can paradoxically worsen weakness through a cholinergic mechanism if excessive.
  54. The correct answer is A (Flush the anesthesia machine or use activated charcoal filters and prepare a trigger-free technique with dantrolene available.): known MH susceptible patients require preparation of a trigger-free anesthetic machine, either through prolonged high-flow flushing or activated charcoal filters, along with a total intravenous technique and immediate availability of dantrolene, even in urgent or emergency settings. Prior successful treatment does not confer immunity to future episodes. Delaying emergency surgery indefinitely is not appropriate when preparation can be accomplished promptly, and succinylcholine is also a recognized trigger that must be avoided.
  55. The correct answer is D (Immediately convert to a trigger-free technique and monitor closely for evolving MH signs.): isolated masseter muscle rigidity after succinylcholine can be an early warning sign of malignant hyperthermia, and current practice favors discontinuing triggering agents, converting to total intravenous anesthesia, and closely monitoring end-tidal carbon dioxide, temperature, and creatine kinase even if other classic signs have not yet appeared. Continuing volatile anesthesia risks progression to a full crisis. Repeating succinylcholine would be inappropriate given the concerning presentation, and permanent cancellation is premature without further evaluation.
  56. The correct answer is D (Treated as MH-susceptible using a trigger-free technique given the significant family history.): in the absence of definitive genetic or contracture testing ruling out susceptibility, a first-degree relative with confirmed or strongly suspected malignant hyperthermia is managed as if the patient is also susceptible, using a trigger-free anesthetic with a prepared machine and dantrolene available. There is no safe test dose of succinylcholine or volatile agent to assess susceptibility intraoperatively, and reduced concentrations of volatile agents can still trigger a reaction.
  57. The correct answer is A (Abnormal skeletal muscle chloride channel function causing sustained membrane depolarization after stimulation.): myotonic dystrophy involves abnormal muscle membrane chloride conductance that leads to sustained depolarization and delayed muscle relaxation after voluntary contraction, mechanical stimulation, or even neostigmine administration, producing myotonic contractions that are not reversible with additional neuromuscular blocking agents or anticholinesterases. This differs mechanistically from residual nondepolarizing block, an allergic reaction, or malignant hyperthermia, none of which explain this classic myotonic phenomenon.
  58. The correct answer is B (Cardiac conduction abnormalities and cardiomyopathy requiring careful monitoring.): myotonic dystrophy is a multisystem disease that frequently involves cardiac conduction system disease, arrhythmias, and cardiomyopathy, along with increased sensitivity to sedatives, opioids, and respiratory depressants, and restrictive lung disease from respiratory muscle weakness. These patients are not resistant to sedative or opioid effects, and both cardiac and respiratory function can be significantly impaired rather than normal, requiring vigilant perioperative monitoring.
  59. The correct answer is C (Abrupt discontinuation risks worsening muscle weakness and precipitating myasthenic crisis.): pyridostigmine is an acetylcholinesterase inhibitor that increases acetylcholine availability at the neuromuscular junction, and stopping it abruptly can lead to worsening muscle weakness or myasthenic crisis, so it is generally continued perioperatively when possible. It does have a clinically important effect on strength, contradicting option A, and while it can complicate interpretation of neuromuscular blockade, it does not eliminate the need for intraoperative monitoring.
  60. The correct answer is A (Autonomic dysfunction causing dysregulated and labile cardiovascular reflexes.): Guillain-Barre syndrome commonly involves dysautonomia affecting sympathetic and parasympathetic outflow, producing wide swings between hypertension and tachycardia and hypotension and bradycardia, often triggered by routine stimuli such as suctioning. This autonomic instability is a well-recognized and potentially life-threatening feature of the disease requiring careful hemodynamic management. The other options are less consistent with the classic pattern of stimulus-triggered hemodynamic lability seen in this condition.
  61. The correct answer is D (Not inherently increased risk, since statin myopathy is not a recognized malignant hyperthermia trigger condition.): statin-associated myopathy is a distinct, generally reversible drug-induced muscle condition that is not among the myopathies specifically linked to malignant hyperthermia susceptibility or exertional rhabdomyolysis with volatile agents, unlike RYR1-related myopathies or dystrophinopathies. Not all myopathies confer MH risk, so blanket high-risk classification or mandatory genetic testing is unnecessary here, and specific succinylcholine avoidance protocols for dystrophinopathies do not automatically apply.
  62. The correct answer is B (Adequate preload and systemic vascular resistance with avoidance of tachycardia.): severe aortic stenosis creates a fixed obstruction to left ventricular outflow, so maintaining adequate preload and systemic vascular resistance preserves coronary perfusion pressure and cardiac output, while tachycardia shortens diastolic filling time and worsens subendocardial ischemia. Reducing systemic vascular resistance can precipitate profound hypotension because cardiac output cannot increase to compensate across the fixed valve obstruction. Aggressive diuresis risks reducing preload below what is needed to maintain forward flow in this preload-dependent lesion.
  63. The correct answer is C (Apply continuous positive airway pressure to the nondependent, nonventilated lung.): applying low-level continuous positive airway pressure to the collapsed, nonventilated lung during one-lung ventilation helps recruit some alveoli and improve oxygenation while still permitting adequate surgical exposure, and it is a standard first-line maneuver when hypoxemia develops. Increasing tidal volume on the ventilated lung risks volutrauma and can worsen shunt through hyperinflation-induced changes in pulmonary vascular resistance. Removing positive end-expiratory pressure would likely worsen atelectasis and oxygenation further.
  64. The correct answer is D (Cirrhotic patients have a rebalanced hemostatic system, and conventional tests may not accurately reflect true bleeding risk.): cirrhosis causes concurrent reductions in both procoagulant and anticoagulant factors, producing a rebalanced hemostatic state that can appear coagulopathic by standard tests like INR while functional clotting may be relatively preserved, and these patients can also be prothrombotic in certain contexts. Viscoelastic testing such as thromboelastography often provides more clinically useful information than INR or platelet count alone. Cirrhotic patients are not universally protected from thrombosis despite abnormal standard coagulation tests.
  65. The correct answer is D (Careful assessment of volume status and electrolytes with judicious fluid management given absent renal excretion.): patients with end-stage renal disease lack normal renal mechanisms to handle fluid and electrolyte loads, so careful, judicious fluid management and close monitoring of potassium and volume status are essential, especially when dialysis has been delayed and hyperkalemia is already present. Aggressive crystalloid administration will not meaningfully promote renal potassium clearance in anuric or oliguric patients and can cause volume overload. Potassium-containing fluids should specifically be avoided, and assuming spontaneous normalization is unsafe given the arrhythmia risk from hyperkalemia.
  66.
