The correct answer is C (Hypoxemia, acidosis, hypothermia, and dehydration.): these four factors each promote polymerization of deoxygenated hemoglobin S and increase blood viscosity, precipitating vaso-occlusive sickling crises in patients with sickle cell disease, so perioperative management focuses on maintaining adequate oxygenation, avoiding acidosis, keeping the patient warm, and ensuring adequate hydration. The conditions listed in option C are the actual risk factors to avoid, not the protective goals described in option A, and hypercapnia contributing to acidosis is clinically significant rather than negligible.
  67. The correct answer is C (Initiate alpha blockade first, followed by beta blockade if needed for persistent tachycardia.): alpha blockade must precede beta blockade in pheochromocytoma preparation because beta blockade alone would leave unopposed alpha-mediated vasoconstriction, potentially causing severe hypertensive crisis and pulmonary edema. Once adequate alpha blockade is established over one to two weeks, beta blockade can be added if reflex tachycardia persists. Beta blockade alone is dangerous, and this preparation is not adequately achieved by starting both agents only on the morning of surgery.
  68. The correct answer is B (Short-acting intravenous vasodilators such as sodium nitroprusside or phentolamine.): acute catecholamine surges during tumor manipulation require rapidly titratable, short-acting intravenous agents that can be quickly adjusted as catecholamine release fluctuates during the procedure. Oral or intramuscular agents have onset times and durations that are far too slow and unpredictable for this rapidly changing intraoperative situation. Glucocorticoids have no role in acutely controlling a catecholamine-mediated hypertensive crisis.
  69. The correct answer is B (Decreased anesthetic requirement with prolonged drug effects and slower emergence.): severe hypothyroidism reduces cardiac output, hepatic blood flow, and overall metabolic rate, leading to slower drug clearance, decreased anesthetic requirements, and prolonged effects of sedatives, opioids, and anesthetics with slower emergence. This is the opposite of increased clearance or increased minimum alveolar concentration, and these patients require careful, often reduced dosing with close monitoring rather than assuming no meaningful pharmacologic change.
  70. The correct answer is D (Severe tachycardia, hyperthermia, and altered mental status.): thyroid storm classically presents with marked tachycardia, hyperthermia, altered mental status, and cardiovascular instability due to a surge in circulating thyroid hormone effect, and intraoperative stress or surgical manipulation of the thyroid can precipitate this life-threatening complication. Bradycardia and hypothermia are inconsistent with this hypermetabolic state. Isolated mild hemodynamic changes without the classic triad are less specific and less concerning for thyroid storm.
  71. The correct answer is D (Exaggerated hemodynamic instability and blunted heart rate response to hypotension.): diabetic autonomic neuropathy impairs baroreceptor reflexes and sympathetic compensation, leading to exaggerated blood pressure swings during induction and positional changes, along with a blunted or absent tachycardic response to hypotension that can mask early warning signs. These patients also frequently have delayed gastric emptying from autonomic neuropathy, increasing aspiration risk, which contradicts option C. Baroreceptor function is impaired rather than enhanced in this condition.
  72. The correct answer is C (Chronic, stable anemia is often well tolerated and transfusion decisions should be individualized based on symptoms and comorbidities rather than a fixed hemoglobin threshold.): patients with chronic anemia typically have physiologic compensation such as increased 2,3-diphosphoglycerate and cardiac output adjustments that allow reasonable tolerance of lower hemoglobin levels, so transfusion should be based on individualized assessment of symptoms, cardiac risk, and anticipated blood loss rather than a rigid universal threshold. Ignoring the anemia entirely is inappropriate, and erythropoietin therapy is a recognized and often used treatment in chronic kidney disease anemia, not a contraindicated one.
  73. The correct answer is C (Multimodal analgesia to minimize opioid requirements combined with careful, extended monitoring.): patients with obstructive sleep apnea are highly sensitive to the respiratory depressant effects of opioids and sedatives, so using multimodal analgesia with regional techniques, nonopioid adjuncts, and minimizing opioid doses reduces the risk of postoperative respiratory depression, while extended monitoring helps detect desaturation episodes before they become dangerous. Maximizing opioid dosing directly increases this risk rather than mitigating it, and early discharge without adequate observation is unsafe in a patient demonstrating recurrent desaturation.
  74. The correct answer is D (Increased aspiration risk from retained esophageal contents warrants a rapid sequence induction approach.): achalasia causes impaired esophageal peristalsis and a poorly relaxing lower esophageal sphincter, leading to retained food and secretions in a dilated esophagus that increase the risk of regurgitation and aspiration during induction, so a rapid sequence induction or other aspiration precaution strategy is generally warranted. Standard fasting guidelines may be insufficient because retained material can persist despite typical fasting. Awake fiberoptic intubation is not universally mandatory, though it may be considered in severe cases based on individual assessment.
  75. The correct answer is B (Octreotide, a somatostatin analog that inhibits release of vasoactive mediators.): carcinoid crisis results from release of serotonin, histamine, and other vasoactive substances from the tumor, and octreotide directly inhibits the release of these mediators, making it the mainstay of prophylaxis before manipulation and treatment during a crisis. Epinephrine and other catecholamines can paradoxically worsen a carcinoid crisis by triggering further mediator release from the tumor in some cases and are generally avoided as first-line therapy. Antihistamines and beta agonists may have adjunctive roles but do not address the broader mediator release the way octreotide does.
  76. The correct answer is D (Use plasma and platelet products from male or never-pregnant donors and coordinate closely with the blood bank while monitoring closely for pulmonary symptoms if transfusion is required.): transfusion-related acute lung injury is thought to involve donor antibodies against recipient leukocyte antigens, most concentrated in plasma-rich components; using plasma and platelet products from male or never-pregnant donors (who have a much lower rate of these antibodies), minimizing plasma-rich component exposure when possible, and closely monitoring for recurrent pulmonary symptoms are reasonable strategies when transfusion cannot be avoided. Refusing all blood products could be dangerous if significant hemorrhage occurs and transfusion is truly life-saving. Assuming the prior event was unrelated ignores an important safety history that should inform careful monitoring.
  77. The correct answer is D (Loss of atrial contraction and shortened diastolic filling time critically reduce left ventricular filling across the stenotic valve.): in mitral stenosis, the left atrium contributes a disproportionately important fraction of left ventricular filling because flow across the narrowed valve is already limited, so loss of coordinated atrial contraction combined with the shortened diastolic filling time from a rapid ventricular rate severely reduces cardiac output and can precipitate acute pulmonary edema or hypotension. Atrial fibrillation does not alter valve area, and while pulmonary and right heart effects can occur secondarily, the primary mechanism is impaired left ventricular filling.
  78. The correct answer is A (Perioperative constipation and increased protein or blood breakdown products raising ammonia levels.): common precipitants of hepatic encephalopathy include gastrointestinal bleeding, constipation, increased nitrogenous load from protein breakdown, infection, and electrolyte disturbances, all of which increase ammonia production or reduce its clearance in a cirrhotic patient with impaired hepatic detoxification. While electrolyte disturbances such as hyponatremia can also contribute, isolated hypernatremia is not the classic precipitant, and hypoglycemia or a new psychiatric disorder are far less likely explanations than a recognized encephalopathy trigger in this clinical context.
  79. The correct answer is C (Maintain systemic vascular resistance above pulmonary vascular resistance to preserve coronary perfusion of the right ventricle.): in severe pulmonary hypertension, right ventricular coronary perfusion depends on maintaining an adequate systemic pressure relative to right ventricular pressure, and systemic hypotension can precipitate right ventricular ischemia and acute failure, so vasopressors are often needed to support systemic pressure while pulmonary vascular resistance is optimized. Deliberately inducing hypotension would worsen right ventricular perfusion. Hypercapnia and hypoxemia both increase pulmonary vascular resistance and should be avoided, not induced, in this population.
  80. The correct answer is C (A direct thrombin inhibitor such as bivalirudin in place of heparin for anticoagulation during bypass.): patients with a history of heparin-induced thrombocytopenia, particularly those with detectable antibodies, require an alternative anticoagulant such as a direct thrombin inhibitor for cardiopulmonary bypass to avoid triggering a recurrent immune-mediated thrombotic reaction. Low molecular weight heparin has significant cross-reactivity with heparin-induced thrombocytopenia antibodies and is not a safe substitute. Cardiopulmonary bypass cannot be safely performed without adequate systemic anticoagulation, ruling out the option of using none.
  81. The correct answer is A (Aminoglycoside antibiotics and magnesium sulfate.): both aminoglycoside antibiotics and magnesium sulfate impair neuromuscular transmission by interfering with presynaptic acetylcholine release or postsynaptic receptor function, and they can precipitate significant weakness or even respiratory compromise in patients with myasthenia gravis, so they should be used cautiously or avoided when alternatives exist. Acetaminophen, nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, and topical local anesthetics do not carry this specific risk of worsening neuromuscular transmission in myasthenic patients.
  82. The correct answer is C (Atelectasis and retained secretions leading to pneumonia due to respiratory muscle weakness.): patients with muscular dystrophies affecting respiratory muscles have reduced vital capacity and impaired cough strength, predisposing them to postoperative atelectasis, secretion retention, and pneumonia, particularly after abdominal surgery that further reduces diaphragmatic excursion. While pulmonary embolism and pneumothorax are general postoperative risks in any patient, they are not the risks specifically driven by this patient's underlying muscle weakness, and bronchospasm from reactive airway disease is not a characteristic feature of limb-girdle muscular dystrophy.
  83. The correct answer is A (It requires more time and personnel to reconstitute adequate doses quickly during a crisis.): the older dantrolene formulation requires reconstitution with a relatively large volume of sterile water per vial and multiple vials are often needed to reach a therapeutic dose, which can meaningfully delay treatment during a rapidly evolving malignant hyperthermia crisis compared with newer, more rapidly reconstituted formulations. The active drug itself is equally effective regardless of formulation, it is administered intravenously in both forms, and shelf life is not the primary clinical concern being tested here.
  84. The correct answer is A (Potential for prolonged weakness and altered sensitivity to neuromuscular blocking agents, warranting cautious dosing.): critical illness myopathy, often associated with prolonged neuromuscular blocker use, corticosteroids, and sepsis, can produce significant and prolonged muscle weakness with altered and often increased sensitivity to neuromuscular blocking agents, so cautious dosing with neuromuscular monitoring is important. These patients do not have normal sensitivity to blockers, and they are not resistant to sedatives or analgesics; monitoring remains important rather than unnecessary.
  85. The correct answer is B (Large carbohydrate loads, hypothermia, and stress-induced catecholamine surges that shift potassium intracellularly.): hypokalemic periodic paralysis attacks are precipitated by factors that shift potassium into cells, including glucose and insulin administration, cold exposure, and catecholamine release from surgical stress, so minimizing large carbohydrate loads, maintaining normothermia, and controlling stress responses help prevent an acute paralytic episode. Hyperkalemia is not the trigger in this hypokalemic form, and deliberately inducing acidosis is not an appropriate or standard preventive strategy.
  86. The correct answer is B (Exaggerated sensitivity to nondepolarizing neuromuscular blockers and risk of hyperkalemia with succinylcholine.): amyotrophic lateral sclerosis is a motor neuron disease causing denervation of skeletal muscle with upregulation of extrajunctional acetylcholine receptors, leading to heightened sensitivity to nondepolarizing agents and a risk of severe hyperkalemia with succinylcholine, similar to other denervating conditions. These patients often have significantly reduced respiratory reserve rather than normal function, and they are not resistant to anesthetic agents; aspiration risk is also increased due to bulbar weakness rather than absent.
  87. The correct answer is B (Exertional rhabdomyolysis, a recognized nonanesthetic manifestation associated with RYR1 mutations.): patients with RYR1 mutations can experience exertional or heat-related rhabdomyolysis independent of anesthetic exposure, since the same abnormal calcium handling in skeletal muscle that predisposes to malignant hyperthermia can also be triggered by exercise or heat stress. Because a trigger-free technique was used and the timing is several days after surgery with a clear exertional trigger, a delayed anesthetic-related malignant hyperthermia reaction is far less likely. This presentation is not a normal recovery process and dark urine with cramps points away from a simple urinary tract infection.
  88. The correct answer is A (Increased contractility, decreased preload, and decreased afterload.): in hypertrophic cardiomyopathy with dynamic outflow obstruction, increased contractility narrows the left ventricular cavity further, decreased preload reduces the volume that normally helps keep the outflow tract open, and decreased afterload increases the pressure gradient favoring obstruction, all of which worsen the dynamic obstruction and can precipitate severe hypotension. The opposite changes, decreased contractility with increased preload and afterload as in option B, are actually the goals of management to reduce obstruction in this condition.
  89. The correct answer is A (Hypokalemia, hypomagnesemia, and exposure to QT-prolonging medications during the perioperative period.): these factors further prolong the QT interval and increase the risk of torsades de pointes in patients with congenital long QT syndrome, so perioperative management focuses on maintaining normal potassium and magnesium levels and avoiding medications known to prolong the QT interval. Hyperkalemia and hypermagnesemia are not the concerning direction of electrolyte imbalance in this condition, and bradycardia, rather than being protective, can actually predispose to torsades in certain long QT subtypes and is not deliberately induced.
  90. The correct answer is D (Deepen anesthesia with a volatile agent and administer a bronchodilator such as albuterol.): intraoperative bronchospasm is treated by deepening anesthesia, since volatile agents have direct bronchodilating properties, along with administering a beta-2 agonist bronchodilator to relax airway smooth muscle, while ensuring adequate oxygenation and ruling out mechanical causes such as tube malposition. Immediate extubation is not the first step and could worsen the situation if the airway is not secure. Neuromuscular blockade alone does not address bronchospasm, and increasing positive end-expiratory pressure without treating the underlying bronchospasm and airway inflammation is not sufficient management.
  91. The correct answer is B (A larynx positioned more cephalad at the level of C3 to C4 vertebrae.): the neonatal larynx sits higher and more anterior than the adult larynx, which is typically at C4 to C6. This higher position, combined with a large occiput and floppy omega-shaped epiglottis, alters the laryngoscopic view. The cricoid ring, not the vocal cords, is the narrowest fixed portion of the neonatal airway. The neonatal trachea is proportionally shorter, not longer, increasing the risk of endobronchial intubation.
  92. The correct answer is A (Immature hepatic enzyme systems that slow clearance of many anesthetic drugs.): neonatal cytochrome P450 and conjugation pathways are immature at birth, prolonging the elimination half-life of many hepatically metabolized drugs. Neonates actually have a larger, not smaller, total body water compartment relative to weight. Plasma protein binding is decreased rather than increased in neonates, raising free drug fraction. Renal clearance is also reduced in neonates due to low glomerular filtration rate.
  93. The correct answer is C (Hypochloremic hypokalemic metabolic alkalosis from prolonged vomiting of gastric contents.): repeated vomiting of hydrochloric acid-rich gastric contents causes loss of chloride and hydrogen ions, producing a hypochloremic metabolic alkalosis with renal potassium wasting as the kidney compensates. This is the classic electrolyte pattern of pyloric stenosis, not a hyperchloremic or anion gap acidosis. Respiratory alkalosis is not the primary process, since the underlying disturbance originates from gastrointestinal fluid and acid loss rather than hyperventilation.
  94. The correct answer is D (Delay surgery and correct fluid and electrolyte abnormalities first.): pyloric stenosis is a medical, not surgical, emergency, and correction of dehydration, hypochloremia, and metabolic alkalosis before induction reduces the risk of postoperative apnea and anesthetic complications. Proceeding immediately risks arrhythmia and prolonged apnea from uncorrected alkalosis. Choice of induction agent does not substitute for metabolic correction. Awake intubation is not the standard approach and does not address the underlying electrolyte disturbance.
  95. The correct answer is A (Avoid increases in intrathoracic pressure and maintain adequate preload.): in Fontan physiology, pulmonary blood flow is passive and depends on a favorable pressure gradient from the systemic veins to the pulmonary arteries, so elevated intrathoracic pressure and hypovolemia both impair flow. Aggressive positive pressure ventilation raises intrathoracic pressure and reduces venous return rather than improving it. Extreme hyperventilation is unnecessary since baseline pulmonary vascular resistance is usually already low after successful Fontan palliation. Excessive tachycardia shortens diastolic filling time and can worsen ventricular filling.
  96. The correct answer is B (Give a fluid bolus and phenylephrine to increase systemic vascular resistance.): a tet spell results from dynamic right ventricular outflow tract obstruction and increased right-to-left shunting, so raising systemic vascular resistance with fluid and a pure alpha agonist reduces the shunt and improves pulmonary blood flow. Nitric oxide targets pulmonary vasodilation, which does not address the dynamic infundibular spasm driving the spell. Deepening the anesthetic without addressing vascular resistance may worsen hypotension. Beta agonists can worsen infundibular spasm and are avoided in this setting.
  97. The correct answer is A (Chronic hypoxemia increases the density of central mu-opioid receptors.): repeated nocturnal hypoxemia in severe OSA upregulates central mu-opioid receptor sensitivity, so these children experience greater respiratory depression from lower opioid doses. Hepatic metabolism is not the primary mechanism responsible for this heightened sensitivity. Polycythemia can occur with chronic hypoxemia but does not itself explain increased opioid potency. Chronic hypercapnia does not directly increase cerebrospinal fluid opioid penetration in this manner.
  98. The correct answer is D (Rapid sequence induction with cricoid pressure and full stomach precautions.): a child with post-tonsillectomy hemorrhage has swallowed significant blood, is hypovolemic, and must be treated as having a full stomach, so rapid sequence induction with cricoid pressure after adequate volume resuscitation and two large-bore intravenous lines is standard. Awake fiberoptic intubation is impractical and poorly tolerated in an anxious, uncooperative child. Inhalational induction risks aspiration and laryngospasm in a bleeding, full-stomach patient. Delaying surgery to empty the stomach is not feasible or safe with active hemorrhage.
  99. The correct answer is A (Administration of intravenous dexmedetomidine at a low sedative dose.): low-dose dexmedetomidine is the treatment of choice for emergence delirium after sevoflurane anesthesia, providing sedation without significant respiratory depression while resolving agitation. Naloxone is reserved for suspected opioid overdose, which does not fit this stable, well-analgesed child. Flumazenil is indicated for benzodiazepine reversal, which is not relevant here. Physostigmine is used for anticholinergic toxidrome, not typical emergence delirium.
  100. The correct answer is B (Rising end-tidal carbon dioxide with tachycardia and masseter rigidity.): the combination of unexplained hypercarbia, tachycardia, and masseter rigidity is concerning for evolving malignant hyperthermia rather than a benign, isolated masseter spasm, and should prompt discontinuation of triggering agents and preparation for dantrolene. Brief, self-limited trismus without other systemic signs is more consistent with isolated masseter rigidity. A family history of difficult intravenous access is unrelated to malignant hyperthermia risk. Isolated extremity myoclonus without hypermetabolic signs does not indicate malignant hyperthermia.
  101. The correct answer is D (Immediate administration of intravenous dantrolene at the recommended dose.): dantrolene interferes with calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum in skeletal muscle and is the definitive first-line treatment for malignant hyperthermia, given promptly at the recommended weight-based dose. Calcium chloride is not indicated as first-line therapy and could worsen the hypermetabolic process. Procainamide is not part of standard malignant hyperthermia treatment protocols. Sodium bicarbonate may be used adjunctively for severe acidosis but does not treat the underlying pathophysiology.
  102. The correct answer is C (Spontaneous ventilation maintained during induction with a fiberoptic backup plan.): preserving spontaneous ventilation during a slow inhalational or titrated induction, with backup fiberoptic or videolaryngoscopic equipment ready, allows the anesthesiologist to secure this anticipated difficult airway while maintaining the ability to rescue with mask ventilation. Rapid sequence induction removes the safety margin of spontaneous ventilation in a known difficult airway. Deep extubation is an emergence, not induction, consideration and is generally avoided in difficult airway patients. Awake nasal intubation without sedation is poorly tolerated and impractical in an infant.
  103. The correct answer is A (Malar and mandibular hypoplasia with a hypoplastic temporomandibular joint.): Treacher Collins syndrome is characterized by bilateral malar and mandibular hypoplasia along with a small, malformed temporomandibular joint, which together limit mouth opening and displace the tongue posteriorly, making both mask ventilation and laryngoscopy difficult. Macroglossia without bony craniofacial changes is not the defining feature of this syndrome. Cervical spine instability is characteristic of conditions such as Down syndrome, not Treacher Collins syndrome. Choanal atresia is associated with other syndromes and is not a typical feature here.
  104. The correct answer is D (Initiate positive pressure ventilation with room air or blended oxygen.): a heart rate below 100 beats per minute after initial stimulation indicates the need for positive pressure ventilation as the next resuscitative step, since effective ventilation is the single most important intervention in neonatal resuscitation. Chest compressions are reserved for a heart rate below 60 beats per minute despite adequate ventilation. Epinephrine is indicated only after ventilation and compressions fail to raise the heart rate. Intubation is not required before a trial of effective bag-mask ventilation.
  105. The correct answer is B (Begin chest compressions coordinated with ventilation at a 3:1 ratio.): once effective ventilation has been confirmed and the heart rate remains below 60 beats per minute, chest compressions should begin immediately, coordinated with ventilation using a 3:1 compression-to-ventilation ratio. Continuing ventilation alone without compressions is inadequate for a heart rate this low. Atropine is not used in neonatal resuscitation for bradycardia of this etiology. Delaying further intervention risks worsening hypoxic injury.
  106. The correct answer is C (Admit for apnea monitoring for at least 12 hours after anesthesia.): former premature infants remain at increased risk for postoperative apnea until roughly 50 to 60 weeks postconceptional age, so admission with continuous apnea and bradycardia monitoring for at least 12 hours after anesthesia is recommended regardless of an uneventful clinical course. Early discharge after only 2 hours does not provide adequate observation for this risk period. Standard recovery room monitoring alone is insufficient for this high-risk population. Sending the infant home with only a monitor and no clinical observation does not meet accepted safety standards.
  107. The correct answer is A (62 mL per hour based on the standard 4-2-1 calculation.): using the 4-2-1 rule, the first 10 kg is calculated at 4 mL/kg/hr (40 mL), the second 10 kg at 2 mL/kg/hr (20 mL), and the remaining 2 kg at 1 mL/kg/hr (2 mL), yielding a total of 62 mL per hour. The other listed values do not correctly apply the tiered weight-based formula to this child's weight. This calculation is a fundamental component of pediatric perioperative fluid management. Errors in this calculation commonly arise from applying a single flat rate per kilogram rather than the tiered system.
  108. The correct answer is C (Replace half the deficit in the first hour and the remainder over the next two hours.): the traditional approach to intraoperative fluid deficit replacement gives half of the calculated deficit in the first hour, with the remaining half divided over the subsequent two hours, in addition to ongoing maintenance fluids. Giving the entire deficit as a single rapid bolus risks volume overload in a young child. Ignoring the deficit fails to address preoperative dehydration from prolonged fasting. Waiting for hypotension to develop before treating the deficit is reactive rather than the accepted proactive approach.
  109. The correct answer is D (Meticulously remove all air bubbles from the intravenous tubing.): with a right-to-left shunt, any air introduced into the venous circulation can bypass the pulmonary filter and reach the systemic circulation directly, causing a paradoxical air embolism to the brain or coronary arteries, so scrupulous air-bubble removal is essential. Supplemental oxygen and hyperventilation can be used adjunctively but do not eliminate the embolic risk from unfiltered air. Lowering systemic vascular resistance below pulmonary vascular resistance would worsen right-to-left shunting rather than protect the patient. These maneuvers do not substitute for meticulous line management.
  110. The correct answer is B (Start a prostaglandin E1 infusion to maintain ductal patency.): in ductal-dependent lesions such as severe coarctation, prostaglandin E1 maintains or reopens the ductus arteriosus, restoring blood flow to the descending aorta and improving lower body and renal perfusion. Indomethacin has the opposite effect, promoting ductal closure, and would worsen this patient's condition. Furosemide addresses volume overload but does not restore ductal patency. Dopamine may support blood pressure but does not correct the underlying ductal-dependent circulation.
  111. The correct answer is A (Placing a folded towel or roll under the shoulders.): a shoulder roll compensates for the infant's proportionally large occiput, bringing the head into a neutral or slightly extended position that aligns the oral, pharyngeal, and laryngeal axes for laryngoscopy. Extreme neck extension without shoulder support can paradoxically worsen the view in infants. Placing a pillow under the occiput would further exaggerate neck flexion rather than correct it. The classic adult sniffing position, which uses head elevation, is not directly applicable given the infant's different cranial proportions.
  112. The correct answer is A (A higher ratio of alveolar ventilation to functional residual capacity.): young children have a much higher alveolar ventilation relative to functional residual capacity than adults, which speeds the rise of alveolar anesthetic partial pressure and accelerates induction. Cardiac output relative to body weight is actually higher, not lower, in young children, which also contributes to rapid uptake. Functional residual capacity relative to body weight is smaller, not larger, in this age group. Tissue solubility differences are not the primary driver of the faster induction seen in pediatric patients.
  113. The correct answer is D (Administer a small dose of intravenous succinylcholine promptly.): when basic maneuvers such as jaw thrust and positive pressure oxygen fail to break laryngospasm, a small dose of intravenous succinylcholine rapidly relaxes the laryngeal musculature and restores ventilation. Chest compressions are not indicated for isolated laryngospasm without cardiac arrest. Racemic epinephrine treats postextubation stridor from airway edema, not acute vocal cord adduction. The patient in this scenario is already extubated, and simple observation risks progression to hypoxic bradycardia and arrest.
  114. Prolonged neuromuscular blockade is not the primary or defining anesthetic risk associated with this myopathy. Local anesthetic systemic toxicity risk is not specifically increased by central core disease. Refractory bradycardia is not a characteristic feature of this condition.

  116. Delaying volume resuscitation risks hemodynamic collapse during induction. Antiemetics are appropriate and do not meaningfully mask ongoing surgical bleeding.   117. The correct answer is B (Preschool age combined with otolaryngologic or ophthalmologic surgery.): emergence delirium after sevoflurane anesthesia is most strongly associated with young preschool age and certain surgical types, particularly ear, nose, throat, and eye procedures. Effective regional analgesia with a caudal block is generally protective, reducing the incidence of emergence delirium rather than increasing it. Intraoperative dexmedetomidine also reduces the incidence of emergence delirium rather than causing it. Very long surgical duration has not been established as the primary or strongest risk factor compared with age and surgical type.
  118. Epinephrine is reserved for a persistently low heart rate despite ventilation and compressions, which is not present here. Chest compressions are not indicated with a heart rate of 130. Routine tracheal suctioning for meconium is no longer recommended for a vigorous infant and is not indicated by the findings described.
  119. The correct answer is C (Place a laryngeal mask airway as a ventilation and intubation conduit.): with adequate mask ventilation but failed videolaryngoscopy, placing a laryngeal mask airway maintains oxygenation and can serve as a conduit for fiberoptic-guided intubation, consistent with the difficult airway algorithm. Emergent surgical airway is reserved for a true cannot-intubate, cannot-ventilate scenario, which has not occurred here since ventilation remains adequate. Simply repeating direct laryngoscopy with a different blade after failed videolaryngoscopy is less likely to succeed and does not follow the escalation strategy. Additional paralytic without a change in airway strategy does not address the underlying anatomic difficulty.
  120. The correct answer is A (Lower plasma concentrations of alpha-1-acid glycoprotein and albumin increasing free fraction.): neonates have reduced plasma protein concentrations, including alpha-1-acid glycoprotein and albumin, which increases the unbound, pharmacologically active fraction of highly protein-bound local anesthetics and raises the risk of systemic toxicity at a given total dose. Hepatic blood flow does not accelerate clearance enough to offset this increased free fraction, and hepatic enzyme systems are actually immature in neonates. Neonates have lower, not higher, body fat percentage relative to older infants. Renal excretion of local anesthetics is reduced, not accelerated, in neonates.
  121. The correct answer is B (Minimizing the preoperative fasting period and maintaining adequate hydration.): children with chronic cyanotic heart disease develop compensatory polycythemia, and dehydration from prolonged fasting can further increase blood viscosity and thrombotic risk, so minimizing NPO time and ensuring hydration is the key perioperative measure. Aggressive preoperative phlebotomy is not routinely performed and can cause hemodynamic instability without addressing the underlying hypoxemia. Routine therapeutic-dose anticoagulation is not standard prophylaxis for elective noncardiac surgery in this population. Withholding intravenous fluids would worsen rather than prevent dehydration-related hyperviscosity.
  122. The correct answer is D (Decompress the stomach fully, remove the tube, then preoxygenate before rapid sequence induction.): thorough gastric decompression in multiple positions reduces the volume of retained gastric contents, after which the tube is removed and the infant is preoxygenated before a rapid sequence induction with cricoid pressure to manage the full stomach risk inherent to pyloric stenosis. Proceeding directly to inhalational induction bypasses important aspiration precautions in this full-stomach patient. Removing the tube before decompression defeats the purpose of gastric emptying. Administering additional oral contrast would increase gastric volume and aspiration risk rather than reduce it.
  123. The correct answer is A (Active cooling with cold intravenous fluids, ice packs, and cooling blankets.): active external and internal cooling measures are an important adjunct to dantrolene in managing the severe hyperthermia of a malignant hyperthermia crisis and should continue until core temperature approaches normal. Aspirin and other antipyretics act on the hypothalamic set point and are ineffective against the hypermetabolic hyperthermia of malignant hyperthermia. Warming the room would worsen rather than help this hyperthermic emergency. Dantrolene should be continued and often redosed, not discontinued, until the crisis resolves.
  124. The correct answer is C (Mandibular growth over the first year of life often improves airway obstruction.): many infants with Pierre Robin sequence experience relative mandibular catch-up growth during the first year of life, which often improves glossoptosis and airway obstruction, sometimes reducing but not always eliminating anesthetic airway difficulty. The condition does not typically worsen progressively in most affected children. Airway difficulty is not universally constant, since many children do show improvement without surgical intervention. Cleft palate repair addresses feeding and speech concerns but does not by itself resolve the underlying mandibular airway obstruction.
  125. The correct answer is D (Administer intravenous epinephrine per the resuscitation algorithm now.): when the heart rate remains below 60 beats per minute despite adequate ventilation and 60 seconds of coordinated chest compressions, epinephrine should be administered, preferably through umbilical venous access, following the neonatal resuscitation algorithm. Simply continuing unchanged efforts without escalation delays a necessary intervention. Calcium is not part of routine neonatal resuscitation for this indication. Needle decompression is reserved for suspected pneumothorax, which is not indicated by the information given.
  126. The correct answer is B (Calculate the maximum allowable blood loss and transfuse once losses approach that threshold.): the maximum allowable blood loss is derived from the estimated blood volume and the difference between the starting and minimum acceptable hematocrit, guiding the anesthesiologist on when ongoing surgical blood loss approaches the point where transfusion becomes necessary. Urine output reflects renal perfusion but is not the primary trigger used for this calculation. Transfusing a fixed volume regardless of actual blood loss does not account for individual patient physiology. Waiting until hemoglobin falls to an extremely low level ignores the proactive planning that maximum allowable blood loss calculations provide.
  127. The correct answer is A (Properly sized cuffed tubes are safe and reduce the need for tube exchanges.): current evidence supports the safe use of appropriately sized cuffed endotracheal tubes in infants and young children, reducing the incidence of tube exchanges due to poor fit or excessive leak. Cuffed tubes are not contraindicated in children under 8 years when used with an appropriate size and monitored cuff pressure. Cuff pressure must be actively monitored and kept below a safe threshold to avoid tracheal mucosal injury. Uncuffed tubes remain an acceptable option but are not universally required, since cuffed tubes are also an appropriate choice.
  128. The correct answer is D (Active upper respiratory tract infection with airway irritability.): a recent or active upper respiratory infection increases airway hyperreactivity and significantly raises the risk of perioperative laryngospasm and bronchospasm, particularly during airway instrumentation. Age alone, without the presence of active respiratory illness, is not the primary driver of increased risk in this scenario. A normal temperature does not eliminate the airway irritability caused by an active viral upper respiratory infection. Absence of wheezing is reassuring but does not negate the increased reactivity associated with the recent infection.
  129. The correct answer is C (Ventricular arrhythmias related to right ventricular scarring and volume overload.): patients with repaired tetralogy of Fallot often have residual pulmonary regurgitation causing right ventricular volume overload and surgical scarring, both of which predispose to ventricular arrhythmias that require vigilant perioperative monitoring. Coarctation and its associated hypertension are not typical features of tetralogy of Fallot. Left-to-right shunting through a patent ductus arteriosus is not the expected residual lesion after tetralogy repair. Persistent right-to-left shunting is unlikely after successful surgical repair of the outflow tract obstruction.
  130. The correct answer is B (Reduced glomerular filtration rate compared with older infants.): glomerular filtration rate is significantly reduced in neonates compared with older infants and matures over the first several months of life, requiring extended dosing intervals for renally cleared medications to avoid drug accumulation and toxicity. Hepatic first-pass metabolism is generally reduced, not increased, in neonates. Plasma protein binding is decreased rather than increased in this age group. Increased total body water affects the volume of distribution but does not directly explain the need for extended dosing intervals for a renally cleared drug.
  131. The correct answer is A (Administration of a single intraoperative dose of dexamethasone.): a single intraoperative dose of dexamethasone reduces postoperative nausea and vomiting and decreases upper airway edema after adenotonsillectomy, making it a standard component of the anesthetic plan for this procedure. Ketorolac is sometimes used for analgesia but is not specifically established for reducing airway edema, and its bleeding risk is a topic of ongoing debate in this surgery. Promethazine carries sedation and extrapyramidal side effect concerns that make it a less favored choice in young children. Oral acetaminophen alone provides analgesia but does not address nausea or airway edema.
  132. The correct answer is C (Sloughing of the eschar overlying the tonsillar bed as it heals.): secondary post-tonsillectomy hemorrhage typically occurs between 5 and 10 days after surgery and is most often related to sloughing of the fibrinous eschar covering the healing tonsillar bed rather than an acute surgical or technical issue. Intraoperative technique alone is more directly associated with primary hemorrhage occurring within the first 24 hours. Transient postoperative hypertension is not the primary mechanism for delayed bleeding of this type. An undiagnosed lifelong coagulopathy would be expected to present with earlier or more diffuse bleeding rather than isolated delayed tonsillar bed bleeding.
  133. The correct answer is D (Markedly elevated creatine kinase with hyperkalemia and mixed acidosis.): the combination of markedly elevated creatine kinase from rhabdomyolysis, hyperkalemia from muscle breakdown, and a mixed respiratory and metabolic acidosis is the classic laboratory signature of malignant hyperthermia. Isolated mild hyperglycemia alone is nonspecific and can occur with many stress responses. A normal creatine kinase with fever alone is not consistent with the muscle breakdown seen in malignant hyperthermia. Isolated hyponatremia without other findings does not reflect the hypermetabolic process characteristic of this condition.
  134. The correct answer is B (Fiberoptic intubation through a laryngeal mask airway conduit under spontaneous ventilation.): using a laryngeal mask airway as a conduit for fiberoptic-guided intubation while preserving spontaneous ventilation is a well-established, safe technique for infants with an anticipated difficult airway from craniofacial anomalies like Treacher Collins syndrome. Standard rapid sequence induction removes the safety net of spontaneous ventilation in a known difficult airway. Blind nasal intubation without direct or fiberoptic visualization carries a high risk of trauma and failure in this population. Retrograde wire-guided intubation is an invasive rescue technique, not a routine first-line approach in infants.
  135. The correct answer is A (Nonshivering thermogenesis through metabolism of brown adipose tissue.): neonates generate heat primarily through nonshivering thermogenesis by metabolizing brown adipose tissue, since they lack the developed skeletal muscle mass and coordination needed for effective shivering. Skeletal muscle shivering is the mechanism used by older children and adults, not neonates. Peripheral vasodilation would increase, not decrease, heat loss and is not a heat-generating mechanism. Neonates have limited sweating capacity and this is not a mechanism for generating heat.
  136. The correct answer is D (Limited hepatic glycogen stores predisposing this neonate to hypoglycemia.): neonates have limited hepatic glycogen reserves and immature gluconeogenic pathways, making them prone to hypoglycemia during prolonged fasting or surgical stress, so dextrose-containing maintenance fluids are often used. Hepatic gluconeogenesis capacity is actually reduced, not increased, in neonates. Glycogen stores are smaller, not larger, relative to body weight compared with older children. Insulin sensitivity is not the primary factor driving the need for dextrose supplementation in this age group.
  137. The correct answer is C (Unrepaired cyanotic congenital heart disease with chronic hypoxemia.): current guidelines reserve endocarditis prophylaxis for high-risk cardiac conditions, including unrepaired cyanotic congenital heart disease, because these patients face the highest risk of adverse outcomes from infective endocarditis. Prophylaxis is not recommended for all congenital heart disease regardless of type or repair status, since most lesions carry only baseline risk. An innocent murmur without underlying structural disease does not warrant prophylaxis. A completely repaired atrial septal defect without residual shunt is not considered a high-risk lesion requiring prophylaxis.
  138. The correct answer is B (Coexisting anemia in addition to a history of prematurity.): anemia is an independent risk factor for postoperative apnea in former premature infants, and its presence alongside a history of prematurity further increases this risk even at a postconceptional age where risk is otherwise declining. Postconceptional age alone, even beyond 44 weeks, does not eliminate risk, particularly when other risk factors such as anemia coexist. Use of regional techniques such as a caudal block with minimal or no sedation may reduce, rather than increase, apnea risk compared with general anesthesia. A short procedure duration does not by itself remove the apnea risk associated with prematurity and anemia.
  139. The correct answer is A (Use a validated behavioral scale assessing eye contact, awareness, and consolability.): validated pediatric behavioral scales assess features such as eye contact, purposeful movement, and consolability, helping distinguish emergence delirium, which is characterized by lack of awareness and inconsolability, from pain, which is often more consolable with comfort measures and analgesia. Assuming all agitation represents pain risks unnecessary opioid administration in a child with true emergence delirium. Empiric naloxone is inappropriate and could reverse needed analgesia without addressing the actual cause of agitation. Withholding all treatment prolongs distress and delays appropriate management for either condition.
  140. The correct answer is C (Total intravenous anesthesia with a vapor-free machine and prophylactic dantrolene availability.): a patient with a prior malignant hyperthermia episode requires a nontriggering anesthetic technique using total intravenous anesthesia, a vapor-free or thoroughly prepared anesthesia machine, and immediate availability of dantrolene should symptoms recur. Re-exposure to any volatile agent, even at a lower dose, risks triggering another episode. Succinylcholine is also a known triggering agent and must be avoided along with volatile anesthetics. Regional anesthesia is not contraindicated and can be a useful component of a nontriggering anesthetic plan when appropriate for the procedure.
  141. The correct answer is A (Place the infant in the knee-chest position and administer 100% oxygen.): a tet spell results from dynamic infundibular spasm that increases right-to-left shunting, and knee-chest positioning raises systemic vascular resistance while reducing venous return, favoring pulmonary flow. Oxygen is supportive but positioning is the immediate first maneuver. Increasing contractility with an inotrope such as dobutamine can worsen infundibular spasm and is avoided. Diuretics and pulmonary vasodilators do not address the underlying dynamic obstruction.
  142. The correct answer is C (Prostaglandin E1 to maintain ductal patency.): in transposition of the great arteries the pulmonary and systemic circulations run in parallel, so survival depends on mixing at the atrial, ductal, or ventricular level, and prostaglandin E1 keeps the ductus arteriosus open to provide that mixing until septostomy or surgical repair. Dopamine and milrinone do not create intracardiac mixing and will not correct the underlying physiology. Furosemide is inappropriate because reducing shunting further would worsen hypoxemia in this ductal-dependent lesion.
  143. The correct answer is B (Insert a laryngeal mask airway to establish a patent airway and oxygenation.): when mask ventilation and direct laryngoscopy fail in an infant with micrognathia and glossoptosis, a supraglottic device bypasses the obstructing tongue base and restores oxygenation, and it can also serve as a conduit for fiberoptic intubation. Repeating the same failed technique wastes time without a management change. Blind nasotracheal intubation risks trauma and failure in a distorted airway. Surgical cricothyrotomy is a last resort reserved for a true cannot-intubate-cannot-oxygenate emergency, which should first be addressed with a supraglottic rescue device.
  144. The correct answer is D (Maintain spontaneous ventilation during induction with a difficult airway cart available.): Treacher Collins syndrome is strongly associated with severe difficult mask ventilation and intubation from mandibular and malar hypoplasia, so preserving spontaneous ventilation until the airway is secured avoids losing the ability to oxygenate if intubation fails. Administering a muscle relaxant before confirming the airway can be managed removes a critical safety margin. Rapid sequence induction and blind nasotracheal intubation both assume an airway that can be quickly secured, which is unreliable in this population.
  145. The correct answer is B (Begin chest compressions coordinated with ventilation in a 3:1 ratio.): once effective ventilation has been confirmed and the heart rate remains below 60 beats per minute after 30 seconds, neonatal resuscitation guidelines call for starting chest compressions coordinated with ventilation at a 3:1 ratio while increasing the oxygen concentration. Continuing ventilation alone without escalation delays needed circulatory support. Epinephrine is reserved for a heart rate that remains below 60 despite adequate ventilation and compressions, not as an immediate substitute for compressions. Reducing oxygen concentration is not indicated when the heart rate is inadequate.
  146. The correct answer is A (Admit for apnea and bradycardia monitoring for at least 12 to 24 hours postoperatively.): infants born prematurely remain at increased risk of postoperative apnea until roughly 60 weeks postconceptional age, and an infant at 46 weeks postconceptional age warrants overnight apnea monitoring regardless of anesthetic technique. Even spinal anesthesia does not eliminate this risk sufficiently to avoid monitoring. A brief recovery room observation period is inadequate for this risk profile, and same-day discharge is not appropriate at this postconceptional age.
  147. The correct answer is D (Prostaglandin E1 infusion to reopen the ductus arteriosus.): critical coarctation is ductal-dependent for systemic perfusion distal to the obstruction, and a closing ductus explains the differential pulses and shock, so prostaglandin E1 reopens the ductus and restores lower body perfusion before definitive repair. Dopamine alone cannot bypass a fixed mechanical obstruction proximal to the ductus. Diuresis and fluid restriction would worsen perfusion in a shocked infant. Proceeding directly to surgery without hemodynamic stabilization increases perioperative mortality.
  148. The correct answer is C (Allowing mild hypercapnia and avoiding supplemental oxygen to balance Qp:Qs.): in single-ventricle physiology like hypoplastic left heart syndrome, systemic and pulmonary circulations compete for a single ventricular output, and a high saturation with poor perfusion signals excessive pulmonary blood flow at the expense of the systemic circulation. Permissive hypercapnia and room air raise pulmonary vascular resistance to rebalance flow toward the systemic circuit. Increasing oxygen, hyperventilating, or giving a pulmonary vasodilator would each further lower pulmonary vascular resistance and worsen systemic steal.
  149. The correct answer is B (Immediate awake intubation avoiding mask ventilation, with permissive hypercapnia.): mask ventilation in congenital diaphragmatic hernia can insufflate the herniated bowel and further compress the hypoplastic lungs, so the airway should be secured promptly by intubation while avoiding bag-mask ventilation, and permissive hypercapnia with gentle ventilation limits ventilator-induced lung injury in hypoplastic lungs. High tidal volume ventilation risks barotrauma and pneumothorax. Aggressive hyperventilation was historically used but is now avoided because it does not improve outcomes and can cause lung injury. Nasal CPAP alone does not protect against gastric and bowel distension in this condition.
  150. The correct answer is B (Asymmetric mandibular hypoplasia distorting airway anatomy on the affected side.): Goldenhar syndrome characteristically produces unilateral hemifacial microsomia with mandibular hypoplasia, which distorts the airway asymmetrically and predicts difficult mask ventilation and laryngoscopy, often requiring a difficult airway plan and awake or video-assisted techniques. Cardiac, renal, and neurologic associations may coexist but do not directly determine airway difficulty the way the facial skeletal anomaly does. The airway assessment should focus specifically on the mandibular and craniofacial deformity.
  151. The correct answer is C (Congenital complete heart block from transplacental maternal antibody-mediated injury.): maternal anti-Ro/SSA and anti-La/SSB antibodies cross the placenta and can damage the fetal cardiac conduction system, producing congenital complete heart block that presents as a fixed, regular bradycardia unresponsive to normal physiologic stimuli. Vagally mediated bradycardia would not persist as a fixed fetal finding on serial echocardiography. Hypoglycemia does not typically cause a fixed regular bradycardic rhythm. Supraventricular tachycardia produces a fast, not slow, heart rate.
  152. The correct answer is C (Left-to-right shunting causing pulmonary overcirculation and congestive heart failure.): as pulmonary vascular resistance falls after birth, a large unrestrictive VSD allows substantial left-to-right shunting, producing pulmonary overcirculation, congestive heart failure symptoms such as tachypnea, poor feeding, and hepatomegaly, and failure to thrive by three months of age. Right-to-left shunting would cause cyanosis rather than this presentation. Eisenmenger physiology from irreversible pulmonary vascular disease typically develops over years, not by three months. Outflow obstruction would produce a different clinical picture without this degree of overcirculation.
  153. The correct answer is B (Apnea requiring readiness for positive pressure ventilation or intubation.): prostaglandin E1 commonly causes apnea, particularly in neonates, so any infant started on this infusion for ductal maintenance should be closely monitored with airway equipment and ventilatory support readily available during transport. Hypertension and hyperglycemia are not typical prostaglandin effects. Bradycardia requiring pacing is not a characteristic adverse effect of this medication, though fever and flushing can occur.
  154. The correct answer is A (Begin positive pressure ventilation promptly without routine tracheal suctioning.): current neonatal resuscitation guidelines no longer recommend routine intubation and tracheal suctioning for a non-vigorous meconium-stained infant, since evidence shows this delays effective ventilation without improving outcomes, so positive pressure ventilation should begin promptly if the infant is not breathing effectively. Delaying ventilation to fully clear meconium first worsens hypoxia and is not supported. Surfactant administration is not part of initial delivery room resuscitation for this scenario.
  155. The correct answer is B (Correct the electrolyte and acid-base abnormalities before surgery, as it is not an emergency.): pyloric stenosis produces a hypochloremic, hypokalemic metabolic alkalosis from repeated vomiting of gastric contents, and pyloromyotomy is a medical, not surgical, emergency, so fluid and electrolyte correction should occur first to reduce the risk of postoperative apnea and arrhythmia. Rapid intravenous potassium boluses risk dangerous hyperkalemia and arrhythmia. Administering bicarbonate would worsen the existing alkalosis rather than correct it.
  156. The correct answer is C (Maintain spontaneous ventilation and position the endotracheal tube tip beyond the fistula.): positive pressure ventilation in tracheoesophageal fistula can preferentially inflate the stomach through the fistula, causing gastric distension that impairs ventilation and risks perforation, so spontaneous ventilation is preserved during induction and the tube is positioned to occlude or bypass the fistula. Maximizing positive pressure worsens gastric insufflation. A nasogastric tube cannot fully decompress a stomach being actively filled through a fistula during ventilation, so airway management should not be delayed for this alone. Full cuff inflation does not reliably prevent gas entry into a fistula located proximal to the tube tip if not positioned correctly.
  157. The correct answer is A (Macroglossia and a smaller subglottic diameter increase the risk of airway obstruction and require a smaller endotracheal tube.): children with Down syndrome commonly have relative macroglossia, midface hypoplasia, and a narrower subglottic airway, so a smaller than age-predicted endotracheal tube should be available and obstruction risk anticipated. Routine cervical spine imaging in asymptomatic children is not currently recommended by most guidelines. Atlantoaxial instability occurs in only a minority of patients with Down syndrome and is not universal. Congenital heart disease exclusion does not eliminate the separate airway risks specific to this syndrome.
  158. The correct answer is B (Intravenous caffeine citrate administered perioperatively.): caffeine citrate is a respiratory stimulant that reduces the incidence and severity of postoperative apnea in former premature infants and is the standard pharmacologic prophylaxis used in this population, in addition to continued apnea monitoring. Naloxone is used to reverse opioid effects rather than as routine apnea prophylaxis. Doxapram is not standard first-line prophylaxis in most practice. Dexmedetomidine is sometimes used to reduce opioid requirements but is not the established standard apnea prophylaxis agent.
  159. The correct answer is A (Avoid excessive supplemental oxygen and permit mild hypercapnia to limit pulmonary flow.): because truncus arteriosus shares a single outflow between the pulmonary and systemic circulations, similar to other mixing lesions, excessive pulmonary blood flow steals from systemic perfusion, so avoiding hyperoxia and hyperventilation while permitting mild hypercapnia raises pulmonary vascular resistance and helps balance flow toward the systemic circulation. Giving high oxygen concentrations or pulmonary vasodilators would worsen pulmonary overcirculation and systemic hypoperfusion.
  160. The correct answer is B (Provide supportive respiratory care and arrange for emergent surgical repair.): obstructed total anomalous pulmonary venous return causes severe pulmonary venous hypertension and edema that worsens rapidly, and unlike many ductal-dependent lesions it is not reliably improved by prostaglandin infusion, so emergent surgical correction of the venous obstruction is the definitive and urgent treatment while providing respiratory support. Prostaglandin E1 does not relieve pulmonary venous obstruction. Delaying surgery for medical stabilization risks fatal deterioration. Septostomy alone does not correct the anomalous venous drainage or its obstruction.
  161. The correct answer is B (Plasma volume expansion exceeding the increase in red blood cell mass.): pregnancy causes plasma volume to increase by up to 50%, while red blood cell mass increases by a smaller proportion, producing a dilutional physiologic anemia of pregnancy that is a normal, expected finding rather than a pathologic process. There is no accelerated hemolysis of maternal red cells from the placenta in normal pregnancy. Iron deficiency can contribute in some patients but is not the primary explanation for this universal dilutional pattern. Progesterone does not suppress maternal bone marrow function.
  162. The correct answer is A (Place the patient in left lateral tilt or manually displace the uterus leftward.): in the supine position the gravid uterus compresses the inferior vena cava and aorta, reducing venous return and cardiac output, and left uterine displacement or lateral tilt relieves this aortocaval compression, restoring maternal blood pressure and uteroplacental perfusion. Fluid bolus and vasopressors are useful adjuncts but do not address the mechanical compression causing the hypotension. Trendelenburg position does not relieve aortocaval compression and may worsen the situation.
  163. The correct answer is D (Dilute local anesthetic combined with a lipophilic opioid infused continuously.): modern epidural labor analgesia uses a dilute local anesthetic such as low-concentration bupivacaine or ropivacaine combined with a lipophilic opioid like fentanyl, which provides effective analgesia for the visceral pain of the first stage of labor while minimizing motor blockade so the patient can still push effectively. High-concentration local anesthetic alone increases motor block and hypotension without added benefit. Repeated single-shot spinal dosing is impractical for ongoing labor analgesia. General anesthesia is not used for routine labor analgesia.
  164. The correct answer is D (Combined spinal-epidural technique with a low-dose intrathecal opioid and local anesthetic.): the combined spinal-epidural technique delivers a small intrathecal dose that produces rapid onset analgesia within minutes while preserving enough motor function for ambulation in appropriate candidates, and the epidural catheter allows continued dosing as labor progresses. A standard epidural alone has a slower onset than an intrathecal dose. Continuous spinal catheters are not standard practice for routine labor analgesia due to complication risks. Paracervical block carries fetal bradycardia risk and does not provide the same quality or duration of analgesia.
  165. The correct answer is A (Phenylephrine as a bolus or infusion to restore maternal blood pressure.): current evidence supports phenylephrine as first-line treatment for spinal-induced hypotension during cesarean delivery because it effectively restores maternal blood pressure and is associated with less fetal acidosis than ephedrine when maternal heart rate is normal or elevated. Ephedrine crosses the placenta more readily and is associated with lower fetal pH, making it a second-line choice, particularly with maternal bradycardia. Epinephrine is not a first-line agent for routine spinal hypotension. Norepinephrine can be used but is not the standard first-line choice ahead of phenylephrine in most practice.
  166. The correct answer is B (Preeclampsia without severe features.): new-onset blood pressure of at least 140/90 mmHg after 20 weeks gestation accompanied by new proteinuria defines preeclampsia, and the absence of severe features such as blood pressure at or above 160/110 mmHg, visual or neurologic symptoms, or organ dysfunction classifies this as preeclampsia without severe features. Chronic hypertension implies elevated blood pressure predating pregnancy or before 20 weeks, which is not described here. Gestational hypertension by definition lacks proteinuria or other organ involvement. HELLP syndrome requires characteristic hemolysis, elevated liver enzymes, and low platelets, none of which are present.
  167. The correct answer is D (Magnesium sulfate infusion for seizure prophylaxis in addition to antihypertensive therapy.): blood pressure sustained at or above 160/110 mmHg is independently sufficient to define severe features of preeclampsia, as are certain severe symptoms such as a refractory headache; this patient meets both criteria, and magnesium sulfate is the standard of care for seizure prophylaxis in this setting, given alongside acute antihypertensive therapy to treat the severe hypertension itself. Antihypertensive treatment alone does not address seizure risk. Oral labetalol has too slow an onset for acute severe hypertension requiring urgent control. Proceeding directly to delivery without first controlling blood pressure and initiating seizure prophylaxis increases maternal risk.
  168. The correct answer is C (Discontinue the magnesium infusion and administer intravenous calcium gluconate.): loss of deep tendon reflexes is an early clinical sign of magnesium toxicity, which can progress to respiratory depression and cardiac arrest, so the infusion should be stopped and intravenous calcium gluconate given as the specific antidote to antagonize magnesium's effects at the neuromuscular junction. Continuing or increasing the infusion risks progression to more severe toxicity. Benzodiazepines do not reverse magnesium toxicity and would worsen sedation and respiratory depression.
  169. The correct answer is D (HELLP syndrome requiring urgent evaluation for delivery.): the combination of hemolysis evidenced by an abnormal peripheral smear and elevated LDH, elevated liver transaminases, and a low platelet count in a pregnant patient with right upper quadrant pain is diagnostic of HELLP syndrome, a severe variant of preeclampsia that requires urgent maternal and fetal assessment and typically prompt delivery. Idiopathic thrombocytopenic purpura would not explain the elevated liver enzymes or hemolysis. Acute fatty liver of pregnancy typically presents with coagulopathy and hypoglycemia rather than this specific hemolysis and thrombocytopenia pattern. Uncomplicated gestational hypertension by definition lacks these laboratory abnormalities.
  170. The correct answer is A (Uterine atony, treated first with uterine massage and intravenous oxytocin.): a soft, boggy uterus after delivery without retained tissue or laceration is the classic presentation of uterine atony, the most common cause of postpartum hemorrhage, and first-line treatment includes bimanual uterine massage combined with intravenous oxytocin, escalating to additional uterotonics if bleeding continues. Placenta accreta and uterine rupture typically present with different findings such as a firm uterus or absent bleeding externally, and are not suggested here. Coagulopathy is a less likely primary cause when the uterus is soft and atonic, and uterotonic therapy should not be bypassed.
  171. The correct answer is D (Ensure large-bore intravenous access, blood products available, and multidisciplinary planning.): placenta accreta spectrum carries a high risk of catastrophic hemorrhage requiring massive transfusion and often hysterectomy, so anesthetic preparation should include large-bore intravenous access, immediately available crossmatched blood products, invasive monitoring as indicated, and coordination with obstetrics, urology, and interventional radiology as needed. A single peripheral line is inadequate given the hemorrhage risk. Vaginal delivery is not an option with placenta previa. Neuraxial anesthesia can often still be used depending on the clinical scenario and is not absolutely contraindicated.
  172. The correct answer is C (Amniotic fluid embolism with cardiovascular collapse and disseminated intravascular coagulation.): the sudden onset of hypoxia, hypotension, altered mental status, and disseminated intravascular coagulation during labor or shortly after delivery without another clear cause is the classic presentation of amniotic fluid embolism, a rare but often catastrophic obstetric emergency requiring immediate resuscitation and supportive care. Local anesthetic toxicity typically presents with neurologic symptoms and possible seizure or cardiac arrhythmia rather than this coagulopathic picture. Vasovagal syncope and hypoglycemia would not produce disseminated intravascular coagulation or this degree of cardiovascular collapse.
  173. The correct answer is B (Give intravenous lipid emulsion therapy in addition to standard resuscitation.): circumoral numbness, tinnitus, seizure, and arrhythmia following an epidural test dose suggest inadvertent intravascular injection causing local anesthetic systemic toxicity, and intravenous lipid emulsion therapy should be administered promptly alongside standard airway management, oxygenation, and cardiovascular support. Administering more local anesthetic would worsen the toxicity. Withholding oxygen is inappropriate since hypoxia and acidosis worsen local anesthetic toxicity. Increasing the infusion continues to deliver the offending drug rather than treating the emergency.
  174. The correct answer is A (Airway mucosal edema, weight gain, and enlarged breast tissue impairing laryngoscopy.): pregnancy causes capillary engorgement and edema of the airway mucosa, along with weight gain and breast enlargement that can interfere with laryngoscope handle insertion and visualization, all of which contribute to a higher incidence of difficult intubation in obstetric patients. Maternal blood volume actually increases in pregnancy rather than decreasing. Metabolic rate increases, not decreases, in pregnancy. Gastric emptying is generally delayed rather than increased, raising rather than lowering aspiration risk.
  175. The correct answer is D (Rapid sequence induction with cricoid pressure and preoxygenation before intubation.): when general anesthesia is required emergently for cesarean delivery, rapid sequence induction with adequate preoxygenation, cricoid pressure, and prompt intubation minimizes the time to airway control while reducing aspiration risk given the delayed gastric emptying and elevated intra-abdominal pressure of pregnancy. Awake fiberoptic intubation is too slow for a true obstetric emergency. Aspiration risk is not overstated in pregnancy and cricoid pressure remains a standard component of this technique in most practice. Inhalational induction with preserved spontaneous ventilation is not the standard approach for emergency cesarean delivery under general anesthesia.
  176. The correct answer is B (Tranexamic acid administered within three hours of delivery.): tranexamic acid, an antifibrinolytic agent, has been shown in large trials to reduce mortality from postpartum hemorrhage when given within three hours of delivery, in addition to standard uterotonic therapy and surgical management as needed. Furosemide would be harmful in a hemorrhaging, potentially hypovolemic patient. Heparin would worsen bleeding rather than help control hemorrhage. Fresh frozen plasma should be guided by coagulation studies and ongoing losses rather than given prophylactically without indication.
  177. The correct answer is B (Placental abruption with a tender, hypertonic uterus.): placental abruption classically presents with painful vaginal bleeding, a tender and often hypertonic or firm uterus, and fetal distress, and preeclampsia is a well-established risk factor for abruption. Placenta previa typically causes painless bleeding without uterine tenderness, unlike this presentation. Round ligament pain does not produce vaginal bleeding, uterine tenderness, or fetal bradycardia. This clinical picture is not consistent with benign, incidental findings given the fetal bradycardia and hemodynamic concern.
  178. The correct answer is A (Uterine rupture at the site of the prior cesarean scar.): sudden severe abdominal pain, loss of fetal station, and abrupt fetal bradycardia during a trial of labor after cesarean are classic warning signs of uterine rupture at the site of the previous uterine scar, representing an obstetric emergency requiring immediate cesarean delivery. Normal progression into the second stage does not typically present with sudden severe pain and loss of station. Chorioamnionitis causes fever and typically a more gradual presentation rather than sudden bradycardia and loss of station. Round ligament pain is a benign finding unrelated to fetal station or heart rate changes.
  179. The correct answer is B (Cardiac output increases immediately postpartum due to autotransfusion from the contracting uterus.): relief of aortocaval compression combined with autotransfusion of blood from the contracting, emptying uterus produces a significant rise in maternal cardiac output immediately after delivery, which is a critical period of risk for patients with limited cardiac reserve such as peripartum cardiomyopathy. Cardiac output does not decline through labor in this way, and it does not return to prepregnancy baseline within minutes, instead normalizing over days to weeks postpartum. This physiologic surge occurs regardless of underlying cardiac reserve, though its consequences are more dangerous in patients with limited reserve.
  180. The correct answer is B (General anesthesia should be strongly considered given the bleeding risk of epidural hematoma with severe thrombocytopenia.): a platelet count this low significantly increases the risk of epidural or spinal hematoma with neuraxial technique, so general anesthesia is often favored in this situation, though the specific threshold and decision depend on trend, rate of decline, and coagulation status. Platelet count is directly relevant to neuraxial safety and cannot be disregarded. Epidural technique is not preferred over spinal specifically due to low platelets, since both carry similar bleeding risk from needle or catheter trauma. Ignoring platelet count entirely in this decision would be unsafe practice.
  181. The correct answer is C (Epidural extension can be achieved more quickly than placing a new block, saving critical time.): when a functioning labor epidural catheter is already in place, extending it with a higher concentration local anesthetic for surgical anesthesia avoids the time needed to reposition and perform a new spinal, which is valuable when urgent delivery is indicated. Spinal anesthesia typically produces a denser, more reliable block than epidural extension, not the reverse. Standard obstetric monitoring is still required regardless of technique. Hypotension can still occur with epidural extension, though it may be somewhat more gradual than with spinal anesthesia.
  182. The correct answer is A (Uteroplacental insufficiency suggesting late decelerations requiring evaluation.): a deceleration that begins after the peak of the contraction and returns to baseline after the contraction has ended is the definition of a late deceleration, which reflects transient uteroplacental insufficiency and requires prompt evaluation including maternal repositioning, oxygen, and assessment for the underlying cause. Early decelerations mirror the contraction and coincide with it, reflecting benign fetal head compression, which does not match this description. Variable decelerations have an abrupt onset unrelated in timing to contractions, reflecting cord compression. Accelerations are increases, not decreases, in fetal heart rate.
  183. The correct answer is A (Magnesium sulfate potentiates the effect of nondepolarizing neuromuscular blocking agents.): magnesium decreases acetylcholine release at the neuromuscular junction and reduces postjunctional membrane excitability, which potentiates and prolongs the effect of nondepolarizing muscle relaxants, so doses should be reduced and neuromuscular monitoring used closely in patients on magnesium infusions. There is a well-established interaction, not an absence of one. An allergic reaction would typically present with additional signs such as rash, bronchospasm, or hypotension rather than isolated prolonged block. Preeclampsia does not directly increase hepatic metabolism of rocuronium; if anything, organ dysfunction could impair clearance.
  184. The correct answer is D (Hyperbaric bupivacaine combined with an intrathecal opioid.): hyperbaric bupivacaine is the standard agent for cesarean delivery spinal anesthesia because its density relative to cerebrospinal fluid allows predictable spread to a T4 sensory level when combined with appropriate patient positioning, and adding an intrathecal opioid such as fentanyl or morphine improves intraoperative comfort and provides postoperative analgesia. Isobaric bupivacaine at low doses intended for lower extremity procedures would not reliably reach the T4 level needed for cesarean delivery. Dilute lidocaine has too short a duration for the length of a cesarean delivery. Plain ropivacaine without additives is less commonly used and less predictable for this indication than hyperbaric bupivacaine with opioid.
  185. The correct answer is C (Decreased functional residual capacity combined with increased oxygen consumption.): pregnancy reduces functional residual capacity due to the elevated diaphragm from the gravid uterus while simultaneously increasing maternal oxygen consumption to support fetal and maternal metabolic demands, and this combination markedly shortens the time to desaturation during apnea, making adequate preoxygenation essential. Functional residual capacity is decreased, not increased, in pregnancy. Oxygen consumption is increased, not decreased. Maternal hemoglobin oxygen affinity changes do not primarily explain the rapid desaturation seen during apnea in pregnancy.
  186. The correct answer is B (Transfuse packed red blood cells, fresh frozen plasma, and platelets in a balanced ratio.): massive obstetric hemorrhage with evolving dilutional and consumptive coagulopathy is best managed with a balanced transfusion strategy, often approximating a 1:1:1 ratio of red cells, plasma, and platelets, guided by ongoing clinical assessment and point-of-care testing when available. Continuing red cells alone worsens dilutional coagulopathy by failing to replace clotting factors and platelets. Using only crystalloid would further dilute clotting factors and worsen bleeding. Waiting for laboratory normalization before further transfusion is dangerous in an actively hemorrhaging patient.
  187.
