The correct answer is C (Decreased plasma colloid oncotic pressure combined with capillary leak.): preeclampsia is associated with decreased plasma colloid oncotic pressure and increased capillary permeability from endothelial dysfunction, both of which favor fluid movement into the pulmonary interstitium and alveoli, making these patients particularly susceptible to pulmonary edema, especially with aggressive fluid administration. Colloid oncotic pressure is decreased, not increased, in preeclampsia. There is no chronic lung disease inherent to preeclampsia explaining pulmonary vascular resistance changes. Capillary permeability is increased rather than decreased in this condition.
  188. The correct answer is B (Maternal hypotension reducing uteroplacental perfusion, treated with fluids, vasopressor, and lateral positioning.): the temporal relationship between spinal-induced maternal hypotension and new fetal bradycardia strongly suggests reduced uteroplacental perfusion, and rapid correction with intravenous fluids, a vasopressor such as phenylephrine, and left lateral positioning typically resolves the fetal heart rate abnormality. Intrathecal local anesthetic doses used for cesarean delivery do not reach the fetus in clinically significant amounts to cause direct cardiotoxicity. Congenital heart block would not appear suddenly and coincide precisely with maternal blood pressure changes. Cord prolapse is not suggested by this clinical scenario and correcting maternal hypotension should be attempted first.
  189. The correct answer is B (Gestational hypertension, pending further evaluation for progression to preeclampsia.): new-onset elevated blood pressure after 20 weeks gestation without proteinuria or other features of end-organ involvement is classified as gestational hypertension, which requires close ongoing monitoring because a subset of these patients will progress to preeclampsia. Chronic hypertension requires elevation predating pregnancy or diagnosis before 20 weeks, which does not apply here since prior visits were normal. Preeclampsia with severe features requires either proteinuria with additional severe criteria or other organ dysfunction, none of which are present. Dismissing the finding as white coat hypertension without follow-up would be inappropriate given the risk of progression.
  190. The correct answer is A (Epidural blood patch using autologous blood injected at or near the puncture site.): an epidural blood patch, in which autologous blood is injected into the epidural space near the site of dural puncture, is the definitive and most effective treatment for postdural puncture headache that has failed conservative management, working by forming a clot that seals the dural leak and restores cerebrospinal fluid pressure. Prolonged bed rest alone has not been shown to reliably prevent or treat established postdural puncture headache. Caffeine can provide temporary symptomatic relief but is not a definitive or reliably long-lasting treatment. Repeat lumbar puncture would risk creating an additional dural defect rather than treating the existing one.
  191. The correct answer is B (Place a well-functioning labor epidural early and confirm it can be extended for surgical anesthesia.): early placement of a reliable epidural allows rapid extension to surgical density, avoiding an emergent general anesthetic in a patient with a predicted difficult airway. Waiting until a cesarean is decided removes the safety margin needed to test and extend the block calmly. Awake fiberoptic intubation remains a valid backup plan but should not be the primary strategy when a functioning neuraxial catheter could avoid general anesthesia altogether. Avoiding analgesia does not improve airway management and removes a proactive risk-reduction opportunity.
  192. The correct answer is C (Proceed with epidural placement after reviewing the trend and confirming no coagulopathy.): decisions about neuraxial anesthesia in thrombocytopenic parturients rely on clinical judgment incorporating the platelet trend, coexisting coagulopathy, and bleeding history rather than a single rigid numeric cutoff. A stable count with normal coagulation studies and no bleeding supports proceeding safely. Bleeding time is not a validated predictor of spinal or epidural hematoma risk and is not required. Requiring an arbitrary threshold like 150,000/microL is not supported by current practice and would deny effective analgesia unnecessarily.
  193. The correct answer is A (Amniotic fluid embolism, presenting with the classic triad of hypoxia and hemodynamic collapse.): the abrupt combination of hypoxia, hemodynamic collapse, and coagulopathy with diffuse bleeding is the classic triad of amniotic fluid embolism and warrants immediate recognition and resuscitation. Local anesthetic toxicity from an intrathecal dose is exceedingly unlikely given the small volumes used for spinal anesthesia. A high spinal typically causes hypotension and possible respiratory compromise but does not explain sudden coagulopathy. A vasovagal reaction would not produce diffuse bleeding or sustained cardiovascular collapse.
  194. The correct answer is D (Have a video laryngoscope immediately available and a clear backup plan before induction.): in an emergent cesarean with known difficult mask ventilation, having video laryngoscopy ready along with a rehearsed failed-airway algorithm maximizes first-pass success while still allowing timely delivery. Proceeding without any additional preparation ignores a known risk factor and increases the chance of a crisis. Awake fiberoptic intubation is too slow for a true category one emergency and is reserved for less urgent, controlled situations. Relying solely on a supraglottic airway does not secure the airway against aspiration in this full-stomach scenario.
  195. The correct answer is B (Administer a vasopressor and ensure left uterine displacement to restore venous return.): spinal-induced hypotension results from sympathectomy-mediated vasodilation combined with aortocaval compression, so a vasopressor to restore vascular tone paired with left uterine displacement to relieve compression directly targets both mechanisms. Crystalloid alone is a slower and less reliable correction than vasopressor therapy in this acute setting. Trendelenburg positioning without medication or displacement does not reliably reverse the sympathetic blockade. Oxygen alone treats a downstream consequence but does not correct the hemodynamic cause.
  196. The correct answer is A (Methylergonovine given intramuscularly as the next uterotonic once oxytocin and massage have failed.): when oxytocin and uterine massage fail to control atony in a normotensive patient without contraindications, methylergonovine is an appropriate next-line uterotonic that promotes sustained uterine contraction. Escalating oxytocin dose further has limited additional benefit once a therapeutic effect has plateaued and risks water intoxication and hypotension. Magnesium sulfate is a tocolytic that relaxes the uterus and would worsen atony. Repeating the same ineffective regimen does not address the persistent atony.
  197. The correct answer is D (Carboprost tromethamine, a prostaglandin analog that can precipitate severe bronchospasm in this patient.): this prostaglandin F2-alpha analog can cause significant bronchospasm and is relatively contraindicated in patients with active or poorly controlled asthma. Misoprostol is a prostaglandin E1 analog that does not carry the same bronchospasm risk and is a reasonable alternative uterotonic in this setting. Continuing oxytocin is not contraindicated by asthma, though it alone may be insufficient. Tranexamic acid is an antifibrinolytic adjunct for postpartum hemorrhage and does not affect airway reactivity.
  198. The correct answer is C (Higher volatile concentrations increase uterine relaxation and can worsen postpartum hemorrhage.): volatile anesthetics produce dose-dependent uterine relaxation, so concentrations are typically minimized after delivery of the fetus to reduce the risk of atony and hemorrhage, accepting a small increase in awareness risk that is mitigated with adjunct agents. Higher volatile concentrations do not improve neonatal Apgar scores and can in fact contribute to neonatal depression if excessive. The premise that lower concentrations have no effect on uterine tone is incorrect since dose directly correlates with relaxation. Volatile agents are well established to affect uterine tone and hemorrhage risk.
  199. The correct answer is A (Consider tranexamic acid and prepare for possible surgical intervention such as uterine artery embolization or balloon tamponade.): when pharmacologic uterotonic therapy fails to control hemorrhage, tranexamic acid as an antifibrinolytic adjunct and escalation to mechanical or surgical measures are appropriate next steps in a massive hemorrhage protocol. Discontinuing uterotonics abandons a component of ongoing therapy that may still contribute alongside other measures. Delaying escalation risks continued blood loss and hemodynamic deterioration. Repeating an already maximized oxytocin dose offers little additional benefit once tolerance has developed.
  200. The correct answer is B (General anesthesia with rapid sequence induction.): a platelet count this low substantially increases the risk of spinal or epidural hematoma, so general anesthesia is generally preferred for emergency delivery in this setting. Single-shot spinal anesthesia still carries meaningful bleeding risk at this platelet level despite the smaller needle. Combined spinal-epidural involves an epidural catheter and needle passage that also carries unacceptable risk. Epidural titration is both risky and too slow for a true emergency indication.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Unrecognized intravascular injection causing local anesthetic systemic toxicity.): cardiovascular collapse and altered mental status immediately following a test dose should raise concern for inadvertent intravascular injection of local anesthetic, prompting immediate lipid emulsion therapy and resuscitation. This presentation is not an expected or normal epidural response. A simple vasovagal episode would not typically cause this degree of hemodynamic and neurologic compromise. While amniotic fluid embolism remains on the differential, the temporal relationship to the epidural dose makes local anesthetic toxicity the more likely and immediately actionable diagnosis.
  2. The correct answer is C (Neuraxial anesthesia can reasonably be offered after individualized risk assessment rather than automatic refusal.): current evidence supports individualized decision-making incorporating platelet trend, etiology, and bleeding history rather than a strict numeric cutoff, and a stable count with normal coagulation and no bleeding history supports proceeding. Automatically denying neuraxial anesthesia below an arbitrary threshold is not consistent with contemporary guidance. Viscoelastic testing can be informative but is not mandatory in every stable case. Preemptively committing to general anesthesia is unnecessary when neuraxial techniques remain a reasonable option.
  3. The correct answer is A (Consumptive coagulopathy from amniotic fluid embolism or severe hemorrhage.): diffuse non-surgical bleeding with prolonged clotting times and hypofibrinogenemia points to a consumptive coagulopathy, which can arise from amniotic fluid embolism or ongoing massive hemorrhage triggering disseminated intravascular coagulation. Isolated surgical bleeding from a single vessel would typically present as localized rather than diffuse bleeding with abnormal global coagulation studies. A single liter of crystalloid is unlikely to cause dilutional coagulopathy of this severity. This pattern is abnormal and requires immediate coagulopathy management, not observation.
  4. The correct answer is D (Provide high-flow oxygen, initiate hemodynamic support, and prepare for possible cardiopulmonary resuscitation.): amniotic fluid embolism management is primarily supportive, prioritizing oxygenation, hemodynamic stabilization with vasopressors and inotropes as needed, and readiness for advanced resuscitation given the risk of rapid cardiovascular collapse. Converting to general anesthesia may become necessary but is not the first priority over immediate resuscitative measures. Large volume fluid administration without addressing vasopressor needs can worsen right heart strain in this setting. This presentation is inconsistent with a benign vagal response and requires urgent intervention.
  5. The correct answer is B (Preoxygenate in a head-elevated or ramped position rather than fully supine.): head-elevated or ramped positioning improves functional residual capacity and extends safe apnea time in obese parturients, making preoxygenation more effective before induction. A fully supine flat position reduces functional residual capacity and shortens the safe apnea period, particularly in obesity. Skipping preoxygenation in a high-risk airway patient significantly increases the risk of hypoxemia during induction. Using room air with a loose mask fails to achieve adequate denitrogenation and undermines the purpose of preoxygenation.
  6. The correct answer is A (Rapid oxytocin bolus causes direct vasodilation and reflex tachycardia.): rapid intravenous oxytocin administration is well recognized to cause transient hypotension and reflex tachycardia through direct vasodilatory effects, which is why slow infusion rather than bolus dosing is generally recommended. Oxytocin clearly has cardiovascular effects when given rapidly, contradicting the premise that it has none. This pattern is a known pharmacologic effect rather than an anaphylactic reaction, which would typically include additional features like rash or bronchospasm. The hemodynamic change reflects a drug effect rather than a worsening of uterine atony itself.
  7. The correct answer is D (Ensure large-bore intravenous access, cross-matched blood availability, and a massive transfusion protocol plan before incision.): anticipated massive hemorrhage from abnormal placentation and cesarean hysterectomy warrants proactive preparation including adequate vascular access, available cross-matched blood, and a clear massive transfusion protocol activated before surgery begins. Standard single-access preparation is inadequate for the anticipated blood loss in this high-risk scenario. Waiting until bleeding occurs to coordinate blood bank resources introduces dangerous delay. While cell salvage can be a useful adjunct, it should not replace preparation for allogeneic transfusion given the magnitude of expected blood loss.
  8. The correct answer is C (Progression of the block to a high or total spinal affecting respiratory muscles.): a rising sensory level with new dyspnea and voice weakness after spinal anesthesia suggests progression toward a high spinal affecting intercostal or phrenic function and requires immediate airway and ventilatory support readiness. While pulmonary embolism is a serious consideration in any peripartum patient, the clear temporal relationship to the spinal block and voice change make block progression far more likely. An allergic reaction to skin preparation would not typically cause these specific respiratory and voice findings. This presentation is abnormal and should not be dismissed as simple anxiety.
  9. The correct answer is D (Magnesium potentiates neuromuscular blockade and may increase sensitivity to vasodilation-related hypotension.): magnesium sulfate potentiates both depolarizing and nondepolarizing neuromuscular blocking agents and can contribute to greater hemodynamic sensitivity during the vasodilation of spinal anesthesia, requiring vigilant monitoring. Magnesium is well known to interact with neuromuscular function, contradicting the premise of no interaction. It does not eliminate the need for vasopressor support and in fact may increase the need for careful hemodynamic management. Magnesium is a vasodilator, not a vasoconstrictor, and would not prevent spinal-induced hypotension.
  10. The correct answer is B (Balancing the individualized bleeding risk against the benefits of neuraxial analgesia and alternatives available.): current practice favors a shared, individualized assessment weighing the specific bleeding risk, etiology of thrombocytopenia, coagulation status, and available alternatives such as intravenous opioids rather than relying on a single data point. Platelet count alone without clinical context is insufficient for a safe decision. Patient preference is important but must be integrated with objective risk information rather than considered in isolation. Rigid institutional cutoffs without individualized assessment are not consistent with current evidence-based guidance.
  11. The correct answer is A (A fibrinogen level this low during postpartum hemorrhage is a strong predictor of progression to severe hemorrhage.): fibrinogen normally rises substantially in pregnancy, so a level of 150 mg/dL during active postpartum hemorrhage is relatively low and has been associated with progression to more severe bleeding, prompting consideration of fibrinogen replacement such as cryoprecipitate or fibrinogen concentrate. This value is not reassuringly elevated given the expected pregnancy-related increase in fibrinogen. Fibrinogen is highly relevant to guiding transfusion and hemostatic management in postpartum hemorrhage. A low fibrinogen level suggests a coagulation contribution rather than confirming a purely surgical bleeding source.
  12. The correct answer is D (Confirm difficult airway equipment and a clear conversion plan are immediately available before starting the case.): given the known difficult airway, proactively ensuring video laryngoscopy and a rehearsed conversion plan are ready before the case begins minimizes risk if general anesthesia becomes necessary intraoperatively. Assuming conversion will not be needed ignores the real possibility that arises during many cesarean deliveries. Waiting until the request occurs to locate equipment introduces dangerous delay in an urgent situation. Standard direct laryngoscopy without preparation does not adequately address a known difficult airway risk.
  13. The correct answer is C (Release or adjust cricoid pressure while maintaining airway protection strategy, since it may be distorting the view.): excessive or misapplied cricoid pressure can worsen laryngoscopic view, so adjusting or releasing it while reassessing is an appropriate step when intubation is difficult, consistent with difficult airway algorithms. Increasing cricoid pressure further when it may already be contributing to a poor view would likely worsen rather than improve conditions. Continuing unchanged technique without adaptation ignores an actionable contributing factor. Abandoning airway management without pursuing algorithm-directed backup steps would be unsafe in this clinical scenario.
  14. The correct answer is A (Eclampsia requiring magnesium sulfate and blood pressure management.): new-onset seizure with severe hypertension and visual changes in a peripartum patient is characteristic of eclampsia and requires immediate magnesium sulfate administration and blood pressure control rather than attributing the presentation to the epidural. Postdural puncture headache typically presents as a positional headache without seizure activity or severe hypertension. Local anesthetic toxicity from an epidural infusion at standard rates rarely causes this presentation and would not explain the severe hypertension. A simple migraine would not account for seizure activity and markedly elevated blood pressure in this context.
  15. The correct answer is C (Misoprostol administered by an appropriate route as an alternative prostaglandin.): misoprostol lacks the bronchospasm risk associated with carboprost and is a reasonable alternative uterotonic in a patient with severe asthma. Carboprost carries a genuine bronchospasm risk that is amplified in patients with significant reactive airway disease, so increasing its dose would not be safer. Methylergonovine has its own contraindications related to hypertension and should be selected based on that patient's specific risk factors, not used reflexively. Withholding all uterotonic therapy during active hemorrhage would allow continued blood loss and is not appropriate.
  16. The correct answer is D (Reduce the inhaled volatile anesthetic concentration and supplement with intravenous agents as needed.): lowering volatile anesthetic concentration after delivery reduces its dose-dependent uterine relaxant effect, supporting improved uterine tone, while intravenous agents such as propofol or opioids can maintain adequate anesthetic depth and reduce awareness risk. Increasing volatile concentration would worsen uterine relaxation and hemorrhage risk. Adding a second volatile agent compounds rather than reduces the relaxant effect. The premise that uterine tone is unrelated to volatile dose is incorrect given the well-established dose-dependent relationship.
  17. The correct answer is B (Transition to a balanced ratio incorporating plasma and platelets alongside red cells.): dilutional coagulopathy from red-cell-predominant transfusion is corrected by shifting to a balanced transfusion ratio that includes plasma and platelets alongside red cells, addressing both the prolonged prothrombin time and thrombocytopenia. Continuing red cells exclusively would worsen the existing dilutional coagulopathy. Discontinuing all transfusion during active hemorrhage with worsening laboratory values would be unsafe. Switching entirely to platelets while withholding plasma would not address the prolonged prothrombin time reflecting clotting factor depletion.
  18. The correct answer is A (Obtain a type and crossmatch and discuss a hemorrhage contingency plan with the obstetric team before surgery.): a history of prior postpartum hemorrhage requiring transfusion is a recognized risk factor for recurrence, so proactive crossmatching and a documented contingency plan appropriately match preparation to elevated risk. Treating this patient identically to a low-risk patient ignores a meaningful historical risk factor. A type and screen alone without crossmatched units may delay transfusion if hemorrhage recurs. Waiting until after surgery has begun to discuss contingency planning removes the benefit of advance preparation.
  19. The correct answer is C (Administer a vasopressor with combined alpha and beta activity such as ephedrine, and consider atropine for bradycardia.): when hypotension is accompanied by significant bradycardia unresponsive to a pure alpha agonist, adding an agent with beta activity such as ephedrine, along with atropine if bradycardia persists, addresses both the vascular tone and heart rate components. Repeating the same phenylephrine dose without change does not address the bradycardia component of the presentation. Withholding further therapy in the setting of profound, persistent hypotension risks maternal and fetal compromise. There is no mechanism by which increasing block level would correct hypotension, and doing so could worsen it.
  20. The correct answer is D (Neuraxial anesthesia can be considered reasonable after confirming adequate factor correction and close hematology coordination.): with hematology-guided factor optimization and confirmed normal coagulation studies, neuraxial anesthesia can reasonably be offered after individualized assessment rather than automatic exclusion. Von Willebrand disease type 1 with adequately corrected factor levels is not an absolute contraindication when properly managed. Factor correction status is directly relevant since it reflects the actual bleeding risk at the time of the procedure. Automatically mandating general anesthesia for any bleeding disorder disregards the possibility of safe, individualized neuraxial management.
  21. The correct answer is A (Early cardiopulmonary support with attention to right ventricular function and aggressive correction of coagulopathy.): amniotic fluid embolism frequently causes acute right ventricular strain and profound coagulopathy, so early hemodynamic support targeting right heart function alongside aggressive blood product replacement is central to resuscitation. There is no diagnostic test that confirms amniotic fluid embolism in real time, so treatment must proceed on clinical suspicion rather than waiting for confirmation. Discharge would be inappropriate and dangerous given the severity and unpredictability of this condition. Oral fluids cannot provide adequate hemodynamic support in a patient with cardiovascular collapse.
  22. The correct answer is D (Use ultrasound guidance to identify the intrathecal space before needle insertion.): preprocedural ultrasound has been shown to improve success rates and reduce needle passes in patients with difficult surface landmarks, such as those with elevated body mass index. Relying exclusively on palpation in a patient with known difficult landmarks does not address the anticipated challenge. Restricting the technique to only the lateral position does not by itself resolve landmark identification difficulty. Needle gauge selection relates to postdural puncture headache risk rather than landmark identification and would not meaningfully improve first-attempt success.
  23. The correct answer is B (A genital tract laceration or retained products rather than uterine atony.): when the uterus is firm and well-contracted yet bleeding continues, a genital tract laceration, cervical tear, or retained placental tissue becomes a more likely cause than atony, since atony is characterized by a soft, boggy uterus. Atony can be excluded as the primary cause here given the firm uterine tone on examination. Disseminated intravascular coagulation is unlikely to be the primary driver when coagulation studies are explicitly normal. Uterine inversion typically presents with a palpable mass or absent fundus on examination, which is not described here.
  24. The correct answer is C (Neuraxial anesthesia may still be attempted with imaging review and awareness of possible higher failure or difficulty rates.): prior instrumented spinal fusion is not an absolute contraindication, and neuraxial anesthesia can often still be performed, though imaging review and awareness of a potentially more difficult or unpredictable block are appropriate. Treating it as an absolute contraindication would deny a viable option to many patients who could still be managed safely. Automatically defaulting to general anesthesia disregards the individualized planning that is possible in this scenario. Prior fusion frequently does alter anatomy and can affect technical difficulty, contrary to the premise of no impact.
  25. The correct answer is A (Reassess tube placement immediately and be prepared to remove and reintubate if esophageal placement is confirmed.): absent end-tidal carbon dioxide and bilateral breath sounds after intubation is a critical sign of possible esophageal intubation, requiring immediate reassessment and correction before proceeding. Continuing ventilation through a potentially misplaced tube risks severe maternal hypoxia. Assuming equipment malfunction and delaying reassessment ignores a potentially life-threatening airway emergency. Proceeding with surgical incision while the airway remains unconfirmed would be unsafe and inconsistent with airway management priorities.
  26. The correct answer is D (The declining trend itself, which increases concern beyond the absolute value alone.): a rapidly declining platelet count suggests active, ongoing consumption or worsening disease process and raises greater concern for neuraxial anesthesia than a stable count at the same absolute value, favoring general anesthesia in this urgent, non-emergent scenario. Relying on the absolute value alone without considering trajectory would underestimate the true risk in this progressively worsening picture. Blood type has no bearing on bleeding risk assessment related to platelet count. Time of day scheduling does not influence the underlying pathophysiologic risk of hematoma formation.
  27. The correct answer is C (Prolonged prior oxytocin exposure can downregulate oxytocin receptors, reducing subsequent responsiveness.): prolonged intrapartum oxytocin exposure has been associated with oxytocin receptor downregulation, which can reduce the uterus's responsiveness to oxytocin during the postpartum period and contribute to atony requiring additional or alternative uterotonic therapy. The premise that prior exposure has no relationship to postpartum responsiveness contradicts this recognized pharmacologic phenomenon. Receptor downregulation is specifically described with oxytocin exposure itself, not exclusively with prostaglandins. While atony can be multifactorial, prior oxytocin exposure is a relevant and recognized contributing factor rather than an unrelated coincidence.
  28. The correct answer is B (Coordinating timing, positioning, and monitoring needs across teams to minimize delays during the highest-risk portion of surgery.): placenta accreta spectrum cases benefit from careful multidisciplinary coordination among anesthesia, obstetrics, and interventional radiology to streamline timing and minimize delays during the highest bleeding risk period of surgery. Treating this as a routine cesarean delivery disregards the substantially elevated hemorrhage risk inherent to this diagnosis. Excluding interventional radiology from planning discussions undermines the coordinated, team-based approach that improves outcomes in these cases. Balloon catheter placement is an adjunct that reduces but does not eliminate the need for blood product availability given the potential for massive hemorrhage.
  29. The correct answer is A (Sensory changes from the high thoracic block causing altered perception of respiratory effort without true respiratory compromise.): a common and benign phenomenon during spinal anesthesia for cesarean delivery is a sensation of chest tightness or difficulty taking a deep breath due to blocked proprioceptive and sensory input from the chest wall, occurring despite normal oxygenation and stable vital signs. Amniotic fluid embolism would be expected to present with objective hemodynamic or respiratory compromise rather than isolated anxiety with stable vitals. Acute myocardial infarction, while important to keep in the differential in appropriate contexts, is far less likely than this well-recognized benign phenomenon in a young, otherwise healthy parturient. Tension pneumothorax would present with hypoxia, hemodynamic instability, and asymmetric breath sounds, none of which are described here.
  30. The correct answer is C (Early administration within the first several hours of hemorrhage onset improves survival by reducing bleeding-related mortality.): tranexamic acid administered early after the onset of postpartum hemorrhage has been shown in large trial data to reduce death due to bleeding, supporting its early inclusion in hemorrhage management rather than reserving it as a last resort. The premise of no proven mortality benefit contradicts established evidence supporting early use. Waiting until all other measures fail delays a treatment shown to be more effective when given early. Tranexamic acid is an antifibrinolytic agent that stabilizes clot formation and does not act on uterine smooth muscle contraction like oxytocin.
  31. The correct answer is D (Successful use of neuraxial anesthesia avoided the need to manage a known difficult airway altogether in this case.): choosing spinal anesthesia in a patient with a known difficult airway successfully avoided airway instrumentation entirely, which is precisely the risk-reduction benefit that makes neuraxial techniques preferable when feasible in such patients. The airway difficulty was in fact central to the anesthetic technique decision rather than irrelevant. General anesthesia would have exposed this patient to unnecessary airway management risk that was avoidable in this elective setting. Spinal anesthesia does not require airway instrumentation and therefore does not carry the same airway-related risks as general anesthesia.
  32. The correct answer is A (Standard induction doses given with prompt delivery generally minimize significant neonatal depression from these agents.): with standard induction doses and a prompt induction-to-delivery interval, clinically significant neonatal depression from propofol and succinylcholine is generally minimized, which supports the safety of rapid sequence induction for emergency cesarean delivery. Succinylcholine is a highly ionized, poorly lipid-soluble molecule that crosses the placenta only minimally, contrary to the premise of extensive transfer due to lipid solubility. While propofol does cross the placenta, standard doses with timely delivery do not reliably cause severe neonatal depression. The induction-to-delivery interval is in fact clinically relevant, since a prolonged interval can increase fetal exposure to anesthetic agents.
  33. The correct answer is B (Neuraxial anesthesia can generally be offered without additional restriction given the reassuring clinical picture.): a platelet count of 100,000/microL with normal coagulation studies, no bleeding history, and no identified hematologic disorder is generally considered acceptable for neuraxial anesthesia without additional restriction. Automatically denying the technique at this level is inconsistent with current evidence supporting individualized risk assessment. A mandatory hematology consultation for every patient at this reassuring level is not necessary and would create unnecessary delay. Preemptively planning general anesthesia disregards the reassuring clinical findings that support a neuraxial approach.
  34. The correct answer is D (Prompt perimortem delivery can improve maternal resuscitation by relieving aortocaval compression and improving venous return.): in maternal cardiac arrest, prompt perimortem cesarean delivery, ideally within minutes of arrest, relieves aortocaval compression and can improve the effectiveness of maternal resuscitation, in addition to potential fetal benefit. The premise that it should never be performed contradicts established resuscitation guidance for maternal cardiac arrest. Delivery timing is directly relevant to maternal resuscitation effectiveness, contrary to the premise of no impact. Delaying delivery for thirty minutes would substantially reduce the benefit, since earlier delivery is associated with better outcomes.
  35. The correct answer is C (Activate the massive transfusion protocol and communicate the evolving hemorrhage severity to the entire care team.): worsening tachycardia, rising lactate, and significant estimated blood loss are objective signs of evolving hemorrhagic shock that warrant proactive massive transfusion protocol activation and clear team communication rather than waiting for further deterioration. Remaining conscious does not rule out significant ongoing blood loss and compensated shock. Waiting for a hemoglobin result introduces dangerous delay, since hemoglobin often lags behind the true degree of acute blood loss. Attributing these findings solely to exertion ignores objective evidence of a hemorrhagic process requiring intervention.
  36. The correct answer is A (Using a smaller gauge, pencil-point spinal needle if a spinal or combined technique is chosen may reduce recurrence risk.): needle gauge and tip design are well established to influence postdural puncture headache risk, so selecting a smaller gauge, pencil-point needle when a spinal technique is used can help reduce the chance of recurrence in a patient with prior history. A prior postdural puncture headache is not an absolute contraindication to future neuraxial techniques when appropriate precautions are considered. Needle design clearly does affect headache risk, contrary to the premise of no relationship. Epidural techniques do carry a real, though generally low, risk of unintentional dural puncture regardless of operator skill.
  37. The correct answer is B (Malignant hyperthermia requiring immediate dantrolene administration and discontinuation of triggering agents.): a rapid rise in end-tidal carbon dioxide, tachycardia, and muscle rigidity following a triggering agent such as succinylcholine is characteristic of malignant hyperthermia and requires immediate discontinuation of triggering agents and dantrolene administration. This presentation is not a routine or expected response to succinylcholine and should prompt urgent evaluation rather than reassurance. Shivering would not typically produce this combination of hypermetabolic signs immediately following a triggering agent. Surgical stimulation alone does not explain the abrupt onset of rigidity and hypercarbia seen in this presentation.
  38. The correct answer is D (Proceed with cesarean delivery using the existing epidural extended to surgical anesthesia.): a functioning epidural that can be rapidly extended to an adequate surgical level is the preferred approach for urgent cesarean delivery, avoiding the risks of general anesthesia when a working neuraxial technique is already in place. Removing a functioning epidural in favor of general anesthesia would expose the patient to unnecessary airway and aspiration risk without benefit. Waiting an additional twenty minutes introduces unnecessary delay in an urgent situation where the block is already adequate. Performing an additional spinal anesthetic on top of a functioning epidural risks an unpredictably high or total spinal block.
  39. The correct answer is A (Maintain hemodynamic monitoring and readiness to proceed to surgical intervention such as laparotomy if tamponade fails.): balloon tamponade is a temporizing measure that does not guarantee hemorrhage control, so continued close hemodynamic monitoring and readiness to escalate to surgical intervention remain essential. Assuming resolution and discontinuing monitoring after balloon placement risks missing continued or recurrent bleeding. Uterotonics generally continue to play a supportive role and should not be reflexively discontinued once a mechanical device is placed. Blood product transfusion should continue as clinically indicated based on ongoing assessment rather than being withheld solely because a mechanical intervention has been attempted.
  40. The correct answer is D (Favor general anesthesia with full hemorrhage preparation given the combination of active bleeding and high-risk placentation.): active hemorrhage combined with suspected placenta previa and prior cesarean deliveries raises significant concern for major hemorrhage and possible placenta accreta spectrum, favoring general anesthesia with full massive transfusion readiness over neuraxial technique in this unstable, high-risk scenario. Proceeding with spinal anesthesia as a default choice does not account for the active bleeding and hemodynamic instability risk present here. Anesthetic technique must be determined before incision to ensure the patient is adequately prepared and monitored. This clinical picture carries substantially elevated hemorrhage risk compared to a routine repeat cesarean delivery without previa, so treating it as unchanged would be inappropriate.
  41. The correct answer is C (Classical vertical incision extending into the active upper uterine segment.): classical hysterotomy incisions extend into the actively contracting upper uterine segment and carry the highest rupture risk of any prior incision type, which is why trial of labor is contraindicated after a classical cesarean. Low transverse incisions are the safest for a subsequent trial of labor. A low vertical incision confined to the lower segment carries intermediate risk but is not the highest. Myomectomy without cavity entry generally does not increase rupture risk during labor.
  42. The correct answer is A (It permits rapid conversion to surgical anesthesia if emergency cesarean becomes necessary.): a functioning epidural catheter allows extension of analgesia to surgical anesthesia within minutes if uterine rupture or another emergency requires immediate cesarean delivery. Adequately dosed epidural analgesia does not reliably mask the breakthrough pain or fetal heart rate changes that often signal rupture, so vigilance remains essential. Continuous fetal monitoring is still required throughout labor regardless of analgesic technique. Epidural analgesia has no effect on the mechanical risk of scar dehiscence.
  43. The correct answer is D (Prolonged fetal heart rate deceleration or sudden bradycardia.): fetal heart rate abnormalities, particularly a sudden prolonged deceleration or bradycardia, are the most common and earliest sign of uterine rupture, often preceding maternal symptoms. Maternal hypotension from hemorrhage typically develops later as rupture progresses and blood loss accumulates. Fever is not a characteristic early feature of acute uterine rupture. Loss of contractions can occur but is a less reliable and less consistent early sign than fetal heart rate change.
  44. The correct answer is B (Immediate emergency cesarean delivery with rapid-sequence general anesthesia if needed.): suspected uterine rupture with fetal bradycardia is an obstetric emergency requiring immediate delivery, and general anesthesia is often chosen when there is maternal hemorrhage, instability, or insufficient time to extend an existing epidural. Delaying delivery to obtain further reassurance risks fetal hypoxic injury and worsening maternal hemorrhage. Tocolytics are not indicated and would worsen an already unstable situation. Placing additional monitoring equipment delays definitive treatment once rupture is suspected.
  45. The correct answer is A (Volatile inhalational general anesthesia titrated for uterine relaxation.): volatile anesthetics produce dose-dependent uterine relaxation that facilitates manual removal of a retained placenta, though the dose and duration must be minimized to limit subsequent uterine atony and hemorrhage. Oxytocin and methylergonovine are uterotonics that increase tone and would make manual extraction more difficult, not easier. Perineal local infiltration does not address the intrauterine pain and muscular resistance encountered during manual placental removal. Regional or general anesthesia with a relaxing agent is the standard approach.
  46. The correct answer is C (Intravenous or sublingual nitroglycerin.): nitroglycerin is a fast-acting, short-duration smooth muscle relaxant that can provide brief uterine relaxation for manual placental extraction while avoiding general anesthesia, though transient maternal hypotension should be anticipated. Oxytocin and carboprost are uterotonics that increase uterine tone and would worsen the resistance encountered during manual extraction. Calcium gluconate does not produce uterine relaxation and is instead used to treat magnesium toxicity or hyperkalemia.
  47. The correct answer is D (Increased risk of postpartum hemorrhage from uterine overdistension and atony.): multiple gestation causes significant uterine overdistension, which increases the risk of atony and postpartum hemorrhage after delivery, so anesthesiologists should anticipate the need for additional uterotonics and possible blood products. Aortocaval compression is worsened, not reduced, by the larger gravid uterus in multiple gestation. Uterine atony risk is increased rather than decreased. Maternal hypotension after neuraxial blockade tends to be more pronounced, not less, given greater baseline aortocaval compression.
  48. The correct answer is B (Rapidly dose the epidural to achieve dense surgical-level anesthesia.): internal podalic version and breech extraction of a second twin require prompt, dense anesthesia to allow uterine relaxation and intrauterine manipulation, so the epidural should be rapidly topped up with concentrated local anesthetic. Discontinuing the epidural would leave the patient without adequate analgesia for a painful, time-pressured obstetric intervention. Intravenous opioid alone provides insufficient anesthesia for intrauterine manipulation. Waiting for gradual deepening risks fetal compromise given the urgency of the situation.
  49. The correct answer is A (Type and crossmatch blood products in anticipation of postpartum hemorrhage.): higher-order multiple gestations carry a substantially elevated risk of uterine atony and postpartum hemorrhage, so crossmatched blood products, large-bore intravenous access, and readiness to activate a massive transfusion protocol should be arranged preoperatively. Avoiding adequate intravenous access would compromise the ability to resuscitate quickly if hemorrhage occurs. Oxytocin doses are not reduced for multiple gestation and are often needed at standard or higher doses. Massive transfusion protocol planning should be proactively discussed, not omitted, given the elevated risk.
  50. The correct answer is D (Uterine inversion, requiring deep anesthesia with a uterine relaxant for reduction.): acute uterine inversion presents with a fundus that is no longer palpable, a mass at the introitus, and profound vagally mediated hypotension and bradycardia, and prompt manual replacement often requires deep anesthesia with a relaxant such as a volatile agent or nitroglycerin. Uterine rupture typically presents with hemorrhage and abdominal or fetal findings rather than this classic inversion picture. Amniotic fluid embolism causes acute cardiopulmonary collapse and coagulopathy rather than a palpable introital mass. Retained products alone would not cause the described hemodynamic instability or loss of the fundus.
  51. The correct answer is A (Recommend scheduled repeat cesarean delivery rather than trial of labor.): a prior classical or vertical hysterotomy extending into the upper uterine segment carries an unacceptably high risk of catastrophic rupture during labor, so scheduled repeat cesarean delivery is recommended rather than a trial of labor. Continuous fetal monitoring alone does not sufficiently mitigate this high rupture risk to justify labor. Avoiding oxytocin augmentation does not eliminate the fundamental risk posed by the prior classical incision. Epidural analgesia does not reduce the mechanical risk of rupture in this setting.
  52. The correct answer is B (Activate the massive transfusion protocol with balanced blood component ratios.): ongoing hemorrhage with hemodynamic instability despite initial transfusion warrants activation of a massive transfusion protocol using balanced ratios of red cells, plasma, and platelets to prevent dilutional coagulopathy. Giving only a single additional unit is inadequate for a patient in ongoing hemorrhagic shock. Waiting for full coagulation results before treating an actively bleeding, unstable patient delays lifesaving therapy. Relying solely on crystalloid worsens dilutional coagulopathy and does not replace lost oxygen-carrying capacity or clotting factors.
  53. The correct answer is C (One twin may be anemic and hypovolemic while the other is polycythemic.): twin-to-twin transfusion syndrome produces unequal blood flow through shared placental vascular connections, leaving the donor twin anemic and hypovolemic and the recipient twin polycythemic and volume overloaded, which anesthesiologists should anticipate for neonatal resuscitation planning. The two fetuses do not share identical volume status, which is the defining feature of the syndrome. Maternal coagulation is not directly altered by this fetal-fetal transfusion process. Neuraxial anesthesia remains an appropriate technique for cesarean delivery in this condition and is not contraindicated.
  54. The correct answer is D (Apply left uterine displacement by tilting the table or using a wedge.): left uterine displacement, achieved with a table tilt or hip wedge, reduces aortocaval compression from the enlarged gravid uterus and is especially important in multiple gestation where compression is more pronounced. Head elevation alone does not adequately relieve compression from the uterus on the great vessels. Opioids do not address the mechanical cause of hypotension and can cause additional respiratory depression. Waiting until hypotension develops is reactive rather than preventive and risks uteroplacental hypoperfusion.
  55. The correct answer is C (Combined general anesthesia with large-bore access, arterial line, and blood products available.): suspected placenta accreta spectrum with planned cesarean hysterectomy carries a high risk of massive hemorrhage, so general anesthesia, multiple large-bore intravenous lines, invasive arterial monitoring, and immediately available crossmatched blood products should be arranged in advance. A single peripheral line is inadequate for anticipated massive transfusion needs. Spinal anesthesia alone is risky because the procedure may be prolonged and hemodynamically unstable, requiring conversion to general anesthesia. Placenta accreta spectrum is frequently suspected antenatally through imaging, allowing proactive rather than purely intraoperative planning.
  56. The correct answer is B (Increased volume of distribution with prolonged elimination half-life.): aging increases the proportion of body fat relative to lean mass, which expands the volume of distribution for lipophilic drugs like fentanyl and midazolam and prolongs their elimination half-life, leading to prolonged sedation with repeated dosing. Volume of distribution for these agents increases rather than decreases with age-related body composition changes. Hepatic blood flow and metabolic capacity typically decline with age, not increase, further reducing clearance. Volume of distribution is directly affected by the shift toward greater fat mass, not unchanged.
  57. The correct answer is C (MAC decreases by approximately six percent per decade after age 40.): minimum alveolar concentration for volatile anesthetics declines predictably with advancing age, decreasing by roughly six percent per decade beyond age forty, so elderly patients require less volatile agent to achieve the same depth of anesthesia. MAC does not increase with age, since the relationship is inverse. MAC is not constant across the lifespan and shows a well-documented age-related decline. Body habitus can influence dosing of other agents, but the age-related MAC decline is a distinct, well-established pharmacodynamic phenomenon.
  58. The correct answer is D (Preexisting cognitive impairment identified before surgery.): baseline cognitive impairment is one of the strongest and most consistently identified predictors of postoperative delirium, and his fluctuating attention and disorganized thinking are classic delirium features superimposed on that vulnerability. Advanced age contributes to risk, but it is the combination with preexisting cognitive impairment that confers the greatest predictive strength, not age in isolation. Short-acting neuromuscular blockers are not established independent risk factors for delirium when appropriately reversed. Regional anesthesia techniques such as nerve blocks are generally associated with a lower, not higher, risk of postoperative delirium compared with general anesthesia.
  59. The correct answer is A (Minimizing benzodiazepine use and using multicomponent nonpharmacologic delirium prevention protocols.): avoiding benzodiazepines and implementing multicomponent strategies such as early mobilization, reorientation, sleep hygiene, sensory aid use, and adequate pain control have the strongest evidence base for reducing postoperative delirium in older adults. Routine prophylactic antipsychotic administration is not supported as a standard preventive strategy and carries its own risks. Deeper anesthesia with higher volatile concentrations has been associated with increased, not decreased, delirium risk in some studies. Delaying mobilization is counterproductive, since early mobilization is itself a component of effective delirium prevention.
  60. The correct answer is B (Combined anticholinergic, sedative, and opioid use increases delirium and oversedation risk.): polypharmacy involving anticholinergic agents, benzodiazepines, and opioids has a cumulative burden that significantly increases the risk of postoperative delirium, oversedation, and adverse cognitive outcomes in older patients, warranting careful perioperative medication reconciliation. These medication classes are highly relevant to anesthetic planning, not irrelevant. Polypharmacy effects extend well beyond hemodynamics to include cognitive and delirium risk. Abrupt discontinuation of chronic medications, particularly benzodiazepines and opioids, risks withdrawal and should instead be carefully managed rather than stopped abruptly.
  61. The correct answer is D (Frailty phenotype as a marker of physiologic reserve.): the combination of slow gait speed, weak grip strength, unintentional weight loss, exhaustion, and low physical activity constitutes the frailty phenotype, which identifies patients with diminished physiologic reserve at higher risk for postoperative complications independent of chronic disease burden. ASA physical status classification is a general anesthetic risk categorization unrelated to these specific physical performance measures. The Revised Cardiac Risk Index focuses on cardiac risk factors such as ischemic heart disease and renal insufficiency. The Charlson comorbidity index quantifies chronic disease burden using diagnoses rather than physical performance testing.
  62. The correct answer is C (Increased postoperative morbidity, mortality, and loss of functional independence after major surgery.): frailty is an independent predictor of increased postoperative morbidity, mortality, prolonged hospitalization, and loss of functional independence, even when chronological age is identical between patients. Frail patients do not experience improved recovery or shorter hospital stays compared with robust patients undergoing similar surgery. The likelihood of complications is increased, not decreased, in frail individuals facing the same surgical stress. Frailty assessment exists precisely because outcomes differ substantially between frail and nonfrail patients of the same age, contradicting the idea of no measurable difference.
  63. The correct answer is A (Reduce initial doses and extend dosing intervals with careful titration.): age-related declines in hepatic blood flow, enzyme activity, and clearance reduce the metabolism of many opioids, so lower initial doses, longer intervals, and careful titration to effect help avoid drug accumulation and oversedation. Standard adult dosing intervals may lead to excessive drug accumulation given reduced clearance in this population. There is no physiologic basis for increasing doses above standard adult dosing, since elderly patients typically show increased, not decreased, sensitivity to opioids. Avoiding opioid analgesia entirely is unnecessary and would leave the patient inadequately treated for pain; cautious titration is preferred instead.
  64. The correct answer is B (Postoperative cognitive dysfunction persisting beyond the immediate recovery period.): postoperative cognitive dysfunction refers to a more subtle, persistent decline in memory and executive function that can last weeks to months after surgery, distinct from delirium's acute fluctuating course and altered level of consciousness. Delirium is characterized by fluctuating attention and consciousness typically arising within days of surgery, not weeks later without those features. Age-related change is a consideration, but the temporal relationship to a recent surgical and anesthetic exposure supports a postoperative etiology rather than coincidental aging. Anesthesia awareness produces distinct recall of intraoperative events and psychological distress, not a generalized memory and concentration decline.
  65. The correct answer is D (Availability of transcutaneous or transvenous pacing in case of progression.): a higher-grade conduction abnormality such as second-degree heart block carries a risk of progression to complete heart block under anesthesia, so ensuring immediate access to transcutaneous or transvenous pacing is an important precaution even when the patient is currently asymptomatic. Being asymptomatic does not eliminate the risk of intraoperative progression to a more severe block requiring intervention. Necessary hip fracture surgery in an elderly patient is generally not delayed for elective pacemaker placement unless hemodynamically significant block is present. Avoiding all vagal stimuli is impractical and does not substitute for having pacing readily available.
  66. The correct answer is A (Prolonged duration of action with slower elimination requiring careful reversal confirmation.): reduced renal clearance in older adults can prolong the duration of action of partially renally eliminated neuromuscular blocking agents, increasing the risk of residual paralysis, so objective neuromuscular monitoring and confirmation of adequate reversal are especially important. Renal decline reduces, rather than increases, drug elimination, so duration of action is prolonged, not shortened. Rocuronium relies partly on renal and biliary excretion in addition to hepatic uptake, so renal function is clinically relevant to its clearance. Elderly patients do not develop resistance to neuromuscular blockers; altered pharmacodynamics can instead increase sensitivity.
  67. The correct answer is C (Enrolling him in a multidisciplinary prehabilitation program before surgery.): prehabilitation programs that combine exercise training, nutritional optimization, and psychological support before elective surgery have been shown to improve functional reserve and postoperative outcomes in frail patients with adequate lead time before their procedure. Proceeding without any intervention forgoes an opportunity to improve his physiologic reserve before a major stressor. Strict bed rest would further deteriorate muscle mass and functional capacity, worsening frailty rather than improving it. Increasing baseline opioids preoperatively has no established role in improving surgical tolerance and could increase sedation and fall risk.
  68. The correct answer is B (Randomized evidence has not shown a consistent, clinically meaningful difference in delirium incidence between the two anesthetic techniques.): large randomized trials comparing spinal and general anesthesia for hip fracture surgery have not demonstrated a consistent, clinically meaningful difference in postoperative delirium rates between the two techniques, suggesting other perioperative factors matter more than anesthetic type alone. Spinal anesthesia has not been shown to definitively eliminate delirium risk. General anesthesia has likewise not been proven superior for delirium prevention. Anesthetic technique is one factor among many, but patient-related factors such as baseline cognitive status and frailty tend to have greater influence on delirium risk than the specific technique chosen.
  69. The correct answer is D (Appropriate cessation intervals must be observed to reduce spinal hematoma risk.): anticoagulated patients require adherence to established cessation intervals before neuraxial anesthesia to minimize the risk of spinal or epidural hematoma, a rare but potentially devastating complication, which is particularly important in older patients on chronic anticoagulation. Neuraxial blockade cannot be safely performed without regard to anticoagulant timing. Antiplatelet agents also require attention, but chronic anticoagulants such as warfarin carry their own specific and often longer required cessation intervals. Anticoagulation status remains clinically relevant throughout the perioperative period, not just before arrival in the operating room.
  70. The correct answer is C (Individualize dosing and monitoring based on her physiologic reserve rather than age alone.): geriatric anesthetic care should be guided by an individualized assessment of physiologic reserve, comorbidities, and functional status rather than chronological age or procedure type alone, since even brief low-stress procedures can be affected by reduced cardiac reserve and altered drug handling. Treating her identically to a healthy young adult ignores well-documented age-related pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic changes. Assuming minimal risk based solely on procedure type overlooks patient-specific vulnerabilities that persist regardless of surgical invasiveness. Preoperative assessment remains valuable even for brief procedures to identify and mitigate individual risk factors.
  71. The correct answer is C (Scheduled acetaminophen, NSAID if not contraindicated, and gabapentinoid with opioid for breakthrough pain.): multimodal analgesia combines agents with different mechanisms to achieve additive or synergistic analgesia while reducing total opioid exposure. This approach lowers opioid-related side effects such as sedation, nausea, and respiratory depression compared to opioid monotherapy. Reserving opioids for breakthrough pain rather than as the sole agent is the modern standard of enhanced recovery protocols.
  72. The correct answer is D (Complex regional pain syndrome.): CRPS is diagnosed clinically using the Budapest criteria, requiring disproportionate pain along with sensory, vasomotor, sudomotor/edema, and motor/trophic changes not explained by another condition. The chronic timeline, allodynia, and color changes without infection or acute compartment findings are characteristic. Early recognition allows prompt initiation of physical therapy and multidisciplinary pain management to prevent chronic disability.
  73. The correct answer is B (Serotonin-norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors, such as duloxetine, which help modulate central pain processing pathways.): SNRIs and certain gabapentinoids are FDA-approved and evidence-supported for fibromyalgia because they modulate central pain processing pathways rather than targeting peripheral inflammation. Opioids are not recommended given lack of efficacy and risk of dependence in this centrally mediated pain disorder. Corticosteroids and NSAIDs are ineffective since fibromyalgia is not an inflammatory or structural process.
  74. The correct answer is B (Add a weak opioid, such as codeine or low-dose tramadol, to the existing nonopioid regimen.): the WHO analgesic ladder progresses stepwise from nonopioids, to weak opioids for mild-moderate pain, to strong opioids for severe pain, adjusting based on response. Jumping directly to a high-potency transdermal opioid in an opioid-naive patient risks overdose and is not standard escalation. NSAIDs have a ceiling effect and dose escalation beyond that ceiling increases toxicity without added benefit.
  75. The correct answer is C (Opioid-induced hyperalgesia, a state of central sensitization in which pain paradoxically worsens with escalating opioid doses.): this state involves central sensitization causing increased pain sensitivity that paradoxically worsens with escalating opioid doses, distinguishing it from tolerance, where the same dose simply becomes less effective without diffuse spread. Withdrawal produces autonomic symptoms rather than a diffuse pain-sensitization pattern. Management often requires opioid rotation, dose reduction, or tapering rather than further escalation.
  76. The correct answer is D (It may provide short-to-intermediate term relief of radicular leg pain but limited effect on chronic axial back pain.): epidural steroid injections are most effective for radicular pain from nerve root inflammation, with benefit typically lasting weeks to a few months rather than being curative. They are not primarily indicated for purely axial mechanical back pain without a radicular component. Injections are generally used as an adjunct alongside physical therapy rather than a replacement for it.
  77. The correct answer is A (Radiofrequency ablation of the medial branch nerves.): concordant relief from two separate diagnostic medial branch blocks confirms the facet joint as the pain generator and supports proceeding to radiofrequency ablation for longer-lasting denervation. Spinal cord stimulation is reserved for neuropathic pain syndromes refractory to other treatments, not confirmed facet pain. Surgical fusion is not the appropriate next step after a positive diagnostic block for facet-mediated pain.
  78. The correct answer is B (Spinal cord stimulator trial.): spinal cord stimulation is indicated for chronic refractory neuropathic pain, including failed back surgery syndrome, when conservative and interventional therapies have not provided adequate relief. A percutaneous trial allows assessment of efficacy before permanent implantation. Repeat surgery without a new structural indication and unlimited opioid escalation are not appropriate next steps given limited evidence and risk of harm.
  79. The correct answer is A (Spinal cord stimulation, an established longer-term option for patients with refractory CRPS symptoms.): spinal cord stimulation has an established evidence base for refractory CRPS when sympathetic blocks and rehabilitation provide inadequate or transient relief. A single-shot peripheral nerve block or Bier block is primarily diagnostic or short-acting rather than a durable therapy. Repeated intramuscular corticosteroids into the limb are not a standard or evidence-supported treatment for CRPS.
  80. The correct answer is A (Continue buprenorphine perioperatively and use multimodal analgesia with full agonist opioids as needed for breakthrough pain.): current guidance favors continuing buprenorphine through the perioperative period while layering multimodal nonopioid analgesics and using full agonist opioids for breakthrough pain when needed, since buprenorphine's partial agonism does not preclude additional analgesia. Abrupt discontinuation risks withdrawal and destabilization of recovery. Naltrexone is a full antagonist and inappropriate to substitute acutely before surgery.
  81. The correct answer is C (Continue the patient's home methadone dose and add additional analgesics as needed for acute pain.): methadone should generally be continued at the maintenance dose perioperatively to prevent withdrawal and maintain opioid tolerance stability, with supplemental multimodal analgesia and additional opioids used for acute surgical pain. Abruptly stopping methadone risks withdrawal and undertreated pain. Arbitrarily doubling the dose increases risk of respiratory depression and QT prolongation without proven analgesic benefit.
  82. The correct answer is D (Preserved quadriceps motor strength allowing earlier mobilization.): the adductor canal block targets predominantly sensory branches distal to the femoral triangle, sparing motor function to the quadriceps and facilitating earlier ambulation and reduced fall risk compared with a traditional femoral nerve block. It does not provide complete lower extremity motor blockade, which is neither desired nor advantageous here. Systemic multimodal analgesics are still typically required alongside the block.
  83. The correct answer is C (Gabapentinoids, such as gabapentin or pregabalin, which modulate calcium channel activity involved in neuropathic pain.): gabapentinoids, along with tricyclic antidepressants and topical lidocaine, are established first-line therapies for postherpetic neuralgia because they modulate calcium channel activity involved in neuropathic pain signaling. NSAIDs and acetaminophen target nociceptive rather than neuropathic pain and offer limited benefit here. Benzodiazepines have no established role in neuropathic pain treatment and carry dependence risk.
  84. The correct answer is C (Lower total opioid dose achieves effective analgesia with fewer systemic side effects.): intrathecal delivery places opioid directly near spinal receptors, allowing markedly lower total doses to achieve analgesia comparable to much higher systemic doses, reducing side effects such as sedation and constipation. Tolerance can still develop with intrathecal opioids over time. Ongoing monitoring for pump function, infection, and granuloma formation remains necessary after implantation.
  85. The correct answer is A (Prioritize multimodal, function-focused treatment with opioids reserved as adjuncts after risk-benefit reassessment.): current guidelines emphasize functional improvement, nonopioid and nonpharmacologic modalities, and periodic reassessment of opioid risks and benefits rather than indefinite dose escalation without reassessment. Complete elimination of pain is often an unrealistic goal and pursuing it can drive unsafe opioid escalation. Physical therapy remains a cornerstone of chronic pain management despite transient discomfort during initiation.
  86. The correct answer is A (Topical capsaicin or lidocaine patches as adjunctive therapy.): topical agents such as capsaicin or lidocaine can be added for localized neuropathic pain refractory to oral first-line agents, providing additional relief with a favorable systemic side effect profile. Corticosteroids are not indicated for diabetic peripheral neuropathy. Opioids are not recommended as a first add-on given modest evidence and substantial risk in chronic neuropathic pain.
  87. The correct answer is D (Attenuation of the surgical stress response and improved pulmonary function postoperatively.): thoracic epidural analgesia blunts neuroendocrine stress responses and improves respiratory mechanics by reducing splinting from incisional pain, supporting earlier mobilization within enhanced recovery protocols. It reduces but does not guarantee prevention of ileus in every patient. It has no effect on unrelated preexisting chronic pain conditions and does not remove the need for postoperative monitoring.
  88. The correct answer is B (Carbamazepine, a sodium channel stabilizer that reduces the paroxysmal neuronal firing responsible for trigeminal neuralgia pain.): carbamazepine is the established first-line agent for trigeminal neuralgia because it stabilizes sodium channels and reduces the paroxysmal neuronal firing responsible for the characteristic shock-like pain. Opioids and NSAIDs are largely ineffective for this specific neuropathic pain pattern. Corticosteroids are not standard monotherapy, though they may have a limited adjunctive role in specific inflammatory etiologies.
  89. The correct answer is C (Adequately treat acute pain with a multimodal plan including opioids if needed, while closely monitoring for relapse risk.): patients with opioid use disorder in remission deserve adequate acute pain control, and undertreatment can increase distress and relapse risk, so a multimodal approach with judicious opioid use and close monitoring is recommended. Blanket opioid avoidance is not evidence-based and can worsen outcomes. Dismissing reported pain as exaggerated reflects bias rather than clinical assessment and can lead to inadequate care.
  90. The correct answer is A (Peripherally acting mu-opioid receptor antagonists, which block gut receptors without crossing the blood-brain barrier significantly.): drugs such as methylnaltrexone or naloxegol block opioid receptors in the gut without crossing the blood-brain barrier significantly, relieving constipation while preserving central analgesic effect. High-dose naloxone would reverse analgesia and precipitate withdrawal. Increasing opioid dosing would worsen constipation rather than treat it, and corticosteroids have no established role for this indication.
  91. The correct answer is B (Reassess for a correctable surgical or mechanical cause of the pain.): persistent severe pain despite optimized multimodal therapy warrants reassessment for complications such as hematoma, hardware malposition, or compartment syndrome before simply escalating opioids. Converting to a long-acting opioid without addressing an underlying cause could mask a serious complication. Nonopioid adjuncts should generally be continued as part of the multimodal strategy, not discontinued.
  92. The correct answer is D (To assess and treat a sympathetically maintained component of the patient's pain.): lumbar sympathetic blocks interrupt sympathetic outflow to the lower extremity, helping determine whether a sympathetically maintained pain component contributes to CRPS symptoms and potentially providing therapeutic relief. It does not cause motor blockade since sympathetic fibers are not motor nerves. It is not a diagnostic test for nerve laceration and does not replace physical therapy, which remains essential to functional recovery.
  93. The correct answer is B (Use a validated equianalgesic conversion and reduce the calculated dose to account for incomplete cross-tolerance.): opioid rotation requires equianalgesic tables combined with a dose reduction, typically twenty-five to fifty percent, to account for incomplete cross-tolerance between opioids and interpatient variability. Failing to reduce the dose risks oversedation and respiratory depression. Transdermal fentanyl also has a delayed onset, so appropriate overlap with the prior opioid is needed during transition.
  94. The correct answer is B (Continue the baseline opioid dose perioperatively and add additional analgesics for surgical pain on top of it.): patients on chronic opioid therapy should generally continue their baseline regimen through surgery to prevent withdrawal and maintain tolerance stability, with additional multimodal and opioid analgesics layered on for acute surgical pain. Abrupt discontinuation risks withdrawal and severe undertreated pain. The chronic baseline dose alone is insufficient for new acute surgical pain given established opioid tolerance.
  95. The correct answer is D (Proceed with permanent implantation of the spinal cord stimulator system.): a successful trial demonstrating substantial pain reduction and functional benefit is the standard criterion for proceeding to permanent implantation of the stimulator system. Trial success reflects a favorable response, not evidence that the pain lacks an organic basis. Moving to an intrathecal pump is not indicated when the stimulator trial itself was effective.
  96. The correct answer is A (Consider opioid rotation or a structured dose taper with multimodal adjuncts.): tolerance with diminishing benefit is often addressed by rotating to a different opioid, which may partially restore analgesic responsiveness due to incomplete cross-tolerance, or by tapering with reinforcement of nonopioid multimodal strategies. Continuously escalating the same opioid increases adverse effect risk without reliably improving analgesia. Adding a second full agonist opioid increases risk without addressing the underlying tolerance mechanism.
  97. The correct answer is A (A pudendal nerve block performed with local anesthetic and corticosteroid to target the affected nerve.): pudendal nerve blockade targets the specific nerve implicated in pudendal neuralgia, serving both to confirm the diagnosis through temporary relief and to provide therapeutic benefit from the corticosteroid component. Stellate ganglion block targets the upper extremity and head sympathetic chain, celiac plexus block targets visceral abdominal pain, and intercostal blocks target thoracic wall pain, none of which are anatomically appropriate here.
  98. The correct answer is D (Celiac plexus neurolysis.): the celiac plexus transmits visceral pain signals from upper abdominal structures including the pancreas, and neurolytic blockade can significantly reduce pain and opioid requirements in pancreatic cancer patients. Cervical epidural injections and lumbar medial branch ablation target spinal and facet pain unrelated to visceral abdominal pathology. Stellate ganglion block targets the head, neck, and upper extremity sympathetic supply, not abdominal viscera.
  99. The correct answer is C (Buprenorphine's high receptor affinity displaces the full agonist at the mu-opioid receptor while providing only partial activation.): buprenorphine binds mu-opioid receptors with high affinity but only partial intrinsic activity, so administering it too soon after a full agonist can displace that agonist and produce a net drop in receptor activation, precipitating withdrawal. Buprenorphine does have significant mu-receptor affinity, making option B incorrect. It is a partial agonist with a ceiling effect on respiratory depression, not a full agonist, and its withdrawal-precipitating mechanism is not related to NMDA receptor antagonism.
  100. The correct answer is D (High-frequency or burst stimulation paradigms.): newer stimulation paradigms such as high-frequency stimulation and burst stimulation can provide effective analgesia while minimizing or eliminating the paresthesias associated with conventional tonic stimulation, improving patient comfort and tolerability. Simply increasing tonic amplitude would likely worsen paresthesias rather than resolve them. A mechanical traction device is unrelated to spinal cord stimulator programming and does not address the reported issue.
  101. The correct answer is A (Individualized dosing based on the patient's prior effective doses with frequent reassessment.): patients with sickle cell disease often have chronic pain and prior opioid exposure, so individualized dosing informed by their known effective regimen with frequent reassessment provides more appropriate and timely analgesia during a vaso-occlusive crisis. A fixed low dose may substantially undertreat pain in tolerant patients. Vaso-occlusive crises are a clinical diagnosis, and withholding analgesia pending imaging delays necessary treatment and increases suffering.
  102. The correct answer is D (Effective management addresses physical, psychological, and social factors together rather than pain alone.): the biopsychosocial model recognizes that chronic pain is influenced by physical pathology, psychological state, and social context, and effective treatment integrates physical therapy, psychological interventions, and pharmacologic management. Comorbid depression and psychosocial stressors can significantly worsen pain perception and disability even with an identified structural cause. Cognitive behavioral therapy has strong evidence for improving pain coping and functional outcomes in chronic pain.
  103. The correct answer is B (Preexisting chronic pain condition and high preoperative anxiety.): preoperative chronic pain, catastrophizing, and heightened anxiety are established risk factors for both severe acute postoperative pain and progression to chronic postsurgical pain. Younger age without prior surgery and shorter procedure duration are not the primary established risk factors for this outcome. Use of regional anesthesia is generally protective against chronic postsurgical pain rather than a risk factor.
  104. The correct answer is C (Evaluate for reversible contributing factors and consider methadone dose adjustment or alternative opioid.): methadone is associated with dose-dependent QTc prolongation and torsades de pointes risk, so a prolonged QTc warrants evaluation of contributing electrolyte or drug interactions and consideration of dose reduction or rotation to another opioid.
