Abrupt discontinuation risks withdrawal and destabilization without addressing the cardiac risk appropriately. Increasing the dose would further worsen QTc prolongation given methadone's established dose-dependent effect.
  105. The correct answer is C (Trigger point injections using local anesthetic or dry needling, directed at the palpable taut muscle bands.): trigger point injections directly target hyperirritable muscle bands responsible for localized myofascial pain and referred symptoms, providing focal relief and facilitating physical therapy participation. Systemic opioids are not first-line for myofascial pain given limited evidence and risk profile. Spinal cord stimulation and celiac plexus blockade are reserved for different pain mechanisms and are not appropriate for isolated myofascial trigger points.
  106. The correct answer is C (Use a short-acting opioid with rapid onset dosed as a fraction of the total daily opioid requirement.): breakthrough cancer pain is typically managed with an immediate-release opioid calculated as a percentage, commonly around ten to twenty percent, of the total daily long-acting opioid dose, providing timely relief. Relying solely on nonopioids is often inadequate for severe breakthrough cancer pain. Doubling the long-acting dose does not address the acute nature of breakthrough pain and increases overdose risk.
  107. The correct answer is D (Evaluate for infection and remove the trial leads if infection is confirmed or strongly suspected.): fever and erythema at a percutaneous lead site raise concern for infection, which requires prompt evaluation and typically lead removal to prevent progression to deeper infection such as epidural abscess. Proceeding to permanent implantation in the setting of possible active infection risks seeding a permanent device. Fever and erythema are not expected normal findings and should not be dismissed.
  108. The correct answer is B (They can provide targeted analgesia while minimizing systemic opioid exposure and associated relapse risk.): interventional and regional techniques deliver analgesia directly to the pain source, reducing reliance on systemic opioids and thereby lowering relapse risk in patients with a history of opioid use disorder. No intervention can completely eliminate relapse risk, since relapse is multifactorial. These techniques are appropriate for and often specifically beneficial to patients with substance use disorder history, not restricted away from them.
  109. The correct answer is A (New progressive myelopathic symptoms with imaging showing a mass at the catheter tip.): intrathecal catheter tip granulomas classically present with worsening pain despite prior good control along with new neurologic deficits, and imaging confirms an inflammatory mass at the catheter tip, particularly associated with high-concentration opioid infusions. A normal MRI with improving examination would argue against this diagnosis. A superficial pocket site infection is a separate device-related complication unrelated to catheter tip granuloma formation.
  110. The correct answer is C (A thoracic epidural or paravertebral block, which covers the dermatomal distribution of the thoracotomy incision.): thoracic epidural analgesia or paravertebral blockade effectively covers the dermatomal distribution of thoracotomy incision pain, reducing opioid requirements and improving respiratory mechanics by decreasing splinting. An ankle block is anatomically irrelevant to thoracic surgical pain. Stellate ganglion and lumbar sympathetic blocks target sympathetically mediated pain in the upper extremity or lower extremity respectively, not thoracic incisional pain.
  111. The correct answer is D (A structured, graded aerobic exercise program, which has strong evidence for improving pain and function.): graded aerobic exercise has strong evidence for improving pain, fatigue, and overall function in fibromyalgia and is considered a cornerstone of nonpharmacologic management. Prolonged bed rest and activity avoidance tend to worsen deconditioning and pain over time. Opioids lack strong evidence in fibromyalgia and are not recommended as an isolated treatment strategy.
  112. The correct answer is A (Compulsive use with loss of control over dosing despite negative consequences.): opioid use disorder is characterized by compulsive use, loss of control, and continued use despite harm, which is distinct from physical dependence. Tolerance and withdrawal are expected physiologic phenomena that can occur with appropriate chronic opioid therapy without indicating a use disorder. Stable benefit at a consistent dose reflects appropriate therapeutic use rather than a substance use disorder.
  113. The correct answer is B (Bisphosphonates, which reduce osteoclast-mediated bone resorption and lower the risk of skeletal-related events from metastases.): bisphosphonates reduce osteoclast-mediated bone resorption and have demonstrated efficacy in reducing pain and skeletal-related events from bone metastases, making them a useful adjuvant to opioid therapy. Benzodiazepines have no established analgesic role for bone pain and carry sedation risk. Anticholinergics and beta-blockers do not target the pathophysiology of malignant bone pain.
  114. The correct answer is C (Central sensitization and psychological factors can amplify pain perception independent of new tissue injury.): central sensitization involves heightened central nervous system responsiveness, and psychological stress can amplify this process, increasing pain perception without new peripheral tissue damage. Pain is modulated centrally as well as peripherally, so peripheral nociceptor activity alone does not fully explain chronic pain experience. Worsening symptoms in chronic pain do not automatically necessitate repeat imaging without new clinical indications.
  115. The correct answer is D (Reduction of local nerve root inflammation and edema around the compressed nerve roots.): epidural steroid injections work by delivering corticosteroid to reduce local inflammation and edema around compressed or irritated nerve roots, providing symptomatic relief of radicular symptoms. They do not mechanically widen the spinal canal or reverse the underlying degenerative stenosis. They also do not regenerate degenerated disc tissue, since the mechanism is anti-inflammatory rather than structural or regenerative.
  116. The correct answer is B (Higher than usual doses of full agonist opioid may be needed due to buprenorphine's receptor occupancy.): because buprenorphine occupies mu-opioid receptors with high affinity, full agonist opioids added for breakthrough pain may require higher than typical doses and closer titration to achieve adequate analgesia. Full agonists can still provide some analgesic effect even with buprenorphine present, so option A is incorrect. Continuing buprenorphine perioperatively with supplemental full agonist analgesia is an accepted and increasingly preferred approach rather than an absolute contraindication or requiring weeks of discontinuation.
  117. The correct answer is B (Transition to a strong opioid such as morphine or oxycodone with continued nonopioid adjuncts.): the WHO ladder's third step introduces strong opioids for moderate to severe pain unresponsive to step two agents, continuing nonopioid adjuncts as tolerated for additive effect. Reverting to nonopioids alone would undertreat progressive cancer pain. Intrathecal delivery is generally reserved for cases refractory to optimized systemic opioid therapy, not bypassed before a trial of systemic strong opioids.
  118. The correct answer is A (Spinal cord infarction from inadvertent injection into a radicular artery.): transforaminal injections carry a rare but serious risk of spinal cord infarction if particulate steroid is inadvertently injected into a radicular artery feeding the spinal cord, a risk minimized by using nonparticulate steroid and careful technique with imaging guidance. The procedure does not guarantee immediate permanent resolution of symptoms. Adhesive arachnoiditis and universal permanent paralysis are not expected outcomes and do not occur in all or even most patients.
  119. The correct answer is D (Additive central nervous system depression including sedation and respiratory depression.): gabapentinoids and opioids both cause central nervous system depression, and combining them has been associated with additive sedation and increased risk of respiratory depression, warranting careful monitoring and dose titration. This combination is not specifically associated with hypertension or hyperthermia. Serotonin syndrome relates to serotonergic agents rather than gabapentin and opioids, and it does not occur in every patient on this combination.
  120. The correct answer is A (Correlation between the imaging findings and the patient's specific reported pain pattern and examination.): appropriate candidacy for disc-directed interventional therapy depends on concordance between imaging abnormalities and the patient's clinical presentation, since incidental disc findings are common in asymptomatic individuals. Imaging abnormalities alone without symptom correlation do not justify intervention. Physical therapy and conservative measures should generally be attempted before considering more invasive interventional options, not avoided.
  121. The correct answer is A (Budapest clinical criteria requiring symptoms and signs across multiple categories.): CRPS type 1 is diagnosed clinically using the Budapest criteria, which require continuing pain plus at least one symptom in three of four categories and one sign in two of four categories, without evidence of a major nerve lesion. A single block response, imaging findings, or a pain questionnaire score can support but never replace this clinical construct. The distinction between type 1 (no identifiable nerve injury) and type 2 (with nerve injury) also depends on this same clinical framework rather than a lab or imaging cutoff.
  122. The correct answer is B (Mirror therapy providing visual feedback of the intact limb.): mirror therapy uses a reflected image of the intact limb to create visual feedback that appears to resolve the mismatch between motor intention and sensory absence believed to drive phantom limb pain. Multiple trials support its use as an adjunct alongside gabapentinoids or antidepressants. Limb elevation, prolonged bed rest, and stimulation of the unaffected side address stump comfort or unrelated targets rather than the central remapping process underlying phantom pain.
  123. The correct answer is C (Carbamazepine titrated to an effective and tolerated dose.): carbamazepine is the established first-line agent for classic trigeminal neuralgia and produces substantial pain reduction in the majority of patients, distinguishing this diagnosis from other neuropathic facial pain syndromes. Gabapentin and tricyclics are used more for postherpetic or diabetic neuropathic pain and are second-line here. Opioids are generally ineffective for this paroxysmal, shock-like pain pattern and are not appropriate first-line therapy.
  124. The correct answer is B (A topical lidocaine patch applied to the affected dermatome, targeting the localized mechanical allodynia.): topical lidocaine is a first-line therapy for postherpetic neuralgia, particularly effective for the localized mechanical allodynia these patients describe, and carries minimal systemic absorption or side effects. Antivirals are used during the acute shingles episode, not for established postherpetic neuralgia. Systemic corticosteroids and routine opioids are not recommended first-line therapies for this chronic neuropathic pain condition.
  125. The correct answer is B (Serotonin-norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors such as duloxetine.): fibromyalgia reflects central sensitization rather than peripheral inflammation, and SNRIs along with pregabalin are FDA-approved and evidence-supported for reducing its diffuse pain. NSAIDs and corticosteroids target inflammatory processes that are not the underlying mechanism in fibromyalgia. Opioids lack good evidence in this condition and are generally avoided given the centrally mediated nature of the pain.
  126. The correct answer is A (Add a weak opioid or low-dose strong opioid to the current regimen.): the WHO ladder progresses from non-opioids to the addition of an opioid for moderate pain while continuing adjuncts, matching this patient's escalating but still non-refractory pain. Exceeding the maximum NSAID dose adds toxicity without added benefit, and stopping non-opioids while relying on a benzodiazepine does not address nociceptive pain. Intrathecal pumps are reserved for refractory pain after systemic options and appropriate trialing have failed.
  127. The correct answer is C (Duloxetine titrated to an effective dose.): duloxetine is FDA-approved and considered first-line for painful diabetic peripheral neuropathy, working through serotonin-norepinephrine reuptake inhibition to modulate the neuropathic pain pathway. NSAIDs target inflammatory rather than neuropathic pain and carry renal risk in diabetic patients. Opioids are not first-line given limited long-term benefit and dependence risk, and topical capsaicin alone is generally an adjunct rather than an initial monotherapy.
  128. The correct answer is D (Transforaminal epidural steroid injection at the corresponding level.): a transforaminal epidural steroid injection delivers corticosteroid directly to the inflamed nerve root causing radicular pain and is well supported for lumbar radiculopathy refractory to conservative therapy. Facet radiofrequency ablation and sacroiliac injections target axial rather than radicular pain sources, and trigger point injections address myofascial pain rather than nerve root compression seen on this patient's imaging.
  129. The correct answer is A (A trigger point injection placed directly into the taut, palpable band reproducing the referred pain.): myofascial pain syndrome is characterized by discrete taut bands with reproducible referred pain, and injecting local anesthetic directly into the trigger point is the targeted first-line procedural treatment. Epidural injections and radiofrequency ablation address structural nerve root or facet-mediated pain rather than muscular trigger points, and stellate ganglion block targets sympathetically maintained pain syndromes like CRPS, not myofascial pain.
  130. The correct answer is C (Opioid-induced hyperalgesia from pronociceptive central sensitization.): opioid-induced hyperalgesia produces a paradoxical increase in pain sensitivity and pain spreading beyond the original site as opioid exposure increases, reflecting central sensitization rather than undertreated disease. Unlike tolerance, where the same pain simply requires a higher dose for equal relief, hyperalgesia worsens with dose escalation and improves with opioid reduction. This distinction is critical because increasing the dose further would worsen rather than relieve her pain.
  131. The correct answer is B (Permanent spinal cord stimulator implantation.): spinal cord stimulation is an established, guideline-supported therapy for failed back surgery syndrome once a percutaneous trial demonstrates meaningful pain relief, and it is favored when no correctable structural lesion justifies further surgery. Repeat surgery is not indicated absent a clear anatomic target. Intrathecal baclofen treats spasticity rather than neuropathic pain, and long-term opioid monotherapy is not the preferred approach once a positive stimulator trial has been achieved.
  132. The correct answer is D (Implanted intrathecal pump delivering opioid directly to the spinal cord.): intrathecal drug delivery achieves potent analgesia at a small fraction of the systemic opioid dose because medication acts directly at spinal opioid receptors, substantially reducing systemic side effects in patients with refractory cancer pain. Switching formulations or adding or increasing oral opioids would maintain or worsen the same systemic burden already causing intolerable side effects rather than resolving it.
  133. The correct answer is A (Celiac plexus neurolytic block under fluoroscopic or CT guidance.): the celiac plexus carries visceral afferent fibers from the pancreas and upper abdominal organs, and neurolytic blockade at this site provides significant pain relief and opioid-sparing benefit in pancreatic cancer. The hypogastric plexus and lumbar sympathetic chain serve pelvic and lower extremity structures respectively, and the stellate ganglion serves the head, neck, and upper extremity, none of which match this patient's visceral upper abdominal pain source.
  134. The correct answer is C (Stellate ganglion block targeting cervicothoracic sympathetic fibers.): the stellate ganglion provides sympathetic innervation to the head, neck, and upper extremity, making it the appropriate diagnostic and therapeutic target when CRPS of the hand shows sympathetically maintained features such as temperature and vasomotor asymmetry. The lumbar sympathetic chain, celiac plexus, and hypogastric plexus supply the lower extremity, upper abdominal viscera, and pelvis respectively, none of which correspond to this patient's affected extremity.
  135. The correct answer is B (Combining acetaminophen, an NSAID if appropriate, and regional anesthesia with lower-dose opioids.): multimodal analgesia layers agents acting through different mechanisms, such as acetaminophen, NSAIDs, and regional techniques, allowing effective pain control with reduced total opioid exposure and fewer opioid-related side effects. Relying solely on opioids, whether by infusion or patient-controlled delivery, forgoes this synergy, and delaying treatment until severe pain develops allows unnecessary suffering and worse pain control overall.
  136. The correct answer is D (At least 30 mL/kg of balanced crystalloid administered promptly.): Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidelines recommend an initial fluid bolus of at least 30 mL/kg of balanced crystalloid within the first three hours for patients with sepsis-induced hypoperfusion or septic shock, guided by dynamic reassessment thereafter. Maintenance rates or a small fixed bolus are inadequate for this degree of hypoperfusion, and delaying fluids until vasopressors start contradicts the recommended sequence of early aggressive resuscitation.
  137. The correct answer is A (Norepinephrine titrated to a mean arterial pressure goal.): norepinephrine is the recommended first-line vasopressor for septic shock because its combined alpha and modest beta activity restores perfusion pressure with a more favorable arrhythmia profile than pure alpha agents. Phenylephrine is reserved for specific situations such as tachyarrhythmia limiting norepinephrine use, dopamine carries greater arrhythmia risk and is not first-line, and dobutamine is an inotrope added for persistent hypoperfusion despite adequate pressure, not an initial pressor.
  138. The correct answer is B (6 mL/kg of predicted body weight with plateau pressure monitoring.): lung-protective ventilation for ARDS uses low tidal volumes of approximately 6 mL/kg predicted body weight, calculated from height rather than actual weight, paired with plateau pressure monitoring to limit alveolar overdistension. Higher tidal volumes worsen ventilator-induced lung injury and mortality in ARDS. An even lower fixed volume of 4 mL/kg is not the standard target and is only considered when plateau pressures remain elevated despite the standard strategy.
  139. The correct answer is C (Below 30 cmH2O to limit alveolar overdistension.): maintaining plateau pressure below 30 cmH2O is a core component of lung-protective ventilation, reflecting the pressure transmitted to the alveoli during an inspiratory hold and correlating with the risk of ventilator-induced lung injury. Allowing pressures up to 40 or 45 cmH2O exceeds the evidence-based safety threshold, while a target as low as 20 cmH2O is not required and may be unachievable while maintaining adequate ventilation.
  140. The correct answer is D (Prone positioning for at least 12 to 16 hours per day.): prone positioning improves ventilation-perfusion matching and has demonstrated a mortality benefit in patients with severe ARDS and a PaO2/FiO2 ratio below approximately 150, particularly when sustained for extended daily sessions. Increasing tidal volume worsens ventilator-induced lung injury rather than helping, and while judicious fluid management may help, aggressive diuresis without regard to volume status is not the evidence-based intervention for refractory hypoxemia here.
  141. The correct answer is C (Titrating PEEP using an FiO2/PEEP table balanced against oxygenation and hemodynamics.): PEEP titration in ARDS uses graded tables pairing FiO2 with PEEP levels to recruit collapsed alveoli while monitoring for hemodynamic compromise from excessive intrathoracic pressure. A fixed low PEEP of 5 cmH2O is inadequate for moderate-to-severe disease, while maximizing PEEP without regard to hemodynamics risks reduced venous return and cardiac output, and zero PEEP promotes atelectasis and worsens oxygenation.
  142. The correct answer is A (Dobutamine to augment cardiac contractility and output.): dobutamine is a beta-agonist inotrope that improves cardiac contractility and output in cardiogenic shock with an already elevated systemic vascular resistance, which is exactly the profile shown by this patient's low cardiac index and high SVR. Adding phenylephrine or crystalloid would worsen afterload or volume overload in a heart already failing to pump forward, and norepinephrine alone without inotropic support does not address the primary contractile failure.
  143. The correct answer is D (Balanced transfusion of red cells, plasma, and platelets.): hemorrhagic shock requires early balanced blood product resuscitation in a ratio approximating whole blood to restore oxygen-carrying capacity and correct coagulopathy, rather than continued crystalloid, which dilutes clotting factors and worsens coagulopathy. Vasopressors do not replace lost intravascular volume and blood components in hemorrhage, and diuretics are entirely inappropriate in a patient who is intravascularly depleted from active bleeding.
  144. The correct answer is B (Distributive shock with low systemic vascular resistance and a compensatory elevated cardiac output.): septic distributive shock classically presents with vasodilation causing low systemic vascular resistance and a compensatory high cardiac output, producing warm extremities and bounding pulses rather than the cool, clamped-down periphery of cardiogenic or hypovolemic shock. Cardiogenic and hypovolemic shock both typically show low cardiac output with elevated SVR, and obstructive shock reflects a mechanical barrier to filling or ejection rather than peripheral vasodilation.
  145. The correct answer is A (Needle decompression followed by tube thoracostomy.): tension pneumothorax presents with tracheal deviation, absent unilateral breath sounds, distended neck veins, and obstructive shock from impaired venous return, and requires immediate needle decompression followed by definitive tube thoracostomy without waiting for imaging. Pericardiocentesis treats tamponade rather than pneumothorax, fluid alone does not relieve the mechanical obstruction, and intubation without decompressing the pneumothorax can precipitate cardiovascular collapse from positive pressure ventilation.
  146. The correct answer is D (Pulsus paradoxus from pericardial tamponade limiting ventricular filling.): the combination of hypotension, muffled heart sounds, jugular venous distension, and an exaggerated inspiratory drop in systolic pressure, or pulsus paradoxus, is classic for cardiac tamponade, which mechanically restricts ventricular filling and produces obstructive shock. Primary contractile failure or isolated hemorrhage would not typically produce muffled heart sounds and pulsus paradoxus together, and elevated SVR alone does not explain this specific constellation of findings.
  147. The correct answer is C (Hypoxemic failure reflects gas exchange defects such as shunt, while hypercapnic failure reflects ventilatory pump failure.): hypoxemic respiratory failure arises from ventilation-perfusion mismatch, shunt, or diffusion impairment as seen in ARDS or pneumonia, while hypercapnic failure results from inadequate alveolar ventilation from causes such as neuromuscular weakness, sedation, or severe airway obstruction. These represent distinct mechanisms requiring different management approaches, so treating them as identical or assuming hypercapnia always implies primary parenchymal disease misidentifies the underlying pathophysiology.
  148. The correct answer is B (The Confusion Assessment Method for the ICU, or CAM-ICU.): the CAM-ICU was specifically validated for detecting delirium in mechanically ventilated and nonverbal critically ill patients by assessing acute change in mental status, inattention, altered level of consciousness, and disorganized thinking through nonverbal tasks. The Richmond Agitation-Sedation Scale measures sedation depth rather than delirium, the Glasgow Coma Scale assesses level of consciousness only, and the Mini-Mental State Examination requires verbal responses unsuitable for intubated patients.
  149. The correct answer is D (Daily sedation interruption, early mobility, and sleep-wake cycle promotion.): bundled strategies such as spontaneous awakening and breathing trials, early mobilization, and preserving normal sleep-wake cycles reduce the duration and incidence of ICU delirium according to strong critical care evidence. Continuous deep sedation and routine benzodiazepine use are independently associated with a higher risk of delirium, and prolonged immobility similarly increases delirium risk rather than preventing it.
  150. The correct answer is A (Early enteral nutrition within 24 to 48 hours of ICU admission if the patient is hemodynamically stable.): critical care nutrition guidelines recommend initiating enteral feeding within 24 to 48 hours of ICU admission in hemodynamically stable patients, as early enteral nutrition preserves gut mucosal integrity and is associated with improved outcomes compared with delayed feeding. Parenteral nutrition is reserved for patients who cannot tolerate the enteral route, and delaying feeding for a week or until discharge contradicts current evidence-based practice.
  151. The correct answer is B (Refeeding syndrome, requiring cautious caloric advancement with electrolyte repletion.): refeeding syndrome occurs when reintroducing nutrition to a chronically malnourished patient triggers an insulin surge that drives phosphate, potassium, and magnesium intracellularly, producing the described electrolyte derangements and arrhythmia risk. Management requires slow caloric advancement with proactive electrolyte monitoring and repletion rather than complete cessation of feeding or attributing the findings to an unrelated process such as pancreatitis or sepsis alone.
  152. The correct answer is C (Vasopressin added at a fixed low dose.): Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidance recommends adding vasopressin at a fixed low dose in patients requiring escalating norepinephrine, as it acts through a distinct receptor mechanism and can reduce catecholamine requirements. Continuing to escalate norepinephrine alone increases arrhythmia and ischemic risk, phenylephrine is not the preferred second agent, and dopamine carries greater arrhythmia risk and is not recommended as an adjunct in this setting.
  153. The correct answer is A (Intramuscular or intravenous epinephrine given immediately.): epinephrine is the first-line and most time-critical treatment for anaphylactic shock, reversing vasodilation, bronchospasm, and capillary leak through combined alpha and beta agonist effects. Antihistamines, corticosteroids, and bronchodilators are useful adjuncts but do not adequately or rapidly reverse the life-threatening hemodynamic collapse of anaphylaxis and should never be used as the sole or first intervention in this emergency.
  154. The correct answer is D (To serve as a resuscitation endpoint reflecting improving tissue perfusion.): serial lactate measurements track the adequacy of tissue perfusion during resuscitation, with declining lactate clearance correlating with improved outcomes in septic shock and guiding the intensity of ongoing fluid and vasopressor therapy. Lactate trending does not identify the causative organism, does not directly calculate drug doses, and is not used to adjust antibiotic dosing for renal function, which relies on separate renal markers.
  155. The correct answer is C (Maximal sterile barrier precautions, chlorhexidine skin antisepsis, and daily necessity review.): the CLABSI prevention bundle includes hand hygiene, maximal sterile barrier precautions during insertion, chlorhexidine skin antisepsis, optimal catheter site selection, and daily assessment of continued line necessity with prompt removal when no longer needed. Prophylactic systemic antibiotics and routine scheduled exchanges are not recommended and may increase resistance or complication risk, and femoral placement is generally avoided due to higher infection risk compared with subclavian or internal jugular sites.
  156. The correct answer is B (Volume control guarantees tidal volume, pressure control guarantees inspiratory pressure.): in volume control ventilation, the clinician sets a fixed tidal volume and airway pressure fluctuates with lung compliance and resistance, while in pressure control ventilation the clinician sets a fixed inspiratory pressure and delivered tidal volume varies with the same factors. Neither mode delivers identical outputs to the other, and both allow the clinician to set defining inspiratory parameters appropriate to the chosen control variable.
  157. The correct answer is A (Auto-PEEP from breath stacking due to inadequate expiratory time.): patients with severe obstructive lung disease like COPD have prolonged expiratory time constants, and when the set respiratory rate does not allow complete exhalation, breath stacking produces auto-PEEP with progressively rising intrinsic pressure and risk of hemodynamic compromise or barotrauma. This is not a normal waveform, is unrelated to circuit disconnection, and reflects inadequate expiratory time rather than intentionally elevated extrinsic PEEP settings.
  158. The correct answer is D (The Berlin definition classifying severity by PaO2/FiO2 ratio.): the Berlin definition diagnoses ARDS based on acute onset within one week of a known insult, bilateral opacities not fully explained by cardiac failure or fluid overload, and classifies severity as mild, moderate, or severe according to the PaO2/FiO2 ratio measured on a minimum PEEP. The Wells, Ranson, and Light criteria apply to pulmonary embolism probability, pancreatitis severity, and pleural effusion classification respectively, none of which apply to this presentation.
  159. The correct answer is B (Driving pressure, which correlates with mortality risk in ARDS.): driving pressure, calculated as plateau pressure minus total PEEP, reflects the tidal stress applied to the functional lung volume and has been shown to correlate more strongly with mortality in ARDS than tidal volume or plateau pressure alone, making it a useful target when adjusting ventilator settings. Minute ventilation, oxygenation index, and dead space fraction are each distinct respiratory parameters that do not represent this specific pressure-based calculation.
  160. The correct answer is C (Low-dose intravenous hydrocortisone for refractory shock.): Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidance supports adding low-dose intravenous corticosteroids such as hydrocortisone in patients with septic shock who remain hemodynamically unstable despite adequate fluids and escalating vasopressor therapy, as relative adrenal insufficiency can contribute to refractory hypotension. ECMO and intravenous immunoglobulin are not standard therapies for this indication, and abruptly discontinuing vasopressors in a hypotensive patient would be dangerous rather than diagnostic.
  161. The correct answer is D (Addressing relative adrenal insufficiency and restoring vascular responsiveness to catecholamines.): critical illness-related corticosteroid insufficiency can blunt the vasculature's responsiveness to endogenous and exogenous catecholamines, and stress-dose hydrocortisone helps restore vascular tone and pressor responsiveness in refractory septic shock. Corticosteroids have no direct antimicrobial activity, do not specifically reverse DIC, and are not administered in this context for a bronchodilatory effect.
  162. The correct answer is A (Prompt source control through drainage or surgical intervention.): Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidance emphasizes that source control, such as draining an abscess or resecting infected or necrotic tissue, should occur as soon as medically and logistically feasible once identified, because antibiotics alone cannot resolve an undrained collection. Delaying source control until full stabilization or relying on broader antibiotics without addressing the anatomic source both risk ongoing sepsis and clinical deterioration despite appropriate medical therapy.
  163. The correct answer is C (Head-of-bed elevation, daily sedation interruption, and oral care with chlorhexidine.): the VAP prevention bundle includes elevating the head of bed to 30 to 45 degrees to reduce aspiration, daily sedation interruption paired with spontaneous breathing trials to expedite extubation, and regular oral care to reduce oropharyngeal bacterial burden. Keeping the head of bed flat increases aspiration risk, continuous deep sedation prolongs ventilator time and increases VAP risk, and routine prophylactic antibiotics are not part of the standard bundle.
  164. The correct answer is B (A spontaneous breathing trial on minimal ventilator support.): once a patient meets readiness criteria, a spontaneous breathing trial using low pressure support or a T-piece assesses the patient's ability to sustain adequate ventilation with minimal assistance and is the standard step preceding extubation. Extubating without this assessment risks premature failure, remaining on full controlled ventilation does not test readiness, and increasing sedation would worsen respiratory drive and mask true weaning tolerance.
  165. The correct answer is D (An adequate cough and gag reflex with minimal secretions.): beyond passing a spontaneous breathing trial, safe extubation requires confirming the patient can protect the airway, reflected by an adequate cough strength, intact gag reflex, and manageable secretion burden, since inability to clear secretions risks post-extubation aspiration and reintubation. A high minute ventilation requirement or an elevated rapid shallow breathing index above roughly 105 actually predicts weaning failure rather than success, and continued deep sedation would impair rather than support airway protection.
  166. The correct answer is A (Milrinone, a phosphodiesterase-3 inhibitor providing positive inotropy along with systemic and pulmonary afterload reduction.): milrinone is a phosphodiesterase-3 inhibitor that increases intracellular cyclic AMP independent of beta-receptors, producing both positive inotropy and systemic and pulmonary vasodilation, which is beneficial in cardiogenic shock with elevated afterload. Phenylephrine, vasopressin, and high-dose norepinephrine are all predominantly vasoconstrictive agents that would increase rather than reduce afterload in a failing heart, worsening rather than improving cardiac output.
  167. The correct answer is B (Intra-aortic balloon pump timed to diastolic augmentation.): the intra-aortic balloon pump inflates during diastole to augment coronary perfusion pressure and deflates just before systole to reduce afterload, providing counterpulsation support in cardiogenic shock refractory to medical therapy. Venovenous ECMO supports gas exchange rather than circulation, venoarterial ECMO is a more invasive option typically considered after simpler measures, and percutaneous ventricular assist devices function continuously rather than through cardiac-cycle timed counterpulsation.
  168. The correct answer is C (Improved comfort and tolerability while delivering heated, humidified high-flow oxygen.): high-flow nasal cannula delivers heated and humidified oxygen at high flow rates, generally with better patient comfort and tolerability than a tight-fitting noninvasive ventilation mask, and has shown benefit specifically in hypoxemic, non-hypercapnic respiratory failure. It does not provide the same level of positive pressure support as noninvasive ventilation for hypercapnic failure, does not offer superior carbon dioxide clearance, and does not guarantee avoidance of intubation if the patient deteriorates.
  169. The correct answer is D (Accept permissive hypercapnia as long as pH remains tolerable.): permissive hypercapnia is an accepted consequence of prioritizing lung-protective tidal volumes and plateau pressures over full carbon dioxide normalization, and mild respiratory acidosis with a tolerable pH is generally well tolerated in ARDS. Increasing tidal volume above protective limits reintroduces the risk of ventilator-induced lung injury the strategy is designed to avoid, and routine bicarbonate correction or extracorporeal carbon dioxide removal are not first-line responses to this expected and generally tolerated finding.
  170. The correct answer is A (Dexmedetomidine, which avoids benzodiazepine-associated delirium risk.): dexmedetomidine is an alpha-2 agonist that provides sedation and anxiolysis without significant respiratory depression and is associated with a lower incidence of delirium compared with benzodiazepines, making it preferred in this setting. Benzodiazepines such as lorazepam, midazolam, and diazepam are independently associated with increased ICU delirium risk and are generally avoided when delirium is present or a concern, particularly during ventilator weaning.
  171. The correct answer is C (Core temperature must be raised to at least 36 degrees C before testing.): Hypothermia below 36 degrees C can suppress brainstem reflexes and slow drug metabolism, mimicking brain death and invalidating the examination. Guidelines require normothermia as a precondition, along with normotension, absence of confounding sedatives or paralytics, and correction of severe metabolic or endocrine derangements. Sodium and hemoglobin thresholds are not standardized preconditions for the exam. Rewarming the patient first ensures the clinical findings truly reflect irreversible loss of brainstem function.
  172. The correct answer is C (Wait longer than standard clearance times or obtain an ancillary test.): Impaired hepatic and renal clearance prolongs drug half-lives well beyond standard guidelines, so residual sedative effect can falsely mimic brainstem areflexia. Reversal agents do not reliably eliminate drug effect on brainstem reflexes and are not a substitute for adequate clearance time. When clearance cannot be assured, an ancillary test such as cerebral blood flow study is used to confirm the diagnosis. The apnea test alone cannot replace the full brainstem examination in this determination.
  173. The correct answer is D (Titrate medications to relieve dyspnea and distress, even if death is hastened.): Under the principle of double effect, medications may be titrated to treat suffering even if an unintended secondary consequence is hastened death, as long as the intent is symptom relief. Withholding treatment until distress is visible needlessly prolongs suffering. A fixed dose ignores individual variability in symptom burden and respiratory drive. A single bolus without ongoing titration fails to address evolving symptoms throughout the dying process.
  174. The correct answer is A (The combination of neuromuscular blocking agents and corticosteroids.): Concurrent use of neuromuscular blockers and high-dose corticosteroids is a well-established risk factor for ICU-acquired weakness, particularly critical illness myopathy. Sepsis, prolonged immobility, and hyperglycemia are additional established risk factors, while younger age is actually associated with lower risk. Enteral nutrition is protective relative to parenteral nutrition and does not increase risk. Brief self-corrected hypoglycemia has not been shown to independently drive this syndrome.
  175. The correct answer is B (Transfusion-related acute lung injury from donor antibody-mediated neutrophil activation.): TRALI presents with acute hypoxemia and bilateral infiltrates within 6 hours of transfusion, caused by donor antileukocyte antibodies activating recipient neutrophils and causing capillary leak, and it is distinguished from volume overload by the absence of elevated filling pressures. Cardiogenic edema is excluded here by the low left atrial pressure evidence. Pulmonary embolism would not typically produce diffuse bilateral infiltrates. Aspiration is not suggested by the temporal relationship to transfusion.
  176. The correct answer is A (An inotrope such as dobutamine combined with careful afterload reduction.): In cardiogenic shock with elevated filling pressures and low cardiac index, inotropic support augments contractility while cautious afterload reduction can improve forward flow, alongside consideration of mechanical circulatory support if refractory. Fluid boluses would worsen pulmonary edema given the already elevated wedge pressure. High-dose beta blockade would further depress contractility and worsen shock acutely. Vasodilator therapy alone without addressing pump failure is insufficient in this hemodynamic profile.
  177. The correct answer is D (Perform emergent needle decompression of the right chest.): Tension pneumothorax is a clinical diagnosis in an unstable patient with absent unilateral breath sounds, tracheal deviation, and jugular venous distension, and immediate needle decompression is life-saving and should never be delayed for imaging or confirmatory studies. Fluids and vasopressors do not relieve the obstructive physiology caused by mediastinal shift and impaired venous return. Echocardiography would delay treatment in a rapidly deteriorating patient. Increasing PEEP would worsen intrathoracic pressure and further compromise venous return.
  178. The correct answer is C (Intramuscular epinephrine into the anterolateral thigh.): Epinephrine is the first-line treatment for anaphylaxis and should be given intramuscularly into the anterolateral thigh without delay, reversing vasodilation, bronchoconstriction, and capillary leak. Antihistamines and corticosteroids are adjunctive therapies with slower onset and do not reliably treat hypotension or airway compromise acutely. Nebulized bronchodilators address bronchospasm but do not treat the systemic vasodilation and shock. Delaying epinephrine for these adjuncts increases the risk of cardiovascular collapse and airway obstruction.
  179. The correct answer is C (Within 24 to 48 hours of ICU admission once resuscitation is underway.): Early enteral nutrition initiated within 24 to 48 hours is recommended in hemodynamically stable critically ill patients, even those on low-dose stable vasopressor support, because it preserves gut mucosal integrity and is associated with fewer infectious complications. Waiting for complete vasopressor discontinuation unnecessarily delays feeding in a stabilizing patient. Delaying to day 7 contradicts early enteral feeding guidelines and risks malnutrition. Parenteral nutrition is reserved for patients who cannot tolerate or achieve adequate enteral intake, not as a first-line substitute.
  180. The correct answer is D (Prone positioning for prolonged daily sessions.): Prone positioning for at least 16 hours daily improves oxygenation and has demonstrated a mortality benefit in patients with moderate to severe ARDS and a PaO2/FiO2 ratio below 150. Inhaled nitric oxide improves oxygenation transiently but has not shown a mortality benefit. High-frequency oscillatory ventilation has been associated with harm in adult ARDS trials and is not recommended. Continuous neuromuscular blockade for the entire stay is not supported and current evidence favors a lighter, more targeted approach limited to the early severe phase.
  181. The correct answer is B (The oculovestibular reflex mediated by the vestibulocochlear and oculomotor nerves.): The cold caloric test evaluates the oculovestibular reflex, which in an intact brainstem produces tonic eye deviation toward the irrigated ear, and its absence supports brainstem areflexia in the appropriate clinical context. The corneal reflex is tested with a wisp of cotton or saline touching the cornea, not caloric irrigation. The gag reflex is assessed with a tongue depressor or suction catheter. The pupillary light reflex is assessed with a bright light shone into each eye.
  182. The correct answer is C (Extubation better honors family wishes to see the patient without tubes and devices.): The choice between immediate extubation and a terminal ventilator wean is individualized based on family preference, expected time to death, and the desire to remove visible medical devices, with extubation often chosen to allow a more natural appearance at the bedside. There is no legal requirement mandating one approach over the other. Time to death is variable and not reliably faster with either method. Comfort-focused sedation is still required regardless of which withdrawal method is chosen.
  183. The correct answer is D (Critical illness myopathy with primary muscle fiber involvement.): Preserved sensory nerve conduction with myopathic features on needle electromyography points toward critical illness myopathy, a primary muscle disorder rather than a nerve process, which is common after corticosteroid and neuromuscular blocker exposure. Critical illness polyneuropathy would show reduced sensory nerve action potentials reflecting axonal sensory involvement, which is absent here. Guillain-Barre syndrome typically shows demyelinating features with markedly slowed conduction velocities and conduction block, not seen in this pattern. Myasthenic crisis is diagnosed by repetitive nerve stimulation showing a decremental response, not by myopathic needle findings.
  184. The correct answer is A (Alveolar hypoventilation from restrictive chest wall disease and blunted respiratory drive.): The combination of restrictive chest wall mechanics from kyphoscoliosis and obesity along with chronically blunted central respiratory drive produces alveolar hypoventilation, evidenced here by markedly elevated PaCO2 with compensatory bicarbonate retention. This pattern is hypercapnic rather than purely hypoxemic respiratory failure and is not explained by diffuse alveolar damage, which would present with bilateral infiltrates and refractory hypoxemia. Pulmonary embolism typically causes hypocapnia from tachypnea rather than the severe hypercapnia seen here. Cardiogenic pulmonary edema does not primarily explain the chronic bicarbonate compensation pattern present.
  185. The correct answer is B (Begin CPR and proceed with unsynchronized defibrillation immediately.): Pulseless ventricular tachycardia is treated as a cardiac arrest rhythm requiring immediate CPR and unsynchronized defibrillation rather than synchronized cardioversion, which is reserved for a patient with a pulse. Waiting to arrange sedation for synchronized cardioversion is inappropriate in a pulseless patient. Amiodarone may be used as an adjunct during ongoing resuscitation but should not delay defibrillation. Esmolol has no role in pulseless ventricular tachycardia and would not restore perfusion.
  186. The correct answer is D (Cardiac tamponade from postoperative pericardial fluid accumulation.): Beck's triad components of hypotension, muffled heart sounds, and jugular venous distension along with pulsus paradoxus exceeding 10 mmHg are classic for cardiac tamponade, which is a well-recognized complication of cardiac surgery. Right ventricular infarction can produce elevated jugular venous pressure but does not typically cause muffled heart sounds or significant pulsus paradoxus. Massive pulmonary embolism causes right heart strain but not muffled heart sounds. Isolated hypovolemic bleeding would typically present with flat neck veins rather than jugular venous distension.
  187. The correct answer is C (Administer additional intramuscular epinephrine and extend the observation period.): Biphasic anaphylactic reactions can occur hours after apparent resolution of the initial episode, and recurrence should be treated with repeat intramuscular epinephrine along with extended monitoring, since this phenomenon is well documented even after appropriate initial treatment. Discharging the patient immediately ignores the risk of biphasic recurrence and could result in an unmonitored life-threatening episode. Withholding epinephrine because it was already used denies effective first-line therapy for a recurrent reaction. Oral antihistamines alone are inadequate for hypotension and do not address the underlying mediator release causing shock.
  188. The correct answer is A (Full caloric replacement early in critical illness may increase complications.): Evidence suggests that early full caloric feeding, particularly in obese or high-risk critically ill patients, may be associated with increased infectious complications and worse outcomes compared with permissive underfeeding during the acute phase, likely related to overfeeding-associated hyperglycemia and refeeding risk. Underfeeding does not accelerate wound healing and inadequate protein delivery can actually impair it. This is an evidence-based practice pattern rather than a universal regulatory mandate. Mechanical ventilation alone is not a contraindication to full nutritional support in all patients.
  189. The correct answer is B (Lower tidal volumes reduce ventilator-induced lung injury and improve survival.): The landmark lung-protective ventilation trial demonstrated that limiting tidal volume to approximately 6 mL/kg predicted body weight and plateau pressure below 30 cmH2O reduces volutrauma and barotrauma, decreasing mortality in ARDS. Higher tidal volumes increase alveolar overdistension and shear injury rather than uniformly improving oxygenation. Tidal volume strategy has a well-documented and significant effect on ARDS outcomes, contrary to having no effect. The rationale for lower tidal volumes is physiologic protection of the lung, not a cost consideration.
  190. The correct answer is C (Cerebral blood flow study demonstrating absent intracranial flow.): Radionuclide cerebral blood flow studies demonstrating absent intracranial perfusion are an accepted and widely used ancillary test when the clinical examination or apnea test cannot be reliably completed. A standard electroencephalogram must follow a specific technical protocol with prolonged recording to be considered valid, and routine unstructured EEG is not sufficient alone. Non-contrast head CT shows structural anatomy but cannot confirm absence of brain function. Transcranial Doppler is only accepted when performed by a qualified, experienced operator following established technical criteria.
  191. The correct answer is C (Present prognosis clearly, acknowledge emotions, and allow time for questions.): Effective family communication in end-of-life discussions involves clearly conveying prognosis, actively acknowledging emotional responses, and giving family members adequate time to process information and ask questions, which improves understanding and reduces conflict. Requiring unanimous agreement before continuing discussion can prolong distress and delay appropriate care decisions. Avoiding prognosis discussion leaves families uninformed and unable to make appropriate decisions. Physicians have a professional obligation to provide clear prognostic guidance rather than leaving families without medical input.
  192. The correct answer is C (Reduced duration of delirium and improved functional outcomes.): Early mobilization protocols in mechanically ventilated patients have been associated with reduced duration of delirium, improved functional status at discharge, and shorter ICU length of stay when implemented safely with appropriate monitoring. Studies have not shown a clinically significant increase in accidental device dislodgement when protocols include proper safety screening. Functional outcomes are generally improved rather than unchanged with early mobilization. Mortality has not been shown to increase with well-implemented early mobilization protocols.
  193. The correct answer is D (Moderate ARDS with PaO2/FiO2 ratio between 100 and 200.): The Berlin Definition requires acute onset within one week of a known insult, bilateral infiltrates, and a PaO2/FiO2 ratio calculated on at least 5 cmH2O of PEEP, with a ratio between 100 and 200 classifying moderate ARDS, and the exclusion of a purely cardiogenic cause here supports this diagnosis. Mild ARDS requires a ratio between 200 and 300, which does not match this gas. Aspiration is a well-recognized precipitant of ARDS rather than an exclusion criterion. The normal echocardiogram without volume overload argues against cardiogenic edema as the primary explanation.
  194. The correct answer is D (Volume loading to maintain right ventricular preload and cardiac output.): Right ventricular infarction causes preload-dependent shock physiology, so volume loading is essential to maintain adequate right ventricular filling and forward cardiac output, while clear lung fields confirm the absence of concurrent left-sided failure that would limit fluid administration. Diuresis would worsen shock by further reducing an already preload-dependent right ventricle. Nitroglycerin reduces venous return and preload, which is particularly dangerous and can precipitate profound hypotension in right ventricular infarction. Positive pressure ventilation increases intrathoracic pressure and further reduces venous return, worsening this preload-dependent state.
  195. The correct answer is A (Massive pulmonary embolism causing acute right ventricular outflow obstruction.): Acute right ventricular dilation and dysfunction with a small hyperdynamic left ventricle, a loud pulmonic component, and hypoxemia are classic echocardiographic and clinical findings of massive pulmonary embolism causing obstructive shock through acute right ventricular outflow obstruction. Cardiac tamponade typically shows chamber collapse and pericardial effusion rather than isolated right ventricular dilation. Tension pneumothorax would show absent breath sounds and tracheal deviation, not this echocardiographic pattern. Acute left ventricular failure would show left ventricular dysfunction rather than a small hyperdynamic left ventricle.
  196. The correct answer is C (Vasopressin added to norepinephrine to achieve target blood pressure.): Current sepsis guidelines recommend adding vasopressin to norepinephrine when moderate to high doses of norepinephrine fail to achieve target mean arterial pressure, allowing a reduction in norepinephrine dose while improving hemodynamics. Dopamine is no longer preferred as a second agent due to increased arrhythmia risk and worse outcomes compared with norepinephrine-based regimens. Phenylephrine is generally reserved for specific situations such as significant tachyarrhythmia rather than as the standard second-line agent. Dobutamine is an inotrope used for low cardiac output states, not as a replacement vasopressor for refractory vasodilatory shock.
  197. The correct answer is B (Initiating nutrition slowly with proactive electrolyte monitoring and repletion.): Refeeding syndrome is prevented by starting nutrition support cautiously at reduced caloric rates in at-risk malnourished patients, with proactive monitoring and aggressive repletion of phosphate, potassium, and magnesium as insulin-driven cellular uptake occurs with refeeding. Starting at full caloric goals immediately is a major precipitant of refeeding syndrome rather than a preventive measure. Delaying all nutrition for two weeks would worsen malnutrition without addressing the underlying risk. Intravenous dextrose alone without electrolyte monitoring can still precipitate the same insulin-mediated electrolyte shifts.
  198. The correct answer is A (Driving pressure reflects lung compliance and correlates with mortality risk.): Driving pressure, calculated as plateau pressure minus PEEP, reflects respiratory system compliance for a given tidal volume, and elevated or rising driving pressure has been independently associated with increased mortality in ARDS, suggesting worsening lung mechanics despite unchanged tidal volume. This measure has demonstrated a well-established relationship to clinical outcomes rather than none. Rising driving pressure reflects changes in the patient's lung mechanics, not a mechanical circuit malfunction. Its clinical significance applies broadly across the lung-protective tidal volume range, not only at higher volumes.
  199. The correct answer is A (This is a spinally mediated reflex that can persist despite brain death.): Complex spinal reflexes, including triple flexion, plantar responses, and even more elaborate movements, can persist after brain death because they are mediated by intact spinal cord circuitry independent of brainstem or cerebral function, and their presence does not invalidate the diagnosis when brainstem areflexia and apnea are otherwise confirmed. This finding does not rule out brain death nor indicate cortical function is intact. The apnea test result is unaffected by the presence of spinal reflexes and does not need to be repeated based on this finding alone.
  200. The correct answer is D (The principle of double effect, permitting appropriate titration for comfort.): The principle of double effect holds that an action intended to relieve suffering is ethically permissible even if it carries a foreseen but unintended risk of hastening death, provided the primary intent is symptom relief rather than causing death. Futility does not apply here since symptom management is an appropriate and active goal of care, not something to be prohibited. Requiring an actively dying patient to remain awake for consent is impractical and inconsistent with prior established goals of care. Beneficence in this context supports appropriate symptom treatment rather than withholding effective comfort medications.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Diaphragmatic dysfunction related to prolonged mechanical ventilation and disuse atrophy.): Reduced diaphragmatic thickening fraction on ultrasound reflects ventilator-induced diaphragmatic dysfunction from prolonged controlled ventilation and disuse atrophy, a well-recognized contributor to weaning failure in patients otherwise recovering from critical illness. Pulmonary embolism would present with acute hemodynamic and oxygenation changes rather than a gradual weaning failure pattern with reduced diaphragmatic function. Cardiogenic pulmonary edema would show characteristic findings on lung ultrasound and chest imaging rather than isolated diaphragmatic thickening abnormality. Upper airway obstruction occurs after extubation and would not be identified by diaphragmatic ultrasound performed before extubation.
  2. The correct answer is B (A trial of noninvasive positive pressure ventilation.): Noninvasive positive pressure ventilation is first-line therapy for acute hypercapnic respiratory failure due to COPD exacerbation in an alert, cooperative patient able to protect the airway, reducing the need for intubation and improving outcomes. Immediate intubation is unnecessary when noninvasive ventilation is feasible and appropriate given the patient's mental status and airway protection. High-flow nasal cannula alone does not provide the positive pressure ventilatory support needed to address significant hypercapnia in this setting. Observation without additional support risks progressive respiratory acidosis and decompensation.
  3. The correct answer is C (Elevated left atrial and pulmonary capillary pressures causing hydrostatic pulmonary edema in this setting.): Cardiogenic pulmonary edema results from elevated left ventricular filling pressures transmitted backward to the pulmonary capillaries, causing hydrostatic fluid transudation into the alveolar spaces, producing the classic frothy secretions and bilateral infiltrates seen here. Increased capillary permeability from direct injury describes the mechanism of ARDS rather than cardiogenic edema. Decreased oncotic pressure from malnutrition would cause a more generalized edematous state rather than this acute pump-failure presentation. Lymphatic obstruction is not a typical acute mechanism in this clinical scenario of pump failure.
  4. The correct answer is B (Fifth intercostal space at the anterior axillary line.): Current guidance favors the fifth intercostal space at the anterior axillary line for needle decompression because chest wall thickness is thinner there in many patients, improving the likelihood the needle reaches the pleural space compared with the traditional anterior approach. The classic second intercostal space midclavicular line site is associated with higher failure rates due to greater chest wall thickness in many adults. The subxiphoid approach directed toward the shoulder describes pericardiocentesis technique, not needle decompression for pneumothorax. The posterior axillary line at the second space is not a standard or recommended site for this procedure.
  5. The correct answer is B (Prescribe and instruct use of a self-injectable epinephrine device with follow-up.): Given the recognized risk of a biphasic anaphylactic reaction occurring hours after apparent resolution, patients should be discharged with a self-injectable epinephrine device, clear instructions on its use, and arranged follow-up with an allergist. Stating no special instructions are needed ignores this well-documented risk of recurrence. Advising return only for identical symptoms may cause the patient to underestimate a recurrence presenting differently. Oral antihistamines alone are inadequate rescue therapy for a life-threatening recurrent anaphylactic reaction.
  6. The correct answer is D (Indirect calorimetry to directly measure energy expenditure.): Indirect calorimetry directly measures oxygen consumption and carbon dioxide production to calculate actual resting energy expenditure, providing the most accurate estimate of caloric needs, which is particularly valuable in obese critically ill patients where predictive equations are unreliable. Weight-based formulas using actual body weight without adjustment tend to substantially overestimate caloric needs in obesity. A fixed universal target ignores individual metabolic variation and body composition differences. Estimating based solely on diagnosis does not account for the metabolic stress response and individual variability in energy expenditure.
  7. The correct answer is A (Transient hypotension from reduced venous return and barotrauma risk.): Recruitment maneuvers involve sustained elevated intrathoracic pressure that can transiently reduce venous return and cardiac output, causing hypotension, and also carry a risk of barotrauma such as pneumothorax, requiring careful hemodynamic monitoring during the maneuver. The benefit is often transient rather than guaranteed, and improvement in oxygenation is not free of hemodynamic risk. Sustained improvement in compliance for days is not a reliable expected outcome of a single recruitment maneuver. PEEP titration is still needed after the maneuver to maintain any recruited lung volume, not eliminated by it.
  8. The correct answer is D (Use the patient's known baseline PaCO2 as the starting point and target.): In patients with chronic hypercapnia at baseline, the apnea test target threshold should be adjusted to account for the patient's known elevated baseline PaCO2 rather than applying the standard threshold used for patients without chronic respiratory disease, since a fixed universal threshold could produce a false negative result. Skipping the test entirely is not necessary when the protocol can be appropriately individualized. Applying a universal threshold ignoring baseline hypercapnia would risk an invalid or misleading result. Preoxygenation before the test remains a necessary safety step and should not be omitted.
  9. The correct answer is B (A do-not-escalate-care approach, distinct from active withdrawal.): A do-not-escalate-care approach means current therapies are continued while no new or additional interventions are added, which is ethically and clinically distinct from actively withdrawing existing life-sustaining treatments such as the ventilator. This scenario does not describe complete withdrawal since existing therapies remain in place. It is not physician-assisted death, which involves a different legal and ethical framework entirely unrelated to withholding escalation. This is clearly not full aggressive care, since new therapies are explicitly being withheld.
  10. The correct answer is B (Hypophosphatemia and hypomagnesemia can impair muscle and diaphragmatic function.): Severe hypophosphatemia impairs adenosine triphosphate production needed for muscle contraction, and hypomagnesemia can similarly impair neuromuscular function, both contributing to skeletal and diaphragmatic weakness that can complicate ventilator weaning in critically ill patients. These derangements clearly affect both muscle and respiratory function, not merely cardiac rhythm alone, though arrhythmia risk is also a concern. Correction of these electrolyte abnormalities is expected to improve rather than worsen muscle function. Ignoring the relationship between electrolytes and weakness would overlook a readily reversible contributor to weaning difficulty.
  11. The correct answer is A (Treat the underlying exacerbation and correct any electrolyte and oxygenation abnormalities that are present.): New atrial arrhythmias during COPD exacerbations are often driven by hypoxemia, electrolyte disturbances, and increased catecholamine states, so initial management should focus on treating the underlying exacerbation and correcting these contributing factors, particularly in a hemodynamically stable patient. Immediate cardioversion is reserved for hemodynamically unstable patients, not a stable one as described here. Surgical consultation has no role in managing a medical arrhythmia related to an exacerbation. High-dose digoxin is not first-line therapy and carries toxicity risk, especially in the setting of hypoxemia and potential renal impairment.
  12. The correct answer is A (Low cardiac output with elevated systemic vascular resistance and elevated filling pressures.): Cardiogenic shock is hemodynamically defined by a low cardiac index, elevated systemic vascular resistance from compensatory vasoconstriction, and elevated cardiac filling pressures reflecting pump failure, exactly matching this patient's profile after myocardial infarction. High cardiac output with low systemic vascular resistance describes distributive shock, such as septic or anaphylactic shock, not cardiogenic shock. Low cardiac output with low systemic vascular resistance is an atypical mixed pattern rather than the classic cardiogenic shock profile. Normal cardiac output would not be consistent with a diagnosis of shock from pump failure.
  13. The correct answer is A (Dynamic hyperinflation causing auto-PEEP and impaired venous return.): In severe bronchospasm with inadequate expiratory time, air trapping leads to dynamic hyperinflation and auto-PEEP, which raises intrathoracic pressure, impairs venous return, and causes obstructive shock physiology that classically improves immediately with ventilator disconnection allowing exhalation. Cardiac tamponade would not resolve immediately with ventilator disconnection since it is unrelated to intrathoracic pressure from breath stacking. Pulmonary embolism occurring coincidentally would not explain the immediate hemodynamic improvement with disconnection. Bronchodilator toxicity causing myocardial depression would not be reversed by simply disconnecting the ventilator circuit.
  14. The correct answer is D (Secure the airway early given the risk of rapid progressive edema.): Anaphylaxis can cause rapidly progressive airway edema that may make later intubation difficult or impossible, so early and decisive airway management is critical once compromise is suspected, performed concurrently with treatment of the systemic reaction. Delaying airway intervention until hypotension resolves risks losing the airway entirely as edema progresses. Extubation would be dangerous and inappropriate given the ongoing risk of airway compromise from an active anaphylactic reaction. Epinephrine should be given promptly and does not need to wait for the airway to be secured, since both interventions may proceed simultaneously by different team members.
  15. The correct answer is C (Enteral nutrition preserves gut mucosal integrity and reduces infectious complications.): When the gastrointestinal tract is functional, enteral nutrition is preferred because it preserves gut mucosal barrier integrity, supports gut-associated immune function, and is associated with fewer infectious complications compared with parenteral nutrition. Parenteral nutrition is not superior when the gut is functional and is generally reserved for patients who cannot tolerate enteral feeding. Multiple studies have demonstrated a difference in infectious complication rates favoring enteral nutrition, not equivalence. Parenteral nutrition as a first-line choice regardless of gut function contradicts established critical care nutrition guidelines.
  16. The correct answer is A (It individualizes PEEP to the patient's oxygenation needs while limiting overdistension.): Using an FiO2-PEEP table individualizes positive end-expiratory pressure to the severity of the patient's oxygenation impairment, aiming to recruit collapsed alveoli while still monitoring plateau pressure to avoid overdistension and volutrauma. Plateau pressure monitoring remains essential throughout PEEP titration and is not eliminated by using this table. Complete alveolar recruitment is not guaranteed in all patients, particularly in severe or heterogeneous ARDS. Hemodynamic compromise from elevated intrathoracic pressure remains a real risk with higher PEEP levels and must still be monitored.
  17. The correct answer is B (Abort the test, reconnect ventilation, and consider an ancillary study instead.): If significant hypotension, oxygen desaturation, or arrhythmia develops during an apnea test before the target PaCO2 is reached, the test should be aborted, the patient reconnected to the ventilator, and an ancillary confirmatory test considered since the apnea test cannot be safely completed. Continuing despite instability risks cardiac arrest and further patient harm. Giving a vasopressor to push through an unsafe disconnection is not standard safe practice for this test. Repeating the identical protocol without addressing the instability risks the same complication recurring.
  18. The correct answer is B (Involve palliative care or hospice services to support the transition.): Palliative care and hospice services specialize in symptom management, goals-of-care discussions, and coordinating comfort-focused transitions outside the acute care setting, making early involvement appropriate for a family expressing interest in this direction. Continuing indefinite ICU-level care does not align with the family's expressed goals for comfort-focused care. Discharging home without arranging support services would leave the family without needed resources for symptom management. Transferring to a general floor without changing care goals does not address the family's specific interest in comfort-focused, hospice-oriented care.
  19. The correct answer is D (Steroid-induced critical illness myopathy with thick filament loss.): Selective loss of thick myosin filaments on muscle biopsy in a patient exposed to high-dose corticosteroids, often in combination with neuromuscular blockade, is a classic pathologic finding of critical illness myopathy, presenting with proximal weakness and elevated creatine kinase. Guillain-Barre syndrome would show demyelinating nerve conduction changes rather than this myopathic biopsy pattern. Myasthenia gravis is diagnosed by neuromuscular junction testing such as repetitive nerve stimulation, not by muscle biopsy showing filament loss. Critical illness polyneuropathy is a nerve process with axonal sensory and motor involvement, distinct from this primary myopathic biopsy finding.
  20. The correct answer is C (Venovenous extracorporeal membrane oxygenation for refractory hypoxemia.): Venovenous extracorporeal membrane oxygenation should be considered in severe ARDS with refractory hypoxemia, generally when the PaO2/FiO2 ratio remains critically low despite optimized conventional therapies including prone positioning and appropriate neuromuscular blockade during the acute phase. Discontinuing ventilatory support is inappropriate in a patient without an established goals-of-care decision to withdraw treatment. Switching to high tidal volume ventilation would worsen ventilator-induced lung injury and contradicts lung-protective strategy. High-frequency oscillatory ventilation has shown harm in adult ARDS trials and is not a recommended next step for refractory hypoxemia.
  21. The correct answer is A (A balanced ratio of approximately 1:1:1 packed red cells, plasma, and platelets.): Massive transfusion protocols based on trauma outcomes data show that early balanced resuscitation with roughly equal ratios of red cells, plasma, and platelets reduces coagulopathy, transfusion requirements, and mortality compared with red-cell-predominant strategies. Delaying plasma and platelets until laboratory abnormalities appear allows dilutional and consumptive coagulopathy to worsen before it is treated. Crystalloid-heavy resuscitation before blood products exacerbates dilutional coagulopathy and should be minimized in hemorrhagic shock.
  22. The correct answer is B (Add fixed-dose vasopressin at 0.03 to 0.04 units per minute.): Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidance recommends adding vasopressin at a fixed low dose once norepinephrine requirements are rising, rather than continuing to escalate a single catecholamine, because this can reduce norepinephrine dose and catecholamine-related adverse effects. Vasopressin acts through a distinct receptor pathway and does not require ongoing dose titration to reach its effect. Continued fluid boluses in a patient already adequately resuscitated risk worsening pulmonary edema without improving perfusion.
  23. The correct answer is C (Feature 1 and Feature 2, plus either Feature 3 or Feature 4.): The CAM-ICU requires an acute onset or fluctuating course (Feature 1) plus inattention on a structured screening test (Feature 2), combined with either disorganized thinking (Feature 3) or an altered level of consciousness (Feature 4) to be scored positive. Feature 1 and 2 alone, without either Feature 3 or 4, is insufficient for a positive result. Requiring all four features simultaneously would make the tool far less sensitive than validated in clinical practice.
  24. The correct answer is D (An analgesia-first strategy treats pain before adding or increasing sedative agents.): Analgosedation prioritizes identifying and treating pain as the underlying cause of agitation before escalating sedative doses, since untreated pain is a common and correctable driver of ventilator dyssynchrony. This approach often reduces overall sedative requirements and shortens time to extubation compared with sedative-first strategies. Benzodiazepine-based sedation is specifically avoided as first-line therapy because it is independently associated with increased delirium and prolonged mechanical ventilation.
  25. The correct answer is B (A daily spontaneous awakening trial paired with a spontaneous breathing trial.): Pairing a daily interruption of sedation with a coordinated spontaneous breathing trial has been shown to reduce duration of mechanical ventilation, ICU length of stay, and overall sedative exposure compared with continuous, uninterrupted sedation. This wake-up-and-breathe approach allows earlier identification of extubation readiness. Maintaining constant deep sedation and routine neuromuscular blockade both increase time on the ventilator and are avoided outside specific indications.
  26. The correct answer is A (Citrate chelates ionized calcium, requiring calcium chloride or gluconate replacement.): Citrate anticoagulant in stored blood products binds ionized calcium, and rapid large-volume transfusion can outpace hepatic citrate metabolism, producing clinically significant hypocalcemia with hypotension, QT prolongation, and myocardial depression. Empiric calcium chloride or calcium gluconate should be administered and titrated to ionized calcium levels during massive transfusion. Hyperkalemia is a separate transfusion-related complication with a distinct mechanism and treatment, and additional plasma would not correct the calcium chelation directly.
  27. The correct answer is D (Evaluate for temporary mechanical circulatory support such as an intra-aortic balloon pump.): When cardiogenic shock persists with worsening perfusion despite maximal inotrope and vasopressor support, temporary mechanical circulatory support devices can restore cardiac output and end-organ perfusion while a definitive plan is pursued. Continuing to escalate already-maximal pharmacologic therapy or stacking additional catecholamines increases myocardial oxygen demand and arrhythmia risk without addressing the underlying pump failure. Corticosteroids have no established first-line role in cardiogenic shock management.
  28. The correct answer is C (Benzodiazepine infusions used for continuous sedation in mechanically ventilated patients.): Benzodiazepine sedation is one of the most consistently identified modifiable risk factors for ICU delirium, and current guidelines recommend minimizing benzodiazepine use in favor of non-benzodiazepine sedatives when continuous sedation is required. Early mobilization and daily sedation interruption are both protective, evidence-based strategies that reduce delirium rather than causing it. Dexmedetomidine is generally associated with a lower delirium incidence compared with benzodiazepine-based regimens.
  29. The correct answer is B (Prone positioning for at least sixteen hours per session.): In patients with severe ARDS and a PaO2 to FiO2 ratio below roughly 150 despite optimized lung-protective ventilation, prolonged prone positioning for at least sixteen hours per session improves oxygenation by redistributing ventilation and perfusion and has been shown to reduce mortality. High tidal volume ventilation directly contradicts lung-protective principles and increases ventilator-induced lung injury. Discontinuing PEEP would promote alveolar derecruitment and worsen hypoxemia rather than improve it.
  30.
