The correct answer is D (The lethal triad of coagulopathy, acidosis, and hypothermia, requiring active rewarming.): The combination of low pH, low platelets and fibrinogen, elevated INR, and hypothermia represents the classic lethal triad seen in massive hemorrhage, and each component worsens the others in a self-perpetuating cycle. Active rewarming with warmed fluids and blood products, along with continued balanced transfusion and calcium correction, is essential alongside efforts to achieve surgical or angiographic hemostasis. Treating coagulopathy or acidosis in isolation without addressing hypothermia will not break this cycle.
  31. The correct answer is A (Methylene blue infusion to inhibit nitric oxide-mediated vasodilation and vasoplegia.): Post-cardiopulmonary bypass vasoplegic syndrome is driven substantially by excess nitric oxide-mediated vasodilation, and methylene blue inhibits guanylate cyclase to reduce this pathologic vasodilation when catecholamines and vasopressin have failed. Adding another catecholamine when vasoplegia rather than pump failure is the problem provides diminishing returns and risks arrhythmia. Further volume expansion in a vasoplegic patient with adequate cardiac output will not correct the underlying loss of vascular tone.
  32. The correct answer is C (Hypoactive delirium, which is more common and often underdiagnosed without screening.): Hypoactive delirium, marked by withdrawal, reduced responsiveness, and quiet behavior, is actually more common in ICU patients than agitated hyperactive delirium and is frequently missed without routine validated screening tools like the CAM-ICU. Hyperactive delirium is the more obvious but less prevalent subtype. Mixed delirium involves alternation between both patterns rather than a purely quiet, hypoactive presentation as described here.
  33. The correct answer is D (Dexmedetomidine infusion, which preserves respiratory drive and allows arousable sedation.): Dexmedetomidine provides sedation and anxiolysis through central alpha-2 agonism while largely preserving respiratory drive, allowing patients to remain arousable and cooperative for neurologic and respiratory assessment during the transition toward extubation. Deep propofol sedation and scheduled midazolam both risk oversedation and are associated with worse delirium and ventilation outcomes. High-dose fentanyl alone would not reliably control anxiety or agitation and carries its own risk of respiratory depression.
  34. The correct answer is B (Passive leg raise with assessment of resulting stroke volume or pulse pressure change.): A passive leg raise produces a reversible autotransfusion of venous blood, and a resulting increase in stroke volume or pulse pressure reliably predicts whether a patient will respond hemodynamically to additional fluid, without the risks of an irreversible fluid bolus. Static measures like central venous pressure correlate poorly with volume responsiveness in most clinical settings. Fluid balance and urine output trends reflect cumulative status rather than dynamic responsiveness to further fluid administration.
  35. The correct answer is A (Intravenous hydrocortisone to address critical illness-related corticosteroid insufficiency.): Low-dose intravenous hydrocortisone is recommended for septic shock patients with ongoing vasopressor requirements despite adequate fluid resuscitation, reflecting management of critical illness-related corticosteroid insufficiency and often allowing more rapid vasopressor weaning. High-dose, prolonged glucocorticoid pulse therapy is not the recommended regimen and carries greater risk of adverse effects. Discontinuing vasopressors in a patient who remains hypotensive would worsen perfusion rather than address the underlying refractory shock.
  36. The correct answer is C (Tranexamic acid administered within three hours of the traumatic injury.): Tranexamic acid reduces bleeding-related mortality in trauma patients when given within three hours of injury, by limiting fibrinolysis during the acute hemorrhagic phase, and this ninety-minute time point falls well within the effective treatment window. Administration beyond this window loses benefit and may increase risk without improving outcomes. Recombinant factor VIIa is not recommended as routine first-line therapy given its cost and thrombotic risk, and desmopressin has a narrower role tied to specific platelet dysfunction rather than general trauma hemorrhage.
  37. The correct answer is B (Evaluate the patient for venovenous extracorporeal membrane oxygenation support.): When severe ARDS remains refractory to lung-protective ventilation, adequate PEEP, prone positioning, and neuromuscular blockade, extracorporeal membrane oxygenation can provide gas exchange support while allowing further reduction in ventilator-induced lung injury. Simply abandoning prone positioning or raising tidal volume above protective limits would worsen lung injury without addressing the refractory hypoxemia. Deepening sedation alone does not improve gas exchange in this setting.
  38. The correct answer is C (Antipsychotics do not reduce delirium duration but may help acute agitation safety.): Current evidence does not support routine antipsychotic use to shorten delirium duration or improve mortality, but agents such as haloperidol may still have a targeted role in managing acute, dangerous agitation to protect patient safety. Non-pharmacologic strategies including reorientation, sleep hygiene, early mobilization, and minimizing deliriogenic medications remain the primary approach to delirium management. Antipsychotics are an adjunct for specific safety concerns rather than a replacement for these prevention strategies.
  39. The correct answer is D (RASS negative two to zero, representing light and arousable sedation.): Targeting a light, arousable sedation level in the RASS negative two to zero range, rather than deep sedation, is associated with shorter duration of mechanical ventilation, reduced delirium, and shorter ICU stay in most mechanically ventilated patients. Deep sedation at RASS negative four to negative five should be reserved for specific indications such as severe ARDS requiring paralysis. An unsedated, agitated state at positive RASS values reflects inadequate rather than appropriately titrated sedation.
  40. The correct answer is A (Lactate clearance of at least ten to twenty percent within six hours.): Trending serial lactate levels and demonstrating meaningful clearance reflects improving tissue perfusion and has been associated with better outcomes in septic shock, making it a more informative resuscitation marker than a single blood pressure or urine output value. A single normal blood pressure reading does not confirm sustained adequate perfusion over time. Reaching a fixed central venous pressure target has not reliably predicted resuscitation adequacy and is no longer emphasized as a standalone goal.
  41. The correct answer is A (Use a 5.0 mm microlaryngeal tube with TIVA and neuromuscular blockade.): a small-diameter microlaryngeal tube preserves surgical exposure while TIVA avoids operating room contamination with volatile agent through an open airway, and paralysis prevents patient movement or coughing during instrument use near the vocal cords. Standard-diameter tubes obstruct the surgical view, and jet ventilation alone increases aspiration and barotrauma risk without airway protection.
  42. The correct answer is B (Rapid sequence induction with cricoid pressure and immediate suction available.): patients with post-tonsillectomy hemorrhage are considered full stomach from swallowed blood, and rapid sequence induction with suction ready and the patient positioned appropriately minimizes aspiration risk while securing the airway quickly. Awake fiberoptic intubation is difficult with active bleeding obscuring the view, and delaying surgery risks ongoing hemodynamic instability from continued blood loss.
  43. The correct answer is C (Remove the endotracheal tube, stop ventilating gases, and pour saline into the airway.): the established airway fire algorithm is to immediately disconnect the breathing circuit, remove the burning tube, stop all airway gas flow, and flood the airway with saline before reestablishing ventilation by mask. Increasing oxygen flow fuels combustion further, and a carbon dioxide extinguisher is never used inside a patient's airway.
  44. The correct answer is D (Extended PACU monitoring with continuous pulse oximetry and early CPAP resumption.): patients with severe OSA undergoing upper airway surgery are at high risk for postoperative airway obstruction and desaturation from residual anesthetic and swelling, so extended monitoring and prompt CPAP use once edema allows is recommended. Opioid-heavy regimens worsen respiratory depression risk in this population, and infrequent ward checks miss early deterioration.
  45. The correct answer is B (Awake fiberoptic intubation maintaining spontaneous ventilation throughout.): when a supraglottic mass causes critical airway narrowing, awake intubation preserves spontaneous ventilation and airway tone, avoiding catastrophic airway loss that can occur if paralysis or deep anesthesia collapses an already compromised airway. Rapid sequence induction and early paralysis both risk an unrescuable cannot-intubate-cannot-ventilate scenario in this setting.
  46. The correct answer is A (Perform emergency front-of-neck access with cricothyroidotomy immediately.): once mask ventilation, intubation, and a supraglottic airway all fail with worsening hypoxemia, the difficult airway algorithm mandates emergency invasive access rather than repeating failed techniques. Further laryngoscopy attempts or deepening anesthesia delay definitive oxygenation, and waking the patient is often not feasible once hypoxemia is severe and progressing.
  47. The correct answer is D (Barotrauma including pneumothorax from air trapping distal to the airway obstruction site.): jet ventilation delivers high-pressure gas without a closed circuit to monitor airway pressure, so when exhalation is impaired by a stenotic or obstructed airway, air trapping can cause barotrauma, subcutaneous emphysema, or pneumothorax. Malignant hyperthermia is unrelated to jet ventilation technique, and neuromuscular blockade delay is not a jet ventilation-specific complication.
  48. The correct answer is C (Inhalational induction in the sitting position with the surgeon and airway equipment ready.): maintaining the upright position during a gentle inhalational induction preserves spontaneous ventilation and avoids sudden complete obstruction that can occur when a swollen epiglottis collapses supine, and having the surgeon present allows immediate surgical airway if needed. Rapid sequence induction with paralysis risks losing the airway entirely before intubation is achieved.
  49. The correct answer is A (Administer a small dose of intravenous succinylcholine to relax the vocal cords.): when jaw thrust and positive pressure ventilation fail to relieve laryngospasm and hypoxemia is worsening, a small dose of succinylcholine reliably relaxes the laryngeal muscles to allow ventilation, with intramuscular route as an alternative if intravenous access is unavailable. Atropine treats bradycardia from vagal stimulation but does not break the spasm itself, and cricoid pressure has no role in laryngospasm management.
  50. The correct answer is C (Avoiding long-acting neuromuscular blockade after intubation to preserve muscle response.): electromyography-based recurrent laryngeal nerve monitoring requires intact neuromuscular transmission at the vocal cords, so avoiding or minimizing paralysis after intubation preserves the ability to detect a genuine electromyographic signal during nerve stimulation. Continuous neuromuscular blockade would abolish the muscle response needed for monitoring, and correct tube-to-cord contact must be confirmed, not skipped.
  51. The correct answer is B (Awake or inhalational induction preserving airway reflexes with suction immediately available.): preserving spontaneous ventilation and airway reflexes during induction allows the patient to protect the airway if the abscess ruptures unexpectedly, with suction ready to clear purulent material quickly. Rapid sequence induction with paralysis removes protective reflexes at the moment aspiration risk is highest, and deep extubation would similarly leave the airway unprotected.
  52. The correct answer is D (Explicitly confirming and documenting removal of the throat pack before extubation.): a retained throat pack left in place after extubation can cause complete airway obstruction, so a deliberate confirmed removal step, often supported by a checklist or visible marker, is essential before the airway is extubated. Suctioning alone does not guarantee the pack has been found and removed, and reversal of neuromuscular blockade does not address this specific risk.
  53. The correct answer is D (Slow, delayed vascular absorption from the tumescent fat compartment lowers peak plasma levels.): the dilute, epinephrine-containing tumescent solution is absorbed slowly from poorly vascularized subcutaneous fat, producing peak plasma lidocaine concentrations well below what the total dose would suggest, with peak levels sometimes not occurring until many hours after infiltration. Lidocaine is not locally metabolized in fat, and epinephrine reduces but does not eliminate systemic absorption.
  54. The correct answer is A (Optimizing intravascular volume status first and using vasopressors judiciously if still needed.): adequate volume resuscitation supports flap perfusion pressure without the vasoconstriction that potent alpha-agonists can cause at the microvascular anastomosis, and if vasopressors are required, lower-dose or mixed-action agents are generally preferred over pure alpha-agonists. Withholding treatment for hypotension risks flap thrombosis from inadequate perfusion pressure just as much as excessive vasoconstriction does.
  55. The correct answer is C (Combining mechanical compression devices with appropriately timed pharmacologic prophylaxis.): high Caprini score patients undergoing prolonged body contouring surgery benefit from combined mechanical and pharmacologic thromboprophylaxis, balanced against bleeding risk with appropriate timing relative to surgery. Bed rest actually increases VTE risk, and omitting prophylaxis entirely leaves a high-risk patient unprotected against a recognized cause of morbidity after body contouring procedures.
  56. The correct answer is B (Myocardial sensitization to catecholamines caused by the volatile anesthetic agent.): volatile anesthetics, particularly halogenated agents, sensitize the myocardium to circulating catecholamines, so epinephrine-containing local anesthetic infiltration can precipitate ventricular ectopy or arrhythmia during volatile anesthesia. Lidocaine itself is antiarrhythmic rather than causative, and this pattern reflects catecholamine sensitization rather than fluid administration or a vagal reflex.
  57. The correct answer is A (Bilateral transversus abdominis plane blocks placed at the end of surgery.): TAP blocks target the abdominal wall sensory nerves involved in abdominoplasty incisions and rectus plication pain, reducing opioid consumption as part of a multimodal analgesia plan. Interscalene and femoral blocks target the upper and lower extremities respectively and provide no benefit for abdominal wall pain, and a high spinal is not standard practice for this procedure.
  58. The correct answer is D (Impaired coagulation and increased surgical site infection risk from hypothermia.): intraoperative hypothermia impairs platelet function and the coagulation cascade while also increasing surgical site infection rates through vasoconstriction-mediated tissue hypoxia, both clinically significant in prolonged body contouring cases with large exposed surface area. Hypothermia does not improve platelet function, and its effects extend well beyond simply reducing anesthetic requirements.
  59. The correct answer is B (Increased systemic vascular resistance combined with decreased venous return from inferior vena cava compression.): elevated intra-abdominal pressure compresses the inferior vena cava, reducing venous return and preload, while also increasing systemic vascular resistance through direct mechanical compression and neurohumoral activation, together raising blood pressure while lowering cardiac output. Absorbed carbon dioxide can cause mild myocardial effects but is not the primary mechanism, and pneumoperitoneum does not eliminate sympathetic tone.
  60. The correct answer is C (Increasing minute ventilation by adjusting respiratory rate or tidal volume.): as carbon dioxide absorbed from the peritoneal cavity raises end-tidal carbon dioxide, increasing minute ventilation, typically respiratory rate, restores normocarbia during prolonged pneumoperitoneum. Reducing insufflation pressure may compromise surgical exposure and is not the first-line ventilatory response, and changing FiO2 does not address carbon dioxide elimination.
  61. The correct answer is C (Cephalad displacement of abdominal contents against the diaphragm, reducing functional residual capacity and lung compliance.): steep Trendelenburg combined with pneumoperitoneum pushes abdominal viscera cephalad against the diaphragm, reducing functional residual capacity and pulmonary compliance while increasing peak airway pressures. This position does not increase functional residual capacity or reduce intra-abdominal pressure, both of which would be the opposite of what actually occurs.
  62. The correct answer is B (Securing reliable intravenous access and confirming airway equipment beforehand, since patient access will be severely limited.): once the robot is docked, direct physical access to the patient, including the airway and intravenous lines, becomes severely restricted, so confirming secure access and having emergency equipment ready beforehand is essential in case rapid intervention is needed. Antibiotic timing and robot calibration are surgical team responsibilities not specific to this anesthetic access concern.
  63. The correct answer is A (Extensive subcutaneous emphysema from extraperitoneal carbon dioxide tracking along fascial and subcutaneous tissue planes.): insufflated carbon dioxide can dissect along fascial and subcutaneous planes, producing widespread crepitus and a carbon dioxide load large enough to exceed ventilatory compensation, sometimes requiring a surgical pause to allow gas resorption. Pulmonary embolism typically causes abrupt hemodynamic collapse rather than gradual hypercarbia with crepitus, and carbon dioxide does not cause an anaphylactic reaction.
  64. The correct answer is D (Carbon dioxide gas embolism from intravascular insufflation.): direct intravascular insufflation causes gas to enter the venous circulation and obstruct right ventricular outflow, producing sudden hypotension, a characteristic drop in end-tidal carbon dioxide as pulmonary blood flow is obstructed, and a mill wheel murmur from gas churning within the heart. Pneumothorax and myocardial infarction do not typically produce this specific combination of findings, and gas embolism is not an allergic reaction.
  65. The correct answer is A (Discontinue nitrous oxide, give 100% oxygen, and place the patient in left lateral decubitus with head down.): stopping insufflation, discontinuing nitrous oxide to avoid expanding the gas bubble, ventilating with 100% oxygen, and positioning the patient left lateral decubitus with head down helps trap air in the right ventricular apex away from the outflow tract, allowing aspiration if needed. Anticoagulation and increasing insufflation pressure have no role, and thoracotomy is not indicated for a venous gas embolism.
  66. The correct answer is D (Reverse Trendelenburg or head-up positioning with positive pressure preoxygenation.): head-up positioning combined with positive pressure during preoxygenation improves functional residual capacity and oxygen reserve in obese patients, meaningfully extending safe apnea time before desaturation. Flat supine positioning reduces functional residual capacity further in obesity, and skipping preoxygenation entirely increases the risk of rapid desaturation during induction.
  67. The correct answer is B (Diaphragmatic irritation from residual carbon dioxide referred via the phrenic nerve.): residual carbon dioxide trapped under the diaphragm irritates the phrenic nerve, which shares a cervical nerve root distribution with the shoulder, producing classic referred shoulder pain after laparoscopic procedures. This pain is not from direct joint trauma, positioning injury, or systemic local anesthetic absorption.
  68. The correct answer is C (Combined mechanical compression and appropriately timed pharmacologic thromboprophylaxis.): obese bariatric surgery patients undergoing pneumoperitoneum, which further reduces venous return, benefit from combined mechanical and pharmacologic thromboprophylaxis to address this elevated VTE risk. Relying on either modality alone, or omitting prophylaxis entirely, leaves a high-risk patient inadequately protected against a recognized major complication of bariatric surgery.
  69. The correct answer is D (Ask the surgeon to immediately release traction on the extraocular muscle.): the oculocardiac reflex is triggered by traction on extraocular muscles or pressure on the globe, transmitted via the trigeminal nerve to the vagus nerve, and releasing the surgical stimulus is the first and often sufficient step to resolve the bradycardia. Atropine or other pharmacologic treatment is reserved for bradycardia that persists despite stopping traction, and epinephrine at resuscitation doses is not indicated for this reflex bradycardia.
  70. The correct answer is C (Considering prophylactic anticholinergic administration and ensuring adequate anesthetic depth before extraocular muscle traction begins.): in patients with a history of significant oculocardiac reflex bradycardia, prophylactic anticholinergic medication along with ensuring adequate anesthetic depth and avoiding hypercarbia or hypoxia before muscle manipulation reduces recurrence risk. Withholding monitoring or communication with the surgical team increases risk rather than reducing it.
  71. The correct answer is B (Rapid sequence induction using a nondepolarizing agent with adequate onset time and cricoid pressure.): a modified rapid sequence induction using a rapidly acting nondepolarizing agent avoids the transient rise in intraocular pressure associated with succinylcholine fasciculations while still providing timely airway control to reduce aspiration risk in this full-stomach open globe patient. Awake intubation risks coughing and straining that could extrude ocular contents, and indefinite delay is not appropriate for an open globe injury requiring urgent repair.
  72. The correct answer is A (Succinylcholine causes a small, transient intraocular pressure rise, but no case of globe content extrusion has been clearly attributed to it.): although succinylcholine can transiently raise intraocular pressure through extraocular muscle contraction, current evidence has not clearly documented globe content extrusion attributable to this effect, and it may still be used when rapid airway control is needed and benefits outweigh theoretical risk. It is not entirely without effect on intraocular pressure, nor is it an absolute contraindication in every case.
  73. The correct answer is A (Inadvertent brainstem anesthesia from intra-arachnoid spread of local anesthetic along the optic nerve sheath.): local anesthetic injected into the optic nerve sheath can track along the subarachnoid space to the brainstem, causing apnea, unconsciousness, and contralateral pupillary changes, a rare but serious complication of retrobulbar block requiring immediate airway support and monitoring until it resolves. This presentation is not typical of a vasovagal reaction, systemic toxicity, or an expected block effect.
  74. The correct answer is C (Peribulbar block injects outside the muscle cone, generally lowering the risk of globe perforation.): peribulbar block deposits local anesthetic outside the muscle cone rather than within it, reducing but not eliminating the risk of globe perforation and other cone-related complications compared to retrobulbar injection, though it typically requires a larger volume for adequate akinesia. The two techniques have distinct needle placement and different, not identical, risk profiles.
  75. The correct answer is D (Address the underlying cause of coughing promptly, such as airway irritation or secretions, while reassuring the patient.): coughing and straining raise intraocular pressure through increased venous pressure and can be dangerous during open eye procedures, so promptly identifying and treating the cause, such as airway irritation, along with calm reassurance, is the appropriate first response. Converting to general anesthesia is a more drastic step reserved for cases where simpler measures fail, and coughing does meaningfully raise intraocular pressure.
  76. The correct answer is B (A smooth, deep extubation technique or use of intravenous lidocaine to blunt airway reflexes before emergence.): techniques that blunt airway reflexes, such as deep extubation when appropriate or intravenous lidocaine before emergence, reduce coughing and bucking that would otherwise raise intraocular pressure and risk disrupting a fresh open globe repair. Forceful coughing on the tube or extubating a fully paralyzed patient without adequate reversal both risk serious complications in this setting.
  77. The correct answer is C (Gradual activation of slow-conducting unmyelinated C fibers not well blocked by regional anesthesia.): tourniquet pain develops over time due to activation of unmyelinated C fibers, which are less reliably blocked by regional anesthetic techniques than faster myelinated fibers, producing dull, poorly localized pain and hypertension despite an otherwise adequate block. This is a genuine physiologic phenomenon, not simply a psychological response or a direct arterial compression pain pattern.
  78. The correct answer is A (Respiratory insufficiency, neurologic abnormalities, and petechial rash.): fat embolism syndrome classically presents with this triad following long bone fracture, typically emerging 24 to 72 hours after injury, caused by fat globules entering the circulation and causing microvascular occlusion and inflammatory injury in the lungs, brain, and skin. The other option sets describe findings more typical of infection, Cushing's reflex from elevated intracranial pressure, or hepatobiliary disease, not fat embolism syndrome.
  79. The correct answer is B (Embolization of marrow fat, air, and cement debris combined with monomer-induced vasodilation.): pressurization of the femoral canal during cementing forces marrow fat, air, and cement debris into the venous circulation, causing pulmonary embolization, while absorbed methylmethacrylate monomer can cause peripheral vasodilation and hypotension, together producing the hemodynamic instability seen in bone cement implantation syndrome. This is not a simple anaphylactic reaction, isolated vagal event, or direct powder cardiotoxicity.
  80. The correct answer is D (Increase inspired oxygen to 100%, ensure adequate volume status, and support blood pressure as needed.): supportive management with increased oxygen delivery, adequate intravascular volume, and hemodynamic support as needed addresses the transient hypoxemia and hypotension of cement implantation syndrome while it resolves, and communicating with the surgeon may allow a pause before further cement pressurization. Continuing additional cementing immediately or withholding oxygen would worsen the physiologic insult during this critical period.
  81. The correct answer is B (An adductor canal block targeting the predominantly sensory saphenous nerve.): the adductor canal block targets a predominantly sensory nerve distribution, providing effective analgesia for total knee arthroplasty while largely preserving quadriceps strength compared to a traditional femoral nerve block, which blocks motor fibers and can impair early ambulation. A full lumbar plexus block or isolated sciatic block either causes more motor weakness or does not adequately cover the anterior knee.
  82. The correct answer is C (Regional techniques may be used cautiously with breakthrough pain treated as a possible warning sign requiring evaluation.): rather than an absolute contraindication, many practitioners use regional anesthesia cautiously in at-risk patients, using dilute solutions, close monitoring, and treating unexpected breakthrough pain or increasing analgesic requirements as a signal warranting prompt clinical evaluation for compartment syndrome. Compartment syndrome can still develop despite a block, and maximizing dense blockade would be more likely to mask early warning signs, not less.
  83. The correct answer is D (Reduced intraoperative blood loss and lower venous thromboembolism rates with neuraxial technique.): neuraxial anesthesia for hip fracture surgery has been associated with reduced intraoperative blood loss and lower venous thromboembolism rates compared with general anesthesia in several studies, though overall mortality benefit remains debated and inconsistent across trials. Neuraxial anesthesia does not eliminate postoperative delirium risk entirely, and general anesthesia is not uniformly superior for hemodynamic stability in this population.
  84. The correct answer is A (Cerebral hypoperfusion from blood pressure measured at the arm underestimating pressure at brain level.): in the beach chair position, blood pressure measured at the arm is higher than the actual pressure at brain level due to the vertical height difference, so induced or unrecognized hypotension can produce cerebral hypoperfusion and ischemic injury that is not apparent from arm cuff readings alone. This position does not eliminate perfusion risk or typically cause hyperperfusion or elevated intracranial pressure.
  85. The correct answer is D (A balanced ratio of approximately one unit each of packed red blood cells, plasma, and platelets.): current damage control resuscitation favors a balanced transfusion ratio approximating one unit each of red blood cells, plasma, and platelets to address the dilutional coagulopathy that develops with large-volume crystalloid resuscitation and ongoing hemorrhage. Red-cell-predominant strategies or delaying plasma administration worsen trauma-induced coagulopathy and are associated with increased mortality in massive hemorrhage.
  86. The correct answer is B (Even a single episode of hypotension or hypoxemia.): in severe traumatic brain injury, even a single episode of hypotension or hypoxemia is strongly associated with worse neurologic outcomes by compromising cerebral perfusion pressure and oxygen delivery to already injured brain tissue, making avoidance of these events a top priority. Mild hyperglycemia, brief hypercapnia, or transient mild hypothermia are secondary concerns compared to the immediate danger of hypotension or hypoxemia in this setting.
  87. The correct answer is C (Hypertonic saline or mannitol administered to reduce cerebral edema.): hypertonic saline or mannitol create an osmotic gradient that draws water out of brain tissue, providing rapid reduction in intracranial pressure during an acute crisis. Prolonged aggressive hyperventilation can cause cerebral ischemia and is not used prophylactically, corticosteroids have not shown benefit in traumatic brain injury, and induced deep hypothermia is not standard acute therapy for this situation.
  88. The correct answer is A (Female sex, nonsmoker, history of PONV or motion sickness, and planned postoperative opioid use.): the Apfel simplified risk score uses exactly these four factors, female sex, nonsmoking status, history of PONV or motion sickness, and postoperative opioid use, with each factor present increasing risk substantially, and this patient has all four. Male sex, smoking, and advanced age are not part of the Apfel criteria, and procedure duration is not one of the four scored factors.
  89. The correct answer is C (Having a responsible adult available to accompany the patient home and provide supervision.): modern ambulatory discharge criteria emphasize stable vital signs, adequate pain control, and the presence of a responsible adult to accompany the patient home, while routine mandatory voiding or tolerating oral intake are no longer required for most low-risk procedures. There is no universally mandated fixed minimum recovery time once objective discharge criteria are met.
  90. The correct answer is D (Local anesthetic systemic toxicity from inadvertent intravascular injection or absorption.): perioral numbness, tinnitus, and seizure following peripheral nerve block injection are classic early central nervous system signs of local anesthetic systemic toxicity, requiring immediate treatment including lipid emulsion therapy and seizure management. This presentation is not consistent with a simple vasovagal reaction, anxiety attack, or expected local sensory block spread.
  91. The correct answer is A (Rapid sequence induction with cricoid pressure, two working suction devices immediately available, and readiness to position the patient head-down or lateral if bleeding increases.): a post-tonsillectomy bleed patient is functionally a full stomach with an actively bleeding, potentially difficult airway. Rapid sequence induction minimizes the time the airway is unprotected while preparation for immediate suctioning addresses ongoing hemorrhage. Inhalational induction and mask ventilation both risk aspiration by delaying definitive airway protection, and awake fiberoptic intubation is impractical and distressing in a frightened, bleeding child.
  92. The correct answer is C (Apply firm sustained positive pressure with jaw thrust at the laryngospasm notch, giving IV succinylcholine if this fails.): this presentation is complete laryngospasm, and the first-line maneuver is jaw thrust with sustained positive airway pressure, escalating to intravenous succinylcholine if the obstruction does not resolve. Nebulized epinephrine treats airway edema rather than glottic closure and would delay effective treatment. Volatile agent cannot reach the lungs when the glottis is closed, and a surgical airway is reserved for cases refractory to pharmacologic laryngospasm treatment.
  93. The correct answer is B (Subglottic jet ventilation through a small-caliber catheter, with barotrauma and pneumothorax as the most serious complication.): jet ventilation delivers oxygen through a narrow catheter that keeps the surgical field clear while still providing effective gas exchange, but high driving pressures can cause air trapping, barotrauma, and pneumothorax if exhalation is obstructed. Apneic oxygenation alone leads to progressive hypercapnia over time and is not a true ventilation strategy for longer cases. A laryngeal mask or standard endotracheal tube would obstruct the surgeon's access to the vocal cords.
  94. The correct answer is D (Awake fiberoptic intubation with topical anesthesia and the surgical team ready to perform an emergency surgical airway.): Ludwig angina distorts the upper airway and can make both mask ventilation and laryngoscopy impossible after induction, so an awake technique that preserves spontaneous ventilation and airway tone is safest. Having the surgical team ready allows immediate conversion to a surgical airway if the awake attempt fails. General anesthesia before securing the airway, whether intravenous or inhalational, risks total airway obstruction with no rescue option, and blind nasotracheal intubation is unreliable and can worsen tissue trauma in this setting.
  95. The correct answer is A (Maintain normothermia and euvolemia, minimize vasopressor use, and treat hypotension primarily with fluid administration.): free flap perfusion depends on adequate cardiac output and microvascular flow, and normothermia with euvolemia supports both while avoiding the vasoconstriction that pure vasopressor use can cause at the anastomosis. Liberal phenylephrine use can compromise flap perfusion through vasoconstriction of the pedicle vessels. Hypothermia and fluid restriction both risk reducing flap blood flow and increasing the chance of thrombosis at the microvascular anastomosis.
  96. The correct answer is D (Slow absorption from subcutaneous tissue with epinephrine-induced vasoconstriction delays peak plasma lidocaine levels for hours.): tumescent lidocaine is deposited into poorly vascularized subcutaneous fat with dilute epinephrine, which slows systemic absorption so that peak plasma concentrations, and therefore toxicity, can occur many hours after infiltration rather than immediately. Lidocaine is metabolized hepatically, not renally, so this does not explain the delay. Toxicity does not reliably occur within thirty minutes, and the vasoconstrictor actually slows rather than accelerates absorption from the injection site.
  97. The correct answer is C (Awake fiberoptic intubation via the nasal route with topicalization while the patient maintains spontaneous ventilation.): severe microstomia and absent neck extension predict an extremely difficult laryngoscopic view and potentially difficult mask ventilation, so preserving spontaneous ventilation until the airway is secured is the safest approach. Rapid sequence induction and deep inhalational induction both risk losing the airway with no ability to achieve direct laryngoscopy or adequate mask ventilation. A supraglottic airway alone cannot reliably be relied upon as the sole airway device when significant anatomic distortion is present.
  98. The correct answer is B (Deliberate hypotension should generally be avoided because reduced perfusion pressure can compromise flap viability.): free flaps depend on adequate perfusion pressure across a microvascular anastomosis, and deliberately lowering blood pressure risks reducing flow through the pedicle and increasing the risk of thrombosis. Deliberate hypotension does not improve flap perfusion pressure, it reduces it, contradicting option A. An arbitrary systolic threshold does not guarantee adequate flap flow, and the concern applies broadly to free flaps rather than only to those with a single pedicle.
  99. The correct answer is D (Increased afterload from aortic compression combined with decreased venous return from caval compression.): raised intra-abdominal pressure mechanically compresses the aorta and inferior vena cava, increasing systemic vascular resistance while reducing venous return, which together raise blood pressure but lower cardiac output. Absorbed carbon dioxide does not primarily cause direct myocardial depression at typical insufflation pressures. Vagally mediated bradycardia would lower rather than raise systemic vascular resistance, and hypercapnia causes pulmonary vasoconstriction rather than vasodilation.
  100. The correct answer is A (Confirm the presence of an audible air leak around the deflated endotracheal tube cuff.): prolonged steep Trendelenburg with pneumoperitoneum promotes facial and airway edema, and confirming a cuff leak assesses whether laryngeal swelling could compromise the airway after extubation. Fluid administration would not reliably resolve edema quickly and could worsen it. Extubating based only on following commands ignores the risk of airway obstruction from edema, and the patient should be returned to a neutral or supine position before extubation, not after.
  101. The correct answer is C (Desufflate the abdomen, give 100% oxygen, and place the patient in left lateral decubitus with head down.): this presentation is a carbon dioxide venous gas embolism, and immediate desufflation stops further gas entry while left lateral decubitus with head down helps trap gas away from the right ventricular outflow tract and pulmonary circulation. Increasing insufflation pressure would worsen gas entry into the injured vessel. Converting to open surgery or continuing the procedure without first addressing the embolism delays the critical initial resuscitative steps.
  102. The correct answer is B (Continue mechanical ventilation with increased minute ventilation until carbon dioxide clearance normalizes before extubation.): extensive subcutaneous emphysema reflects a large absorbed carbon dioxide load that can take time to clear, and delaying extubation while maintaining increased minute ventilation allows this to resolve safely. Needle decompression is reserved for signs of tension pneumothorax, which is not described here. Extubating with persistently elevated carbon dioxide risks postoperative hypoventilation, and bicarbonate does not address the underlying ventilation problem.
  103. The correct answer is D (Diaphragmatic irritation from residual pneumoperitoneum gas, a self-limited and common finding.): retained carbon dioxide irritates the diaphragm, and this pain is referred to the shoulder through the phrenic nerve, a well-recognized and typically self-limited consequence of laparoscopic surgery. The normal vital signs and unremarkable abdominal exam argue against an acute surgical complication like cholecystitis. Cardiac ischemia and rotator cuff injury are less consistent with the classic postlaparoscopic shoulder pain pattern occurring in an otherwise stable patient.
  104. The correct answer is A (Apply an appropriate level of positive end-expiratory pressure along with periodic recruitment maneuvers.): obesity combined with pneumoperitoneum promotes atelectasis and reduced functional residual capacity, and positive end-expiratory pressure with recruitment maneuvers helps reopen collapsed alveoli and improve oxygenation. Tidal volume should be based on ideal, not actual, body weight, making option B inappropriate as stated. Discontinuing positive end-expiratory pressure would worsen atelectasis, and simply decreasing respiratory rate does not address the underlying lung volume loss.
  105. The correct answer is B (Ask the surgeon to stop insufflation and desufflate the abdomen while preparing to give atropine if bradycardia persists.): rapid peritoneal stretch during insufflation can trigger a vagally mediated bradycardic reflex, and removing the stimulus by stopping insufflation is the first step, with atropine available if bradycardia continues. A beta blocker would worsen the bradycardia rather than treat it. Increasing insufflation pressure would intensify the vagal stimulus, and chest compressions are not indicated without evidence of inadequate perfusion or arrest.
  106. The correct answer is C (Avoid shoulder braces when possible and instead use a bean bag or footboard to prevent sliding.): shoulder braces placed over the brachial plexus can compress or stretch the nerves during steep positioning, so alternative methods such as a bean bag or footboard reduce this risk while still preventing the patient from sliding. Firm bracing directly over the acromioclavicular joint or medially near the neck increases rather than decreases plexus compression risk. Increasing the degree of tilt does not address the underlying mechanism of nerve injury from the braces themselves.
  107. The correct answer is A (Ask the surgeon to release traction on the extraocular muscle immediately and reassess the heart rate.): the oculocardiac reflex is triggered by traction on extraocular muscles, and releasing the stimulus is the first and most effective step, often resolving the bradycardia promptly. Epinephrine is an excessive response for reflex bradycardia and carries its own risks. Deepening anesthesia does not reliably block the reflex arc, and continuing traction without intervention risks progression to more severe bradycardia or asystole.
  108. The correct answer is D (Inadvertent brainstem anesthesia from injection tracking along the optic nerve sheath.): local anesthetic can track along the optic nerve sheath into the subarachnoid space and reach the brainstem, producing apnea, hemodynamic instability, and contralateral pupil changes, a recognized rare complication of retrobulbar block. Intravascular injection causes seizures or cardiovascular collapse rather than this specific pattern of contralateral mydriasis. Globe perforation typically causes local ocular findings rather than apnea and contralateral pupil dilation, and anaphylaxis would present with cardiovascular and cutaneous findings rather than this specific neurologic picture.
  109. The correct answer is C (Modified rapid sequence induction using a nondepolarizing agent such as rocuronium instead of succinylcholine.): succinylcholine can transiently raise intraocular pressure through fasciculations, risking extrusion of intraocular contents, so rocuronium at an intubating dose provides comparably rapid airway control while avoiding this risk. Inhalational induction is too slow and does not adequately protect against aspiration in a full stomach. Awake intubation with coughing and straining would itself raise intraocular pressure and risk globe content extrusion in an uncooperative or urgent setting.
  110. The correct answer is B (Discontinue nitrous oxide well before gas injection because it can diffuse into the bubble and raise intraocular pressure.): nitrous oxide is far more soluble than sulfur hexafluoride and can diffuse into the intravitreal bubble, causing it to expand and dangerously raise intraocular pressure. Increasing nitrous oxide near the time of gas injection would worsen this risk rather than reduce anything meaningfully related to awareness in this context. The interaction between nitrous oxide and intravitreal gas is well established, and substituting acetazolamide does not eliminate the need to avoid nitrous oxide.
  111. The correct answer is A (Coughing, straining, or bucking during laryngoscopy and intubation, which transiently raise intraocular pressure.): sudden increases in venous and airway pressure from coughing or straining can transiently raise intraocular pressure enough to risk extruding intraocular contents through an open globe, so adequate depth of anesthesia and neuromuscular blockade before laryngoscopy are important. Volatile anesthetics and opioids generally lower or have minimal effect on intraocular pressure rather than raising it. A properly placed supraglottic airway typically causes less airway stimulation than intubation, not more.
  112. The correct answer is D (Multimodal antiemetic prophylaxis such as combined ondansetron and dexamethasone given the high emetic risk of this procedure.): strabismus surgery is one of the procedures most strongly associated with postoperative nausea and vomiting, particularly in children, and combining agents from different drug classes is recommended for high-risk cases. Strabismus surgery does not carry low emetic risk, contradicting option A. Waiting until symptoms develop, or avoiding prophylaxis entirely, ignores the well-established evidence supporting proactive multimodal prophylaxis for this specific procedure.
  113. The correct answer is B (Tourniquet pain mediated by slowly conducting C fibers that are relatively resistant to local anesthetic blockade.): prolonged tourniquet inflation produces a dull, poorly localized pain and hypertension that becomes increasingly difficult to control with time, related to slowly conducting unmyelinated C fibers that are less reliably blocked by neuraxial local anesthetics. This presentation with an otherwise adequate sensory block argues against inadequate spinal anesthesia. Compartment syndrome would present with focal limb findings rather than diffuse pain under an inflated tourniquet, and systemic local anesthetic toxicity would present with neurologic or cardiovascular toxicity rather than isolated tourniquet-related pain.
  114. The correct answer is C (Bone cement implantation syndrome from marrow and fat embolization with associated vasodilation.): cementation and reaming of the femoral canal can force marrow, fat, and air into the venous circulation, producing hypotension, hypoxemia, and arrhythmia shortly after cement implantation, a well-recognized syndrome. Anaphylaxis to a cement additive is a much less common cause and would typically include cutaneous findings. Timing so closely tied to the cementing step argues against an unrelated myocardial infarction, and there is no described history of central line placement pointing toward pneumothorax.
  115. The correct answer is D (Supportive respiratory care with oxygen and, if needed, mechanical ventilation while the syndrome resolves.): this triad of hypoxemia, confusion, and petechial rash after a long bone fracture is classic for fat embolism syndrome, which is managed supportively since there is no specific reversal agent. Anticoagulation is not the primary treatment for fat embolism and should not be given empirically without appropriate evaluation. Corticosteroids have not shown consistent definitive benefit, and delaying fracture stabilization is generally not recommended since early fixation is associated with reduced risk of fat embolism syndrome.
  116. The correct answer is A (A regional technique such as spinal anesthesia combined with a fascia iliaca compartment block.): combining neuraxial anesthesia with a peripheral nerve block provides effective analgesia while substantially reducing systemic opioid requirements and their associated side effects, such as respiratory depression, sedation, and constipation, in frail elderly patients. Deep general anesthesia with high-dose opioids maximizes opioid exposure rather than minimizing it. General anesthesia or opioid-based analgesia alone without a regional component fails to take advantage of the opioid-sparing benefit of the regional technique. Large randomized trials such as REGAIN have not shown a clear reduction in postoperative delirium with regional versus general anesthesia, so opioid sparing, not delirium prevention, is the best-supported benefit of this approach.
  117. The correct answer is C (Keeping the head level with or above the heart and avoiding prolonged severe hypotension.): postoperative visual loss after prone spine surgery has been associated with venous congestion and reduced optic nerve perfusion, and keeping the head at or above heart level while avoiding prolonged severe hypotension are recommended risk-reduction strategies. A dependent head position below the heart would worsen venous congestion. Large-volume crystalloid administration has been associated with increased risk in some analyses, and deliberate hypotension is generally avoided in cases with this recognized risk.
  118. The correct answer is B (Pulmonary embolism from venous thromboembolism, a recognized risk after major orthopedic surgery.): major lower extremity orthopedic surgery, particularly knee and hip arthroplasty, carries a substantial risk of venous thromboembolism, and sudden dyspnea, tachycardia, and hypoxemia in this setting should prompt immediate evaluation for pulmonary embolism. An unrelated exacerbation of chronic lung disease is a less likely first consideration in this high-risk surgical context. Pneumonia typically has a more gradual onset with fever, and acute coronary syndrome, while possible, is less specifically associated with this postoperative timing and risk profile than venous thromboembolism.
  119. The correct answer is D (A balanced ratio of approximately 1:1:1 of packed red blood cells, plasma, and platelets.): current damage control resuscitation for massive hemorrhage favors an early balanced ratio of red blood cells, plasma, and platelets to address both oxygen-carrying capacity and coagulopathy simultaneously. Waiting for a hemoglobin threshold before adding other products delays treatment of the coagulopathy that accompanies massive hemorrhage. Favoring crystalloid over blood products, or omitting platelets, both fail to address the dilutional and consumptive coagulopathy that develops in massive hemorrhage.
  120. The correct answer is A (Permissive hypotension targeting a systolic blood pressure around 80 to 90 mmHg until surgical hemorrhage control is achieved.): in penetrating trauma without traumatic brain injury, allowing a somewhat lower blood pressure until surgical control of bleeding reduces the risk of dislodging early clot and worsening hemorrhage compared with normalizing pressure prematurely. Aggressive crystalloid resuscitation to a normal pressure can dilute clotting factors and increase bleeding. Titrating vasopressors to normal pressure and withholding all resuscitation both fail to balance perfusion needs against the risk of exacerbating ongoing hemorrhage.
  121. The correct answer is B (Avoiding even brief episodes of hypotension and hypoxemia to maintain adequate cerebral perfusion.): even single episodes of hypotension or hypoxemia are strongly associated with worse outcomes after traumatic brain injury because they compromise cerebral perfusion in an already injured brain, making avoidance of both a central management priority. Deliberately lowering blood pressure would worsen rather than protect cerebral perfusion. Aggressive hyperventilation causes cerebral vasoconstriction that can precipitate ischemia, and permissive hypercapnia raises intracranial pressure through vasodilation, both of which are counterproductive.
  122. The correct answer is C (Rapid sequence induction with a reduced dose of induction agent if hypotensive, and a rapid-onset muscle relaxant.): trauma patients are considered to have a full stomach regardless of reported fasting time, so rapid sequence induction with a rapid-onset muscle relaxant minimizes the unprotected airway interval, and reducing induction agent dose in hypotension avoids further hemodynamic compromise. Gentle mask ventilation before intubation increases aspiration risk by insufflating the stomach. Awake fiberoptic intubation is impractical in most emergent trauma airways, and delaying intubation to place a nasogastric tube is unsafe when airway protection is urgently needed.
  123. The correct answer is D (Tranexamic acid administered within three hours of injury to reduce bleeding-related mortality.): tranexamic acid inhibits fibrinolysis and has been shown to reduce mortality from bleeding when given within three hours of injury in trauma patients, with reduced or absent benefit and possible harm when given later. Recombinant factor VIIa is not used as routine prophylaxis and carries thrombotic risk. Protamine is used for heparin reversal and is not indicated empirically in trauma patients without a specific anticoagulant history, and plasma alone does not address hyperfibrinolysis as directly as an antifibrinolytic agent.
  124. The correct answer is A (The lethal triad of hypothermia, acidosis, and coagulopathy, addressed with active warming and hemostatic resuscitation.): these three findings together define the trauma lethal triad, a self-perpetuating cycle in which each derangement worsens the others, and management requires active warming, correction of acidosis through perfusion restoration, and hemostatic blood product resuscitation. The findings reflect a combined process rather than an isolated dilutional coagulopathy alone. There is no clinical basis presented for diabetic ketoacidosis or an undiagnosed infection explaining this specific combination of findings in an actively bleeding trauma patient.
  125. The correct answer is C (Angioembolization or resuscitative endovascular balloon occlusion of the aorta to control bleeding.): unstable pelvic fracture hemorrhage is often arterial and retroperitoneal, and when a binder alone is insufficient, angioembolization or aortic balloon occlusion provides targeted hemorrhage control. Removing the binder would release the tamponade effect and worsen bleeding. Exploratory laparotomy does not effectively address the retroperitoneal pelvic venous plexus and arterial bleeding that typically requires angiographic or endovascular control, and observation alone is inappropriate in a persistently unstable patient.
  126. The correct answer is B (Manual in-line stabilization by an assistant while the anterior portion of the collar is opened for intubation.): manual in-line stabilization allows an assistant to limit cervical spine motion while the anterior collar is opened to permit mouth opening and airway access during laryngoscopy. Removing the collar entirely and allowing unrestricted extension risks further spinal cord injury. Full neck flexion is contraindicated in a suspected unstable cervical spine, and blind nasotracheal intubation is less reliable and does not provide the same degree of controlled airway access as a direct or video-assisted technique with manual stabilization.
  127. The correct answer is A (Damage control laparotomy with abbreviated surgery, temporary abdominal closure, and staged definitive repair.): when physiologic derangement is worsening intraoperatively, an abbreviated damage control approach that controls hemorrhage and contamination, followed by temporary closure and staged return once the patient is resuscitated, improves survival compared with continuing a prolonged definitive operation. Continuing complete definitive repair risks worsening the lethal triad. Abandoning the operation entirely without controlling hemorrhage or contamination is unsafe, and transferring to interventional radiology does not substitute for controlling ongoing surgical sources of bleeding and contamination.
  128. The correct answer is D (Use thromboelastography or rotational thromboelastometry results to guide targeted component transfusion.): once active massive hemorrhage slows and point-of-care viscoelastic testing is available, transitioning from a fixed empiric ratio to results-guided component transfusion allows correction of the patient's specific coagulation deficits while avoiding unnecessary product exposure. Continuing a fixed ratio indefinitely ignores available laboratory guidance. Stopping all further transfusion or transfusing platelets alone does not account for the individualized coagulation abnormalities that viscoelastic testing can identify.
  129. The correct answer is C (Adequacy of postoperative pain control without excessive opioids and stable recovery on room air.): a high obstructive sleep apnea risk score is not by itself an absolute exclusion from ambulatory surgery, and the key determinant is whether the patient achieves adequate analgesia with minimal opioid use and maintains stable oxygenation on room air during an appropriate observation period. A high risk score alone does not automatically exclude every patient from ambulatory surgery, contradicting option A. The surgical procedure type does remain relevant to overall risk assessment, and regional anesthesia is not always feasible or required for every procedure in this population.
  130. The correct answer is B (Multimodal antiemetic prophylaxis with two or more agents from different drug classes given her high risk score.): this patient has four recognized Apfel risk factors, female sex, nonsmoking status, history of nausea or motion sickness, and planned postoperative opioids, placing her at high risk and warranting combination prophylaxis from different pharmacologic classes. Antiemetic prophylaxis is equally relevant to ambulatory surgery, not limited to inpatient cases. A single antihistamine dose or nonpharmacologic measures alone are insufficient for a patient with this many risk factors.
  131. The correct answer is D (Combining a peripheral nerve block with scheduled acetaminophen and a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug.): a multimodal approach that combines a peripheral nerve block with scheduled non-opioid analgesics targets pain through multiple mechanisms, reducing reliance on opioids for adequate pain control after ambulatory shoulder surgery. Relying primarily on a long-acting opioid increases the risk of opioid-related side effects without the benefits of a multimodal approach. Using opioids alone intraoperatively, or withholding all analgesia until after discharge, both fail to provide the preemptive and multimodal coverage that reduces overall opioid consumption.
  132. The correct answer is A (Mandatory oral intake of fluids and voiding before discharge for every patient.): current ambulatory anesthesia guidelines no longer require routine oral fluid intake or voiding as universal prerequisites for discharge in low-risk patients, since these requirements can unnecessarily prolong recovery time without improving safety. Stable vital signs, adequate pain control, and safe ambulation remain important and expected components of standard discharge readiness assessment. Removing the fluid and voiding requirement reflects updated evidence rather than a reduction in overall discharge safety standards.
  133. The correct answer is C (The patient should protect the insensate limb from injury and use assistive devices until sensation returns.): a long-acting peripheral nerve block will likely still be active at the time of discharge, so counseling on protecting the numb limb from thermal or mechanical injury and using assistive devices is essential to prevent unrecognized harm. The block is not expected to resolve before discharge given its 18-hour duration. Unrestricted weight-bearing is unsafe with an insensate limb, and omitting discharge instructions ignores the real risk of injury while protective sensation is absent.
  134. The correct answer is B (A trigger-free anesthetic technique with dantrolene and a prepared response plan available, along with extended monitoring.): patients at risk for malignant hyperthermia can often still be managed safely as outpatients using a trigger-free anesthetic, with dantrolene and an emergency response plan readily available and a period of extended postoperative monitoring. Susceptibility is not an absolute contraindication to ambulatory surgery when appropriate precautions are taken. Using standard volatile-based anesthesia would expose the patient to unnecessary triggering agents, and regional anesthesia is actually a preferred and safe option for this population.
  135. The correct answer is A (Discharge before full block resolution is acceptable if the patient receives clear instructions to protect the insensate limb.): current practice supports discharging ambulatory patients home before a peripheral nerve block has fully resolved, provided they receive thorough instructions about protecting the numb extremity, since waiting for complete resolution would unnecessarily prolong facility stay. Requiring full motor and sensory return or documented pinprick sensation before discharge is not standard practice and would negate much of the efficiency benefit of regional anesthesia. Avoiding peripheral nerve blocks entirely would eliminate a valuable analgesic tool without addressing the actual safety concern through counseling.
  136. The correct answer is C (The infant should be admitted for postoperative apnea monitoring rather than discharged same day.): former premature infants remain at increased risk for postoperative apnea for a period after birth based on postconceptional age, and at 46 weeks this patient still warrants overnight monitoring rather than same-day ambulatory discharge. Postconceptional age, not simply reaching term-equivalent chronological milestones alone, guides this risk assessment, so option A understates the ongoing risk. Regional anesthesia is not absolutely contraindicated and may reduce apnea risk in some contexts, and sedation alone does not eliminate the apnea risk that warrants monitoring regardless of technique used.
  137. The correct answer is B (Reduced postoperative pain scores and opioid consumption in the immediate recovery period.): a peripheral nerve block provides targeted analgesia that reduces both pain scores and the amount of opioid required in the immediate postoperative period compared with relying on opioids alone. Because opioid consumption is reduced, nausea and vomiting rates are typically lower, not higher, with effective regional analgesia. Peripheral nerve blocks are generally associated with faster, not slower, achievement of discharge criteria, and studies consistently show measurable analgesic benefit compared with opioid-only regimens.
  138. The correct answer is A (Total intravenous anesthesia with propofol instead of a volatile anesthetic agent.): propofol-based total intravenous anesthesia has intrinsic antiemetic properties and is associated with lower rates of postoperative nausea and vomiting compared with volatile anesthetic techniques, making it a useful additional strategy for high-risk patients. Nitrous oxide is itself associated with increased postoperative nausea and vomiting risk, contradicting option B. Longer-acting volatile agents do not reduce this risk, and avoiding antiemetic prophylaxis in a high-risk patient would increase rather than decrease the likelihood of nausea and vomiting.
  139. The correct answer is D (Overall functional status, cognitive baseline, and stability of comorbid conditions rather than age alone.): current evidence supports evaluating elderly patients for ambulatory surgery based on functional capacity, cognitive status, and how well-controlled their comorbidities are, rather than using chronological age as an exclusion criterion. Age alone should not automatically disqualify a patient with good functional status, contradicting option A. While arranging appropriate transport and support is important logistically, it does not override the primary clinical determination of surgical suitability, and insurance status is not a clinical consideration for anesthetic risk assessment.
  140. The correct answer is A (Prolonged surgical duration combined with poorly controlled postoperative pain or nausea.): longer procedures and inadequately controlled pain or nausea are among the most consistently identified predictors of unplanned admission after ambulatory surgery, reflecting both surgical complexity and failure to meet discharge readiness criteria. Marital status and social support may influence discharge planning logistics but are not the strongest clinical predictors of admission. The specific brand of intravenous fluid and an administrative triage category are not recognized clinical risk factors for unplanned hospital admission.
  141. The correct answer is C (Direct stimulation of the parasympathetic centers producing a vagal surge.): the electrical stimulus first activates the central parasympathetic outflow, producing a brief vagal-mediated bradycardia or even transient asystole lasting several seconds. This is followed by a much longer sympathetic discharge that produces the characteristic tachycardia and hypertension seen during the tonic-clonic phase. Baroreceptor reflexes and hypoxemia do not account for the immediate timing of this response. Myocardial depression from induction agents would not produce this biphasic autonomic pattern.
  142. The correct answer is D (Reprogram the pacemaker to an asynchronous mode before the stimulus.): electromagnetic interference from the ECT stimulus can be misinterpreted by a demand pacemaker as intrinsic cardiac activity, causing inappropriate inhibition of pacing, so switching to an asynchronous mode with a magnet or reprogramming prevents this during the procedure. Anticholinergics are often used deliberately to blunt the vagal bradycardic phase and are not contraindicated. A pacemaker is not an absolute contraindication to ECT when properly managed. Defibrillator pads should be available proactively, not placed only reactively.
  143. The correct answer is B (Etomidate.): etomidate has minimal anticonvulsant properties compared with propofol and barbiturates and is preferred when seizure threshold has risen or when seizure duration has become progressively shorter across a treatment course. Propofol and thiopental both shorten seizure duration due to their anticonvulsant effects. Dexmedetomidine is not used as a primary induction agent for ECT and can blunt the hemodynamic response needed for adequate seizure monitoring.
  144. The correct answer is B (Administer intravenous benzodiazepine to terminate the seizure.): a seizure lasting longer than approximately 120 seconds is considered prolonged and should be actively terminated, typically with a small dose of intravenous benzodiazepine, to reduce the risk of sustained cerebral metabolic demand and cardiovascular strain. Waiting until the next session does not address the acute event. Prolonged seizures are not simply observed. Repeating succinylcholine would not stop the electrical seizure activity, since it only produces peripheral neuromuscular blockade.
  145. The correct answer is B (Higher dose of succinylcholine than usual.): patients with fragile bones from severe osteoporosis or fracture risk benefit from a higher dose of succinylcholine to achieve more complete neuromuscular blockade and attenuate the intense tonic-clonic muscle contractions that could otherwise cause fractures. Anticoagulation and bisphosphonates given acutely do not address the mechanical risk of the seizure itself. Magnesium alone does not provide adequate neuromuscular blockade for this purpose.
  146. The correct answer is D (Low-dose rocuronium with a peripheral nerve stimulator to titrate blockade.): in patients unable to receive succinylcholine, a small titrated dose of a short or intermediate acting nondepolarizing agent such as rocuronium, guided by a peripheral nerve stimulator, provides adequate attenuation of the motor seizure while avoiding prolonged paralysis. Proceeding without any relaxant risks significant musculoskeletal injury. Higher succinylcholine doses will not overcome a true enzyme deficiency and risk prolonged apnea. A full intubating dose is unnecessary and would prolong recovery beyond what ECT requires.
  147. The correct answer is C (ECT can be performed safely with left uterine displacement and fetal heart rate monitoring.): ECT is considered relatively safe in pregnancy when standard precautions are applied, including left uterine displacement to avoid aortocaval compression and fetal heart rate monitoring during the procedure, particularly in later gestation. It is not an absolute contraindication. Awake ECT is not a standard or safe alternative. Fetal monitoring remains important because maternal hemodynamic changes and hypoxemia risk can affect the fetus even though the electrical current itself is localized.
  148. The correct answer is B (Aggressive hyperventilation lowering the arterial carbon dioxide tension excessively.): while mild hyperventilation before the stimulus can help lower the seizure threshold, excessive hyperventilation causing marked hypocapnia can paradoxically raise the seizure threshold and shorten seizure duration. Mild hypercapnia is generally not associated with shortened seizures. Preoxygenation itself does not shorten seizures. Spontaneous ventilation is common practice and does not inherently reduce seizure duration.
  149. The correct answer is A (Bilateral electrode placement.): bilateral electrode placement produces more robust antidepressant effects but is also associated with greater postictal confusion and short-term memory impairment compared with right unilateral placement, which spares the dominant hemisphere and produces fewer cognitive side effects. Bifrontal placement has an intermediate profile rather than exclusively low cognitive impact. Placement clearly influences the degree of cognitive side effects, so all techniques are not equivalent.
  150. The correct answer is C (Evidence of significantly increased intracranial pressure on imaging.): ECT transiently increases cerebral blood flow and intracranial pressure during the induced seizure, so a space-occupying lesion associated with significantly elevated intracranial pressure poses a substantial risk of herniation and is considered the most concerning relative contraindication. A small stable lesion without mass effect, a remote resolved hematoma, or asymptomatic mild ventriculomegaly do not carry the same acute risk during the seizure-induced cerebral blood flow surge.
  151. The correct answer is C (Absence of brainstem reflexes and a positive apnea test in the setting of an irreversible cause.): the clinical determination of brain death requires an established irreversible cause of coma, absence of all brainstem reflexes on examination, and a positive apnea test demonstrating no respiratory effort despite adequate carbon dioxide stimulus. An isolated electroencephalogram is a confirmatory ancillary test, not the primary clinical criterion. Absent motor response alone does not assess brainstem function. A Glasgow Coma Scale score reflects overall responsiveness but does not itself establish brain death.
  152. The correct answer is D (Diabetes insipidus from loss of antidiuretic hormone, treated with vasopressin or desmopressin.): brain death frequently disrupts hypothalamic-pituitary function, causing central diabetes insipidus with massive free water loss, polyuria, hypernatremia, and hypovolemic hypotension, which is treated with vasopressin infusion or desmopressin along with fluid replacement. Adrenal insufficiency does not typically present with this diuresis pattern and would not be treated with insulin. Hypothyroidism does not cause polyuria, and furosemide would worsen the volume depletion. Growth hormone deficiency does not produce this clinical picture, and water restriction would worsen hypernatremia.
  153. The correct answer is D (Intravenous methylprednisolone combined with thyroid hormone replacement, typically triiodothyronine, added to ongoing vasopressin support.): hormonal resuscitation protocols for brain-dead donors add corticosteroids and thyroid hormone, typically triiodothyronine or levothyroxine, alongside vasopressin to counteract the loss of pituitary hormone secretion and improve hemodynamic stability and graft function. Escalating a single vasopressor to very high targets does not address the underlying hormonal deficiency and risks organ ischemia. Therapeutic hypothermia is not a standard component of hemodynamic resuscitation in this setting. Discontinuing vasopressin would remove a key component of the hormonal replacement strategy.
  154. The correct answer is C (Organ procurement begins only after cardiac arrest and a mandatory observation period confirm death.): in donation after circulatory death, life support is withdrawn, and after cardiac arrest occurs a defined observation period, typically several minutes, is required to confirm the absence of any spontaneous return of circulation before death is declared and procurement begins. This donor does not meet brain death criteria, unlike donation after brain death. A wider range of organs beyond kidneys, including liver and lungs, can be recovered depending on warm ischemia time. Because life support is withdrawn, no ongoing general anesthesia is administered during the dying process.
  155. The correct answer is A (Loss of hypothalamic thermoregulatory function.): brain death eliminates hypothalamic control of body temperature, leaving the donor poikilothermic and prone to progressive hypothermia from heat loss without compensatory mechanisms such as shivering or vasoconstriction. An increased metabolic rate would raise, not lower, temperature. Warmed fluids would not cause hypothermia. Shivering requires an intact central nervous system response and does not occur after brain death.
  156. The correct answer is D (Spinal reflex responses can occur despite brain death, so volatile anesthetic or opioid may still be administered for hemodynamic control.): spinally mediated autonomic and even motor reflexes can persist after brain death because the spinal cord is not necessarily affected, and these reflex hemodynamic surges are managed with volatile anesthetics, opioids, or additional vasoactive medications rather than being interpreted as a sign of consciousness. Spinal reflexes do not invalidate a properly performed brain death determination. Withholding all anesthetic management would leave significant untreated hemodynamic instability during surgery. Additional paralytic alone does not address the sympathetic hemodynamic surge.
  157. The correct answer is B (Lung-protective ventilation with low tidal volumes and conservative fluid management.): donor lung management follows lung-protective principles similar to those used in acute respiratory distress syndrome, using low tidal volumes, appropriate positive end-expiratory pressure, and conservative fluid strategies to minimize pulmonary edema and improve the chances of successful transplantation. Large tidal volumes risk ventilator-induced lung injury. Aggressive fluid administration would worsen pulmonary edema. Eliminating positive end-expiratory pressure would promote atelectasis and worsen oxygenation.
  158. The correct answer is B (The request for donation should be made by a trained representative from the organ procurement organization, separate from the physician delivering the death notification.): decoupling the death notification from the donation request, and having a trained organ procurement organization representative lead that conversation, is associated with higher consent rates and reduces potential conflicts of interest for the treating physician. The treating physician making the request immediately can create a perceived conflict of interest. Consent is not inferred automatically in most jurisdictions without explicit authorization or a documented donor registry status. Risk management is not the appropriate party to lead this clinical and ethical conversation.
  159. The correct answer is D (Whether an intervention that does not benefit the dying patient can be justified solely for the benefit of a future organ recipient.): pre-mortem interventions such as heparinization or cannulation performed for the benefit of a future transplant recipient rather than the dying patient raise the ethical question of whether nontherapeutic procedures can be justified with appropriate informed consent from the surrogate, and most protocols require explicit family authorization for these specific steps. This is not a question of family authority to consent generally, confidentiality, or organ ownership.
  160. The correct answer is A (Insulin infusion titrated to a moderate glucose target to reduce inflammatory injury to donor organs.): hyperglycemia in brain-dead donors is common and is managed with an insulin infusion targeting moderate glucose control, since uncontrolled hyperglycemia is associated with increased inflammatory mediators and worse graft outcomes. Dextrose infusion would worsen hyperglycemia. Thyroid hormone is part of standard hormonal resuscitation and is not typically the primary cause requiring discontinuation. Completely withholding glucose-containing fluids is unnecessary and does not directly treat the hyperglycemia.
  161.
