The correct answer is A (Brain death interrupts vagal innervation of the heart, making atropine ineffective, so direct chronotropic agents such as epinephrine or isoproterenol are needed.): because the vagus nerve nucleus originates in the brainstem, brain death eliminates vagal tone to the heart, so atropine, which works by blocking vagal input, has little to no effect on donor bradycardia, and direct-acting chronotropic or beta-agonist medications must be used instead. Atropine is not absolutely contraindicated, it is simply ineffective for this indication. Bradycardia unresponsive to atropine is an expected finding after brain death, not evidence of returned brainstem function. It has no relationship to pupillary findings.
  162. The correct answer is A (Confirm the specific implant is MRI-conditional and screen for any other ferromagnetic objects.): before any patient enters the MRI environment, screening must confirm that implanted devices are labeled MRI-conditional or MRI-safe for the specific field strength used, and a thorough check for ferromagnetic objects on the patient, equipment, and personnel is essential to prevent projectile injury. Contrast administration should follow renal function assessment, not precede it. The intravenous catheter should remain in place for medication access, not be removed. Standard operating room anesthesia machines contain ferromagnetic components and are unsafe inside the MRI suite unless specifically MRI-compatible.
  163. The correct answer is B (Radiofrequency-induced heating of monitoring cables can cause skin burns.): looped or coiled monitoring cables and leads can act as antennas that concentrate radiofrequency energy during MRI, generating enough heat to cause skin burns, so MRI-compatible leads must be used and positioned without loops. Standard leads do not eliminate arrhythmia detection risk, they actually distort the electrocardiogram tracing due to the magnetohydrodynamic effect. Pulse oximetry can function with MRI-compatible fiber-optic probes, it is not universally impossible. Magnetic field strength does directly affect monitoring equipment performance and safety.
  164. The correct answer is C (Tension pneumothorax from lung puncture during needle advancement.): needle advancement through lung parenchyma during a percutaneous lung biopsy carries a recognized risk of pneumothorax, and acute hypotension with tachycardia during the procedure should immediately raise concern for a tension pneumothorax requiring urgent decompression. Contrast-induced nephropathy presents later with renal dysfunction, not acute hemodynamic collapse during needle placement. Local anesthetic toxicity from small-volume skin infiltration is uncommon and would present with neurologic or cardiac toxicity signs rather than isolated hypotension tied to needle depth. Malignant hyperthermia is not triggered by percutaneous needle procedures.
  165. The correct answer is C (Ensure remote monitors, alarms, and infusion pump displays are clearly visible from behind the shield.): remote-location anesthesia often requires the anesthesiologist to work at a distance from the patient for radiation protection, so all monitors, alarms, and pump displays must be positioned or extended so they remain clearly visible and audible from the shielded position to maintain the same standard of monitoring as the operating room. Relying only on visual chest movement is inadequate and does not meet standard monitoring requirements. Positioning the machine closer to the radiation source increases occupational exposure unnecessarily. Capnography can and should still function via extended sampling lines at a distance.
  166. The correct answer is B (Increased risk of airway obstruction and desaturation combined with limited immediate physical access to the airway during scanning.): children with obstructive sleep apnea and recent upper respiratory infection are at elevated risk of airway obstruction and desaturation under sedation, and the remote MRI environment limits immediate hands-on access to the airway if a problem occurs inside the bore, making vigilant monitoring and a clear rescue plan essential. Magnetic field strength is not reduced for pediatric patients and does not by itself lower risk. Deep sedation does not eliminate supplemental oxygen needs in a high-risk patient. Nephrotoxicity concerns relate to contrast administration but are not the primary factor increasing risk in this scenario.
  167. The correct answer is D (Epinephrine, given intramuscularly or intravenously as first-line treatment.): an acute anaphylactic reaction to iodinated contrast presenting with bronchospasm, flushing, and hypotension requires immediate intramuscular or intravenous epinephrine as first-line treatment to reverse the hemodynamic and airway compromise. Oral antihistamines act too slowly and cannot be reliably absorbed during an acute anaphylactic event. An inhaled beta agonist alone addresses bronchospasm but not the systemic hypotension. Corticosteroids have a delayed onset of action and are adjunctive rather than first-line for acute anaphylaxis.
  168. The correct answer is B (Maintain blood pressure near the patient's baseline to preserve collateral cerebral perfusion.): before recanalization, maintaining blood pressure close to the patient's own baseline supports collateral cerebral blood flow to the ischemic penumbra, and avoiding both hypotension and excessive fluctuation is a key goal during thrombectomy anesthesia. Aggressively lowering blood pressure to normal targets can compromise collateral perfusion and worsen ischemia before the vessel is opened. Allowing uncontrolled severe hypertension is not a standard goal and increases hemorrhage risk. Inducing hypotension before recanalization would further reduce perfusion to already ischemic tissue.
  169. The correct answer is D (That no personnel are in contact with the patient or fluoroscopy table during shock delivery.): as with any defibrillation, all personnel must clear contact with the patient and conductive equipment such as the table before the shock is delivered to prevent inadvertent shock transmission, and this remains true in the catheterization laboratory environment. Active fluoroscopic imaging during the shock is unnecessary and increases radiation exposure without benefit. Contrast injection should not continue during an arrhythmia emergency. Modern defibrillators and anesthesia equipment do not require full electrical disconnection from the outlet for safe defibrillation.
  170. The correct answer is A (Remove the patch before the scan and replace with an equivalent non-metallic formulation if therapy must continue.): transdermal patches with metallic foil backing can heat significantly during MRI due to radiofrequency energy absorption, causing skin burns, so these patches should be removed before scanning and an alternative non-metallic formulation used if ongoing therapy is needed. These patches are not free of risk in the MRI environment. A lead apron does not protect against radiofrequency-induced heating, since lead shielding is designed for ionizing radiation, not MRI fields. Increasing radiofrequency power would increase, not mitigate, the heating hazard.
  171. The correct answer is C (Honor the refusal of blood products and document a clear plan for managing the patient without transfusion.): a competent adult patient has the right to refuse specific treatments, including blood transfusion, even if that refusal carries significant risk, and respecting this decision while documenting a clear alternative management plan reflects the ethical principle of autonomy. Transfusing against an explicit informed refusal violates the patient's autonomy regardless of clinical severity. Canceling surgery outright is not required if a mutually acceptable bloodless management plan can be developed. Seeking a court order to override a competent adult's informed refusal is not appropriate in this scenario.
  172. The correct answer is A (Engage the patient preoperatively in a required reconsideration discussion to clarify which specific interventions should still apply during anesthesia.): professional guidelines require a required reconsideration process before surgery, in which the anesthesiologist discusses with the patient which specific resuscitative interventions, such as chest compressions, defibrillation, or vasopressors, should remain suspended or be permitted during the perioperative period, since many interventions used to treat anesthesia-related complications overlap with resuscitation. Automatically suspending or automatically honoring the order without this discussion fails to respect patient autonomy. Refusing to provide care to a patient with such an order is not appropriate or ethical.
  173. The correct answer is A (Disclose the error to the patient, explain what happened and what was done to correct it, regardless of the absence of lasting harm.): ethical and professional standards require disclosure of medical errors to patients even when the error results in only transient or no lasting harm, because patients have a right to know what happened during their care and how it was managed. Waiting for the patient to ask, documenting internally only, or withholding disclosure because harm was minimal all fail to meet the standard of honest, proactive communication expected after an error.
  174. The correct answer is B (Proceed under the doctrine of implied consent for emergency treatment while continuing efforts to reach a legal guardian.): when a minor requires urgent, potentially life-threatening treatment and a legal guardian cannot be immediately reached, the doctrine of implied consent for emergencies allows treatment to proceed in the patient's best interest while reasonable efforts continue to contact the guardian. Delaying necessary emergency surgery indefinitely risks harm to the patient. An adult sibling without legal guardianship generally cannot provide binding consent unless specifically authorized. Refusing all treatment without a court-appointed guardian is inconsistent with the emergency exception to informed consent.
  175. The correct answer is B (She likely has decision-making capacity for this decision because capacity is decision-specific and she demonstrates understanding, appreciation, reasoning, and choice.): decision-making capacity is assessed relative to the specific decision at hand, not as a global, all-or-nothing trait, and a patient who can understand relevant information, appreciate her situation, reason through options, and communicate a consistent choice demonstrates capacity for that decision even with a diagnosis such as early dementia. A dementia diagnosis alone does not automatically negate capacity. Court determination of guardianship is a separate legal process, not a prerequisite for every clinical capacity assessment. Capacity should be assessed based on the complexity and consequences of the decision, not simply the procedural risk category.
  176. The correct answer is B (Provide the temporary mechanical ventilation because the advance directive applies to terminal, irreversible conditions, not reversible perioperative complications.): advance directives are typically written to apply specifically to terminal or irreversible conditions, and a temporary, treatable perioperative complication such as brief ventilatory support does not fall within that scope, so standard supportive care should be provided as part of routine perioperative management. Interpreting the directive as prohibiting all life-sustaining treatment under any circumstance misapplies its intended scope. Routine reversible complications generally do not require emergency ethics committee consultation before treatment. Advance directives are not automatically voided by the perioperative setting.
  177. The correct answer is A (Delay the procedure and ensure the surgeon or an appropriate provider completes an adequate informed consent discussion before proceeding.): while obtaining consent for the surgical procedure itself is primarily the surgeon's responsibility, the anesthesiologist has an ethical obligation to advocate for the patient and should not proceed with anesthesia until the patient has genuinely understood the material risks, benefits, and alternatives of the planned procedure. Proceeding despite recognizing inadequate consent disregards this advocacy role. Having the patient sign a new form without additional explanation does not remedy the underlying lack of understanding. Simply documenting the concern without action fails to protect the patient's right to informed consent.
  178. The correct answer is A (Respect the refusal, document the discussion, and proceed with an alternative monitoring plan if the case can safely proceed without it.): a patient with intact decision-making capacity has the right to decline specific interventions, including invasive monitoring, after being adequately informed of the risks and benefits, and the anesthesiologist should document this discussion and adapt the plan accordingly if the case can still be conducted safely. Canceling the entire procedure is unnecessary if safe alternatives exist. Placing the line anyway after induction violates the patient's explicit informed refusal and constitutes battery. Reporting the patient for noncompliance is not an appropriate or ethical response to an informed refusal.
  179. The correct answer is B (The designated healthcare proxy's decisions, when consistent with the patient's known wishes, take priority over disagreement from other family members.): a legally designated healthcare proxy has the authority to make decisions on the patient's behalf based on the patient's previously expressed wishes and living will, and this authority generally supersedes disagreement from other family members who do not hold that legal designation. Choosing the more aggressive option to avoid liability is not an appropriate ethical basis for decision-making. Decisions should not be made by majority vote among family members outside the designated proxy. A living will does not become void simply because family members disagree with it.
  180. The correct answer is D (Informed consent should be obtained before any sedating premedication is given, since sedation can impair the patient's capacity to understand and voluntarily agree.): valid informed consent requires that the patient possess the cognitive capacity to understand information and make a voluntary decision, and administering sedative premedication before this discussion risks impairing comprehension and voluntariness, so consent should be obtained beforehand. The ability to speak does not confirm adequate comprehension or capacity. Sedatives can meaningfully impair judgment and memory even at low doses. Surgical consent does not substitute for a separate discussion and consent specific to the anesthetic plan and its distinct risks.
  181. The correct answer is D (Have a detailed discussion with the patient about which specific blood-related interventions she will and will not accept, since beliefs vary among individuals.): individual patients, including those who identify as Jehovah's Witnesses, vary considerably in which specific blood products or techniques such as cell salvage they find acceptable, so a direct, individualized discussion is essential rather than assuming a uniform position. Assuming universal acceptance or universal refusal of cell salvage risks violating the patient's actual wishes in either direction. Relying solely on generic hospital policy without an individualized conversation fails to respect patient autonomy and specific religious interpretation.
  182. The correct answer is C (Root cause analysis, a structured systems-based investigation method.): root cause analysis is a structured, systems-based process used after a serious adverse event such as wrong-site surgery to identify underlying contributing factors, including process failures like a checklist being verbally completed without genuine verification, rather than attributing the event solely to individual error. A malpractice liability assessment focuses on legal exposure rather than systems improvement. An individual performance review targeting one team member ignores the systemic factors that allowed the error to occur. A billing audit is unrelated to patient safety event investigation.
  183. The correct answer is D (Swiss cheese model.): the Swiss cheese model illustrates how adverse events typically occur only when the holes in multiple independent layers of defense align, meaning several safeguards must fail simultaneously for harm to reach the patient, which matches the scenario described. The plan-do-study-act cycle is a method for testing and implementing improvement changes, not a model of layered defense failure. Failure mode and effects analysis is a proactive risk assessment tool used before an event occurs, distinct from this retrospective description of layered failure. A just culture framework addresses how organizations respond to error without blame, not the mechanism of how multiple defenses failed.
  184. The correct answer is D (Sign-in, before induction of anesthesia.): the WHO Surgical Safety Checklist's sign-in phase occurs before induction of anesthesia and includes confirmation of patient identity, surgical site and marking, completed informed consent, and equipment or allergy checks. The time-out phase occurs just before skin incision and focuses on team introductions, procedure confirmation, and anticipated critical events. The sign-out phase occurs before the patient leaves the operating room and reviews the procedure performed, specimen labeling, and instrument counts. There is no formal debriefing phase built into the standard three-part WHO checklist structure.
  185. The correct answer is B (A never event, a preventable serious safety occurrence.): a retained surgical item is classified as a never event, a category of serious, largely preventable patient safety events that should not occur if established prevention protocols, such as instrument and sponge counts, are properly followed. This is not an acceptable known complication of the underlying procedure, an adverse drug reaction, or routine variation in care, since the entire premise of the never event category is that these incidents are considered avoidable with correct process adherence.
  186. The correct answer is C (The plan-do-study-act cycle, an iterative quality improvement method.): the plan-do-study-act cycle is an iterative quality improvement methodology in which a change is planned, implemented on a small scale, the results are studied against a target, and the process is adjusted before repeating the cycle, which matches the described sequence of protocol implementation, measurement, and refinement. Root cause analysis is used retrospectively after a specific adverse event, not for iterative protocol improvement. Failure mode and effects analysis is a proactive risk assessment performed before implementation, not a repeated improvement cycle. A sentinel event review specifically investigates a serious unexpected event resulting in death or major harm.
  187. The correct answer is D (Failure mode and effects analysis.): failure mode and effects analysis is a proactive, systematic method used to identify potential failure points in a process before an adverse event occurs, ranking each potential failure by severity, likelihood of occurrence, and detectability to prioritize interventions. A root cause analysis is performed reactively after a sentinel event has already happened. A morbidity and mortality conference reviews cases that have already occurred rather than proactively assessing an unimplemented system. A malpractice claims review focuses on legal case outcomes rather than prospective process design.
  188. The correct answer is B (A just culture that balances accountability with organizational learning.): a just culture framework distinguishes between simple human error, which should be addressed through system improvement and console rather than punishment, at-risk behavior that warrants coaching, and reckless conduct that may warrant disciplinary action, balancing accountability with a supportive, learning-oriented environment. This differs from a blame-free culture, which would eliminate accountability entirely even for reckless behavior. A uniformly punitive culture discourages error reporting and does not distinguish between error types. Autonomy-based decision-making refers to respecting patient choice, not to organizational error management.
  189. The correct answer is A (Conduct a structured internal review to identify whether implicit bias or systemic access barriers are contributing to the disparity.): when a healthcare disparity is identified along demographic or community lines, the appropriate response is a structured, data-driven internal review to identify contributing factors such as implicit bias, communication barriers, or unequal access to services like regional anesthesia, so that targeted interventions can be developed. Attributing the disparity to patient preference without investigation risks overlooking a real systemic problem. Discontinuing data collection would eliminate the ability to detect and address the disparity. Assuming a biological difference in pain tolerance is not supported by evidence and would reinforce rather than address inequity.
  190. The correct answer is C (Accuracy of informed consent and equitable quality of communication regardless of language barrier.): professional medical interpreters are trained to accurately convey complex medical information and reduce the risk of misunderstanding or omission that can occur when family members, who may lack medical vocabulary or may filter information, are used instead, improving the accuracy of informed consent and equity of care for patients with limited English proficiency. This policy is not primarily aimed at reducing costs, and using professional interpreters may actually take more time, not less. Written consent documentation is still required regardless of interpreter use.
  191. The correct answer is C (A healthcare disparity, a difference in care not explained by clinical need.): a healthcare disparity refers to a difference in the quality, access, or outcomes of care between population groups that is not explained by clinical need or patient preference, and a statistically significant difference in pain treatment after controlling for procedure type and reported pain scores meets this definition. This is not expected clinical variation, since it persists after appropriate adjustment. It should not be dismissed as random noise once identified through rigorous analysis. There is no valid physiologic basis for differing pain thresholds by race, and attributing the finding to biology rather than systemic factors is a recognized source of bias in pain management.
  192. The correct answer is D (Train residents to recognize how unconscious assumptions about a patient's background may influence clinical judgments and to apply structured, standardized approaches to reduce that influence.): effective implicit bias training helps clinicians recognize how unconscious assumptions related to race, gender, socioeconomic status, or other factors can subtly influence decisions such as pain assessment or treatment offered, and pairs this awareness with structured, standardized clinical approaches to reduce variability driven by bias. Applying an identical protocol regardless of legitimate individual medical differences is not the same as reducing bias and could itself cause harm. Focusing only on legal compliance ignores the clinical decision-making component. Physicians are not exempt from implicit bias, and limiting training to administrative staff would fail to address the clinical source of the disparity.
  193. The correct answer is C (Limited access to transportation and reliable follow-up care after discharge.): social determinants of health such as limited transportation, unstable housing, or lack of reliable follow-up support disproportionately affect low-income communities and can lead to delayed presentation for care and higher rates of unplanned readmission after ambulatory procedures due to inadequate postoperative support. Genetic predisposition tied to ancestry is not an established explanation for this type of disparity. Attributing the pattern to intentional noncompliance is a stigmatizing assumption not supported by evidence and ignores structural barriers. There is no valid scientific basis linking intelligence to socioeconomic outcomes in this context, and this option reflects a harmful and inaccurate stereotype.
  194. The correct answer is D (First case on-time start rate.): the first case on-time start rate specifically measures the percentage of first cases of the day that begin at their scheduled time, which is a key operational efficiency metric because delays in the first case tend to cascade throughout the entire operating room schedule. Total annual revenue reflects overall financial performance rather than a specific efficiency measure of the first case. The number of anesthesiologists employed is a staffing metric, not an efficiency measure. Overall hospital patient satisfaction is a broad quality metric not specific to first case timing.
  195. The correct answer is C (The time from when one patient exits the operating room until the next patient enters the same room.): turnover time specifically refers to the interval between successive patients using the same operating room, encompassing room cleaning, equipment setup, and any other preparation needed before the next patient arrives, and reducing this interval is a common target for improving operating room throughput. The total time a patient spends under anesthesia describes case duration, not turnover. Instrument sterilization time in central processing is a related but distinct logistical process. The interval between scheduling and performance of a case describes scheduling lead time, not turnover time.
  196. The correct answer is A (A subsidy arrangement to compensate for unprofitable coverage obligations.): a hospital stipend or subsidy is a common practice management arrangement in which the hospital directly compensates the anesthesia group for maintaining coverage of call responsibilities or lower-volume, less profitable cases that would otherwise not generate adequate revenue through billing alone. A pure fee-for-service model relies solely on billing for services rendered without a supplemental hospital payment. Capitation involves a fixed per-patient payment regardless of services used, which is a different reimbursement structure. A global surgical package bundles the surgeon's fee with related services, which is distinct from an anesthesia group's stipend arrangement with a hospital.
  197. The correct answer is A (The anesthesiologist must be involved in specific key steps of each case.): accepted medical direction standards require the anesthesiologist to personally perform or be involved in specific defined steps, such as the preanesthetic examination and evaluation, and to remain immediately available for the concurrently directed cases, typically limited to a defined maximum number of rooms. There is a defined limit to the number of concurrent cases that qualify for medical direction billing, so directing an unlimited number is not compliant. Medical direction does not require continuous physical presence throughout the entire case, which would instead describe personally performed anesthesia. These requirements are defined by regulatory and reimbursement standards, not simply left to individual hospital discretion.
  198. The correct answer is C (Implement standardized, protocol-based pain management order sets applied consistently across all patients.): standardized, evidence-based order sets applied uniformly across the patient population reduce the opportunity for unconscious bias to influence individual prescribing decisions, helping to close disparities in pain treatment that arise from variable, subjective clinical judgment. Relying entirely on unstructured individual judgment without standardization is the pattern most associated with bias-driven variation in the first place. Restricting improved protocols only to certain communities would not address the systemic issue and could create new inequities. Eliminating pain score documentation would remove important clinical information rather than reduce bias.
  199. The correct answer is A (It allows the department to detect disparities in care quality that might otherwise be masked by aggregate data.): stratifying quality metrics by demographic characteristics such as race, ethnicity, language, or socioeconomic status allows a department to identify disparities in outcomes or experience that would be hidden if only overall aggregate rates were examined, since good average performance can coexist with significant subgroup inequities. This stratification has clinical as well as administrative value and is not solely a billing requirement. It does not eliminate the need for root cause analysis when a specific adverse event occurs. It complements rather than replaces the collection of patient satisfaction data.
  200. The correct answer is A (Strategic planning based on market and operational assessment.): evaluating case volume trends, payer mix, and competitive pressures such as a new ambulatory surgery center is a core component of strategic planning in practice management, used to guide decisions about growth, service lines, and long-term sustainability of the anesthesia practice. Root cause analysis is used to investigate a specific adverse safety event, not general market conditions. Failure mode and effects analysis evaluates process or equipment failure risk, not business strategy. An institutional review board protocol review pertains to research oversight and is unrelated to practice management planning.
